The Gleaning Ground, Part One

Going In After Her

Chapter 1. Outside

Outside, there's no such word as standing.

I didn't know the word until I was sixteen. I'd heard it — people from
the city used it sometimes. But I didn't know it was a word you
attached to a person. I thought it was a word you attached to things.
This wheat has standing, that wheat doesn't. Something like that.

By the time I learned it was a word for people, I was already standing
at the city gate.

Our settlement had no name.

It was never a problem. A name is only useful when someone far away
comes looking for you, and no one ever came looking for us from far
away.

There were twenty-two houses. I could count all twenty-two, and I knew
the name of everyone who lived in them. I knew whose door got knocked
on at night, and I had a rough idea of who wouldn't make it through
winter.

I can still count those twenty-two houses in order, even now. For a
while after I came into the city, whenever I couldn't sleep, I counted
them. It was the only list I had.

We planted a little wheat, kept a few goats, drew water from the
channel. When the harvest failed, we went hungry. Hunger was a bad
thing, but it wasn't a strange thing.

The strange thing was the city.

They said no one in the city ever went hungry. I didn't believe it for
a long time. How could no one go hungry in a year the wheat failed?
Wheat was something heaven gave, or didn't.

Later I learned it was true. In the city, truly, no one goes hungry.
And I learned why that's a frightening thing to say.

People from the city didn't look at us with pity. That surprised me.
I'd expected them to feel sorry for us, but they didn't. They looked
at us the way you'd look at something that needed explaining.

Once, one of them stood looking at our field for a long time, then
said:

"…Why do you spill so much here?"

I didn't know what he meant.

"…You drop it while you're reaping. There, and there." He pointed.
"Add it all up and that's a sack, easy."

"…We don't pick up what falls," I said.

"…Why not?"

I couldn't answer. I'd never once thought about why. You didn't pick
up what fell. Everyone did it that way. My grandmother had done it
that way, and hers before her, probably.

He tilted his head and wrote something down.

I didn't wonder for a long time what he wrote. Now I can guess. He
probably wrote: loss rate.

I entered that household when I was fourteen.

Outside, marriage comes early. You need hands to work, and you never
know when someone might be gone. Some people marry for love, but
mostly those two reasons come first.

The household had a mother and two sons. The father, they said, had
died some years before. I married the elder son, and not long after,
the younger married a girl from another house.

The mother's name was Glyke.

I said that name several times a day for two years. I didn't know it
would change into another name. I thought names didn't change. That's
how it is outside. Outside, no one ever asks to have their name
changed.

Glyke wasn't a talkative person. But she was a person who laughed. She
laughed even on days with nothing to laugh about. At first I thought
it was forced, but living with her, I learned it wasn't. She really
did find things funny.

There was a year we lost the whole crop. A late frost came. In three
days the field turned black.

I sat at the edge of the field and cried. I was fourteen.

Glyke came and sat beside me. After a long while, she said,

"…This year's wheat had a temper on it."

I looked at her, not understanding.

"…Everyone else waited, and it came out early, all on its own," Glyke
said. "Being in a hurry never pays, anywhere."

And she laughed.

That was the first time I laughed. Someone had once told me you go
ugly when you laugh through tears, but Glyke never said that. She just
laughed with me.

I think it was that laugh, not the marriage, that made me one of
that household.

My own family's house was two doors down. So even married, I hadn't
gone far.

My mother said this, the day I married:

"…That mother is a good person."

"…I know."

"…What you know and what I know are different," my mother said.
"I've seen that woman laugh after losing something."

That day, I let the remark pass.

Two years later I would remember it again. And a few months after
that, I would watch the laugh stop. My mother said she'd seen the
woman laugh after losing something. She never told me how much a
person has to lose before it stops.

I won't write my husband's name here.

There's a reason I won't. I'll set it down later. For now I'll only
note this: in this record, I will not call him by name, not once.

He had big hands. So big that whatever he held looked small. A water
jar looked small in them. A sickle looked small in them.

He spoke slowly. He'd say a word, wait a long while, then say another.
At first I couldn't stand the gaps and kept filling them myself. Then
at some point I just started waiting.

I learned waiting from him. I became a person who waits, for the rest
of my life. A person who watches nothing fall in the corner of a
field and still waits for next year. I know where that came from.

We lived together two years.

Some people would call two years short. I don't. Two years is enough
to know a person completely — which side he slept on, what sound woke
him, which finger moved first when he was angry.

I knew him completely.

In the autumn of our first married year, he taught me to use a sickle.
Until then, in my own family, I'd been the one who bound sheaves, not
the one who cut.

He watched me for a while, then said,

"…You're doing it too clean."

"…What's wrong with that?"

"…Clean like that, your wrist gives out," he said. "Spill a little."

"…That's a waste."

He waited a long while before answering. As he always did.

"…What you spill isn't wasted."

"…Then what is it?"

Another long while passed.

"…It's left behind."

I took it for a joke, that day. I thought he just meant to spare my
wrist. He was good at that — saying something that sounded like
nothing, that turned out, later, to be everything.

Two years later I would use that line again, standing in front of a
field where nothing fell. By then I knew he hadn't been joking. There
was still, in that world, a person who knew that spilling and leaving
something behind were the same act.

The day he died was a day nothing happened.

That's the strangest part. I wish there'd been something — an omen,
some unusual word. There wasn't.

He went out in the morning. A section of the channel bank had
collapsed, he said, and needed fixing. Three of them went — him, his
brother, and a neighbor.

At midday the neighbor came running back alone.

I won't set down what happened after that. I could. I'm choosing not
to. This record isn't being written for that.

I'll set down only this. After that day, I could never again say the
words a day nothing happened. There is no such day. There's only the
evening, when you learn what the day held.

His brother lived four more months.

The injury from the bank never healed. At first it seemed like it
would. By the second month he was walking. By the third he was back in
bed, and by the fourth he died.

So this is how Glyke lost them. A husband, two sons. In order, across
several years, one at a time.

I used to think losing people one at a time was easier than losing
them all at once. I don't think so now. Losing them all at once, you
fall apart once. Losing them one at a time, you have to get up again,
every time. Getting up is harder than falling.

Glyke got up all three times.

The third time took a long while.

After the brother died, his wife stayed on with us for a while. Her
name was Daphne. A year younger than me.

The three of us were left in that house. Three women. Two of us had
belonged to that household for less than two years.

At night we didn't talk much. Not for lack of things to say — I think
it was that once we started, we wouldn't know where to stop.

Once Daphne woke crying. She bit the blanket to keep from making a
sound.

Glyke sat up. But she didn't go to her. She just sat there.

At the time I wondered why she didn't go.

Now I know. It wasn't that she couldn't. It was that she chose not
to. Glyke knew that if she went to her, Daphne would stop crying. And
she knew that night was not a night that should be stopped.

Everyone in that household knew things like this — when to reach out
a hand and when not to. I learned it here. In the city, no one knew
it.

It was some two months after the brother died.

A person came from the city. Someone who came twice a year or so, to
do what he called a Roll renewal check. Even living outside, if you
were originally from the city your name stayed on the Roll, and it had
to be confirmed the name was still valid.

That was the first time I properly heard the word Roll.

He took out a device and entered Glyke's name. He looked at something
for a long while. Then he nodded, wrote something down, and left.

Glyke watched his back for a long time.

"…What did he say?" I asked.

"…He didn't say anything," Glyke said.

"…So it's fine, then?"

Glyke looked at me. And for the first time, she didn't laugh.

"…No," Glyke said. "The number went down."

I didn't know what she meant.

"…What number?"

"…Mine," Glyke said.

I really didn't understand what that meant, then. People had
died — why would the number of the one left behind go down? The ones
who died were them. Why was Glyke the one being cut?

Now I know. That's how this world counts a person. The Roll doesn't
count a person alone. It counts a person together with what belongs
to them. So much for a husband, so much for a son, so much for
hands that work. So Glyke hadn't only lost three people. The number
attached to those three had vanished along with them.

The grief came from outside. The arithmetic came from the city.

That night I asked Glyke,

"…What happens when the number goes down?"

"…Nothing happens," Glyke said.

"…Then why worry?"

Glyke looked at me.

"…That nothing happens is exactly the problem," Glyke said. "If
someone takes something from you, at least you can say you were
robbed. When someone just gives you nothing, there's nowhere to say
it."

I didn't understand.

"…What's the difference?"

Glyke thought for a moment, then said,

"…When you married into this house, what did I give you?"

"…A great deal."

"…I gave it because I had something to give," Glyke said. "Once I
have nothing to give, I become a person who's done nothing for you.
Not a person who's wronged you. A person who's done nothing. But from
where you're standing, receiving, it looks exactly the same."

I would think back on this countless times after I entered the city.
The Roll never wrongs anyone. It simply does nothing. Glyke already
knew that, before she ever went back in.

That winter, Glyke sat a great deal.

Not that she stopped working. She did everything that needed doing.
But when the work was done, she sat in the doorway and looked in one
direction. Always the same direction.

I knew which direction it was. Toward the city.

I never said anything. Asking would have meant she had to answer, and
I didn't want to make her.

One evening, Glyke spoke first.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…I've been thinking about where I want to be buried."

I set down what I was holding.

"…Don't say things like that."

"…No, listen," Glyke said. "This isn't a sad thing to say. It's a
thing you say when you're getting ready."

I couldn't say anything.

I didn't believe, then, that it was a thing you say when you're
getting ready. I believe it now. Glyke really was getting ready. I
don't think she understood how frightening it is, to be near someone
who is getting ready.

Glyke looked toward the city again.

"…My people are all there," Glyke said. "All three of them."

I knew what she meant. All three were in the city cemetery. Even
someone who died outside, if they were city-born, was carried back
and buried there. It was something like an old agreement between the
outside and the city.

"…I'm the only one left here," Glyke said.

And she laughed. This time she laughed, but it was a different laugh
than before.

I remember that laugh. It was the last time Glyke ever laughed as
Glyke.

After that, she asked to have her name changed.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. Three

_Previously_ — In the outer settlement, Xene married into Glyke's
household at fourteen. She lost her husband, and not long after, his
brother, to an accident at the water channel. Glyke lost a husband
and two sons in order, and each time, her standing on the city Roll
quietly fell. That winter, Glyke looks toward the city and laughs — a
different laugh than before.

That winter was the first time the number three frightened me.

Three is a small number, ordinarily. Three fingers, three steps. But
that winter I learned how large three becomes once it's attached to a
person. Glyke lost three — a husband, two sons. I witnessed only two
of the losses. The first happened before I was born, or at least
before I belonged to that household.

I mean to set down these three losses in order now. There's something
you can only understand that way. Told all at once, it becomes she
lost a great deal. Told in order, it becomes she had to get up,
every single time. Those are different stories.

I'll set down a great many numbers in the course of this book —
standing scores, loss rates, renewal dates. Every one of them will
come out clean, precise to the decimal. But I want to note here, in
this chapter, that behind every number is something like this three.
Numbers don't lie. They simply don't say what stands behind them.

The first was Glyke's husband.

I never met him. He was already gone by the time I entered the
household. When I first heard that the father had died some years
before, I took it as a passing detail. If a household you're marrying
into has no father, you assume that's simply how it is.

I heard the rest later, from Daphne. Daphne had come to this household
around the same time I had — but she'd been from this village before
she married in, so she knew more of the story.

"…It was the flood, they say," Daphne told me. "He was fixing the
roof."

"…Fixing the roof, and the flood?"

"…He fell while he was up there, and it happened that the ditch below
had swollen with the flood."

I had nothing to say to that. Falling from a roof was an accident. The
ditch swollen below him was an accident. Two accidents, each one alone
nothing, that met and became a death.

For a long time after, I stopped trusting the word accident*. An
accident never comes alone. Accidents stack. A roof grown old, rain
that particular day, being in that particular place at that particular
hour — each one nothing, on its own. Stacked, they kill a person.*

Glyke told me the story herself, just once. I hadn't asked. She
brought it up one evening on her own.

"…Even falling off that roof, he was quiet about it," Glyke said. "He
didn't cry out."

"…How do you know?"

"…Because I was in the yard," Glyke said. "I watched him fall. There
was no sound. He just fell."

That frightened me. A person falling without a sound.

Now I know that wasn't what Glyke meant. She was already, then,
telling me what it is like for something to happen so fast there's no
time even to cry out. I would come to understand it again, years
later — when I learned that this is exactly how the Roll erases a
person.

Glyke said she'd managed alone for a long time after that. The boys
were still small. The field was more than one person could work, and
she never made it smaller.

"…Why didn't you make it smaller?" I asked.

"…Because then it wouldn't be the field he made," Glyke said. "He was
the one who laid out its shape. I wanted to leave it as it was."

That made me think of the small pile of stones in the corner of the
yard. I'd once asked who had built it, and no one had given me a
straight answer. That day I finally understood — it was near the
place where he'd been fixing the roof.

I saw that pile of stones for the last time the day we left for the
city. No one suggested moving it. That household had a way of drawing
the line between what to leave and what to carry. The Roll has no such
line. The Roll either takes everything, or erases everything.

The second and third I've already written. My husband, and his
brother.

Both began the same day, at the same channel, with the same accident.
What came after was different. My husband's ended that day. His
brother's dragged on four more months.

I won't write those four months again. I already have. But there's one
thing from those four months I haven't set down yet.

On the second month, someone came from the city again. Not the regular
check — this one came separately. News that the brother was injured
had already reached the city.

That struck me as strange. It was three days' walk from our settlement
to the city. And yet the news had gotten there ahead of me.

"…Who told them?" I asked.

"…No one told them," Glyke said. "They just know."

"…How, if no one went?"

Glyke looked at me for a long while.

"…I don't know either," Glyke said. "I only know that they know."

At the time, that didn't frighten me. Now it does. The Roll knows
without sending anyone. By what path it knows, I never learned
exactly, not by the end of this book. I only confirmed, again and
again, that it does.

The person who came that day took out a device, checked something, and
asked,

"…You have two married sons?"

"…Yes."

"…One has already been processed as renewal-ended."

Glyke closed her eyes for a moment at that, then opened them.

"…Yes."

"…The other is under treatment, so I'll mark him pending."

And with that, he left. The word pending stayed in my ears for days
afterward. The brother was still alive then. He could even still walk.
But the Roll had already begun calling him pending.

Pending doesn't mean nothing has been decided yet. I learned this
later. Pending means the decision is already on its way. The Roll
never announces anything ahead of time. It only names it ahead of
time.

For those four months, people from the village came in turns. They
helped with the field, left food, sat a half-day without a word and
went. No one called it help. They just came.

One old man said the same thing every time he came.

"…Three is hard."

That was the first time three struck me as strange. We weren't three
yet. The brother was still alive. But the villagers were already
counting three.

The village had no Roll. But the village counted too. It counted
differently than the Roll did. The village counted while grieving. The
Roll counted without grief. The number was the same, but the two
things were entirely different.

The day the brother died, that old man came first. He said nothing,
just sat beside Glyke. After a long while, he said only this:

"…Now it's really three."

Glyke nodded. And for the first time in front of him, she cried. I had
never seen Glyke cry before that.

After the brother's death, the city's man came a second time. That was
the day I wrote of earlier — the day Glyke said, the number went
down.

I've thought back on that visit many times since. He entered something
into his device, looked at the screen for a long while, nodded, and
left. He hardly spoke — no unkind word, no word of comfort either.

At the time I thought he was simply curt. Now I know. It wasn't that
he had nothing to say. In the Roll's language, that was saying
everything there was to say — the number has changed, and that is
all. There is no field for grief on the Roll.

That night I asked Glyke — I've already written this exchange, but
there's one thing I left out.

After Glyke said, if someone takes something from you, at least you
can say you were robbed, she added, after a long while,

"…All three times, I never heard a single word."

"…What kind of word?"

"…An apology," Glyke said. "The words I'm sorry. The words we were
wrong. I never heard them from anyone. Not when he fell off that roof,
not when it happened to our boys. Every time, they just said the
number has changed, and left."

I had no answer for that.

"…It was an accident — who is there to blame?" I finally managed,
after a long while.

"…That's true," Glyke said. "But Xene, there being no one to blame,
and no one being sorry — those are different things."

I remember that sentence exactly, all these years later. I think this
whole book began, in the end, from that sentence. The Roll is not
something to blame. The Roll does no wrong. And yet no one is sorry.
This story lives in the space between those two things.

That winter, the three of us — Glyke, Daphne, and I — were left in one
house.

The work didn't lessen. The field was still there, the goats were
still there, and winter had its own work as always. Only the hands had
gone down.

Around then, Daphne began talking about her own family's house. At
first it was just passing remarks — my mother says, followed by
something trivial. But it happened more and more.

I didn't understand what that meant, then. Now I know. All that
winter, Daphne was counting a way back.

I don't fault her for it. Daphne did nothing wrong. But I first felt,
then, that Daphne and I were looking at the same thing and counting
something different. Daphne was counting a way back. And I — I
honestly don't know what I was counting, then. Probably nothing at
all.

Glyke grew quieter that whole winter. Instead, she spent more time
sitting in the doorway. Always looking the same direction. Toward the
city.

Once I sat beside her. Said nothing, just sat. After a long while
Glyke said,

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…When was the first time you heard the word standing, in this
house?"

I thought for a moment.

"…When that city man said something, looking at our field."

"…That's right," Glyke nodded. "I wonder what he wrote down."

"…I don't know."

"…Neither do I," Glyke said. "But I think I understand now."

I didn't ask what she meant. It felt like I didn't need to.

Now I know. That winter, Glyke was already calculating how far her
own number had fallen — a husband, two sons, and how much further it
would fall once her daughters-in-law left too. She wasn't afraid of
it. She simply counted. Counting was how she bore her grief.

As spring drew near, Daphne spoke first.

"…Mother," Daphne said. "I don't know what I'm supposed to do."

Glyke looked at her for a long while.

"…Take your time," Glyke said. "There's nothing to decide right now."

Daphne's eyes filled with tears. "…I don't think I should leave you
here alone."

"…That isn't for you to worry about," Glyke said.

I said nothing. In Daphne's voice, an undecided heart was still
plainly there.

That night, Glyke sat the two of us down and said,

"…I've made my decision too."

Daphne and I waited for it.

"…I'm going back to the city."

Daphne asked, startled. "…But your standing will only go lower there.
Here you don't even have to think about standing."

"…I know," Glyke said.

"…Then why?"

Glyke was quiet for a moment. The firelight made shadows on her face,
and unmade them.

"…What I know and what I can put into words for you aren't quite the
same thing," Glyke said. "I'll tell you one, for now. I don't want to
be left alone. If being alone here and being alone there come to the
same thing, I'd rather be alone where my people are."

Hearing that, I thought again about the number three. A husband, two
sons. All three were in the city cemetery. Glyke wasn't going because
she'd calculated her standing. She knew her number would only fall
further in the city, and she meant to go regardless, to be near those
three.

I didn't know then that this would be the line pulling the whole rest
of this book. People always move for reasons that don't add up. The
Roll keeps no field for reasons like that. To the Roll, a reason with
no field is a reason that doesn't exist. And yet what doesn't exist is
what moves a person.

Daphne asked quietly. "…Then what about me? Do I have to go to the
city too?"

Glyke looked at her. After a long pause, she said,

"…Come with us for now. Think about it on the way."

That answer struck me as strange, at the time — speaking like a
person who'd already decided everything, and then suddenly leaving
room, only for Daphne.

Now I know. Glyke had already decided everything, then. She simply
hadn't said it yet, not at that moment. Words that release a person
don't come out of the mouth until they're ready.

I said nothing there. But that exchange stayed with me a long time.
You have somewhere of your own to go — words said to Daphne — I
didn't wonder, then, whether they applied to me too. Strangely, I
didn't wonder.

Looking back now, that's strange. I had a family's house too. Two
doors down. And yet that spring, not once did I think of going back to
it. Why I didn't, I'll set down in the next chapter. For now I'll only
note the fact that I didn't.

I remember this night exactly. Glyke's voice was not a grieving
voice. It was the voice of someone who had decided. I didn't know it,
that night — that grief was not the only reason behind that decision.
I would only come to understand it properly in the next chapter.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. I'm Going Back

_Previously_ — Having lost her husband and two sons in order, Glyke
declares that winter that she will return to the city. Daphne decides
to go back to her own family. Xene knows she has a family's house to
return to as well, but that whole spring, she never once thinks of
it.

I asked Glyke, more than once, why she wanted to go back, after that
night. The answer was different every time. At first that struck me as
strange. Now I know. Every different answer was true. A person's heart
doesn't move for just one reason.

I'll set down those reasons in order, in this chapter. Not that there
was an order to them. I'll only set them down in the order I heard
them.

Even now, people sometimes ask me, "Why did you go into the city?" I
always answer with one of these three reasons. Which one I pick
depends on my mood that day. All three are true, so none of them is a
lie.

The first reason I'd already heard. I don't want to be left alone.

But the day after I heard it, I noticed there was more to it than
that. Glyke had spent the morning picking things up one by one around
the house. She'd lift a bowl, set it down; touch an old blanket, fold
it.

"…What are you looking for?" I asked.

"…Nothing," Glyke said. "Just touching things."

"…Why?"

"…Everything here is going to stay here," Glyke said. "There's not
much I can carry. So I'm touching it now, while I can."

Hearing that, I looked again at the things in that house — the hearth,
the water jars, the sickle he'd used. All of it worn smooth by use. It
was only then that it truly hit me — none of it could go to the city.

I return to this scene often. After I came into the city, I had to
relearn what it means to own something. In the city, without standing,
even what you have isn't fully yours. But the things Glyke was
touching that day had been hers since before the word standing
existed. I didn't understand the difference then. I do now.

The second reason I heard a few days later, while we were milking the
goats in the yard.

"…Xene, do you know what happens here when someone dies?" Glyke
asked.

"…You bury them. Here."

"…That's right, you bury them," Glyke said. "But you don't mark it.
One stone on top, that's all. A few years on, no one even remembers
whose stone it was."

Hearing that, I thought of the pile of stones in the corner of the
yard.

"…Is the city different?"

"…In the city they carve the name," Glyke said. "There's a thing
called a headstone. They cut the name into the stone. That way, a
hundred years on, you can still tell whose place it is."

"…Why does that matter?"

Glyke's hands stopped.

"…I thought it didn't matter," Glyke said. "But after losing them, I
understood. If a name doesn't remain, even the fact that they existed
starts to blur. I don't want that — the fact of them — to blur."

That answer made sense to me, at the time. It's strange that it did.
I had never lost anyone like that, not yet. And still I understood, or
felt I did. Maybe there's another road to understanding grief besides
grieving yourself. Watching from beside someone might be one of them.

That night I went out alone to the pile of stones in the yard. Moonlight
let me make out their shapes — small stones, large ones, square ones,
broken ones. Stacked with no order to them.

I touched one with my hand. It was cold. I wondered who had set this
stone down first, in the place he'd fallen from the roof. Glyke, or
someone from the village. I'd never asked.

Looking back, it's strange. I wasn't a curious person, ordinarily.
But that night I truly was curious. Standing in front of that pile of
stones, I had a thought for the first time. Without a name, these
stones would just become stones, in time. Stones no one remembers as
anyone's place.

The third reason came latest, and shortest.

It was the day we packed, in spring. I was watching what Glyke packed.
Not much. A few clothes, a few bowls, and one small bundle of cloth.

"…What's that?" I asked.

Glyke unwrapped it. Inside were small stones. A few taken from the
pile in the corner of the yard.

"…I have to take at least these," Glyke said.

"…That's it? That's the reason?"

Glyke looked at me. After a long while, she said,

"…Xene, honestly, I can't put the whole reason into words. Not
wanting to be left alone, that's real. Wanting a name to remain,
that's real too. But the truth is just — my feet are going that way.
If you go looking for reasons, more keep coming. But my feet are
already moving."

I didn't quite understand that, at the time.

I know now. It was the same for me. Whenever people later asked me
why I followed, I gave a different answer each time. All of them were
true. But the truth was simply that my feet went that way.

In the last few days before we left, I looked over every corner of
that house once more. No one told me to. My feet just moved that way.

The threshold I'd sat on the first day I came to that house. The
corner of the yard where my husband taught me the sickle. The corner
of the room where Daphne and I had bitten our blankets and cried at
night. I went and touched each of those places, one by one.

Glyke did something similar. Once I found her standing a long while in
the shed, though there was nothing much there to see.

"…What are you looking at?" I asked.

"…Nothing," Glyke said. "I'm just looking at the time I spent
standing here."

I didn't quite follow that, at the time.

Now I know. A place holds the time a person spent there. You can't
see that time, but standing in that place, you feel it again. Glyke
spent nearly thirty years in that shed. She was leaving that time
behind. I was only leaving behind two years, and still there were
places my feet wouldn't come away from.

The morning we left, I went into our room one last time. The
belongings were already packed; the room stood empty. I stood there a
moment.

I said nothing in that room. No prayer, no farewell. I just stood
there. I think, looking back, that was the only farewell I had to
give that room. Standing without a word.

Word got around the village, and people came one or two at a time.
Most of them hadn't come to talk us out of it. Most of them had simply
come to say goodbye.

The old man — the one who'd come every day for those four months —
came last.

"…Live well in the city," he said.

"…I don't know if living well is even possible there," Glyke said.

The old man laughed. It was a sad laugh.

"…I don't know either," he said. "I've never once been to the city.
But every time I've seen people come from there, they always seemed to
have more than us and be in more of a hurry."

"…A hurry, how?"

"…They're always measuring something. Measuring time, measuring
people," the old man said. "Nobody measures anything here. I don't
know what's left over once you're done measuring."

I thought back on this conversation often, after I came into the
city. The old man was right. The city measures. What the old man
didn't know was this: measuring itself isn't the wrong thing. The
problem is what happens after the measuring. The city measures, and
then gives nothing to anyone who doesn't fit the standard. That's the
frightening part.

The old man turned to go, then pulled me aside.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Why are you going?" he asked. "You're not one of us, not
originally. You were born here — you could stay here."

I couldn't answer right away.

"…No reason," I said.

The old man looked at me a long while, then nodded.

"…No reason is the heaviest answer there is," he said. "A light
person gives you a reason. A heavy person says no reason."

I didn't know if that was a compliment. I still don't, really.

Now I understand it this way. A choice you can give a reason for is a
choice you can calculate. If you can calculate it, you can calculate
it again, later, and go back. A choice you can only call no reason
has no room left to go back to. The old man probably knew that.

The night before we left, I had my last talk with Daphne. I didn't
know then that it would be the last.

"…Xene," Daphne said. "Are you really going all the way to the
city?"

"…Yes. Following Mother."

"…I might too," Daphne said. "But I'm scared. You've heard the
stories. They say people from outside get ranked low."

"…I've heard."

"…And still?"

"…I don't know," I said. "I just want to be near Mother."

Daphne looked at me a long while, then said, "…You're strange, Xene.
Not kind. Strange."

I didn't take it badly. If anything, it felt true.

Looking back now, Daphne had it exactly right. I didn't follow
because I was kind. Kindness is a matter of enduring or bearing
something, and I wasn't enduring or bearing anything. I simply thought
it was what I was meant to do. The two are different things.

The next morning, the three of us set out together, our packs on our
backs.

Daphne still hadn't made up her mind, but she came with us anyway. The
road to the city ran alongside the road to her own village for a long
stretch, she said. They'd walk together to the fork and decide there.

A few people from the village came out to see us off. The old man was
among them. No one cried. Instead, everyone pressed something into our
hands — a loaf of dry bread, a pinch of salt, a spool of thread. Glyke
took it all, saying thank you some twenty times.

We walked all that day. The road, familiar at first, turned strange
around noon. I had never before been more than half a day from our
settlement.

I don't remember that day in much detail. That we walked, that the
sun rose and set, that our feet ached. That's about it. But a few
scenes remain.

Around noon we rested by a dry streambed. Daphne shared out the bread
she'd been given earlier. As the three of us ate, Daphne said, out of
nowhere,

"…I think I've been on this road once before. When I was little."

"…When?" I asked.

"…Following my mother. We went to see the city. Just the once,"
Daphne said. "But I didn't like it at all. Everyone was in such a
hurry."

Glyke laughed quietly at that. "…That's just how it is there."

"…Then why are you going?"

Glyke walked on without answering.

I watched her back, and asked Daphne quietly,

"…How old were you then?"

"…Around eight. Why?"

"…Just curious. What do you remember most?"

Daphne thought for a moment, then said,

"…That people didn't call each other by name," she said. "Here
everyone calls everyone by name. There, they call out something like
numbers. That scared me."

I had nothing to say to that.

I would remember this conversation again, a few days later, at the
city gate. What Daphne had seen wasn't numbers. I learned, later. It
was standing rank. That an eight-year-old's eyes had found it strange
too — I still find that remarkable, thinking of it now.

By afternoon, fields began appearing on either side of the road. Far
more evenly divided than the fields back home. The furrows ran
straight as though drawn with a ruler.

"…Are those city fields too?" I asked.

"…Not quite yet," Glyke said. "That's just the edge of the city's
reach. The real city fields are further along — straighter than this.
Quieter, too."

"…What does it mean for a field to be quiet?"

Glyke thought a moment.

"…You'll see when we get there."

I didn't think much of it at the time. I thought it was just a
manner of speaking, a field being quiet. Days later I learned exactly
what it meant. There was hardly any birdsong in the city's fields, or
insect sound either. The machines were so precise that whatever lived
there couldn't seem to find a place to settle.

As evening came and the fork drew near, Glyke stopped walking.

"…Let's rest a while here," Glyke said.

We set our packs down under a large tree by the road. I can still
draw that tree exactly, even now. An old tree, its trunk split into
two. The road split into two there as well. At the time, I thought it
was only coincidence.

Now I don't think it was coincidence. In this world, every place
where a road forks seems to carry some mark of a fork — a tree, a
rock, a river. No one made the mark. It's only that, in a place like
that, even the world itself seems to take a forked shape.

The sun hung low. Daphne set her pack down and sat first. I sat down
too. Glyke didn't sit. She stood.

I felt something strange, then. Glyke had been silent the whole way
here. But I sensed, in that moment, that her silence wasn't the
silence of exhaustion — it was the silence of someone preparing
something.

Glyke looked back and forth between the two of us, then, after a long
while, opened her mouth. Her voice was different from usual.

"…Girls," Glyke said. "From here, each of you goes your own way."

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. Three Times

_Previously_ — Glyke, Xene, and Daphne set out together on the road
to the city. Daphne still hasn't decided where she'll go. As evening
comes and the fork in the road draws near, Glyke stops walking and
speaks in a voice they don't recognize: "From here, each of you goes
your own way."

At first I didn't understand what she meant. Go your own way. I
already had somewhere to go — following my mother, into the city. That
already-decidedness felt so obvious that it hadn't once occurred to me
that it might need deciding again.

Daphne wore the same face I did. Neither of us said anything.

Glyke leaned back against the tree.

"…Go back," Glyke said. "Both of you. Each to your own family."

I remember this sentence exactly. I only learned, years later, how
strangely close what followed was to a story that appears, whole, in
the first chapter of the book of Ruth. I didn't know such a book
existed, then. I'm only setting down what happened to me.

The sun had nearly gone down. The tree's shadow had stretched long
enough to cover the three of us. When the wind blew, the leaves made a
sound. I remember that sound still. Without it, I don't think I'd have
felt how quiet that evening was.

Glyke didn't wear the face of someone trying to persuade us. She wore
the face of someone who had already decided everything, and only
needed to put it into words now. Seeing that face, I knew this hadn't
come to her suddenly. She'd probably been preparing it the whole way
here.

That evening, Glyke said it three times. I wasn't counting as I
listened. But looking back, it strikes me strangely that it was
exactly three times. The last chapter set down the story of losing
three. This chapter sets down three sayings. The number three kept
following this household.

I thought it was coincidence. I still think so. But when a
coincidence repeats, a person tries to read something into it. So did
I.

The first saying.

"…Mother." Daphne spoke first. "Why should we?"

"…You were my sons' wives, not my daughters," Glyke said. "My sons are
dead. Whatever bound you to me is gone."

"…Even so."

"…Listen," Glyke said. "I can't bear you another son now, and you're
not at an age to wait for one even if I could. There's nothing left
for you here, staying by me."

I listened to that. It was arithmetic. Precise arithmetic. Glyke was
speaking in numbers, not in grief.

I know now. That wasn't because there was no grief. Wrapping grief in
arithmetic was, in that moment, the only way Glyke could manage to
speak at all. Spoken as arithmetic, you can say it without crying.

Daphne began to cry. Silently, just the tears.

"…I married your son partly because I liked him," Daphne managed.
"But partly because I liked you too, Mother."

Glyke's face wavered for a moment at that. Then it set again.

"…I'm grateful for that," Glyke said. "But gratitude and your life are
two different questions."

I said nothing. I just stood beside Daphne, listening. I think, by
then, I'd already decided what I would do. It only took a little more
time to turn it into words.

The second saying.

Glyke looked at the two of us again. This time her voice shook a
little.

"…Go back," Glyke said again. "To your mothers. I know how hard it is
for a woman to live alone. You don't need to walk that road too."

Daphne said through her sobbing, "…What about you? Who looks after
you?"

"…I'm going to the city," Glyke said. "It doesn't matter to me if
there's no one there. I'm just going to my own place. You're not like
that. You can still find a new place."

I caught something in that, then. Glyke was drawing a line between
herself and us. Herself, a person already finished. Us, people who
could still begin. That distinction struck me, strangely, as sad.

I would meet this same distinction again later, in the Roll. The
Roll divides people too — those still usable, and those already
finished. The only difference is, Glyke drew that line only against
herself. The Roll draws it against others.

Daphne wiped her tears and said, "…So you're going to the city alone?
It's days away."

"…I'm not going alone," Glyke said. Then she glanced at me. Something
complicated was in that look — wanting me, and being sorry for wanting
me, both at once.

Seeing that look, I understood for the first time. Glyke was trying to
send me back, and at the same time hoping I wouldn't go. I learned
that day that a person can want two things at once, fully, in the same
moment.

Daphne finally broke down and sobbed. I stood beside her, stroking her
back. My hand was trembling.

"…Mother, are we really parting like this?" Daphne asked.

Glyke couldn't answer. After a long while, she said,

"…It isn't parting," Glyke said. "Each of us is going back to where we
live. Parting is too sad a word."

I knew that wasn't comfort. It was something Glyke was saying to
herself.

The third saying.

This time Glyke said nothing for a long while, just looking at the two
of us. Then, slowly, she said,

"…Listen to me," Glyke said. "Even if I took a new husband tonight,
you couldn't wait around for his sons. You're young. I have no right
to hold your lives back."

This was the strangest thing she said, to my ear. At the time I took
it for some old-fashioned figure of speech. Now I know. This was the
language of an old law — the law by which a dead man's brother takes
his widow, to carry on the line. Something like that custom must still
survive somewhere outside, in this world too. Glyke was speaking in
that law's own language, to say that the law was no longer possible
for her.

Daphne heard that and, through her tears, half laughed.

"…Oh, Mother," Daphne said. "I never thought you'd go that far."

"…I never thought I'd say it either," Glyke said. "But once I started
looking for reasons to hold you back, I ended up here. That's what
happens when you go hunting for reasons that aren't there."

I felt, for the first time, that I was seeing Glyke's true heart.
She knew she had no reason to hold us — and still she was
manufacturing reasons to let us go. I sensed, dimly, in that moment,
that the reasons to hold on and the reasons to let go come from the
same place.

"…I'm old," Glyke said. "There's nothing more I can do for you. This
is the last of it. Go."

Daphne collapsed into Glyke's arms. She cried like that for a long
while. Then she pulled away.

Daphne kissed Glyke's face — her forehead, then both cheeks.

"…I'm sorry," Daphne said. "I can't do it."

"…It's all right," Glyke said. "It's nothing for you to be sorry
for."

Daphne looked at me. She kissed me too, briefly.

"…Take care, Xene."

And Daphne walked off from the fork, toward her own family. She looked
back several times. She waved. I watched until she was entirely out of
sight.

Glyke watched that same retreating figure alongside me. When Daphne
was completely gone, Glyke let out a short breath. I couldn't tell if
it was relief or grief. Probably both.

"…That girl will do well," Glyke said, almost to herself. "Her family
is good people. She'll marry well again too."

I said nothing. Listening to Glyke speak with such certainty about
Daphne's future, I felt there was something else behind that
certainty. It was a comfort to herself. A voice telling herself that
what she'd just done had been right.

I never saw Daphne again after that. I don't know how her life went.
But I've never once thought the choice she made that day was wrong. It
was her choice, and it was the right one.

Now it was just Glyke and me.

The sun had nearly set. One road at the fork was the one Daphne had
vanished down. The other led to the city. We still stood at that fork.
Neither of us had taken a step, either way.

Glyke looked at me. Her eyes were red.

"…Xene, you heard that too," Glyke said. "Your sister-in-law's going
back. You go back too."

I said nothing.

"…Why aren't you going?" Glyke said. Her voice rose a little.

I still said nothing.

"…Xene." Glyke stepped toward me. "Didn't you hear me? Do you know
what happens if you follow me?"

"…Yes."

"…And you still won't?"

I couldn't answer that question. Not exactly that I had no words for
it — I simply wasn't ready yet to speak it.

"…You're just being stubborn right now," Glyke said. "It's because
you're attached. Attachment fades with time. Go back to your family
and within half a year a good match will turn up. That's how it goes,
here."

"…That's not what this is."

"…Then what is it?" Glyke asked. "Say something, so I know."

I still couldn't answer. What I felt in that moment wasn't that I had
no answer to the question — it was that saying the whole answer out
loud, right there, would make it too small.

Glyke suddenly grew angry. I had almost never seen her angry, until
then.

"…If you follow me, your standing goes to zero," Glyke said. "Zero,
Xene. Do you understand? You don't become a city person. You become
nobody at all. I don't mind that for myself. I've already lost
everything there was to lose. But you — you haven't lost anything
yet!"

I listened to that quietly.

"…I didn't take you into this family to make you into that," Glyke
said. Her voice shook. "Go, Xene. Please, go."

"…Mother," I said.

"…Don't listen to me, just go," Glyke said. It was nearly a plea now.
"What are you going to do, being nobody at all, at your age? I'm old,
it's fine. When an old person is nobody, well, that's just how it
goes. But you're sixteen. If a sixteen-year-old lives as nobody, that
isn't living at all."

Listening to her, I felt that Glyke wasn't talking about me anymore.
She was talking about herself. In those words — when an old person
is nobody, that's just how it goes — was already the trace of
someone who had accepted that this was what she herself would become.

I thought, for the first time, then: this woman is pushing me away
and settling her own future at the same time. Setting me, who would
become a zero, and herself, whose standing would fall to the floor,
side by side, hoping that at least one of us could avoid it.

I count this the scene I've returned to most often in this whole
book. Glyke wasn't angry to push me away. I know now. That was love,
come out as anger. There are moments when a person is angriest in
front of the one they cherish most. That was one.

I looked at Glyke's face. She was angry, but underneath the anger was
something else. Fear. The face of someone afraid I might ruin my life
because of her.

The road around us had gone quiet. Daphne was already far off. Even
the birds had stopped. I felt, in that moment, as if Glyke and I were
truly the only two people left in the world.

"…Answer me," Glyke said again. Her voice was smaller now than before.
Closer to a plea than to anger. "Why won't you say anything."

I still couldn't answer. Not because there was no answer. I didn't
know how to say it out loud.

I remember that silence exactly, even now. I don't know how many
seconds it was. I only know it felt long. In those seconds, I was
confirming something already settled inside me. It wasn't a time for
deciding. It was a time for finding what had already been decided.

Glyke took my hand. It was cold.

"…Xene," Glyke said. Her voice was very small now. "I really don't
want to make you into this."

I took her hand in return. This was the hand that had taught me to
grip a sickle, that autumn I learned. Two years ago that hand had led
me. Now it was trying to push me away. The same hand, pointed the
opposite direction.

Looking at that hand, I knew what I had to say didn't need to be long.
Say it at length, and it becomes a reason, and Glyke would knock down
every reason, one by one. I wanted to say something that couldn't be
argued against.

"…Listen," I said.

Glyke looked at me. Her face was waiting.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. Your People

_Previously_ — At the fork in the road, Glyke tells her daughters-in-
law three times to go back. Daphne weeps, kisses them both, and
leaves for her own family. Xene stays. Glyke grows angry — if she
follows, her standing will fall to zero. Xene sees the fear beneath
that anger. Glyke takes her hand and asks, "Why won't you say
anything." Xene tries to answer.

I didn't say much, standing there.

People still ask me, even now, why I answered so briefly. It was such
an important moment — why didn't I say more? I always give the same
answer. There wasn't more to say.

Once, someone I met here in the city asked me, "You made a decision
that big and didn't explain yourself? Didn't anyone ask?" I told him
Glyke had asked. And that I hadn't answered. He looked at me as if
that were a strange thing.

I understand that look. In this city, an explanation is proof itself.
No explanation means no grounds, and no grounds means nothing to
trust. I would learn that rule the hard way, later. But at the fork
that evening, I didn't know it yet. I only spoke the way I knew how
to speak.

I was most careful, writing this chapter. There was a temptation to
write this scene large — as though I'd said something momentous. But
that would be a lie. I didn't say anything momentous. I said something
short.

Up to that very moment, I hadn't decided in advance what I would say.
Not while I watched Daphne's back walking away, not while Glyke's hand
held mine. I wasn't choosing words in my head. My mouth simply opened,
and the words came.

I've found this strange, many times since. Shouldn't the more
important the words, the more you need to prepare them? For me it was
the reverse. Every prepared answer came attached to a reason. Only the
unprepared answer came with no reason attached. And a reasonless
answer, in the end, was the one that never wavered.

"…I go where you go," I said.

Glyke looked at me. Her face waited for more.

I said nothing more.

"…That's all?" Glyke asked.

"…Yes."

"…Why?"

I didn't answer that question either. Not because there was no reason.
If I gave the reason, the choice would become a choice made for a
reason, and a choice made for a reason can waver once the reason is
gone. I didn't want to make a choice that could waver.

Glyke started to ask again, then stopped. Whatever she'd meant to say,
she closed her mouth on it, and looked at me a long while instead. I
didn't look away. There was no reason to.

I can describe that silence more precisely now. I hadn't failed to
say the reason, then. I had decided not to say it. Those are two
different things.

I didn't know, at the time, how important that distinction would
become across this whole book. Not being able to say something, and
choosing not to say it. The Roll makes no distinction between the two.
To the Roll, silence with no explanation is all the same silence. But
to a person, the two are entirely different things.

Glyke looked at me a long while more, then asked again.

"…Have you decided where to stay, too? I might not even be able to
feed myself, once we get there. With you along."

"…That's all right."

"…What's all right about it. Were you even listening to what I
said?"

"…Yes."

"…Then answer me."

"…I did," I said. "I go where you go."

Glyke let go of my hand. She stepped back a few paces.

For a moment I thought it was a refusal, and my heart dropped. It
wasn't. Glyke simply wanted to see me from a little further off.

For a long while neither of us said anything. The wind moved the
branches. I was afraid of that silence. Wondering if I'd said
something wrong, I began to open my mouth again.

But just then, an expression I'd never seen before crossed Glyke's
face. Not anger, not grief. Something like acceptance. Like the face
of someone who had finally finished a calculation they'd long put off.

I still haven't found the right words to describe that expression
exactly. But after seeing it, I understood something. This woman had
never meant to win against me, not from the start. Stopping us three
times wasn't because she truly wanted to stop us — it was because she
needed to fulfill the duty of trying. And a person who has fulfilled
their duty is, after that, free to accept.

"…You really are stubborn," Glyke said at last. There was no anger in
her voice.

"…Yes."

"…I never taught you to be this stubborn."

"…I learned it from you," I said.

Glyke laughed shortly at that, as if it were absurd. There were still
tears in her eyes.

"…When did I ever."

"…All the time," I said. "Not shrinking the field. Standing in the
shed. Taking the stones. That's all the same thing, isn't it."

Glyke had no answer to that.

That was the first time I felt like I had won. I hadn't spoken to
win. Maybe it wasn't a matter of winning or losing at all. I was
simply handing back what I'd learned from her.

I thought, in that moment, of what my husband had told me too. That
what you spill isn't wasted, it's left behind. I'd taken it, at the
time, for a joke told in a field. That evening it sounded different.
Leaving something behind isn't a loss. It's the making of a place
where something remains. I thought, right then, that staying beside
Glyke was exactly that kind of act. Not a loss — the making of a
place.

These two things — what I learned in a field, and the stubbornness I
learned in that house — seemed to arrive together, at that fork in the
road. I didn't connect them, at the time. Only now, writing this down,
do they connect.

Glyke came close again and took my face in both hands. Her hands were
rough.

"…Xene," Glyke said. "I have nothing to give you. I'm not taking you
because I have something to give. You understand that?"

"…I understand."

"…In the city, people will treat you carelessly. They might not even
call you by name — just a number."

"…I understand."

"…When it's cold, no one will help you. You won't starve, but you
won't be full either."

"…I understand."

"…There, I might not be able to protect you either. My own standing
is on the floor."

"…I understand."

"…Xene, this isn't me taking you because I can do something for you.
It's the opposite. I'm going to become someone who can do nothing for
you."

"…That's all right."

"…Why is everything all right with you. Don't you know any words
besides all right."

I nearly laughed at that. I held it back.

"…And still you'll come."

"…Yes."

Glyke looked at me a long while. Then she lowered her hands and
nodded.

"…Then let's go," Glyke said. Her voice had completely changed. It
was no longer a voice trying to stop me.

I've pictured this moment many times since. Some days it feels like
consent. Some days it feels like surrender. I think now the two are
the same thing. Ceasing to let go of someone, and taking someone in —
in the end, it's the same motion.

We slept on the road that night, near the fork. We had no strength to
go further.

We built a fire and shared out the dry bread. Daphne's absence felt
strangely large. Neither of us looked at that empty space. The habit
of dividing our load three ways was still in my body — I caught myself,
more than once, holding out bread toward where Daphne would have sat,
then stopping.

Many stars were out. The same stars we'd watched back at the
settlement, and yet they felt different, seen from this unfamiliar
road.

"…Aren't you cold?" Glyke asked.

"…I'm fine."

"…Fine again," Glyke said, laughing a little. It was the first time
she'd laughed since that evening.

I was glad of that laugh. I'd thought I might never see it again. It
wasn't the same laugh as before, but a laugh was still a laugh.

I added another branch to the fire. Sparks rose and faded out.

"…Mother," I asked. "Will Daphne be all right?"

"…She'll be fine," Glyke said. "She has somewhere to go back to.
People like that are usually fine."

"…What about me?"

Glyke looked at me. "…You'll be fine because you have nowhere to go
back to."

I looked at her, not understanding.

"…When you have somewhere to go back to, you keep looking back," Glyke
said. "You won't look back anymore. There's nothing behind you to
look back at. That'll make you harder, in the end."

I took that as comfort at the time. Now I hear it differently. It was
comfort and an exact prophecy, both at once. I really have never
looked back, not once, since. Whether that made me hard, or whether it
was simply that I had nothing to look back at, I still don't quite
know.

"…Xene," Glyke said, across the firelight.

"…Yes."

"…Can I ask you one thing."

"…Yes."

"…What's your real reason. You didn't have to say back there. Can you
tell just me, now?"

I looked at the fire a long while.

"…I don't know," I said. "I honestly don't know. It just felt
stranger, the thought of not being wherever you are."

Glyke listened to that and nodded quietly. She didn't press further.

I think this is the most precise account I can give, in this whole
book, of my own choice. I didn't go for a reason. It only felt
stranger not to go. Some choices are made that way — not by a force
pulling forward, but by the force of not going another way.

I lay awake late into that night. Glyke fell asleep first. I sat up a
long while, watching the fire die down.

I'll set down here that I wavered, once, that night. Only for a
moment. As the fire was nearly out, I found myself thinking, for a
moment, of which direction Daphne's road had gone. The thought crossed
me that I could have gone that way too. It was very brief. I didn't
hold onto it. I knew that holding it would only make it grow.

I set down that wavering because I don't want to say I never
wavered. I did waver. I simply didn't follow where it led. Those are
different things.

Before dawn I slept a little, then woke to the sound of Glyke packing.
The sky was still dark.

"…Sleep more," Glyke said.

"…No, I'll get up."

We set off again without much of a breakfast. My legs were stiff.

The next morning, we shouldered our packs and started walking toward
the city again.

It was just the two of us on the road now. The space Daphne had walked
was empty. I was aware of that empty space all day.

The road widened as we went. More people passed us. Unfamiliar faces,
all of them. They glanced at us and kept walking. I kept lowering my
head at their unfamiliar looks.

"…Lift your head," Glyke said. "No need to look down."

"…People are looking."

"…Let them look. We haven't done anything wrong."

I lifted my head at that. I didn't know, then, how often I'd think
back on those words in the days to come.

Around noon, Glyke said, out of nowhere,

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Thank you."

That was all. I didn't answer. It wasn't a thing that needed
answering.

That afternoon, we saw a field far off for the first time. Larger and
straighter than anything we'd passed the day before. The furrows
seemed to run all the way to the horizon.

"…That's where the city's jurisdiction begins," Glyke said.

I looked at that field for a long while. It was strangely quiet. Even
with the wind blowing, there was no sound but the wheat itself moving.

"…No birds," I said.

Glyke said nothing and kept walking. I didn't know, then, what that
silence meant.

Now I know. Glyke already knew that field. How much it had changed
from the one she'd left thirty-some years before — she must have
taken it in at a glance. I couldn't read her expression, then. Only
later did I call it back to mind, and read it properly.

I've named this chapter Your People. Those weren't words I spoke
that day. I read them, much later, in a certain book. And when I read
them, I knew that what happened at that fork was exactly those words.
I went where my mother went. Which meant her people had become my
people. I didn't know the words then, but I was already living them.

I cried a long while after reading that book. The woman in it spoke
briefly too, the way I did. Gave no reason, the way I didn't. Reading
it, I thought that the choice I'd made that day might not have been
mine alone to make. Maybe someone had already walked that road, and I
only walked behind her. I didn't know that, then. There are roads you
can walk without knowing.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. Pikra

_Previously_ — Xene answers briefly: "I go where you go." She gives
no reason. Glyke accepts the answer. Daphne has gone back to her own
family. Xene and Glyke, now alone, keep walking toward the city. In
the distance, they see a city-jurisdiction field for the first
time — strangely quiet.

The first time I heard the request to change a name, it was the third
day of walking.

I wrote in Chapter 1 that outside, no one ever asks to have their name
changed. And the very person who'd said that did exactly that thing,
barely three days later. Sometimes a person does fastest the thing
they were sure they'd never do.

We walked the whole day again. The road kept widening and hardening.
It was no longer dirt but paved stone. Our soles hadn't adjusted to
that hardness, and we had to rest often.

I'll set down the naming in this chapter. But first there's something
I should note. That third day, I saw Glyke stop walking, for the first
time. It wasn't from pain. She'd stopped because she'd seen
something.

Stone roads wore a person down the longer you walked them. Dirt gave a
little under the foot and absorbed the shock; stone sent it straight
up through the legs. That day, for the first time, I learned what it
meant for it to be your bones aching, not just your legs.

More people passed us, day by day. Someone pulling a cart, someone
carrying a load, someone walking fast alone. None of them looked our
way. I found that strange. Outside, when a stranger passed, everyone
looked at least once. Here, no one did.

"…Why does no one look at us?" I asked.

"…No reason to," Glyke said. "There are too many strangers here for
strange to feel strange anymore."

I turned the phrase over. A place where strange isn't strange. I
couldn't decide whether that was a comfort or a fright.

Now I know. It was neither comfort nor fright. It was simply nothing.
Strangeness not feeling strange means no one reacts to a stranger. A
place with no reaction has no welcome and no rejection either. It just
has nothing.

We sat by the road at noon to rest our feet. Pikra looked at my foot
and clicked her tongue.

"…You've got a blister."

"…It's fine."

"…Fine, sure. Give it here." Pikra set my foot on her knee and
wrapped it in a strip of cloth. Her hands moved with practice. She'd
probably done the same for her own sons' feet, once.

As I felt her hands, I thought that these small things might be all
this woman had left to give me from now on. And somehow that didn't
feel like nearly enough at all.

A little past noon, there was a small grave marker beside the road. A
name was carved into the stone. An unfamiliar name.

Glyke stopped in front of it. Looked at it a long while.

"…Someone you know?" I asked.

"…No," Glyke said. "A stranger."

"…Then why look so long?"

Glyke answered after a while.

"…There's a name," Glyke said. "A dead person, and their name
remains. I find that remarkable."

I thought of the settlement's pile of nameless stones.

"…Is that how it is everywhere in the city?"

"…Probably. If you have money," Glyke said. "Even without money, at
least the name is kept. That's the law."

I felt relieved, hearing that. At least a name remains.

I would learn how naive that relief was, later. A name remaining, and
what that name comes to mean — those are two different questions. I
didn't yet know what the word standing does to a name.

We passed the marker and walked on. After a few steps, Glyke spoke
again.

"…Xene, do you know what your name means? Your own name."

I was a little startled by the question. "…No. It's just my name."

"…I don't know what mine means either," Glyke said. "I don't know who
named me, or why. It was just there, when I was born."

"…That's odd, come to think of it."

"…It is," Glyke said. "The word you use most, in your whole life, and
you spend your whole life not knowing what it means."

I remember this exchange for a long time. At the time I took it for
a passing remark. Now I know. The meaning of names is revealed only
very late in this book. That day on the road, we were planting the
tiniest seed of that story, without knowing it.

That evening we lodged in something like a roadside shed. It seemed
built for travelers passing through. Old straw was heaped inside.

Glyke lay down on the straw and stayed a long while without sleeping.
I had my eyes closed beside her, but I wasn't really asleep either.

"…Xene," Glyke called.

"…Yes." I opened my eyes.

"…Asleep or not."

"…Not asleep."

Glyke sat up. In the dark, I couldn't make out her face well.

"…I have a favor to ask."

"…Yes."

"…Once we're in the city, don't call me Glyke."

I didn't understand and stayed still a moment.

"…Then what should I call you?"

"…Pikra," Glyke said.

The instant I heard that name, my tongue felt as if it had stiffened.
I didn't know what it meant, but the sound itself was foreign.

"…What does it mean?" I asked.

"…You don't need to know the meaning," Glyke said. "Just call me
that."

"…But why all of a sudden—"

"…It isn't sudden," Glyke said. Her voice was firm. "I've been
thinking about it the whole way. Glyke is the name he called me by.
The name my sons called me by. There's no one left to call me by that
name. I don't want to be called by it anymore."

"…But I still know you as that name—"

"…That's exactly why I'm asking," Glyke said. "If you don't call me
by that name, I can consider it truly ended. But if you keep calling
me by it, I'll keep having to answer to it. Every time I answer to it,
I'll know all over again that the people who used to call me by it are
gone."

I had no answer to that.

Now I know. This wasn't abandoning a name. It was changing the weight
a name had to carry. Glyke had been the name of someone who was loved.
To go on using that name where the love no longer existed was to
confirm, every time, that the love was absent.

That night I thought of my husband's name. I've never once written it
in this record. I decided that from the start. But that night, for the
first time, I asked myself why.

I won't give the full reason here either. But writing this chapter, I
realized something. By not writing that name, I was doing the opposite
of what Pikra was doing. Pikra was letting go of an old name to be
called by a new one. I hid the old name away so that no one could ever
call it at all. The methods were opposite. What we were trying to do
may have been the same. Some way of managing the weight a name
carries.

"…You really won't tell me what Pikra means?" I asked.

"…No."

"…Where does the word come from, then?"

"…It's an old word," Pikra said. "My grandmother's generation used
it, I'm told. I don't know it exactly myself. I heard the elders use
it, sometimes, when they were angry."

"…Then why do you want it as your name?"

"…The sound fits," Pikra said. "You don't need the meaning. You hear
the sound and you know. This is my sound now."

I found that explanation strange, and understood it too. Some words
reach you through their sound before their meaning does.

"…Why?"

Glyke — I should write Pikra from here on, though that night I still
couldn't say the name aloud — was quiet a long while, then said,

"…If you knew, you'd look at me with pity," Pikra said. "I don't want
that."

"…I wouldn't look at you with pity."

"…You would. Once you knew. So just call me by the sound."

I never once managed to say that name aloud that night. My mouth
wouldn't form it.

Instead, I asked,

"…Then what do I get called? If you're changing your name..."

"…No, your name stays yours," Pikra said. I'll use that name for the
rest of this chapter, even just in writing. "I don't want to change
your name. It's mine I want to change."

"…Why not mine too?"

Pikra was quiet a moment.

"…You haven't lost anything yet," Pikra said. "Your name is still only
carrying the weight your name can carry. Mine isn't. Mine is carrying
weight it can't carry anymore."

Hearing that, I suddenly felt like a person who'd lost nothing. Even
though I'd lost my husband. That thought felt strange.

"…I lost something too," I said quietly.

Pikra reached over and took my hand at that.

"…You did too," Pikra said. "But Xene, the size of what was lost is
different. I lost everyone who made my life. You still have your life
left. I won't pretend that's the same."

I couldn't answer that. It sounded right, and it sounded wrong, both
at once.

I think now that the distinction was right. Grief has size. Admitting
that isn't cruel. Flattening it all into the same thing is crueler.
Pikra already knew that, that night.

The next morning, I tried it.

"…M-mother." I started, then stopped.

"…That's fine as it is," Pikra said. "Keep calling me Mother. It's the
name I want changed."

"…Right." I took a breath. "……Pi—Pikra……."

The sound came out awkward. As if it hadn't come from my own mouth,
but someone else's.

Pikra heard it and laughed quietly. A sad laugh, but a laugh.

"…Everyone's like that at first," Pikra said. "You'll get used to
it."

I practiced the name all that day as we walked. Silently, in my mouth,
not aloud. Pikra. Pikra. However many times I repeated it, it still
felt like someone else's name.

Around noon, without thinking, I said,

"…Gly—" and stopped myself.

Pikra looked at me. Not a scolding look. Just a waiting one.

"…Sorry," I said.

"…It's fine," Pikra said. "I'm still not used to being that name
myself. We're both learning it new."

That put me a little more at ease. I wasn't the only one failing at
it. She wasn't used to her own new name either.

By afternoon I'd gotten a little better. Once, I got it right, right
on time — "Pikra" — and Pikra answered. In that moment I felt I'd
crossed something.

"…There," Pikra said quietly. Almost to herself.

"…What is?"

"…Nothing." Pikra shook her head. "Just — there."

Now I know. In that moment Pikra was confirming that her name had
finally been accepted by someone new. Changing a name isn't something
you can do alone. It needs someone to call it. That afternoon, I put
my hand to that completion for the first time.

I noticed that afternoon that Pikra's walk had changed a little. Not
in any obvious way. But something in it was straighter. Before, she'd
kept glancing back as she walked; from that afternoon on, she only
looked ahead.

"…Are you uncomfortable somewhere?" I asked.

"…No, why?"

"…Your walk seems a little different."

Pikra thought for a moment, then said,

"…Maybe it is," Pikra said. "Once you take a new name, it feels like
you have to walk as the kind of person who carries it."

I didn't fully understand what she meant, but I got the gist. A name
wasn't just a sound you were called by. It changed how the person
carrying it stood, how they walked.

It took me a very long time to get used to it. Even months later I'd
still catch, for a moment, before that name. I don't catch anymore.
Now it comes out as naturally as if it had always been her name. That
fact strikes me as strange, sometimes. A person can get used to
anything. Good things and sad things both.

Late that afternoon, we finally saw the city in the distance.

I'll write that moment in the next chapter. In this chapter I'll set
down only one thing first. Seeing the city for the first time, the
first thought that came to me wasn't its size, or its splendor.

I looked at the person beside me. Pikra. I was already calling her by
that name aloud. In three days. She stood narrowing her eyes toward
that city. I saw fear and longing pass across her face at the same
time. It was the first time I'd seen two feelings fight like that on
the same face.

Names change that fast. People change that slowly. Walking toward
that city, I felt, dimly, for the first time, that it takes far longer
for a person to change than for a name to.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. On the Road

_Previously_ — On the third night, Glyke asks Xene to stop calling
her Glyke and call her Pikra instead. She won't say what it means.
Xene can't say the name aloud at first, then finally speaks it that
afternoon. The two see the city for the first time, in the distance.

I can still draw the moment I saw that city, exactly, even now.

We were crossing a low hill. Pikra reached the crest first. I was a
few steps behind. Seeing her stop, I hurried up after her.

And I saw it.

I tried writing this scene several different ways before settling on
this one. I tried magnificent. I tried dazzling. I crossed them
both out. Neither word holds what I felt, that moment. It took me a
long time to find the right word. Here it is — I was afraid.

It was an unusually hot afternoon. The season was turning. Four or
five days into our walk, the air had begun to change. Mornings still
cool, but by noon the heat rising off the ground reached you through
the soles of your feet.

The dust had gotten worse from around then too. The dirt roads back at
the settlement turned to mud in rain and hardened when it was dry, but
they never raised much dust. The closer we got to the city, the more
dust gathered on the road. Every cart that passed kicked up a pale
cloud, and before it settled, the next cart was already through it.

"…Is this all dirt dust?" I asked Pikra.

"…No. A little different," Pikra said. "This time of year, threshing
dust mixes in too. It means someone's already beating grain,
somewhere. It rides the wind all the way out here."

I rubbed my nose. The inside of it felt gritty.

I took that dust, at the time, for nothing but an annoyance. I
learned later. That dust meant the harvest was close. This whole city
was entering its busiest season of the year. We had arrived, as it
happened, right into that season.

Fields stretched along both sides of the road. What had been scattered
patches at first grew closer together, until it all looked like one
endless plain. And that whole plain, every part of it, without
exception, had ripened to the same color.

That struck me as strange. Back at the settlement, even within one
field, the ripening speed differed — sunny patches came in first,
shaded ones later. Here there was none of that. It looked as if
everything had agreed to be planted the same day and ripen the same
day.

"…Here the whole field ripens together," I said.

"…That way you reap it all at once," Pikra said. After a moment she
added, "The seed is matched. The water's matched. The feed's matched.
It's all calculation."

I took that, that day, for a remark about farming technique. Now I
know. It wasn't about technique at all. It was a miniature of what
this city does to people too. Matching everything. Refusing to let
anything grow differently.

The Harvest was vast. It looked dozens of times larger than my whole
settlement. There was a wall, and even the wall alone was larger than
any building I'd ever seen.

Inside the wall, roofs packed close together. Roofs of every height,
overlapping into one great mass. And beyond that, fields spread out.
Even all the fields I'd ever seen put together seemed like they
wouldn't match this one.

The wheat was ripe. Golden, as far as the eye could see.

"…So much wheat..." I couldn't finish.

"…Yes," Pikra said. Something complicated was in her voice.

I looked at that field a long while. When the wind blew, the wheat
rippled like water. But the ripple felt strange. Too even. As if
someone had calculated exactly how it should be planted.

Now I know. It really had been calculated exactly. That even ripple
was the beginning of the fear I first felt. I couldn't put it into
words then. I only felt that something was wrong.

We stood a long while at the top of that hill. Pikra spoke first.

"…Thirty-some years," Pikra said. "Since I left there."

"…That long?"

"…It was before I married," Pikra said. "The walls weren't so high
then. The fields weren't so wide either."

"…The city grew bigger?"

"…Much bigger," Pikra said. "See that field there. That wasn't there
when I was a girl. There used to be a small woods, there."

I looked at the field again. Where a woods had been, there was now
wheat. Not a single tree, all even.

"…Where did the woods go?"

Pikra didn't answer. Probably she didn't know. Or knew and didn't want
to say.

I got the answer later, from someone else. The woods had been
cleared. As the Harvest grew, the woods, the marshes, the fallow
ground — all of it became field. Nothing was left unused. I took that,
at first, for diligence. Later I found another word for it.
Leaving-nothing.

We started down the hill. The road grew more crowded the closer we
came to the city.

Carts went back and forth. Carts loaded with wheat, empty carts, carts
carrying people. Everyone looked busy. No one slowed their pace.

One cart passed close by us without stopping and splashed us with
muddy water. Mud spattered Pikra's skirt. The driver didn't even look
back.

"…He didn't even say sorry," I said, a little angry.

"…They're busy," Pikra said, brushing off her skirt. No anger in her
voice. "Everyone's like that here. There's more to be gained from one
more step forward than from stopping to apologize."

That remark stayed with me.

I took that exchange, that day, for nothing but a passing annoyance.
Later I learned this was a sentence that summed up how the whole city
runs. There's more to be gained from one more step than from an
apology — that's exactly how the Roll treats people, too. That's
precisely why there's no field for sorry.

We walked the fields road all afternoon. Some sections had already
begun harvest.

I saw a machine cut wheat for the first time. It wasn't a person with
a sickle. A great piece of iron moved at a steady pace from one end of
the field to the other. Behind it came sacks filled not with bound
sheaves, but with grain already threshed clean.

I watched that machine a long while. It was far faster than any
sickle. What our household's three of us would have taken all day to
cut, that thing would probably finish in an hour.

"…Is that all wheat?" I asked, pointing at the sacks piled beside the
machine.

"…Yes," Pikra said.

"…One of those would feed our whole settlement for years."

Pikra said nothing. I didn't understand, then, what that silence
meant.
