Now I know. The arithmetic was right — one of those sacks could feed
our settlement for years. But something was missing from that
arithmetic. That sack was never going to our settlement. It already
had somewhere else to go. Abundance wasn't something shared. It was
something calculated, and assigned.

A few people followed behind the machine, checking something. I
thought they were gleaners.

"…What are those people doing?" I asked.

"…Checking if anything fell," Pikra said. "Not gleaners from below —
that's Roll work. They're checking there's nothing left, because
there shouldn't be."

"…Why shouldn't anything fall?"

Pikra looked at me for a moment before answering.

"…Because it's a loss," Pikra said. "Wheat on the ground goes to no
one. They don't like that, here."

I turned that over as we walked. Wheat that goes to no one. I didn't
know, then, that the phrase held another meaning too. Fallen wheat
wasn't wheat that went to no one. It was wheat that was, by rights,
meant to go to someone like me.

I would learn that a few chapters later. That afternoon, I only
marveled at the machine and walked on.

As we neared the gate, a different kind of line came into view. Not a
line of people — a line of carts.

Carts loaded with grain stretched in a long queue before a smaller gate
beside the main one. Each one went in, someone came out and counted
the sacks, wrote something down somewhere, and only then let it
through.

I watched that scene a long while. It didn't take long for each cart
to pass. The counter's hands were fast. They didn't even touch each
sack. Their eyes swept it, they called a number, and wrote it down.

"…What are those people measuring?" I asked.

"…Quantity," Pikra said. "Which field it's from, how much came in.
That all goes into the record too."

"…There are records even for wheat?"

"…For wheat, and for people," Pikra said. Her voice dropped. "Here,
everything has a record. That's how this city runs."

Hearing that, I thought that tomorrow would be our turn to be counted
that way. Eyes sweeping, a number called, written down. It looked
quick and simple, for a cart. Whether it would be the same for a
person, I didn't know yet.

I learned the answer the next day. A person is counted exactly the
same as a cart. The only difference is that a cart isn't afraid of
being counted.

As the sun sank, we reached a village not far from the gate. Just
outside the wall, a settlement of people who made their living from
those passing through it.

A small market stood at the edge of the village. I'd never seen one
like it.

Every stall was piled with goods. Bread, dried fruit, cheese,
vegetables preserved in oil. One stall sold nothing but white bread.
Back at the settlement, we saw white bread once or twice a year, only
on holidays. Here it was just being sold, every day. Anyone could buy
it.

"…They sell that every day?" I asked.

"…Seems so," Pikra said.

I stopped a moment in front of the stall. The smell was good. Fresh-
baked bread. Breathing it in, I felt something that wasn't quite
hunger. I didn't know exactly what it was, then.

Now I know. It wasn't envy. It was something stranger than envy. The
thought that something so common had never once, not once, been this
plentiful at our settlement. I'd thought scarcity was simply how
things were. Standing there, I felt for the first time that scarcity
wasn't the natural state of things — we had simply been on the side
where things were scarce.

Pikra gave my hand a light tug.

"…Let's go," Pikra said. "We can't afford it."

I felt ashamed at that. Had I looked like I meant to buy something?

"…I was just smelling it."

"…I know," Pikra said. And smiled a little. "Smelling's free, so
that's fine."

We left the market and walked toward an inn. Walking, I kept glancing
back. The white bread on the stall stayed in my sight. No one hesitated
in front of it. People just came, set down money, and took the bread.
I found that ease strange. So common, and yet so at ease with it.

I would meet that ease again, later. People were just as at ease in
front of the Roll. To someone who possesses the common thing, it looks
as though it was always naturally theirs. Someone who's never gone
without is clumsy at even imagining what it is to go without.

We decided to stay there that night. Something like a small inn. We
had almost no money, but what Pikra had covered one night.

At night I looked at the city wall through the window. Lights were lit
in a row along the top. Those lights felt, somehow, like eyes watching
us.

"…We go in tomorrow?" I asked.

"…Yes. In the morning," Pikra said.

"…Are you scared?"

Pikra was quiet a long while before answering.

"…If you ask am I scared, I'm scared," Pikra said at last. "But scared
doesn't mean I'm not going."

I liked that. Admitting you're scared, and going anyway. I think that
was the day I first learned that this is what courage is.

Courage isn't the absence of fear. I would confirm that sentence
again and again through the rest of this book. There wasn't a single
person who stood before the Roll unafraid. There were only those who
stood there afraid, and those who were too afraid to come at all.

Before sleep that night, I asked one more thing.

"…Pikra." The name came fairly naturally now. "What will I be called
tomorrow?"

"…What do you mean?"

"…My name isn't on the Roll. What do they write, when I'm
registered?"

Pikra looked a little flustered by the question, for the first time.

"…They'll write Xene," Pikra said. "It's your name."

"…That's all? No more questions?"

"…There will be," Pikra said. "Where you're from, whose daughter you
are, who you married. They'll ask things like that."

I didn't know, then, how my answers to those questions would be
turned into arithmetic.

I'll write, in the next chapter, about standing at the gate. Before I
close this one, I'll set down one thing. That night, before I fell
asleep, I said my own name aloud a few times. Xene. Xene. I sensed,
dimly, that the sound would carry a different meaning from tomorrow
on. I didn't know exactly what meaning.

Outside the window I could hear insects crying. The same sound I used
to hear at the settlement. That gave me a strange comfort. However
large the city was, the insects that cried at night made the same
sound they'd made in our field.

I remember that insect sound for a long time. After that night, I
could never again listen to it so easily. After I went into the city,
I developed a habit — even hearing insects cry at night, trying to
tell whether the field they came from had standing or not. That night
was the last night I ever slept without calculating anything.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. The Gate

_Previously_ — The two lodge overnight in the village outside the
gate. Xene wonders how her name will be entered on the Roll, then
falls asleep. Listening to the insects cry, she spends her last
night sleeping without calculating anything.

I woke to find Pikra already packed.

"…Sleep well?" I asked.

"…Not really," Pikra said. The shadows under her eyes were darker than
the day before.

We shared bread and left the inn. The gate was less than half an
hour's walk. As soon as we left the village, the crowd thickened.
Everyone was walking the same direction.

I looked at their faces. All of them wore no expression. Some looked
bored, some looked set hard. I wondered if we would look like one of
those faces too, from the outside.

The gate wasn't what I'd imagined.

I'd pictured a door — a great gate with someone standing before it,
letting some through and turning others away. But there was no door,
not separately. Or rather, there was one, but it stood always open.
What kept people out wasn't the gate. It was something inside the
gate.

A line.

The line split in two. One short, one long. A sign hung over the short
one: RENEWAL. Over the long one, a different sign: NEW REGISTRATION.

Looking at those two words, something struck me as wrong. That the
short line was the renewal side. Confirming someone already there took
no time; bringing someone new in took a long while.

"…Which one?" I asked Pikra.

"…That one, for me." Pikra pointed to the short line. "That one, for
you." And the long line.

That was the moment I first truly understood that from today, we
would stand in different lines. Until yesterday, we'd walked the same
road.

I can still draw those two lines with my eyes closed. The short one
and the long one. I would meet those same two lines again, many times,
later in my life. The names changed every time. The shape never did.

We each stood in our own line, close enough to see each other. I spent
the time watching the people ahead of me.

Inside the line stood a wide roof. No walls, only pillars, so you
could see clear through. A device was mounted on every pillar. I'd
never seen anything like it. A smooth, polished panel — letters
appeared on it without anyone touching it.

Beside each device sat another box, about waist height. A low sound
came from inside it. A humming sound — not from just one, but from the
whole line, so the space under the roof was filled with it.

Now I know. That was the Roll. Part of it, at least. No one ever told
me where the Roll as a whole was kept. But that day, I could tell at
least this much — that sound was the sound the Roll made. The
calculating never stopped. For every person standing in that line, at
once.

A large board hung on the inner wall under the roof. Numbers kept
changing on it. PROCESSED TODAY. The number beneath climbed slowly. I
learned later that the number was how many people had passed through
that gate that day.

I watched that board a long while. Each time the number climbed by
one, it struck me as strange — that one number was one person. I would
soon be added to that number too.

At the front of the line, a man stood before a device. The person
watching the device asked him something. He answered briefly. The
screen changed a few times. He nodded and walked on. It took barely
five minutes.

I was surprised at how brief it was. I'd imagined something far more
complicated.

Now I know. Renewal was meant to be that fast. There's no reason it
should take long to confirm a name already there. What takes long is
making a name that doesn't exist yet. And my line was the line doing
that work.

Several people sat in a row at the devices. All dressed alike. There
were as many screens as people, and every screen kept shifting numbers
and letters without pause. Watching those screens, I saw with my own
eyes, for the first time, what the city called the Roll.

It was quieter than I'd expected. I'd imagined something loud — bells
ringing, people shouting. It wasn't. Fingers tapping screens, low
voices, that was all. That quiet made me more tense, if anything.

Pikra's line moved first. I watched from my own line.

The person seated at the device was young. A few years older than me,
maybe. When Pikra stepped up, the person looked at the screen and
asked,

"…Your name."

Pikra paused a moment. I knew what that pause meant.

"…Glyke," Pikra said. Her voice was low.

Hearing that name come out of her mouth, something in me felt strange.
For three days I'd been practicing not calling her by that name — and
here, the very owner of the name had to speak it herself, today.

That was the first time I understood something. Even wanting to
change a name, there are names you cannot change before the Roll. The
Roll doesn't know a new name. It only knows the old one. Pikra had to
call, with her own mouth, at that gate, the very name she wanted to be
rid of.

The person tapped the screen a few times. "…There's a prior
registration. Some thirty-some years back. Exited by marriage,
relocated to an outer settlement."

"…Yes."

"…And the reason for returning?"

Pikra paused again, then said,

"…My husband died, and both my sons. I'm alone now."

The person showed no reaction to that at all. No change of
expression. He entered something into the screen.

"…Confirmed," the person said. "I'll process the re-registration."

The screen changed a few more times. A number came up. I couldn't see
it from where I stood. The angle was wrong.

"…This should keep your housing," the person said. "Though it's lower
than before. Fewer dependents."

Pikra nodded. "…I know."

I thought about that moment for a long time afterward. Fewer
dependents. The person said it without any weight at all. That was the
first time I heard a husband and two sons called dependents.

The person handed Pikra a small card. About the size of a palm.
Numbers and letters were etched on one side.

"…What's this?" Pikra asked.

"…A confirmation card," the person said. "Show it when you claim
housing or rations. If you lose it, you'll need to have it reissued."

Pikra took the card in her hand. I didn't fully understand what she
was holding, but I understood at least this — it was the object that
proved Pikra existed, as a person, in this city.

I saw that card for the first time that day. And for a long time
after, I wanted something like it, for myself. I wanted to be someone
with something to show as proof. How long that wish would last, I
didn't know then.

Pikra stepped aside and waited for me. I kept moving forward in my own
line the whole time.

Most of the people ahead of me took long. New registration meant a lot
of questions. Listening to their answers, I tried to guess what I
would be asked myself.

"…Where are you from?"
"…Your parents' names?"
"…Marital status?"

One person couldn't answer any of it. Said they didn't know their
parents. The person at the device checked something a few more times,
then sent that person to a third line. I only noticed then that this
third line had no sign at all.

I would meet that third line again, later. I'll only note its name
here for now. It had no sign. It was more frightening for having
none.

Another couple stood ahead of me. Young, holding an infant. As the
husband answered, the wife kept rocking the baby to sleep. The person
at the device pointed at the child and asked,

"…No birth registration on this child."

"…Born outside," the husband said. "There was nowhere to register."

"…Would you like to register today, together?"

The couple looked at each other. Neither spoke for a moment. Then the
wife nodded.

"…Yes."

The person entered something into the screen. I didn't see what number
appeared. I only saw the wife hold the baby a little tighter.

I never saw that couple again. I don't know what number the child was
given that day. But I remember, still, the hands with which the wife
held her child a little tighter. Even at the moment a person's own
child becomes a number, they hold the child with their body first.

My turn finally came.

Seen close up, the device was simpler than I'd expected. One panel,
beside it a small box with a few holes in it, and thin lines running
from the box down under the floor. I wondered where those lines led.
Probably not just to this line — probably connected to every other
line, all across the city.

The person at the device smiled at me. A courtesy smile. Not an
unkind smile, but that only made it stranger to me. Outside, no one
smiled at a stranger like that.

"…Your name."

"…Xene."

"…Family name?"

I was caught off guard by that question. "…We don't have family
names. We just use one name."

The person marked something on the screen. I didn't know what it
meant.

"…Where are you from?"

"…Outside. A settlement. It doesn't have a name."

"…The settlement has no name?"

"…No."

The person tapped the screen, then stopped. "…It's not on the
registered settlement list. I'll substitute coordinates."

That struck me as strange. That the place I was born and raised would
be written down as coordinates instead of a name.

"…Your parents?"

I gave their names. The person tapped, then shook their head. "…No
registration record. I'm guessing they were born and raised outside
too?"

"…Yes."

"…Marital status?"

"…I was married. My husband died."

"…Your husband's name?"

I couldn't answer right away. I said earlier that I never once write
that name in this record. That day at the gate, I hesitated too. But
it was for a different reason than this record's. I was afraid to say
the name. Afraid that saying it would turn it into a handful of
numbers.

Now I know. The name did turn into numbers, in the end. Whether I
said it or not made no difference. The Roll is a place that turns
names into numbers. My hesitation changed nothing.

I said the name at last. The person entered it into the screen.

"…No marriage record," the person said, looking at the screen. "A
marriage outside isn't subject to registration. Neither is a death."

Something inside me felt like it was collapsing. A person I'd lived
with two years had, inside the Roll, simply never happened.

"…What about the fact I was married?"

"…It doesn't stay on record," the person said. No apology in their
voice. A voice simply stating a fact. "What happens outside doesn't
count, by our standard."

"…What about him dying?"

"…That too," the person said. And then, just for a moment, looked at
me again. For the first time, not a purely clerical look. "I'm sorry.
I'm not saying it didn't happen. I'm only saying it doesn't count, by
our standard."

That struck me, strangely, as honest. The person hadn't said my
husband never died. They'd only said that death held no place inside
this screen.

I didn't understand that distinction, that day. Now I do. The Roll
doesn't lie. The Roll simply doesn't build a place to count certain
things. The two are very different acts, but the result feels
identical.

Pikra had been listening to that exchange, standing behind me. I
didn't turn around, but I could feel it. Her breathing had quickened,
a little, from its usual pace.

The person pressed one last screen. A small mark in the middle of it
began to spin. A circle, turning.

"…Calculating," the person said. "One moment."

I watched that spinning mark. It looked like nothing much, but those
few seconds felt longer than any I'd ever known.

Pikra took my hand beside me. Only then did I realize my hand had been
shaking.

Outside the roof, the sound of carts, of people passing, went on as
before. None of those sounds cared what state I was in. The city
wasn't going to wait for my calculation to finish. In another line,
someone was already giving their name for the next turn.

I felt that, strangely, as sorrow. The most important seconds of my
life, and to someone else, just a scrap of passing noise.

I would meet that sorrow again and again, after that. This city never
stopped for my circumstances. It never stopped for anyone's
circumstances. I thought, at first, that this was unfair. Later I
learned that the city took pride in exactly that. No special
treatment for anyone. That fairness never once felt fair to me.

I remember those few seconds precisely, even now. My whole life, as
I'd known it, was inside them. The settlement. The summer I learned
the sickle. My husband. The three days Glyke became Pikra. All of it
was, in that moment, turning into a single number on a screen.

The spinning stopped.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. No Standing

_Previously_ — At the gate, Xene finishes the new-registration
process. Name, origin, parents, marital status — all of it goes into
the screen. The mark in the center of the screen spins, the
calculation beginning. Pikra takes her hand. The spinning stops.

A number came up.

0

I looked at it a long while. Thinking I might have misread it, I
looked again. It was still 0.

The person at the device stopped for a moment, looking at the screen.
I didn't miss that pause. Up to then, the person hadn't stopped once.

"…One moment," the person said. And signaled to someone else nearby.

A moment later, another person came.

This one walked unhurried. No trace of rushing at all. Their clothes
were different from the first person's too — a darker color, with a
small insignia on the collar.

"…What's the matter," the person asked. Their voice was low and even.

"…New registration, output value zero," the first person said.

"…Let me confirm."

The new person took over the screen. Fingers moved across it a few
times. I couldn't read anything from those movements.

"…That's correct," the person said. Still the same even voice. "Zero
is correct."

I remember that moment still. The person said the number without
lowering their voice at all. No trace of apology. Just a plain
statement of fact. I found that stranger than any comfort would have
been.

"…Miss Xene." The person looked at me and spoke. It was the first
time all day anyone had called me by my name that way. "May I explain
a little?"

I nodded. My voice wouldn't come.

"…A standing of zero isn't rare," the person said. "It happens when
there's no origin record, when a marriage occurred outside and isn't
recognized, and when there's no inherited value. The calculation isn't
wrong."

"…So what happens to me?"

"…Nothing happens," the person said.

I didn't understand what that meant.

"…Are you expelling me?"

"…No." The person shook their head. Very slightly. "We don't expel
anyone."

This is a sentence I probably return to more often than any other in
this whole book. We don't expel anyone. I heard it many more times
after that. Every time, it was true. And every time, the fact that it
was true was the most frightening part of it.

A few people waiting nearby glanced over at the exchange. No one
interrupted. Everyone simply waited their turn. I found that strange.
That someone receiving a zero wasn't, here, a particularly unusual
event.

"…Does this happen often?" I asked.

The person glanced at a corner of the screen for a moment. I saw a
small table there. A column of numbers.

"…This is the seventeenth today," the person said. As casually as
checking the time. "It varies by season, but ten to twenty a day,
usually."

I lost my words at that number. Twenty a day. Then, in a year. I
couldn't finish the calculation.

That was the first time I understood I wasn't suffering some special
misfortune. That fact should have been a comfort. It wasn't. It
frightened me more. This city was already very much used to
manufacturing twenty people like me, every single day.

I looked around. Here and there in the line, a few people took their
confirmation cards and quietly stepped away. I couldn't tell, from the
outside, which of them had received a zero. Everyone walked at the
same pace, the same gait. No one cried. No one raised their voice.

That quiet struck me as the strangest part of all. I learned later.
Those people already knew that shouting would do nothing. A person who
knows it's pointless goes quiet. I read that quiet, that day, as
grief. Now I know it was resignation as well. The two wear similar
faces. They aren't the same thing.

"…Expulsion isn't within our authority," the person said. "What we do
is renewal. If you have standing, we renew you. If you don't, we
don't. That's all."

"…What happens if it's not renewed?"

The person looked at me a moment before answering.

"…No rations will come. You can't apply for housing. You can't take
formal work, either," the person said, one item at a time, as if
reading a list. "Your name doesn't go onto the Roll. That's all."

"…That's the same as expelling me."

"…No," the person said again. No anger in it, no change in tone. "We
aren't telling you to leave, Miss Xene. You may stay here. We simply
have nothing to give you."

I turned that sentence over many times, that night. Nothing to give
you. Not taking something away — giving nothing. The person knew that
distinction with total precision, and said it precisely. That
precision was what frightened me.

"…What did I do wrong?" I asked. The question came out of me before
I'd meant to ask it.

The person looked at me for a moment. For the first time, the answer
was a little slow.

"…No," the person said. "You've done nothing wrong. Standing isn't a
measure of wrongdoing. It's a measure of what you have and what you
don't."

"…So this happened because I have nothing?"

"…That's right," the person said. Calmly. "No possessions, zero. It
isn't meant unkindly. It's simply arithmetic."

"…Can I see how it was calculated?"

The person looked at me a moment, then turned the screen so I could
see. I hadn't expected that.

A short list was on the screen.

Origin contribution — 0
Inherited value — 0
Marital union value — not recognized
Residency history — 0
Total — 0

I read it line by line. Each line had a number, and every number was
zero. None of the lines looked miscalculated. That was what made it
harder.

"…That's everything?" I asked.

"…That's everything," the person said. "Five fields. You had no value
in any of the five."

"…What about the marriage? I was married."

"…Marital union value is a field that combines two people's standing,"
the person said. "But it only applies to a marriage registered on the
Roll. Your marriage took place outside, and your husband died before
registration. There was no standing inside the Roll for it to combine
with."

Listening to that explanation, I watched the two years of my life
shrink into five lines. And every one of those five lines, zero.

I still have that list memorized. I tried to forget it. It wouldn't
let go. Some numbers cling to a person that way.

Hearing that, I felt, oddly, a little relieved. And that relief
shamed me at once. I was feeling relieved by the words of someone who
had just told me they would give me nothing.

Now I know. That relief wasn't my fault. A person finds it easier to
bear being calculated without feeling than being told they are hated.
The Roll didn't know that and speak that way on purpose. It only
happens that speaking that way moves a person's heart that direction,
regardless.

"…Can it change?" I asked. My voice shook.

"…Standing is a calculated value," the person said. "If the
calculation changes, the value changes. If you marry, or a relation
forms with someone who has standing, or the value comes through some
other channel."

"…And right now?"

"…Right now, it's zero," the person said. Very simply. "That's all
there is."

I looked at their face. Not angry, not sorry. Just the face of someone
doing their job.

"…This was done according to the law?" I asked. I didn't know why I
asked. I just wanted to ask it.

"…Of course," the person said. And for the first time, smiled a
little. A kind smile. "We don't do anything outside the law."

I remember that smile for a long time. It wasn't an unkind smile. It
was truly a kind one. And yet every time I saw that smile, a chill ran
through me. That person genuinely believed they were doing something
right. And they were right — at least, before the law.

Behind that person, on the wall, I saw a small placard, tucked among
other notices. "Roll Law, Article 2 — All residency standing is
determined by calculation, and shall be neither granted nor revoked
for any reason outside that calculation."

I read that sentence twice. I understood about half of it.

Now I know. That article was the sentence holding up this whole city.
Neither granted nor revoked for reasons outside calculation. So the
person giving me nothing wasn't doing it from hatred — they were
keeping that article. And if I was ever to receive something, later,
it too would have to be arithmetic, not pity, not hatred. I read that
article that day without yet knowing what this city truly feared.

"…Any further questions?" the person asked.

I said no. In truth there were many, but none of them would form into
words, right then. Why me rose to my throat and went back down. I
knew, even without being told, that it wasn't a question.

"…Then that's all," the person said. "Thank you for coming such a
distance."

I turned that last remark over for a long time. Thank you for coming
such a distance. A courteous thing to say. Perhaps meant sincerely. But
that courtesy was the most frightening part of all. If the person had
been throwing me out, I could have hated them. But they didn't throw
me out. They simply gave me nothing, and said goodbye, politely.

Pikra came and took my arm. She said nothing. I could tell, from the
strength in her grip, how worried she was.

We walked a few more steps into the city and stopped, just off the
main flow of people.

"…Are you all right?" Pikra asked.

"…Yes," I said. It wasn't a lie. It wasn't the whole truth, either.

Pikra studied my face for a long while.

"…I tried to stop you three times," Pikra said. Not an angry voice.
"Go, go, go, I said. And you didn't."

"…I don't regret it."

"…I know," Pikra said. "But I'm still sorry."

I shook my head at that.

"…It wasn't something you made me do. I chose it."

Pikra said nothing for a moment, then gripped my hand a little
tighter.

"…That's true," Pikra said. "You chose it. That doesn't make the
sorry go away."

I didn't understand that, at the time. Why Pikra should be sorry for
something I'd chosen. Now I know. Some people, seeing another person
carry something in their place, don't let go of the sorry even knowing
it was that person's own choice. That sorry wasn't guilt. It was love,
turned into weight.

I glanced back once as we passed through the gate. The person was
already looking at the next in line. Whatever had happened to me was
already, to them, something past.

At the far end of the line, I saw that couple again. The one with the
baby. The wife stood with the child in her arms, receiving something
onto her confirmation card. I didn't know what number they'd received.
Only that their faces looked a little brighter than ours.

I envied that. And the envy shamed me.

I remember that retreating figure for a long time. Not a bad person.
I still think that, even now. That person had simply done their job
exactly, precisely as they'd set out to do it. But inside that
precision, there was no room for mercy to stand. Mercy had no field in
the calculation. There was no reason it should.

Pikra kept walking, not letting go of my hand. Walking beside her, I
felt that something had gone missing, between the self I'd known this
morning and the self I was now. I didn't know, then, exactly what it
was.

Now I know. What was gone wasn't standing. I never had standing to
begin with. What was gone was the self I'd been before I knew I would
be recorded nowhere in this city. That self doesn't come back.

"…We don't expel anyone." Walking, I said the words over to myself
again, silently. However many times I repeated them, they sounded
strange. How could not expelling and giving nothing live inside the
same sentence at all — I didn't know the answer, that day.

I would come to know the answer a little at a time, in the next
chapter — going deeper into the city, seeing how people like me
actually lived. The answer was inside the gate. It only took a few
days for me to learn to recognize it.

Pikra walked on ahead. I followed, and felt, freshly, that I was
holding nothing in my hands. No confirmation card, no small tablet,
nothing. Nothing to receive meant nothing to hold. I kept telling
myself that was only natural, and still that day I learned, for the
first time, how heavy empty hands could be.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. Nobody Drove Us Out

_Previously_ — Xene's standing computes to zero. The administrator
explains: it isn't expulsion, only a refusal to renew. He was
courteous. That courtesy frightened Xene more than anything else
had.

Inside the gate was another city again.

Seen from outside, the city had been one great, distant mass. Inside,
that mass became roads, became houses, became people. Among all of it
I lost my footing for a moment.

Pikra took my hand and led me.

"…Housing first," Pikra said.

The housing office wasn't far from the gate. Another device stood
there. That was the third device I'd seen that day alone.

Pikra held up her confirmation card. Something appeared on the screen.

"…Alone?" the person at the device asked.

Pikra glanced at me. Very briefly.

"…Yes," Pikra said.

I said nothing to that answer. I knew it wasn't a lie. On paper, Pikra
really was alone. I was a person who existed nowhere on paper.

I remember that moment still. It would be a lie to say it didn't
hurt. But I didn't resent Pikra for it, even then. She'd only stated a
fact. It was the fact that hurt me, not her.

The room was small. One room. Near the wall, in an old building, on
the third floor. The stairs were narrow and dark.

Inside, Pikra set down our things first. Then she looked at me.

"…I was worried whether we could live here at all," Pikra said. "Even
if the paper only says me, no one comes in and tells you to leave. No
one checks inside a room."

"…Are you sure?"

"…I'm sure," Pikra said. "That isn't what this city does. This city
only asks at the door. It doesn't ask what's inside."

Strangely, that gave me comfort.

I would learn how shallow that comfort was, later. No one checking
inside the room wasn't kindness. It was indifference. Not thinking
something worth checking is also a form of indifference.

That evening, Pikra came back with our first ration, using her
confirmation card. One sack. A little flour mixed with dried beans.

"…This is one person's share," Pikra said, setting the sack down.
"There isn't a separate share for you."

I heard that and tried not to think about being hungry. It didn't
work well.

We shared that one person's share between the two of us, that night.
The amount was cut in half. Pikra kept pushing more of her own portion
toward me. I let her, pretending I wasn't giving in easily.

That night I learned hunger all over again. There'd been hungry days
at the settlement too. But that was from a failed harvest. It wasn't
that here. The fields were overflowing with wheat. That wheat simply
had no road that led to me.

The next day, I went out alone. Pikra had come down with a fever and
was lying in. No surprise, after a full day's walk.

I just walked. No destination. I only wanted to see what shape this
city took.

The streets were clean. That was the first thing I noticed. Back at
the settlement, the road was always mixed with dirt and animal dung.
Here there was none of that. The pavement was smooth. No litter in
sight.

Now I know. It wasn't that there was no trash — it was that there
were people who cleaned it. I didn't see a single one of them that
day. They worked in hours no one watched.

At the edge of the market, I saw people carrying loads. Sacks on their
backs, hauling. They didn't seem to be paid on any regular wage. When
the work was done, the owner of the goods pressed something into their
hand and walked off. A few coins.

I watched that scene a long while. The one who'd carried the load
didn't even count the coins before pocketing them. I found that
strange. If the money were precious enough to count, you'd count it.

Now I know. There was no need to count. Whatever the amount, they
already knew it was all they'd get that day. Where there's no set
wage for a job, there's no room to dispute it either.

I watched that porter walk off looking for the next load. No one
asked his name. Once the work was done, he simply became a nobody
again.

In another alley, a few children were picking up things off the
ground. Fruit peels, wood scraps, unidentifiable things. At first I
thought it was a game. Coming closer, I saw it wasn't. Some of the
things they picked were genuinely useful, and went into a sack.

I watched those children, and my eyes met one child's for a moment.
The child looked at me briefly, then turned away without any
expression. Not wariness, not welcome. Just a look that knew I was
nothing to it at all.

At the mouth of one alley, I saw someone like myself for the first
time.

An old man. Sitting against a wall. His clothes were worn but clean.
He was thin, but not the wasted look of someone starving to death.

I passed by and slowed my step. The old man looked up at me.

"…First time here?" the old man asked.

"…Yes," I said. "…How did you know?"

"…Your shoes," the old man said. "Worn different from ours."

I didn't understand and stayed quiet.

"…We all wear our shoes about the same way," the old man said. "We
walk the same rounds, over and over. Yours are still shoes that go
anywhere."

"…What do you do for food?" I asked.

"…Go by the market around closing time," the old man said. "What
didn't sell, what's a shame to throw out — some people set it aside.
Not bad people. Kind, if anything."

"…If kind people are giving it to you, isn't that all right?"

The old man looked at me a moment, then laughed. A bitter laugh.

"…Giving from kindness and giving you a place are two different
things," the old man said. "They'll give food. But no one registers
you here. No one gives you a room. It just keeps you from going hungry
that one day."

"…Where do you sleep?"

"…Here," the old man said. He gestured at the spot he sat in. "This
wall blocks the wind. When it rains I go to that eave over there."

"…What about the cold days?"

The old man was quiet a moment.

"…Some people didn't make it through the cold ones," the old man
said. Calmly. As though it weren't about himself. "But that wasn't the
city killing them, either. The city didn't do a thing."

I only found that strange, that day. Now I know. That's the sentence
that became this chapter's title. Nobody drove us out. Nobody killed
anyone. And yet people die. With no one having done anything.

Just then, two people who looked like patrol officers passed through
the alley. Tidy clothes. An unhurried walk.

One of them stopped in front of the old man. I tensed for a moment.
Outside, that kind of stopping usually meant someone about to be
driven off.

"…Good morning, sir," the officer said. Genuinely wishing a good
morning, by the sound of it.

"…So it is," the old man said. As if used to this.

"…Nothing happened overnight?"

"…Nothing."

"…Good to hear." The officer smiled and walked on. Talking with their
partner about something else entirely, in a voice that had already
moved on.

I watched their backs for a long while.

"…What were they here for?" I asked.

"…A patrol," the old man said. "Comes by a couple times a day. Asks if
anything's wrong, and goes."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes, that's all," the old man said. "They're not bad people. They
really do ask because they're concerned. But after asking, they don't
do anything about it. There's nothing for them to do. That isn't their
job."

I remember that moment precisely, even now. That was the first time
this city's kindness gave me a chill. Those people were sincerely
kind. They genuinely wanted to know if the old man was all right. And
that kindness changed nothing at all. That kindness and indifference
could wear the same face — I saw that for the first time, that day.

"…I've lived here going on fifteen years," the old man said. "In all
that time, no one's ever once told me to leave. Truly. Not once."

"…Then why live this way, here?"

"…Because it's the same everywhere you go," the old man said. "It
isn't that this city is cruel to me. This city does nothing to me at
all. It just doesn't give me anything either. How long that can go on,
you'll find out for yourself soon enough."

I found that frightening. That word, soon.

"…Were you born here?"

"…No," the old man said. "I came from somewhere too. Doesn't matter
where, anymore. It's been too long since I came."

"…What's your name?"

The old man smiled a little at that.

"…I have one. No one asks it here. No reason to," the old man said.
"You asked, so I'll tell you sometime."

"…What was it like at first, for you?"

The old man looked up at the sky for a moment.

"…Everyone was kind to me at first," the old man said. "Gave me bread
in passing, told me directions. I thought that was a good sign. But
that was all there was to it. Plenty of kind people. No one gives you
a place."

The old man held out half a piece of bread to me.

"…It's all right," I said. "I still have food."

"…Today," the old man said. "Tomorrow, who knows. Take it."

I took it, unable to refuse. Holding it, my hand shook. Not from
hunger.

I thought a long time that night about why my hand had shaken. I'd
just received help from someone. And it gave me no comfort at all. The
city hadn't driven me out. And this old man had fed me. Both were true
at once. Between those two truths, I felt a chill. This city's
kindness came from people like this old man. It didn't come from the
city itself. The city only hadn't stopped this old man from feeding
me.

I sat a while longer, watching people pass. The old man kept talking.

"…See that one there." He pointed to someone walking quickly, device
in hand. "People like that don't see people like us. Not that they
won't look — they truly don't see. To someone with standing, we look
about the same as a road, or a wall."

"…Because they're bad people?"

"…No," the old man shook his head. "If they were bad, that'd be
better, even. A bad person at least sees you. Those people don't. No
reason to."

I turned that over. I thought of what Pikra had said, on the road.
Strange isn't strange here. I'd taken that, then, for nothing at
all. Now I was learning how heavy that nothing could be. Not being
seen, and being chosen not to be seen — the results looked the same,
but they were different things. From then on I tried to tell the two
apart.

I stayed with the old man a little longer. The sun was going down.
People's steps quickened around us. Everyone was on their way back
somewhere.

"…Aren't you going back somewhere?" I asked.

The old man smiled.

"…This is where I go back to," the old man said. "While everyone
else is going back to their own place, I'm already at mine. Sometimes
that's a good thing. Sometimes it's a sad one."

I had nothing to say to that.

"…It's right there in the law, you know. That article," the old man
said, suddenly.

"…Which article?"

The old man looked at me, then waved a hand.

"…Next time," the old man said. "I've talked too much today."

I kept thinking about that, that night. Some article. I didn't know
what it was, then. I only felt, from the old man's eyes, that it
wasn't a bad thing. For the first time, a different light passed
through his eyes. Not resignation, not kindness. Something else. I
had no name for it, then.

I came home to find Pikra awake.

"…Where were you?" Pikra asked.

"…Just walking," I said. I didn't mention the old man. I didn't know,
even then, why I didn't.

Pikra studied my face.

"…Did something happen?"

"…No." I quietly hid the half a loaf in my hand. "…Just seeing the
city."

I ate that bread that night, in secret, while Pikra slept. Guilt
came with it. I didn't quite know why. Maybe it was that this was the
first thing I'd received in this city, and it hadn't come from the
city at all, but from some stranger, an old man. That fact left me
strangely ashamed.

Pikra pushed herself up against the wall.

"…Xene," Pikra called.

"…Yes."

"…If it's hard, say it's hard," Pikra said. "You can, with me."

I couldn't answer that. Saying it wasn't hard would be a lie, and
saying it was hard would only make Pikra feel worse. In the end I said
nothing at all.

Looking back, I should have said something, then. But I was already
learning, by then, that in this city, sometimes not speaking is
safer. I didn't know, then, how long that lesson would follow me.

That night, before sleep, I thought again of the old man's words. An
article in the law. I would learn what it was within a few days.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. Article Nine

_Previously_ — From an old man she meets in the city, Xene hears how
people without standing actually live. No one drives them out. No
one simply gives them anything. Even the patrol was kind. The old
man mentions "an article in the law," then puts it off for another
time.

I did nothing much, those three days. Pikra and I set the room in
order. I baked bread from the flour we'd been given. I learned where
to draw water. Becoming a city person, it turned out, meant a
succession of small, ordinary tasks like these.

Pikra grew quieter over those few days. That worried me, but I didn't
ask. I was already learning, by then, that asking also takes a kind of
strength.

Those three days looked like nothing happened, on the outside. But
the whole time, inside, I was turning the old man's words over and
over. An article in the law. I pretended to forget it. I never did. At
night, closing my eyes, it was the first thing that came to mind, and
in the morning, opening them, it was still there.

Three days later I went back to that alley.

I told myself, at the time, that I hadn't gone on purpose. That I'd
just been walking and my feet happened to go that way. Looking back
now, that was a lie. I went on purpose, wanting to know what that
article was.

The old man was in the same spot. Same wall, same posture. As if not
three days but a single hour had passed.

"…Back again," the old man said. No surprise in it.

"…Yes." I sat down beside him. "…Will you tell me about that
article?"

The old man looked at me for a moment. Something gauging in his eyes.

"…In a hurry, aren't you," the old man said. And laughed.

"…It's called the corner clause," the old man said. "Ever heard of
it?"

"…No."

"…When they harvest, they're supposed to leave the corners of the
field," the old man said. "By law. A very old law. They say it's been
there since this city was founded, though I don't know that for
certain."

"…What does leave the corner mean?"

"…That the designated corners of a field — the four corners — are left
unreaped," the old man said. "And they're not supposed to gather the
fallen grain, either. It isn't the harvesters' share."

That struck me as strange. At the settlement, not gathering what fell
was simply how things were. Here, they'd made it a law. That felt
foreign.

"…So whose share is it, then?"

The old man looked at me and said, slowly,

"…Ours," the old man said. "The share of those with no standing."

Something inside my chest lifted a little, in that moment. The first
feeling like that since the zero I'd been handed at the gate.

"…Is that true? So if I go there, there's actually something?"

"…There's supposed to be," the old man said. His voice sank a
little. "By law."

I let that phrase — by law — catch in me, but not enough to weigh it
down, not then.

"…Why is there a law like this?"

The old man thought for a moment.

"…I don't know the real reason either," the old man said. "I only
know it's a rule from way back. Not to reap it all clean, at harvest.
Something about the ones who have leaving a little for the ones who
don't. Something like that, I think."

"…Was it ever abolished?"

"…Not as far as I know," the old man said. "This city's remade and
changed a hundred other laws in that time. That one stayed. Maybe it's
just too old for anyone to think of touching."

"…Strange, isn't it. Why wouldn't an old law like that just
disappear."

The old man shrugged.

"…Maybe because no one loses anything by leaving it be," the old man
said. "Now that the Roll handles the reaping, that clause hardly
changes anything, whether it's there or not. So no one bothers taking
it out."

I let that pass, lightly, at the time. I didn't yet see the distance
between no reason to remove it and the real reason being that it
left nothing behind anyway.

Now I know. The old man already knew the answer, then. He simply
didn't say it to me straight out. I think he wanted me to go see for
myself. He knew there's a difference between being told a thing and
seeing it with your own eyes.

I remember that moment for a long time. It was the first thing I
could call hope, since coming into this city. A very small hope. And
still, that smallness was large, to me, that day. Up to then I'd only
thought in terms of receiving — receiving, or not receiving. But
Article Nine told me not of receiving, but of a place to go and glean.
The difference didn't feel large, then. Now I know it is. Gleaning
carries a share that's mine — the walking there, and the bending
down.

"…Where do I go, then?" I asked.

The old man gestured roughly in a direction.

"…All around the city is field," the old man said. "Somewhere in
there are the marked-off sections. Stakes driven in, they said. I
haven't been myself, not in a long while."

"…Is it at every field, this spot?"

"…Probably. There's more than one field, so probably more than one
spot," the old man said. "Start with the near ones. I heard there are
a few within half an hour's walk of the wall."

I gauged that distance. Half an hour meant a place I could go every
day.

"…Can I go every day?"

"…There's no law against going every day," the old man said. "Whether
there's anything there, every day — that I don't know."

I let that too — what's there, I don't know — pass, lightly. I was
already picturing tomorrow morning.

"…Why don't you go?"

The old man paused a moment at that question.

"…No point," the old man said. His voice went low.

I didn't quite catch what he meant, then.

"…No point, how?"

"…I've been a few times. Not much there," the old man said. "The law's
the law, but these days the machines do the reaping. When it was done
by hand, there was always something dropped. These days…" He let the
words trail off.

"…It was different, before?"

The old man looked into the distance.

"…Before — when I first came here," the old man said. "You'd go to
the corner and there really would be something. Stalks, grain. A day's
worth, off that. Because a person cutting with a sickle naturally
spilled some."

"…And now?"

"…Now the machines reap," the old man said. "A machine doesn't spill.
No reason to. I've watched it with my own eyes. What's left in the
corner shrinking, year by year. First a sack. Then half a sack. Then a
handful. These days…"

The old man stopped there.

"…These days what?"

"…Go see for yourself. Better than my telling you," the old man said.

Writing this exchange down now, I understand why the old man didn't
finish it, that day. Some facts, heard in words, become only
information. Seen with your own eyes, they become something else. The
old man didn't want to hand me information. He wanted me to know it
myself.

That comment troubled me, but the hope that had just risen in me was
larger.

"…I'll go anyway," I said.

The old man looked at me. Not a face trying to stop me. Not a face
egging me on, either.

"…Go," the old man said. "Go see for yourself. I just don't have the
strength for it anymore. That doesn't mean you shouldn't go."

"…If it's a place set by law, it's no trouble for someone with no
standing to glean there, is it?"

"…None at all," the old man said. "That's ground set aside as ours
from the start. Even the Roll doesn't touch that far. It's the law."

I felt easier, hearing that. At least going to that place, itself,
couldn't be stopped by anyone.

I didn't really hear the old man's no point that day. I only heard
what I wanted to hear. There's a law. There's a place. That place is
ours. Those three sentences were enough for me. I let every condition
attached to them pass, that day.

"…What's it called, that law? The proper name," I asked.

The old man thought for a moment, searching his memory.

"…I've heard it called Article Nine," the old man said. "I don't know
if that's exactly right. I've only heard the people working the
fields call it that."

Article Nine. I said the number over to myself.

"…I'll remember that number," I said.

The old man smiled a little at that.

"…You've got the face of someone who could live on that alone, for
months," the old man said.

I didn't understand what that meant, then. Now I do. The old man
already knew, that day, how long I'd cling to that single hope.

On my way home, I passed through the market. In front of a bakery I
saw again the person carrying a basket I'd seen before. I worked up
the nerve to approach.

"…Excuse me, I have something to ask."

The person looked at me and nodded.

"…Have you heard of the corner clause?"

"…Oh, that." No surprise showed on their face. "It's in the law, yes.
The corners of a field, unreaped."

I felt relieved all over again, that it was real.

"…So it's all right for someone with no standing to glean there?"

"…By law, yes," the person said. "That's how it's always been, I've
heard."

"…Is it still kept, these days?"

The person looked at me a moment and made an odd face.

"…It's kept, sure," the person said. Their tone was strange. "The
corners get left, for certain. I've seen the markers myself, passing
by."

"…That's a relief, then."

"…Yes, well," the person said. Without saying more, they went on
their way.

I took that well for nothing but a vague answer, that day. Looking
back now, that person hadn't told me everything either, just like the
old man. It's kept — but whether there's anything inside it is a
different question. Everyone in this city seemed to know that
difference. I was the only one who didn't.

I hurried home that night. I went back and forth for a long while
over whether to tell Pikra.

In the end I didn't. I still don't quite know why. Maybe this hope was
too small and fragile, and speaking it aloud felt like it might break
it.

Pikra glanced at me as she prepared dinner.

"…You look different today," Pikra said.

"…Do I?"

"…Yes. Something happen?"

I hesitated a moment, then shook my head.

"…I just walked around a bit."

Pikra didn't press further. Instead, she put an extra ladle of soup
in my bowl.

"…Whatever it was," Pikra said. "If it's good, it's good, and if not,
it's not. I can tell, mostly, from your face."

I smiled a little at that. "…Then why not ask?"

"…If I ask, you'll have to make up an answer," Pikra said. "You'll
tell me, when it's time."

I didn't fully understand that kindness, then. Now I know. Pikra had
already learned not to rush me. Probably raising her sons, or maybe
just on herself, she'd learned that lesson first.

Looking back, that was a wrong call, on my part. I went on carrying
things alone like this, often, after that. Good news and bad both.
Pikra didn't know, that day, what I'd found. What she would have said,
if she had, I still wonder sometimes.

Before sleep that night, I kept repeating the words to myself. The
corner clause. Article Nine of the law. The share set aside for us.

I pictured that place in the dark. I didn't know exactly what it
looked like. I only imagined a corner of a field, untouched, quiet
wheat standing there. The wind would blow, and it would sway too. Just
imagining that sway made me feel, for the first time in days, that my
body had grown lighter.

Looking back now, that imagining was naive. I don't regret the
naivety. There are days a person can only bear by imagining naively.
That was one of those days for me.

I thought again of what I'd heard at the gate. We don't expel
anyone. At the time, that had sounded only bleak. That night, it
sounded different. Not expelling might also mean not stopping me from
standing in that corner. If the Roll gave me nothing, I wanted to
believe, at least, that no one could take away that small place the
law had set aside.

I remember that night precisely, even now. It was the first night in
this city I fell asleep waiting for something, rather than afraid.
Fell asleep on expectation instead of fear, for the first time. The
next morning, I made up my mind to go to that field before anything
else.

I set down here that this was the first of a resolve that would
repeat, many times, through this book. I would go on making this same
resolve, again and again, after that. Some days the resolve was right.
Some days it wasn't. That night, I didn't know which it would be. I
only looked forward to tomorrow.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. The First Day

_Previously_ — From the old man, Xene hears of the corner clause,
Article Nine of the law — that the corners of a field are to be left
for those without standing. For the first time since entering the
city, she feels something like hope, and falls asleep looking
forward to the day ahead.

The next morning, I woke before the sun had fully risen.

Pikra was still asleep. I got up quietly, drank a mouthful of water,
and went out the door. I didn't say where I was going. Nothing was
certain yet, and I thought there was no good in speaking it.

I tried, more than once, to write this morning differently — more
reverently, or more full of feeling. I crossed it all out. That
morning I simply woke, ordinarily, and put on my shoes, ordinarily.
There was no special premonition.

Almost no one was out yet when I left. Traces of water still lay on
the street. I couldn't tell if someone had sprinkled it in the night,
or if it was only dew.

The air was cool. I liked that coolness, even knowing the heat would
come back by noon. I liked walking through that early chill. The dust
hadn't risen yet either. The pale haze I'd seen on the road lay still
that morning.

It had been a long time since I'd set out to do something. Since
coming into the city, I'd always been either dragged somewhere or
pushed out of somewhere. This time I was walking toward a destination
I'd chosen myself.

I walked about half an hour from the wall. The direction the old man
had pointed.

The road was different from the market street I'd seen the day before.
Fewer people. Grain warehouses stood in a row instead, their doors all
shut. I passed those doors trying not to imagine how much lay stacked
inside. I didn't manage it well.

Past the warehouses, the fields began. I was struck again by their
size. Seeing it from the hill and walking straight into it were
entirely different feelings.

The wheat was nearly all ripe. A deep gold color. Seeing that color, I
knew the harvest would begin soon.

I remember that color still, even now. That field, that morning, was
truly beautiful. I'll call this city frightening, many times, in this
book. But that fright and that beauty don't cancel each other out.
Both were true. I carried both truths with me, walking, that day.

I walked along the edge of the field. Not knowing which corner was
which, I circled it for a long while.

Once I spotted something that looked like a stake and ran toward it.
It was only a marker for the water channel. I swallowed the
disappointment and kept walking.

People were already moving inside the field. The harvest hadn't
properly begun yet, but some were checking something. I tried not to
meet their eyes. I didn't know why, myself. Probably I wasn't sure I
was even allowed to be there.

After a long while walking, I saw a wooden stake. Visible even from a
distance. It looked different from the other stakes.

I hurried my steps toward it.

Coming close, I saw small letters carved into the stake. I read them
slowly. Some article, some clause — I couldn't take in the exact
wording, then. But I knew, at least, that this was the clause the old
man had spoken of.

I had pictured this moment for a long time. Every night, for three
days. Now that the moment had actually come, I found, strangely, that
I could only stand there, saying nothing. The stake wasn't old. It
looked freshly new. That newness comforted me, at the time. It looked,
to me, like proof that this law was still being carefully kept, even
now.

I stood before the stake and looked inside.

There was ground.

No wheat.

At first I thought I'd seen it wrong. I blinked a few times and looked
again. Still nothing. Only dirt, and the marks of things pressed into
it.

I crouched down. My hand dug through the dirt. Something hard came
under my fingers. A stalk. A wheat stalk, trampled and broken. Some
had only the stem left, the head torn away. Some still had the head
attached, but crushed flat, the grain nowhere to be seen.

I found a few more like that. Gathered in my palm. It didn't come to
more than a few handfuls.

There were footprints in the ground too. Not human footprints. Broad,
flat tracks. I recognized them — the marks of a machine that had come
to the boundary and turned back. The line stopped exactly at the
stake. Not a hand's width over.

I stared at that track for a long while. That precision was what hurt
worse. If the machine had drifted a little further in by accident and
then turned back, I could at least have understood the accident. But
that line was straight, as if drawn with a ruler. It wasn't an
accident. It was arithmetic.

I walked the rest of the inside of the corner too, in case there was
more in the opposite side. There wasn't. Every corner was the same.
Trampled stalks, crushed heads, and dirt.

In one spot, the earth had been dug through, more than anywhere else.
It looked like the trace of someone who'd been there before me. A
handprint remained. I laid my own hand over it. It was about the same
size.

I never learned whose hand that was. I still don't. I only know they
crouched, as I did, and dug through this same dirt, as I did. And
probably went home empty-handed, as I would. I never met that person.
But that one handprint made me feel, somehow, as if I'd shared
something with them. They too, probably, had heard of this place from
someone like the old man. And come, hoping, as I had, and crouched
before this same dirt, as I did.

I stayed crouched there a long while.

I looked around further. Wondering if I'd come to the wrong place, if
the real corner was somewhere else. I checked the stake again. It was
the right place. The mark was exact. The boundary was clear.

The law was being kept. That place had, in fact, been left. There
were no marks of any machine ever having been brought inside it.
Only, there was nothing worth leaving, in there.

That was the first time, I think, that I saw this city's true face.
Until then, I'd believed that if the law wasn't broken, everything was
all right. That day I learned that a law can go unbroken and still
leave a place entirely empty.

I sat there a long while, then wrapped the few handfuls I'd gathered
in a small cloth. Better than nothing. I told myself I should still
carry it home.

As I gathered it, I did a strange arithmetic. How many spoonfuls of
gruel these few handfuls would make. It didn't look like it would come
to even one day's worth. I found myself a stranger, doing that
arithmetic. A few days before, I hadn't known how to make calculations
like this at all. This city was teaching me.

Now I know. This was the first skill a person without standing
learns in this city. The arithmetic of making what little you have
last through days you have nothing. I learned that arithmetic for the
first time that day, and for a very long time after, I couldn't put
it down.

Getting up, my legs had gone numb. I didn't know how long I'd been
sitting. The sun had climbed high.

Voices carried from far off. Someone working, on the other side of the
field. Machine sounds mixed in. I felt, freshly, how close that empty
place stood to those sounds. Over there, abundance. Here, emptiness.
The line between them was a single stake.

I've never forgotten that distance. A few steps, that was all. A few
steps, and abundance and emptiness were divided. And crossing those
few steps was forbidden by law. Cross it, and you became a thief. I
didn't want to become a thief.

I thought, suddenly, of the day my husband taught me the sickle. Don't
cut too clean, he'd said, spill a little. What you spill isn't
wasted, he'd told me. It's left behind.

I'd said, before, that I took that for a joke, that day. I never
thought I'd stand here, before this empty corner, and think of it
again.

He knew that spilling and leaving something behind were the same
act. This city had turned the two exactly backward. It took pride in
never spilling, never leaving anything. That was the first time I felt,
in my body, how different the world he'd lived in was from this
city's. The law used the same words. Leave the corner of the field.
But the meaning of that leaving had changed entirely.

A single grain came up out of the dirt. Half broken. I set it on my
palm and looked at it a long while. This was the whole of what the law
had left for me. I searched for someone to blame it on. I couldn't
find anyone. Blaming the machine was pointless — the machine was only
a machine. Blaming the law was pointless — the law was being kept.
Blaming a person was pointless — I didn't even know who that person
would be.

That was the first time I understood there is a grief with no one to
blame. It's far heavier than a grief with someone to blame. With
nowhere to send your anger, that anger, in the end, turns back inward.

Walking home, I thought again of the old man's words. No point. I'd
let it pass, at the time. Now I knew exactly what it meant.

I tried not to cry, walking. Whether I managed it, I don't know. My
face was hot.

I won't say, in this passage, whether I cried or not. There's a
reason. Whether I cried or not isn't what matters here. What mattered
was whether I would go back to that place again. That day, I walked
home without having decided that yet. I kept holding that one grain in
my hand the whole way.

I came home to find Pikra waiting at the door.

"…Where were you?" Pikra asked. Worry was plain in her voice.

I slipped the cloth in my hand into my pocket.

"…Just went for a walk," I said.

Pikra studied my face. She looked as if she wanted to ask something,
and held it back.

"…Dinner's ready," Pikra said. "Let's eat."

We ate together again that evening. I said nothing. Pikra asked
nothing. We ate dinner like that, and lay down like that. As if
nothing had happened at all.

I set the few handfuls, wrapped in cloth, down quietly in one corner
of the room. I don't know if Pikra saw them. Even if she had, she
wouldn't have said anything. I didn't eat that handful that night. Only
the next morning did I mix it into the gruel. The amount was so small
I could barely tell it was there at all.

At the time, I thought that was consideration. I learned later. It
wasn't consideration. It was that Pikra didn't know the answer either.
It had been too long since she'd left this city. She only knew the
corner clause was still there. She didn't know what was left in the
corner.

I lay awake in the dark, eyes open. Beside me, Pikra's breathing was
steady.

I went back over the day in my mind. Waking before dawn. Walking half
an hour. Seeing the stake. Seeing there was nothing there. Gathering a
few handfuls. Walking home again.

The law had been kept. That was the strangest part. I thought I would
rather have been deceived by someone — then I could have hated the one
who deceived me. But no one had deceived me. The corner really had
been left. There was simply nothing inside it.

I lay in the dark thinking whether to go again tomorrow. At first I
leaned toward not going. The old man had been right. No point. It had
been a wasted trip. But thinking a little further, another question
rose. If I don't go, then what will I do. Sit in this room, taking my
half-portion from whatever Pikra shares out, day after day?

I found no answer to that question. Without an answer, I made up my
mind to go again. Not because the hope had returned. Whatever hope I'd
felt that day, I'd already spent it, entirely, in that field. I decided
to go again simply because going would shame me less than not going.

I thought little of this decision, at the time. Now I know. This
decision made the months that followed. I didn't go out because hope
was there — I went out to test whether I could go, even with no hope
at all. I didn't know the difference, then. Now I think it's the most
important difference in this whole book.

I turned onto my side and looked at Pikra's back. I had come this far,
following her. When she told me three times to go back, I hadn't gone.
If someone asked whether I regretted that choice, I would have said no,
even that night. Only that night was the first time I paid the price
of that choice, in my own body.

I close the first part of this book on this chapter. Part One was the
story of my following this woman into the city. Following my mother,
into a place I knew would cost me my standing. I didn't know, then,
exactly what that price was. Now I do. The price wasn't expulsion. It
was standing, every day, before an empty corner.

A different story begins from here. The story of going out, day
after day, to a field where nothing falls. I set down that first day
today. I went out the next day too. And the day after that. Why, I'll
let come clear, little by little, in the part that follows. For now
I'll only set down this much — I came home empty-handed, and said
nothing, and the next day, went out again.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Gleaning Ground, Part Two

The Field Where Nothing Falls

Chapter 13. The Loss Rate

_Previously_ — Xene went looking for the corner of the field that was
supposed to be left, even for those with no standing. What she found
there was a few handfuls of trampled, broken stalks.

That night I didn't ask Pikra anything.

She must have seen me come in empty-handed. Our room was one room; there was nowhere to hide it. But Pikra didn't ask either. We split what there was for supper, and both lay down as if nothing had happened.

I thought, at the time, that this was consideration. I learned later that it wasn't. Pikra didn't know the answer any more than I did. She had been gone from this city too long. She still believed the corner clause was there — she just didn't know what the corner actually held.

In the night, the machines were running out toward the fields. The same sound as by day. I found that strange. Outside, we put the sickle down when the sun went down. Cutting in the dark meant cutting your own hand. The machines here didn't know it was dark.

The sound went on all night. It never cut off and started again. It just went, level and continuous. I lay listening to it and thought of our field back home. Our field went quiet when the sun set. I learned that night, for the first time, how precious that quiet was.

There was no light in the room, so I couldn't see the ceiling. I lay in the dark replaying what I'd seen that day. Trampled stalks. Broken grain. Things so mixed into the dirt I couldn't tell grain from clod.

Outside, that would have meant someone had stolen the place bare. If there was a thief, you could curse the thief. Here there was no thief. Nobody had simply left anything there.

I didn't know that night how large that difference was. When you're robbed, you know whose fault it is. When nothing at all is left, you go looking for someone to blame, and eventually you end up wondering whether you came to the wrong place, whether the fault is your own. I kept turning that thought over for a long time afterward.

The next day I went out again.

This time I didn't go to the field. I went toward the market. Not to buy anything. I thought if I went where the crowds were, I might be able to ask something.

The Harvest's market was bigger than any market I had ever seen. Outside, there had been nothing you could call a market — a few households bartering, that was all. Here the lines went on without end, and every line had something piled behind it. Sacks of flour stacked as high as a person in some places, fruit sorted by color in others.

I walked past trying not to think about being hungry. It didn't work well.

There was a bakery too. Every time the oven door opened, the smell reached to the end of the street. The line wasn't long. People stood as a matter of course, bought as a matter of course, left as a matter of course. Nobody looked at that bread as if it were anything special.

I still remember that bakery. I remember its name too. I won't write it here. I'll only note that I stood in front of it for a few seconds without moving. It wasn't only hunger. Well — it was hunger too. But more than that, it was the strangeness of the fact that this was nothing at all, to everyone but me.

There was a board standing at the market's entrance. I had never seen anything like it. A wide flat screen, and numbers on it that kept changing. People glanced at it in passing and kept walking. Nobody stood there long. It was just always there.

I stood there a long time.

At the top of the screen was the city's name. Underneath were several lines, most of them words I didn't understand. But one line caught my eye.

Harvest efficiency: 99.98%

I didn't even know at first whether that was a good number or a bad one. Ninety-nine point ninety-eight out of a hundred was close enough to a hundred that I assumed it must be good. But I didn't know what it was an efficiency of.

An older person was standing beside me, a shopping basket on their arm — someone who lived nearby, by the look of it. I worked up the nerve to ask.

"…What's that number?"

The person glanced at me, then at the screen, and answered without much thought.

"…Ratio of what's brought in to what's spilled. That close to the top, it means almost nothing's spilled."

"…How much is spilled?"

"…Point-oh-two out of a hundred." the person said. "They used to be nowhere near this good at bringing it all in. Machines are better now."

Then they went on their way. Without a second thought.

I don't resent that person. I still don't. They were only passing along good news. In this city, that really was good news. It only became bad news on the side where people like me stood.

I read the small print under the board further. There was a line comparing it to last year's figure. Last year it had been 99.91 percent. The year before, 99.7. I traced the numbers below that with my finger. Five years back, the high 98s. Ten years back, the high 96s.

I didn't understand what that meant at the time. I understand now. It was a record of how, year after year, less and less had been falling in this field. No one had made it happen suddenly, in a single season. It had been reduced, little by little, steadily, over a very long time. Every time I look at this number, it's that steadiness that frightens me most.

Another person standing nearby glanced at me, took in my clothes, and added a word.

"…First time here, huh."

"…Yes."

"…What are you going to do with that number?"

I couldn't answer. I didn't know myself what I meant to do with it.

The person shrugged and walked off. There didn't seem to be any unkindness in it. They'd just been curious, and when no answer came, the curiosity went with them.

I stood there and did the arithmetic.

A hundred minus point-oh-two left ninety-nine point ninety-eight. Which meant those who did the harvesting took ninety-nine point ninety-eight parts out of a hundred, and what was left for people like me was point-oh-two.

I had no feel for what point-oh-two meant at the time. I saw it later, out in the field, with my own hands. One sack out of one plot — that was roughly what was left over: a little more than a single grain.

That was the day I first started wondering who had set this number. If a person had set it, there would be someone to be angry at. But that wasn't how it turned out. It wasn't that a person had decided who should be angry. It was that a person had arranged things so no one would need to be.

There was more small print beside the board. I read that too.

"This figure is refreshed each season by the Roll's harvest management system, and continues to improve through the optimization of path, speed, and machine placement."

I read that sentence over several times. Path. Speed. Placement. Improve.

Outside, we harvested with sickles. There was always spillage. Like the summer my husband taught me to cut and told me, leave a little. A hand doing the work would always miss something — that was only natural.

Here, it wasn't a hand.

It was only then that I understood what those words meant. Optimizing the path meant the machine left no stretch of the plot uncrossed. Optimizing the speed meant it never moved too fast for a stalk to be cut and passed over uncut. The places where a person would have made a mistake — the machine did not make.

Spillage is a mistake. The Roll had made mistakes impossible. That was all it was.

I left the market and walked toward the field.

It was a long walk. The Harvest's fields ringed the whole city; whichever direction you went, you eventually arrived at field. Outside, the field had belonged to the village. Here it felt like the city belonged to the field.

I could see the harvester moving far off — large and slow, and yet not a single degree off true. That was the first day I watched it for any length of time. Before that I'd only thought of it as a big machine.

Coming closer, I saw it wasn't one machine but several. First came one with a wide blade fanned out in front; a little behind it, a narrower, taller machine followed; behind that, another leveled the soil. Three machines in a single line, spaced exactly apart. No one stood near them. Off in the distance, a handful of people stood watching.

That was when it hit me that none of them was holding a sickle.

Where the machines had passed, a row was left with nothing on it. No stalk, no grain, nothing. I walked the length of it. Only dirt and stubble. The stubble was all cut to the same height.

I remember that height. Wrist height. Lower than where my husband's sickle used to cut. A person cuts a little high, to save their strength. The machine had no strength to save.

I walked to the end of the row. There I found the corner marker — a wooden stake, small letters carved near the top: "Article 9 Preservation Zone." The stake was clean as new. Even the little guard plate hadn't lost its paint.

Someone was standing beside the stake, measuring something with a ruler. I went over.

"…What are you doing?"

The person answered without looking up.

"…Checking the boundary. Every season." They set the ruler down again. "It has to be exactly twenty paces from this stake to that one. If it's off by even a little, I have to report it."

"…Why so exact, when it doesn't even matter here—"

I didn't finish the sentence. The person looked up at me then, for the first time.

"…Because it's the law." they said, as if it weren't an odd question at all. "The corner designation falls under Article 9. If it's not kept exact, that's a violation."

Then they bent back to the ruler. I could see they were conscientious. They were genuinely trying to keep those twenty paces exact. The exactness seemed to have nothing to do with what lay inside the boundary, and they didn't appear to care. Or maybe they'd learned it wasn't something to care about.

I've remembered that person a long time. I never learned their name. They did nothing wrong. They only did their part exactly. But that exactness felt colder to me than any laziness could have. A lazy person will spill something by accident, eventually. An exact person doesn't spill. And this city, at least in this one matter, kept no lazy people on.

I walked to the end of the row. On a hunch, I crouched and dug through the dirt. One grain came up. Just one. Half-buried, cracked.

I set it in my palm and looked at it for a long time.

That was everything I gleaned that day. The point-oh-two percent, sitting in the palm of my hand. I didn't eat it. I carried it home. I still know where it is.

Back home, I set the grain down in front of Pikra.

Pikra looked at it, then at me.

"…Is this all?" she asked.

"…Yes."

"…Why are you smiling?"

I hadn't known I was smiling. I touched my face. I really was.

"…I don't know." I said. "It's just — it's strange, that this is a number now."

I told her about the board in the market. The number, the small print beside it, last year's figure, the year before that. Pikra listened without a word.

"…It wasn't like this before." Pikra said, after a while.

"…What wasn't?"

"…The corner." she said. "When I lived here, there really used to be something at the corner. Not much — but enough to fill a hand."

I didn't know what to say to that.

"…When did it stop?"
