"…I don't know." Pikra said. "I left before then. It was still there when I left. When it stopped, I never knew."

Pikra picked up the grain. She held it in her hand a moment, then split it and ate half, giving me the other. Her half tasted mostly of dirt.

It wasn't until that night that I understood why I'd smiled. It wasn't that the number was strange. It was that the number didn't hate me. If a person starves me, I can hate that person. But that number starved me in a way that left no one to hate. I should have known that was the frightening part. That night I only found it strange.

It took me a few more days to learn the difference. It was all right not to know yet. I could go out every day whether I knew it or not.

Outside, when a bad harvest came, everyone went hungry together. It was sad, but it wasn't strange. Heaven hadn't given. You could resent heaven, but you couldn't take heaven to court.

Here, heaven gives. The wheat grows well. The rain comes on time. The harvesters don't rest a single day. No one intends to starve anyone.

And yet I was starving.

It took me a long time to put that difference into words. I can now. Hunger outside was hunger no one had made. Hunger here was hunger that came from no one making anything otherwise. The first is a disaster. The second is a design. Only, if you ask who did the designing, no one raises a hand. That's the difference.

I fell asleep that night with the taste of dirt from that grain still in my mouth. I meant to go out the next day, and the day after that. Knowing full well that nothing would change.

I thought, at the time, that it was stubbornness. Looking back now, it wasn't. I simply didn't want to let that number decide for me, even after I'd seen it with my own eyes. The Roll had decided that 0.02 percent was my portion. That didn't mean I had to stop going to claim it. If I didn't go, the number won. If I went, the number still didn't lose. But I chose not to be the one who didn't go. That's all it was.

Writing this chapter, I went and looked the number up again. It's still sitting right where it was. Much later, a day comes when that number really does change. But that's a later story, and before I tell it I need to set down, first, how long and how calmly this number kept its place.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. The Second Day

_Previously_ — Xene learned why nothing was left at the corner of the
field. The Roll had optimized the harvest until the loss rate was
0.02 percent. And that it was no one's fault.

The next day I went out again.

I could write a reason here. It would sound convincing on the page. But what I actually thought that morning wasn't convincing at all. I simply opened my eyes, sat up, and put on my shoes. The thinking came trailing slowly along behind.

Writing this book, I keep finding myself checking that order. People assume the reason comes first and the action follows. I lived through the reverse of that, too many times over. My body got up first, and the reason caught up with it later. On some days the reason never came at all.

Past the door there was a road. I had it memorized within a day. Past the well, through an alley hemmed by a low wall, past three grain silos, and the field began. The silos always had their doors shut. I tried not to imagine how much was piled up inside them. That didn't work well either.

On the way to the corner I always stopped in the same spot to retie my shoelaces. Some days I stopped there and retied them even when there was no need. I learned then, for the first time, that habit can arrive before reason does.

There was a low stone at that spot. I would sit and catch my breath there. Its surface was worn smooth — which meant plenty of others had sat on it before me. I often wondered who they'd been.

Pikra saw me leaving. She said nothing.

The third day was the same. I woke before dawn, drank a mouthful of water, and went. Pikra pretended to be asleep. I knew it was pretense. She must have known that I knew. We never spoke a single word about it.

I still can't explain that silence. Anyone watching would have thought we were indifferent to each other. We weren't. Pikra didn't know why I went out, and neither did I. When two people who don't know say it aloud, they end up lying to each other. So we didn't say it.

Instead Pikra did something else. Before I left, she always had a bowl of water waiting. She never said anything about it. I'd simply wake to find the bowl full. I never once caught her getting up to fill it. She must have risen before me.

I'm writing about that water bowl in this book for the first time. I thought it was nothing at the time. I don't think that now. When Pikra couldn't find words to stop me, she left water instead. That was the whole of what she could do, and I took it.

By the fourth day, I knew there were others who went out around the same hour.

An old man was sitting on the path to the corner, ahead of me. A sack lay across his knees, empty.

We didn't greet each other. We just happened to walk the same direction, and at the corner, we each took our own place. He sat in one corner as if it were the seat he always took.

I learned his name later, but I won't write it here. What matters in this chapter isn't who he was, but the fact that he was there. I wasn't strange for being the only one. There were others who came, every day, already knowing there was nothing there.

From then on I started counting how many came to the corner. On the busiest days, five or six. They scattered themselves as though each had a fixed seat. Few spoke to one another. Everyone kept their eyes on their own patch of dirt.

I thought that was strange. People in the same situation might be expected to help each other, but no one did. I understood later. It wasn't that there was nothing worth helping with. It was that there was so completely nothing, that no one could face the others.

I'd been told that poverty binds people together. I only half believe that now. Poverty binds people when there's still something, however little, to share. When there's truly nothing left to share, poverty scatters them. I learned that at the corner.

On the fifth day, I timed it for the first time.

Half an hour to walk from home to the corner. Another half hour standing there sifting the dirt. Another half hour to walk back. An hour and a half spent, and for that hour and a half, what I gained was usually nothing at all.

I did the arithmetic and asked myself what use it was.

No answer came.

Outside, I had never timed anything that way. Work was something you did until it was finished. You went out when the sun rose and came in when it set. Counting the hours you'd spent didn't matter. Here, for the first time, I turned my time into a number. An hour and a half. From the moment I made that number, I began to price that time. And I came to see that the price was traded, always, for nothing.

It took time even to stop doing that arithmetic. Once a person starts measuring, they want to keep measuring. I kept timing myself for several more days after that. Then one day I stopped looking at my wrist. My body seemed to understand, before my mind did, that not timing it made the loss hurt less. There were more and more things, around then, that my body knew before my head did.

Looking back now, I think the question itself was wrong. I'd already decided it had to be useful before I asked, and that's why no answer came. I was doing something else entirely then, something other than use. I still don't fully know, even having finished this book, what that was. But I need to set down two things together — that I found no answer to that question that day, and that I went out again the next day regardless.

On the sixth day, a little rain fell.

Not much. Just enough to wet my clothes. I went out anyway. The corner looked no different from any other day. Trampled stalks, dirt, an occasional broken grain. Only the color was a little darker for the wet.

Standing in that rain, a thought crossed my mind. If the rain got heavier, the machines wouldn't be able to come in. Wouldn't something be left, then? I didn't hold that thought long. The rain stopped soon after. I forgot it.

I'm setting this thought down in advance. A day comes, later, when it actually happens. On that day I remember this sixth day again.

I came home soaked, and Pikra held out a dry cloth. I took it and dried my hair first.

"…You'll catch cold." Pikra said.

"…I'm fine."

"…Stop saying that." Pikra said. Her voice wasn't angry. "You never look fine when you say it."

I laughed. Pikra laughed a little too.

I remember that brief laugh. Laughter had grown scarce between us by then. That was the first one in days.

On the seventh day, I saw a stranger far off in the field for the first time.

He was standing beside a harvester, looking something over. What he held wasn't a spade or a sickle — something flat. His clothes were different too. Not sweat-soaked like the other hired hands, but clean.

I didn't watch him long. I decided he had nothing to do with me. He didn't seem to notice me either.

I don't remember his face from that day. It was too far. What I remember is the way he stood. The stance of someone checking something. Not the stance of someone fixing something. I couldn't tell the difference then. Later I could.

He kept turning up in that same spot after that. Some mornings, some evenings. I guessed it must be his job — tending the harvesters, or watching over them. I didn't think further than that.

I'll write no more of him than this in this chapter. Anything more would be a lie. That was all I knew about him then. I didn't know his name. I didn't know his face. Only in the next chapter do I hear his name, and learn it belongs to him.

On the eighth day, I thought for the first time about giving up.

My feet ached badly that morning. My shoes were worn through at the heel. Walking was hard. I stood at the door a long while, hesitating.

Pikra didn't pretend to sleep that day.

"…Rest today." Pikra said, her voice thick with sleep.

"…I'm fine."

"…I can see your feet."

I said nothing.

"…Resting won't change anything anyway." Pikra said. It was impossible to tell whether that meant stay or go.

In the end I went out. I wrapped my feet in rags instead of shoes.

I still can't explain exactly why I went that day. It wasn't that my feet had stopped hurting. It wasn't that I was expecting anything either. I simply felt that if I didn't go, everything I'd gone out for until now would become, from that moment, as if it had never happened. I didn't want that.

I walked slowly that day, twice as long as usual. I stopped several times to rub my feet. And at some point it struck me, all over again, that I was going through all this to reach a place with nothing in it.

I laughed then. A strange laugh. Not sad, not happy. I just found what I was doing so absurd that laughter came out of me. Aching feet, dragging myself toward a place with nothing — for a moment I looked like a stranger to myself.

I agonized a long time over whether to write this laugh into the book. I worried it would look comic. But I decided to write it in. Because that laughter was really there, in that spot, that day. This book doesn't pretend nothing but sorrow happened.

On the ninth day, I spoke with the old man for the first time.

I wasn't the one who started it. He looked at his sack and said, seemingly to no one in particular:

"…Why do you come every day, miss?"

I wasn't ready with an answer.

"…I don't know." I said. "What about you, sir?"

He laughed a little — silently.

"…I don't know either." he said. "Been at it years now. Still don't know."

"…So why do you keep coming?"

"…Good question." He gathered a fistful of dirt and let it fall again. "If somebody asks, I just say it's habit. A habit probably had a reason once. Where the reason went, I couldn't tell you. Just the habit's left."

"…How many years?"

"…Counted long enough, then quit." He set the sack back on his knee. "What's the point of counting."

That frightened me a little — that he'd come every day for years and still didn't know why. But it comforted me a little too. I wasn't the only one without an answer.

I met him often after that. We never became friends. Just two people who came to the same place. But I learned later that this could be steadier than friendship, some days. Standing beside someone who doesn't ask you why is, sometimes, its own kind of strength.

From that day I started watching his sack more closely. It was always empty. Occasionally there was a thin layer at the bottom, but I never once saw it take on the shape of a full sack. I wondered when it had last been full. I didn't ask. We'd only exchanged a single conversation in nine days. That wasn't a question you asked, at that stage.

I still think of that man's sack, even now. What shape an empty sack takes after years of being carried empty — I learned that by watching him. A sack, like a person, comes to fold a certain way if it goes unused long enough.

On the evening of the tenth day, I stopped counting for the first time.

I mean I stopped counting the days. Until then I'd walked along ticking off the number on my fingers. From that day I didn't.

I think that's the true end of this chapter. As long as I was counting the days, I was proving something to myself — how many days I'd held out, how many more I could hold out. When I stopped counting, I stopped proving. I simply became someone whose body rose at the same hour every day. What was left where the proving used to be wasn't much of anything. Just the one fact that morning would come again, and I would get up again.

I think this is the easiest chapter in the whole book to write, and at the same time the hardest. Easiest because nothing happened. Hardest because I know that, even so, it's the most important chapter. Beginning in the next one, things start to happen. Someone recognizes me. But the reason that person could recognize me is that I had been there, at the same hour, in the same spot, every single day.

Ten days on, the corner was still exactly as it had been. The dirt was the same color, the stubble the same height, and what my hand found there was still next to nothing. I could feel that fact growing duller with every day that passed. The first few days, every time I came home empty-handed, something had tightened just below my ribs. By the tenth day, that tightness had settled into something more like the ordinary weight of a day. Not a special grief anymore — just a burden I carried around all the time.

I don't think that was getting better. Growing dull and getting better are not the same thing. I was only learning how to carry that weight while I walked. And that walk, without my knowing it, had already caught someone's eye. Someone had been watching me, day after day, in the same spot — and had begun, day by day, to find me strange. I didn't know that yet.

I went, every day. That's the whole of this chapter.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. Lytron

_Previously_ — Xene went to the corner every day. She stopped counting
the days. Then, one day, she saw a stranger on the far side of the
field. She didn't know, at the time, that he had been watching her too.

On the twelfth day, that man walked over to me.

The morning was unusually clear. The whole field was visible at a glance — wheat ripened the same gold color out to the far edge, and above it three harvesters moved, small and distant. I was sitting in my usual spot at the corner, sifting the dirt as always.

I heard footsteps. At first I assumed they were only passing by. But they were heading straight for me. I hesitated over whether to stand or stay seated, and in the end just stayed as I was. There was nowhere to hide my dirt-covered hands.

Up close, his clothes were plainly different — no fading anywhere, good material. His shoes weren't worn at the toe either. In his hand was something flat, and it was only then that I learned what it was: a Roll terminal. He looked a year or two older than me. His expression was calm in a way that didn't match his age. Nothing hurried about him at all.

That was the first time in this city that I saw someone walking straight toward me. Until then, people had always passed beside me or gone out of their way around me. No one had come toward me. So it took a few seconds before I even recognized that the steps were meant for me.

"…What are you doing there?"

His voice wasn't loud. Not angry either. Just the voice of someone curious.

"…Gleaning." I said.

"…What?"

I opened my palm to show him. Two grains. One of them cracked.

He looked at it a moment. His expression didn't change. Not surprised, not mocking. He simply looked.

"…What do you do with that?"

"…Eat it."

He looked at me again. Longer this time. As if he were repeating what I'd said to himself, more than once.

"…Two grains?"

"…I gather this much every day." I said. "Some days not even this much."

He tilted his head slightly. Less confused than someone hearing an unfamiliar sum for the first time.

"…That must be hard." he said.

There was no pity in it. It was the tone of someone confirming a fact. I found that easier, actually. If it had been pity, I wouldn't have known how to answer.

"…You've been coming here for days." he said. It wasn't a question. It sounded like confirmation.

I found that strange. I thought this was my first time seeing him, but it meant he'd already known about me for days.

"…How do you know?"

"…It's in the records." he said, lifting the terminal slightly. "Entry logs for this zone. If the same person comes in and out at the same time every day for more than ten days, it gets flagged."

That was when I first learned that my going out every day hadn't been mine alone to know. It had all been recorded somewhere. I found it strange more than frightening. I'd thought no one paid me any attention — but a machine had. Before any person did. I didn't know whether that record was kept under my name or just a spot number. I didn't have the nerve to ask.

"…I'm assisting with management of this zone." he added. "I check the traffic, the entry logs, the loss rate, that kind of thing, every day. Things end up catching your eye that way. Like someone coming in and out of the same spot at the same hour for more than ten days."

"…There are other people who come every day too, besides me."

"…I know. There are records for them too." he said, unconcerned. "But yours was a little different."

"…What was?"

"…The others taper off after a while. A few days coming, then gone, then a few more, then gone again. You didn't miss a single day."

Hearing that, I felt all over again the fact that I'd been under observation. And at the same time I could tell he hadn't said it to make me out as especially wrong for it. It was simply the tone of someone remarking on a figure that stood out.

"…Why do you come every day to a place with nothing in it?"

He asked it without any malice at all, and that was what unsettled me more. If it had been asked unkindly, I'd have found it easier to answer — I could have gotten angry, or ignored him. But he was genuinely curious.

I had no answer.

"…Is it because of the corner clause?" he went on. "The one that says people without standing can glean there."

"…Yes."

"…That is what that place is for." he said. "I know that. Article 9. Only…" he chose his next words. "Only, these days there isn't much left there. Efficiency's improved."

I noticed he said "efficiency's improved" in a tone that was neither boasting nor apologizing. Simply stating a fact.

"…Do you know?" I asked. "How high the loss rate is?"

My voice came out sharper than I'd expected, sharp enough to surprise even me. He looked slightly startled. For the first time his expression moved.

"…0.02 percent." he answered. Without hesitation. "It's the figure for the zone I manage."

"…Then you'd know. That I can come every day and get nothing."

"…I know." he said. "But separate from that, I'm still curious. Knowing that, why you still come."

I had no answer to that question. Truly none. Not then.

Writing this now, I find myself wondering what would have happened if I'd made something up that day. Something like, "I still think there might be something someday," or "It's a right the law gives me." Those kinds of lines did cross my mind at the time. I said none of them out loud. Because they weren't true.

The silence went on a while. Lytron didn't press. He didn't seem to be waiting for an answer either — he looked more like someone considering his next question.

"…Have you thought about trying a different field?" he asked. "There might be zones with a higher loss rate than this one, not this one."

That question surprised me a little. That he knew loss rates differed from field to field, and that he'd think to tell someone like me.

"…I didn't know there was such a thing." I said.

"…It's not a big difference." he added right away. "They're all similarly low. It won't be very different wherever you go."

I couldn't tell whether that was meant to comfort me or was just information. Thinking about it now, I'd say it was both. In him, the wish to comfort and the wish to state facts precisely didn't seem to be two separate things.

"…Have you been to other fields yourself?" I asked.

"…I manage this one." he said. "I only see the other zones as numbers. I don't go out to them myself."

"…Then how do you know they're all similar?"

Lytron went quiet a moment. It was the first time an answer hadn't come right away.

"…From the reports." he finally said. Something about the answer must have sounded insufficient even to him, because he glanced down at his terminal for a moment after saying it.

I remember that brief pause. It was the first time I saw that even he didn't know everything. What he knew was numbers, and numbers could be known without ever going out to the field. But what those numbers turned into, once they were sitting in a person's palm — numbers alone couldn't show him that.

He looked at me a moment, then wrote something on the terminal. I felt a stab of unease — wondering what sort of record he was making about me.

"…What are you writing?"

"…Oh, this is something else." he said. "Time of today's zone inspection."

The answer was so ordinary that I felt myself sag a little. I'd thought something significant was happening. It wasn't. To him, I was just one person he'd crossed paths with during that day's inspection.

"…What's your name?" I asked. I don't know why I asked.

"…Lytron." he said. Then he asked back, "And you?"

"…Xene."

Lytron didn't write the name down anywhere. He just nodded once.

"…Xene." he repeated it once. Not the face of someone curious about what it meant. Just the face of someone committing a name to memory.

"…What's your standing?" he asked. Casually, the way you'd ask about the weather.

"…None."

"…Ah." he said, shortly. He didn't look surprised. He might already have known — with an entry log on the terminal, he could hardly have missed it.

He didn't ask anything further. He didn't wear the expression that usually followed hearing someone had no standing — not pity, not wariness. He simply took in one more piece of information. Up to that point, I'd watched people's faces change, however slightly, at the words no standing. Some looked away. Some walked faster. Lytron did neither. His was the face of someone confirming what he'd already understood.

I turned that "ah" over in my mind for a long time. There had truly been nothing in it. I couldn't decide, for a long while, whether that nothing frightened me or was a relief.

And then he went on his way.

I watched his back for a long time.

His walk was unhurried — the walk of someone with nowhere urgent to be. Watching it, I thought: he probably won't think of me again for the rest of today. For me, that conversation had been the first time in twelve days that I'd exchanged words with anyone. For him, it had been one thing among many during an inspection.

I thought about this a long time that night. How many words does a person exchange in a day? Around then, I rarely crossed ten words in a whole day. Someone like Lytron probably exchanged hundreds. So the few words we'd traded took up a completely different amount of room in his day than they did in mine. I'm not writing this to resent him for it. I'm only recording that it was true.

I won't write that this was a bad thing. He hadn't done anything wrong. He'd asked what he was curious about, heard the answer, and gone off to his work. The trouble was that his day and my day didn't rest on the same scale — and that this was no one's fault.

I sat at the corner a little longer than usual that day. I stopped sifting the dirt and watched the direction he'd walked. Far off, the three harvesters were still moving at the same steady pace. Lytron walked up to one of them and bent over his terminal. He seemed to be checking something, correcting something. The motion looked entirely familiar. Clearly something he did every day.

I thought about how well he must know those machines. If I'd never touched a sickle in my life and were handed one of those, I'd have been afraid. Lytron wasn't afraid of it at all. He handled it the way a person handles their own hand.

That difference stayed with me a long time. I knew the field by hand. Lytron knew the field by numbers. The same field, and yet we were looking at two different fields.

At home, I told Pikra what had happened. It was the first time I'd told her anything that had happened out in the field.

"…I met someone named Lytron."

Pikra's hands went still.

"…Lytron?"

"…Yes. Why?"

"…Nothing." She started moving again. "The family name sounds familiar. I heard it when I lived here."

"…What kind of family is it?"

Pikra thought for a moment.

"…A good family." she said. "Very high standing. Strange, that a son from a family like that would ask about you."

"…What's a family like that doing out in the field?" I asked. "He didn't look like the people who work out here."

"…High-standing families put their sons through a stretch of that hands-on work sooner or later." Pikra said. "It's supposed to teach them, so that when they end up in a higher seat later, they understand what's below them. That was already the talk when I lived here."

"…So he really does understand it, then?"

Pikra looked at me a moment.

"…Knowing and living through it are different things." she said. "That boy has a house to go home to in the evening. Not sleeping here, the way we do."

I thought she was right. And at the same time it was also true that Lytron hadn't seemed like a bad person. That both of those could exist together — I learned that for the first time that day.

I lay awake a long time that night. That Lytron wasn't a bad person, and that what Lytron lived through was not what I lived through — these two things didn't clash against each other. I'd believed, up to then, that only bad people made bad things happen. From that day I began, slowly, to learn how wrong that was.

I didn't quite understand, at the time, why that should be strange at all.

I understand now. A meeting of two people at the corner of a field, one with the highest standing and one with none, was not a common thing in this city. Rare enough that even he had been curious. I didn't know that then, and so I simply fell asleep thinking I'd met an odd sort of person. I found myself, that night, repeating his name to myself a few times, silently. A name I'd never heard before, and yet, oddly, it fixed itself in my memory the first time.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. I Haven't Done Anything Wrong

_Previously_ — Xene met Lytron. He was not unkind to her. He simply
couldn't understand why she came every day to a place with nothing in
it. Pikra said his was a high-standing family.

This is a chapter I wasn't there for.

Most of what's written here, I didn't live through myself. Some of it I heard, much later, from Lytron directly. Some of it I learned only sideways, while Mena was researching this city's records. I hesitated a long time over whether to write it at all. Setting down something I never saw felt like it went against how I learned to keep this record.

Mena once said to me, "If you only write what you saw, you end up with a story that has only the person who saw it in it. But this world isn't a place where only one person lives." This is the chapter where I first felt the truth of that.

So this chapter isn't my memory. It's a day I pieced together afterward. But nothing in it is invented. I made up none of the conversation. What I set down here is only what Lytron told me himself, and what Mena confirmed as fact.

Lytron's morning began earlier than mine.

His house stood in the innermost part of the city, the district farthest from the field. I passed through that neighborhood once, much later, and remember it — wide streets, quiet. Every house had a high wall, and behind every wall, the sound of running water. It was a neighborhood with no reason to conserve it.

Lytron woke before dawn and checked his terminal first thing. Reading through the overnight zone reports was always his first task. Loss rate, machine uptime, labor assignment. If the numbers looked like yesterday's, he was at ease. If anything was even slightly off, he went looking for the cause.

I was a little surprised, hearing this. I'd assumed Lytron woke each morning and started his day at leisure — the son of a high-standing family, after all. He didn't. He too had numbers to check every morning. The only difference between us was that when those numbers went bad, he didn't go hungry for it.

His house had many rooms, I was told — though I never learned exactly how many. Lytron himself didn't seem to have counted. I found that strange. Our room was one room, and I had every corner of it memorized. A house with several rooms would have some that went mostly unused, and I could only imagine what it would feel like not to need to count them at all.

There was a small irrigation channel running through the yard. It was ornamental — not dug out of any need, but simply because it was pleasant to look at. Hearing that, I went quiet for a while. Back at our settlement, people had come to blows over a single water channel. Here, letting water simply run for no reason at all was enough to make a yard.

At breakfast, his father would ask for the day's report.

"…Yesterday's loss rate."

"…0.02 percent. No change."

"…Good work."

That was often the whole of the conversation. Lytron's father wasn't a man of many words. Though on days the numbers turned bad, the words multiplied.

I've imagined that short exchange many times over. What "good work" must have meant to Lytron. I've never once heard those words myself, so at first I felt a pang of envy. I see it a little differently now. That phrase was praise and standard at once — it also meant that on a day the numbers turned bad, that phrase wouldn't come.

Lytron's family had done harvest management work for three generations.

His great-grandfather's generation had already had a hand in this city's fields, I was told. The family's standing score had been built up over those generations. Not gained by theft. Not gained by deceiving anyone. Earned steadily, over a long time, by doing the work well.

I write this part carefully. I don't want anyone reading this book to hate Lytron for it. That family really had worked hard. Generation after generation rising early, generation after generation reading the numbers closely, generation after generation trying not to make mistakes. They hadn't reached their position by doing anything wrong. They had simply done well.

But I also came to understand, later, that this "doing well" stood in exact opposition to what was left for someone else. In a structure where one side's doing better always meant less remaining for the other, I doubt that family had ever once thought of it in those terms.

Lytron had an older brother. His brother already managed a different zone and lived independently. The two weren't on bad terms — they ate together on holidays, sometimes walked the fields together. What the brother usually told him came down to the same thing: Keep the numbers right, and everything else falls into place.

Lytron believed it. It had been true for him, so far, exactly as he'd been told.

I thought about this a long time. "Keep the numbers right and everything falls into place" — that was true for those two brothers. In their world, it really was. The trouble is that a world where that sentence isn't true was laid directly over the same city, the same ground. Writing this chapter, I came to see most clearly of all how those two worlds could sit on the same soil and almost never see each other.

Lytron was seventeen. A year older than me.

Already, at that age, he was handling zone management support work. The Roll set children of high-standing families to real work early. The reason was simple: they would sit in bigger seats later, so best to learn it now.

Lytron didn't dislike the work. He was good at it, actually. He had a feel for reading numbers, and he was quick at adjusting machine placement. His superiors trusted him. His reputation among his peers wasn't bad either — conscientious, courteous, never one to treat anyone carelessly, people said.

Hearing this reputation, I didn't believe it at first. But over time I came to understand it was true. Lytron really was that sort of person. What I'm trying to do in this book isn't to erase that fact. It's the opposite — to let it stand as it is.

Lytron had a few friends, all children of similarly placed families. On their days off, they gathered and shared meals together. I once heard the list of dishes at one of those gatherings — it was more than I'd eaten in a whole year. Lytron didn't think of it as anything special. Just what he usually ate.

Among his friends, he was sometimes teased for being too conscientious, too meticulous. You like numbers more than people, they'd joke. Lytron only laughed at it. He never denied it. He had no reason to deny it.

I think this is the most important part of this chapter. Lytron was someone who could hear that he liked numbers more than people, and laugh. Because he didn't think it made him a bad person. I think that's the face of harmlessness. A bad person bristles at a remark like that. A harmless person laughs it off. Because he feels no reason to bristle.

That afternoon, Lytron made his rounds of the zone. It was the day he met me.

The rounds followed a set order: check machine condition, check zone boundaries, check labor assignments. Somewhere in that order was the corner zone. Since it fell under Article 9, it was on the list of things to be checked regularly.

So the fact that I met Lytron that day wasn't a coincidence, I learned later. It was a coincidence, in a sense — that I was there that day, and that it happened to be inspection day, was a coincidence. But the round itself wasn't. It was his job. I had been in that spot at the same hour every day for twelve days, and Lytron passed through it following a fixed order. Two repetitions simply collided.

Lytron logged me in that day's inspection record like this: "One unregistered individual within zone, repeated access confirmed. No irregularities."

I saw that sentence later. "No irregularities." I laughed, seeing it. Whether I laughed because it was sad, or because it was absurd, I still don't quite know. That day had been the biggest thing that happened to me — and in his record it was a day with nothing irregular in it at all.

Once the round ended, Lytron went back to the office and drew up a summary of what he'd checked that day. Loss rate summaries by zone, machine uptime, changes in labor assignment. That report was written every single day, and every single day it went up to his superiors. Lytron liked that work. He said it felt good when everything fit together exactly, when not a single number was off.

"…What's good about that?" I asked him once, later.

"…Hard to explain." Lytron said. "When something fits together exactly, it feels like I've done something well."

I said nothing to that. For him, it must really have been an achievement. I don't think that achievement was a bad thing in itself. Only that it seemed strange to me that I had been standing right where that achievement completed itself.

That evening, back home, Lytron said a few words to his father about the day — the round, the machines. Among it, a brief mention of me.

"…There's someone who comes every day, out by the corner."

"…Unregistered?"

"…Yes."

"…Report it if it's something. Otherwise, don't worry about it." his father said. "That's just what that spot is for."

And that was the end of it. In that room, I'd been a story that lasted a few seconds.

I never feel angry, picturing that scene. I know that sounds strange to hear. But it's true. His father said nothing unkind. Even "don't worry about it" wasn't said out of dislike. It was simply that he'd judged it an unimportant item in his son's day. I think the judgment was wrong. But I don't want to paint the man who made it as a villain.

That night, Lytron went over the day's round record once more in his room. He said he'd paused, briefly, at the entry for my access log.

He told me this much later.

"…I felt strange that night." he said.

"…Strange how?" I asked. Much later.

"…Hard to explain." Lytron said. "Looking at your access log, it suddenly hit me — for the first time — that there was a person behind that number. Before then it had just been an entry."

"…And then?"

"…And then no conclusion came of it." Lytron said. "I just felt that, for a while, and then forgot about it the next day, reading some other report."

I heard this confession much later still. By then a great deal had already changed. I thought it belonged in this chapter. Lytron felt something, very briefly, that night. And then he forgot it. I don't hold that forgetting against him. If I'd been his age, with that many reports to check in a day, I probably would have forgotten too.

That forgetting wasn't especially cruel. After that night, Lytron woke the same way, made his rounds the same way, wrote his reports the same way. Nothing changed. And that nothing-changing, I learned later, is the most common thing in this city. Most people feel something, carry that feeling through a single night, and then set it down the next day because there's work to do. It isn't malice. A day simply passes that way.

I don't want to write this as a bad thing. If I'd been able to live that way, I would have too. Only I never got the chance to live that way. My days had nothing else filling them that I could forget under. So I had no choice but to return, every day, to the same spot in my mind. Maybe that was the biggest difference between him and me. He had the room to forget. I didn't.

I want to close this chapter with something Lytron said to himself. This too I heard later.

Around that time, he sometimes asked himself: had he done something wrong. The answer was always the same.

"…I haven't done anything wrong."

That was true. Lytron truly hadn't done anything wrong. He hadn't taken anything from anyone. He hadn't deceived anyone. He hadn't broken the law. He hadn't lied. He had only done his own work, in his own place, conscientiously.

I'll write this sentence again more than once in this book. For now I'll only set down this much: that it was true. And that even while it was true, there could still be a day when someone like me sat with two grains of wheat in her palm and had to laugh. That both of these things could be true at the same time — I held onto that longer than anything else while writing this book.

I rewrote this chapter several times. When I first wrote it, I painted Lytron a little worse than this. I thought it would land the book's point more clearly. Reading it back, I saw it was a lie. In the end I cut all of it and settled for only this much.

I tried, more than once while writing this chapter, to make myself hate Lytron. I couldn't manage it. However hard I searched for a place to put that hatred, all I found there was a seventeen-year-old conscientiously checking his numbers. Hating that person wasn't different from hating conscientiousness itself.

I know that's exactly not what this book is aimed at. This book isn't aimed at Lytron. It's aimed at the comfort he never once thought to question. And I am someone who never had that comfort — and so didn't even know it was comfort, when I had none.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. Trampled Stalks

_Previously_ — Xene learned Lytron's day only afterward. He hadn't
done anything wrong. And that fact stayed true even while she was the
one coming home empty-handed, day after day.

Even after the day I met Lytron, I kept going out.

Nothing changed. The corner stayed exactly as it had been. Trampled stalks, grain broken up in the dirt, an occasional whole one. I grew steadily more practiced at picking out what could be eaten.

This is the first time I use the word "practiced" in this chapter. Being practiced doesn't mean getting better. I want to be clear about that here. I grew practiced at hunger. Not that the hunger went away — only that I grew better at enduring it.

The walk to the corner was the same every day.

Out of the lodging, along the main road, then left where the field began. About half an hour on foot. At first that half hour felt like a waste. Later it came to feel like the better part of the day — walking, I didn't have to think about anything.

The field was vast. Too vast to take in at a glance. The machines moved in formation. The first time I saw them, I'd thought them frightening. Now I just looked at them. What frightened me wasn't the machines — it was what was left behind them. Or rather, precisely: what wasn't.

The corner sat at the field's edge, against a drainage ditch. A single stake stood there: "Zone Under Article 9." I passed that stake every day. The first few days I stopped in front of it and read the letters. Later I stopped reading. There was no longer any need.

I could still walk that path today. With my eyes shut. Half an hour, left, the stake, the ditch. Some roads a person's feet remember. I became someone whose feet remembered that one.

For the first several days, others came besides me.

An older woman, and a man who brought two children. All of us ran our palms through the dirt the same way. We didn't speak to each other. We shared the same ground and never once met each other's eyes.

After about a week, the older woman stopped coming. The week after that, the man with the children stopped too. I don't know where they went. I didn't ask. It didn't seem like asking would have brought an answer.

I guessed, later. They must have done the arithmetic. That walking half an hour to gather less than half a palmful wasn't worth it, when that half hour could go toward something else. Not a wrong calculation. I was simply the only one who never made it.

I still can't explain exactly why I kept going out. It wasn't a thing arithmetic could explain. Maybe that was precisely why I kept going.

What I actually gathered at that corner never added up to a full day's food.

I once measured it exactly. On my best day, I gathered enough to fill two cupped palms. Even that was half dirt and broken husk. Sifted down to the grain itself, it was less than a fistful.

On my worst day, there was nothing at all. Some days there were. I don't remember well what we ate on those evenings. Probably just water, before sleep.

I want to set these numbers down exactly. An amount that never came to a full day's food. Some days, nothing at all. This was the whole of what Article 9 put into my hands. The law was being kept. I confirmed that fact more than once. If the law had been broken, that would have been easier to bear.

Once, I saw two crows at the corner.

They'd arrived before me. Pecking at the dirt. At first I was glad — if there was enough there for birds to peck at, there must be something left for me too.

Up close, it wasn't so. The birds couldn't peck long before moving on either. A few pecks in one spot, and if nothing came of it, they flew to the next. I followed them and checked the places they'd left. Nothing there either.

That was the day I first understood that this corner wasn't stingy with people alone. It was stingy with birds too. I understood why, later. The Roll hadn't been aimed at people. It had simply eliminated spillage. But eliminating spillage meant that people and birds alike came away with nothing, equally. The Roll didn't starve me out of hatred. It simply hadn't thought about anyone, or anything, at all.

The birds didn't stay long. I never saw a crow at that spot again. They must have found somewhere else. I had nowhere else to go.

Back home, I shared it with Pikra.

The ritual never changed. I spread what I'd gathered on the floor. Sorted the whole grain from the fragments. Divided the whole grain in two. Ground the fragments and stirred them into water to drink.

We did it without a word. At first I divided it and Pikra received her share. A few days on, Pikra began doing the dividing herself. Her hand was more exact.

"…How do you divide it so evenly?" I asked once.

"…Practice." Pikra said.

I didn't ask further after that.

I learned later. During the years she lost her husband, and then both her sons, Pikra had already divided small portions among survivors many times before. There was a reason her hand had grown that exact.

Sometimes Pikra didn't finish her own share. At first I didn't notice. One night I woke and found her quietly pushing what was left in her bowl toward mine. I pretended to be asleep.

From that night on, I got in the habit of never quite emptying my own bowl. I left a little behind, so Pikra wouldn't see. We shared, that way, by each pretending to have what we didn't. I don't think that was a lie. It was the only way we knew, in those days, to look after each other.

One evening, the grain came out to an odd number.

Three. It couldn't be split evenly. I caught my hand before it could reach naturally for two.

"…Pikra, you take two."

"…No." Pikra said. "You're the one who went out and gathered it."

"…Even so."

We sat a moment with the three grains between us, saying nothing.

In the end Pikra picked up one and broke it in half. We each took our half, ending up with two and a half apiece.

I still remember that breaking hand. She pressed it with a fingernail and split it exactly in two — neither half larger nor smaller. I watched, that night, how carefully a person can try to be fair over the smallest possible thing.

Around then, I noticed my hands had changed.

They had once been hands that held a sickle. Now they were hands that searched the dirt for a single grain. My fingertips had cracked. Only natural, digging through dirt and dry stalks every day.

Standing up sometimes blurred my sight. Not for long. Just a moment. I knew why. I only pretended not to.

I tried to write this passage plainly. I didn't want to look pitiable. I wasn't a pitiable person, then. I was someone who got up every morning and walked that same road again. My body was, in fact, changing. That fact doesn't make me pitiful. I choose to believe that.

Around then I got in the habit of hiding what I gleaned.

I sewed a small pocket inside my clothes and carried what I gathered there as I walked. No one had ever said anything to me about it. Still, I didn't want to be seen. If I carried it in my hand, someone might notice, so I hid it against my body every time, without fail.

Looking back later, I found that habit strange. I hadn't done anything wrong. What I gathered was what the law allowed. And yet I hid it. No one had ever told me to. I thought a long time about where that habit had come from. I understand now. A person with no standing learns, on her own, without being taught, to carry even what she's been permitted to have where no one can see.

Trampled stalks couldn't be eaten. Even so, I gathered them up every time.

We used them for fuel — to heat water. Not much, but better than nothing.

One day, burning that trampled straw, a strange thought struck me. These stalks had once stood upright. The machine had laid them down as it passed. Lying flat, half-buried in dirt, by the time I found them they were already crushed apart.

Every time I saw them, my husband came to mind. Those stubbles cut off at wrist height. A sickle's cut and a machine's cut had a different grain. A sickle cuts on the slant. A machine cuts straight down. I could feel the difference now with my fingertips. That was the first skill I learned in this city.

Around then I began a counting of my own.

I gathered small stones and lined them up like tallies — grains gleaned that day, compared to yesterday's. Good days went on the right, bad days on the left. The right side almost never grew.

I realized later what an odd habit this was. I had come into this city and, for the first time, become someone who counted. But what I counted was the exact opposite of what the Roll counted. The Roll didn't count how much I had. It only counted whether I had standing. I counted the opposite — not standing, but what my hands actually held. No one would ever read that tally. I kept counting anyway.

Those stones didn't last. Later I moved on to something else. But the habit stayed. Even now, when I count something, those stones are the first thing that comes to mind.

Around then I began watching the people who worked the field.

The hired hands. Some drove the harvesters directly; others followed behind, checking their condition. They were different from people like me. Not entirely without standing — they had some standing, however low, enough to take work for a wage.

I envied them. If you were paid, at least you were sure of one day's food.

The hired hands came in early morning and left toward evening. Those handling the machines didn't speak — the noise was too loud for words. Only in their breaks did they gather briefly, drink water, exchange a few words.

I got in the habit of watching those breaks from a distance. I didn't yet have the nerve to go closer. I just watched where they sat, who sat beside whom. That was all I could manage, at that point.

I didn't know then how shallow that envy was. The hired hands' lives weren't comfortable either. They were only better off than mine. I picked up the habit, around then, of measuring people by that standard — better than me. Even now, I can't say that habit has entirely gone.

On the way home, I began to notice other things.

Some days my route home passed through an inner alley of the city. On those days I'd see what had been set out near back doors. Some of it spoiled, some of it perfectly fine, simply discarded. One day, half a loaf of bread was sitting there, whole, right on the road.

I stood in front of it a long while. Hesitated over whether to take it. In the end I didn't. I don't fully know why, even now. Gleaning at the corner was fine — that was the place the law had marked out. But this was a place no one had marked out at all. I couldn't explain the difference at the time.

I can explain it now. Gleaning at the corner was a right. Gleaning from the alley was closer to begging. I was a person with no rights left, but I still wasn't willing to beg. That was the last shred of pride I had left, at the time. Looking back it's an absurd distinction — the hunger was exactly the same either way. But I held onto that distinction anyway.

There was something discarded, and something that never fell, both together in this city. I saw that first in that alley. Nothing fell in the field. But something was thrown away in the alley. The two things were happening, at once, inside the same city.

One evening, Pikra asked me something for the first time.

"…Do you see people out there?"

"…Yes. There are a lot who work there."

"…Talked to anyone yet?"

"…Not yet."

Pikra thought a moment, then said:

"…Talk to them. Can't hurt."

I found that unexpected. Pikra had always told me to be careful — never before to reach out.

"…Why?"

"…Might be better than gleaning alone." Pikra said. Then, after a pause, she added, "When I picture you sitting there by yourself every day, I feel a little—"

Pikra didn't finish the sentence.

I still wonder, sometimes, what that unfinished sentence would have been. I think Pikra believed she had no right to be angry, no right to worry — not after having tried three times to stop me from coming with her at all. So the sentence never came all the way out. I never finished it for her. Some sentences are right left unfinished.

That night, I decided that the next day, in the field, I'd try speaking to whoever I could.

It wasn't a grand resolution. Pikra's words had simply stayed with me. That it might be better than gleaning alone.

I didn't decide beforehand who I'd speak to, or what I'd say. I just thought that in the morning, whoever happened to be nearby, I'd at least say hello. That was all.

I don't want to write this decision as though it were momentous. In the next chapter I get a little closer to the people working the field. One of them later becomes very important. But I didn't know that that night. I just fell asleep thinking, tomorrow, maybe I'll say hello to someone.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. The Hired Hands

_Previously_ — Xene gathered less than a day's food at the corner and
shared it with Pikra. Neither spoke of it. On Pikra's word, Xene
decided to try speaking to the people who worked the field.

The next morning, I went out earlier than usual.

The reason was simple. I wanted to arrive when the workers gathered. At the corner there was never anyone at all. I had to go closer to where the machines stood, into the field itself, to find people.

I don't think it's like me to have carried out that resolve within a single day. I was, by nature, someone who put things off. But there was no reason to put it off, just then. Putting it off didn't seem likely to make tomorrow any better than today.

The whole walk there, I rehearsed what to say. I ran a few greetings through my head. All of them sounded awkward. Outside, greetings hadn't been necessary — you simply opened whoever's door and went in. The city wasn't like that. Here, speaking to a stranger felt like asking permission first.

I understood later where that feeling came from. Outside, no one needed standing to speak to another person. In the city, somewhere along the way, it had come to feel like something you needed. No one had ever told me I needed permission to check. I was already feeling it, regardless.

The hired hands gathered before dawn.

I learned that for the first time that day. They gathered in front of a small storehouse at the edge of the field and received their assignments for the day — who would take which zone, who would follow which machine. It was decided fresh each morning.

Someone else handed out the assignments. Not part of the Roll — I learned later he worked under the administrator. He called names one by one and handed out slips of paper. I didn't recognize a single name.

Watching that morning scene, a strange thought struck me. These people had names. I didn't. More precisely: I had a name too, but it wasn't in the Roll. If you have a name but it's never called, is that having one, or not. I still haven't answered that question.

I stood some distance from the storehouse and watched.

I couldn't quite bring myself to go closer. I'd decided the night before to greet someone, anyone — but standing there, my legs wouldn't carry me forward.

One group took their assignments and scattered. I followed one direction — people heading toward the corner. Since we were going the same way, I naturally ended up walking beside them.

The first person I spoke to didn't even look at me.

"…Excuse me, hello." I said to the man walking beside me.

He glanced at me and quickened his pace. No answer. I stopped and watched his back.

I remember that moment for a long time. It wasn't the being ignored that stung. I was already used to that. What stayed with me was that he'd quickened his pace. I wasn't a dangerous person. But in his eyes, maybe I'd looked like something better avoided. Being unregistered meant that just standing still could make you something someone needed to steer around.

I almost gave up. But I started walking again.

A young woman was the first to actually see me.

"…What is it?" she asked. Not unkindly. Just curious.

"…Oh, just — walking the same way." I said. I heard, as soon as I said it, how foolish that sounded.

She laughed. Not a mocking laugh.

"…Unregistered, right?" she said. "We know about you people, coming here every day. We know."

I didn't know what to say, so I just nodded.

That was the first day I learned that I was called "you people." I wasn't anonymous. I'd thought I went unnoticed, but I'd already been sorted into a category. The one who comes every day. Those people. I was inside that category.

Her name was Damia. A few years older than me.

Damia had low standing, but she was registered — some points inherited from her parents' generation, enough to let her take wage work in the city. She said her household wasn't well off. But she had a day's food, without fail.

"…I envy that." I said, without thinking.

Damia looked at me a moment.

"…Nothing to envy." Damia said. "We're day-to-day too. Only difference is, we work today, we get paid today, for sure. You don't."

I had no answer to that.

That was the first time I heard the phrase "day-to-day." I learned later it was common among the hired hands — people living off what they earned that same day. I heard it and thought I was day-to-day too. Except I was day-to-day without even the day part certain.

Walking a while longer beside Damia, the man who'd been handing out assignments came over.

"…That's an unregistered, isn't it?" he asked Damia. He didn't ask me directly.

"…Yes, she is." Damia answered.

"…Can't come further into the field. Know that?"

"…We're heading to the corner." I answered for myself. "Just walking together, that's all."

He looked at me a moment. Not looking me up and down — just checking.

"…The corner's fine." he said. "That's what it's for. Just don't come into the work zones. It's in the law too."

"…All right."

He turned away, satisfied.

I think that brief exchange is the most important thing in this chapter. He didn't chase me off. He didn't hate me. He simply confirmed a distinction laid out in the law. The corner is fine. The work zones aren't. I saw, for the first time that day, that distinction being kept exact. No one pushed me back any further than that. Exactly as much as the law allowed — no more, no less.

There were a great many people in the field that day.

Three driving the machines directly. Six following behind, checking their condition. Some carried tools from the storehouse; others drove boundary stakes. That was the first day I saw their faces one by one. Before, I'd only seen them, from a distance, as a mass.

Toward noon the sun grew fierce. The machines never stopped. Only the people took turns going in and out of the shade. Watching that rhythm, I thought of the outer settlement. There had been a rhythm to work there too. Only there, when people rested, the field rested with them. Here, when people rested, the field didn't. The machine kept going in their place.

I saw that difference clearly, for the first time, that day. Outside, people and field lived at the same pace. In the city, the field moved faster than the people. People were allowed to rest; the field wasn't allowed to. I didn't know, then, whose sake that pace was for.

Damia was assigned to follow one of the machines. I followed her out toward that zone and stood off to the side while she worked.

When break came, people gathered in the shade by the storehouse. Drank water, traded a few words, scattered again. I stood at the edge of the group.

"…Come sit." Damia called to me.

I went over carefully. No one said anything. I sat down beside Damia.

There was a man in that shade.

I saw him then for the first time. I couldn't guess his age — neither young nor old. His clothes were no different from anyone else's. Nothing distinctive about his face. He just sat quietly, drinking water.

While the others traded jokes back and forth, he said little. He laughed when there was something to laugh at, but he never spoke first.

"…Has he worked here long too?" I asked Damia, low.

"…Don't know." Damia shrugged. "Doesn't say much. But he's good at the work. Handles the machines, drives the stakes — whatever you ask, he does it."

"…What's his name?"

Damia thought a moment.

"…No idea, actually. Everyone just calls him 'him.'"

Hearing that answer, I didn't think it strange. I didn't yet know it was strange. The field was full of people whose names I never learned. I was one of them myself. One more nameless person didn't seem worth remarking on.

He looked at me once.

Our eyes met. Flustered, I looked away. But he showed no particular expression — just gave one small nod. Something like a greeting.

I nodded back, awkwardly.

That was all. No conversation followed. He finished his water, and when the break ended, he got up quietly and went back to his zone.

I've gone back over this scene many times since. Nothing at all remarkable about it. One nod. That was it. I didn't hold onto any special memory of him that day. Just — there's a quiet one, I thought. Nothing more than that.

Toward the end of the break, someone carrying tools dropped a spade handle. It had split. He was turning it over in his hands with a troubled look when the quiet man walked over, without a word, and took it from him.

He turned it a few times, then bound the crack with cord. It didn't take long. He handed it back and returned to his own spot without another word.

"…Thanks." the man who'd received it said, to his back.

He didn't turn around. Just raised one hand.

I didn't think much of this scene either, that day. There was always one person like that in a field — quick-handed, fixing things before being asked. I put it down to just how he was, nothing more than that.

That afternoon, I went back to the corner.

The time with the hired hands lasted only through the break. The rest of the time, they worked, and I sifted dirt alone as always. Nothing had changed. I filled about half a palm again that day.

But something felt a little different. Sifting the dirt, I wasn't lonely. Voices carried faintly from a distance — the machines, people calling out to one another. Before, those sounds had had nothing to do with me. That day they did.

I don't want to make too much of this small change. Nothing was solved. I was still unregistered, still someone who came home with half a palm and no more. Only, that day, for the first time, sifting the dirt didn't feel like something I was doing alone. That was all.

That evening I told Pikra about the day. Not right when I walked in — after supper, after the bowls were cleared away. Pikra never asked about things like that before we'd eaten. A conversation had on an empty stomach, she said, never gets properly heard by either side.

"…Did you talk to anyone?" Pikra asked.

"…Yes. Someone named Damia."

"…And?"

"…Not bad." I said. "She says she's day-to-day too. Same as us."

Pikra nodded.

"…That's true." Pikra said. "Everyone's day-to-day, in one way or another. Only for some, today's certain. For others, even today's not certain."

I sat a long while without answering.

That night I only talked about Damia. I said nothing about the quiet man. Not that I meant to hide him — I just had nothing much to say. How do you describe a single nod to someone? I didn't think about him again, not once, that night.

Now I know. That was my first meeting with him. And that the meeting began as quietly as anything in this book begins. I noticed nothing that day. There was nothing to notice.

The next day, I went back to the storehouse.

Damia was there again. We exchanged a few words. She treated me, by then, as someone she knew a little. That felt good.

The quiet man was there too. That day too, I just nodded and went on. He went to his zone; I went to the corner.

The days that followed went much the same. Stop by the storehouse in the morning, trade a few words with Damia, nod to the quiet man, go on to the corner and sift the dirt. In the evening, tell Pikra briefly what had happened that day. It was mostly about Damia. There was rarely much to say about the quiet man.

In those days I also learned a few of the other hired hands' names. The one who drove the stakes was called Nicol; the one who carried tools, Petros. I memorized those names. It had been a long time since I'd memorized a new name. I'd hardly learned a new one since leaving the outer settlement.

Writing these names down again now, I feel something strange. Nicol and Petros never appear again in this record. They were only people who stood near me for a few days. I set their names down here so as not to forget them. A person doesn't stop having existed just because this record has no name for them.

There were a few others in the field whose names I never heard. The quiet man was one. I never thought it strange. Seeing a face every day without ever learning its name — that was common enough in the city.

Around then I started walking, every day, the same path — a stop at the storehouse, then on to the corner. On that path, I saw him every day. Nodded every day. That was all. I thought he was just a quiet person. I thought nothing more than that.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. The One Who Gleans Beside Me

_Previously_ — Xene began to break the ice with the hired hands. She
got to know Damia. There was a quiet man there too. She never learned
his name. She thought nothing of it.

It was about ten days later.

Through those ten days, stopping by the storehouse before heading to the corner had become my fixed routine. Greet Damia, exchange a few words, nod to the quiet man, go on to the corner. That order almost never changed. Even the weather stayed much the same — hard sun, dry wind, the smell of dust every evening.

But that day, the quiet man was heading not toward the storehouse but toward the corner.

His assignment must have changed, I thought. That was all I made of it.

I haven't written those ten days in any detail here. There was little enough to write. The fact that every day was much like the last is, I think, the most accurate picture of what my days were like then. I never found that repetition tedious. Being able to repeat at all was, itself, a rare thing, after I'd left the outside.

The corner was never officially a work zone. But now and then it needed tending — clearing the ditch, straightening the marker, redriving a boundary stake. When there was work like that, one of the hired hands would be assigned.

That day, it was him.

I sat as always at one side of the corner and began sifting the dirt. He worked over by the ditch. Our spots didn't overlap. I didn't pay him any particular mind and kept at what I was doing.

I don't remember that day as a special one. Looking back now, this whole chapter is special. But to me, that day, it was just an ordinary day. Nothing different, except that there happened to be one more person there.

At one point, in the middle of leveling the dirt, he reached a hand toward me. I flinched. But what he was pointing at wasn't me — it was the ground by my foot.

A grain lay half-buried in the dirt beneath me. I'd have walked right past it.

"…There." he said, briefly.

I bent and picked it up. Before I could say thank you, he'd already turned back to the ditch.

I thought, that day, that he simply had a sharp eye. Someone who handles dirt every day might well spot a single grain before anyone else. I thought nothing more than that.

Sifting the dirt for a long while, I noticed at some point that he had drifted a little closer to me.

He hadn't said anything. The distance had simply narrowed naturally, as he tended the ditch. I felt uneasy at first. But he never looked at me — just kept at his own work. I settled again and went back to mine.

About half a day passed that way, and near where he'd been working, I began finding more grains.

More than usual.

I didn't think it strange.

Working a ditch turns up dirt — that was only natural, I thought. Whatever had been buried had come up with the churned soil. That seemed the obvious explanation. I believed it. I had good enough reason to.

I'm careful writing this part. I didn't notice a thing that day. Truly, I didn't. Whoever reads this chapter later will already know. I want to say this to them in advance: I didn't know, then. If I write that I knew, that would be a lie.

That evening, I gathered a little more than usual. More than two cupped palms. Even after sharing with Pikra, a few grains were left over.

"…Bit more today." Pikra said.

"…Must be from working the ditch." I said.

"…Must be." Pikra said.

We didn't press it further. Pikra wrapped the extra in a small cloth and set it aside for the next morning's share. We were learning, around then, to stretch a day's worth over two.

He was at the corner the next day too.

I wondered if his assignment had changed again. I didn't know whether that was common or not. I was curious, but I didn't ask. He was still as quiet as ever. He did his work. I did mine.

That day too, more grains than usual turned up near where I sifted — especially near where he'd been.

That was when I first thought, briefly, that it was strange. But the thought didn't hold. I was hungry, and a hungry person doesn't examine strange things too closely. I just gathered them.

I've gone back over that moment many times since. Why didn't I press further? The answer is simple. Nothing good could have come of it. If it wasn't a coincidence, asking might have made him stop. I wasn't calculating that carefully at the time. But some instinct told me that looking too closely at a good thing breaks it.

He still hardly spoke.

If I asked something, he gave short answers. "…Yes." "…No." Rarely more than that. At first I found it uncomfortable. A few days on, I found it a relief instead. There was no pressure to keep a conversation going.

"…Do you come here often?" I asked him once.

"…Sometimes." he said.

"…What brought you here today?"

"…Fixing the ditch."

That was the whole conversation. He went back to his work.

Reading those short answers again now, I smile. There's no lie in any of them. "Sometimes" was true. "Fixing the ditch" was true. He never once lied to me. He simply never told me what I hadn't asked.

Around then, Damia said something to me.

"…You're looking better these days." she said, when we crossed paths near the storehouse.

"…Am I."

"…Eating well?"

I didn't know what to say, so I just smiled. It wasn't as if I was eating well. But it was true things had improved.

"…Corner working out for you lately?" Damia asked again.

"…A bit better these days."

Damia nodded and went back to her work without pressing further. I watched her go and felt that her idle question had made the day a little lighter. Someone being curious about my day, at all, was a rare thing for me then.

I think Damia's question was just a passing pleasantry. We weren't close enough yet for her to press into my circumstances. I didn't say more than that either. I didn't think it rude, at the time. Some questions are complete just by being asked, without needing an answer in full.

A few days on, I noticed a difference between the days he came and the days he didn't.

The days he came were, on the whole, better. The days he didn't were like before — very little, or nothing at all.

I put it down to the assignment. Days the corner needed tending, things got more disturbed, so naturally more turned up. I repeated that explanation to myself more than once. Not because I was trying not to doubt it. At the time, it really did seem the most plausible one.

I understand now that it wasn't that I tried not to doubt. It's more accurate to say I felt no need to doubt at all. It was enough, at the time, that my days had gotten a little better. Why they'd gotten better was a question for later. For a hungry person, later questions always get pushed back.

Pikra seemed to notice too.

One evening, she paused for a moment over what I'd laid out — more grain than usual. She said nothing, and went back to dividing it.

I saw that pause. I knew she'd seen it too.

"…Better lately." Pikra said, as she divided it. Not a prying tone.

"…Yes."

"…Good."

That was all. Pikra never asked why it had gotten better. I never explained. We divided the grain in half that evening, as always, ground the fragments and shared them in water, as always.

Looking back on that evening now, I wonder why Pikra didn't ask. Whether she didn't know, or knew and chose not to ask. I still can't say for certain. Pikra was someone who knew when not to ask, her whole life through. Whichever it was, that evening, she chose not to.

Once, an unusual amount of grain had gathered around where he'd been working.

I was gathering it when I happened to look up. He'd already moved off to another spot. Not looking at me.

"…There's a lot here." I said.

He glanced my way. Only briefly.

"…Is there." he said.

His expression stayed the same. Not startled, not concealing anything. Just — is there. That was all.

I can still picture that expression exactly. An expression holding nothing at all. I read it, at the time, as indifference. I read it differently now. It wasn't indifference — it was the face of someone who had decided not to answer. I couldn't tell the difference then.

I learned something from him, too.

At first I just brushed my fingers over the dirt carelessly. Then one day I happened to watch the way he sifted it. Fingers laid flat instead of stiffly upright, sweeping like a rake. Done that way, the grains caught against the fingertips far more easily.

From that day I started sifting the same way. Within a few days my hands had taken to it. My gathering grew a little faster.

"…This works better." I told him once.

"…Yes." he said. Nothing in his manner suggested he'd taught me anything. I didn't make a show of having learned it either. We simply both became people who sifted that way.

I still use that technique now. Fingers laid flat, sweeping the dirt like a rake. For years I never had occasion to explain to anyone who taught it to me. Some memories don't need explaining. There are things a hand simply knows.

Once, I nearly saw something.

Looking up, I caught his hand moving from near his waistband toward the dirt. It was a very brief motion. I didn't quite make out what it was. The next moment he was already gathering his tools, his hands empty.

"…Drop something?" I asked.

"…No." he said.

I accepted the answer without question. There was no particular reason to doubt it. A person's hands move around, working. I thought of that moment once, that night, and forgot it the next day.

I'm setting this scene down again now. I nearly saw something that time. Truly, only nearly. I didn't see it. Writing this book, I didn't want to inflate what I might have seen. I confirmed nothing. Not writing something unconfirmed as though it were confirmed — I believe that's one of the principles this record has to keep.

Around then, working beside him at the corner came to feel natural to me.

We'd never made any arrangement. It was simply that he came to be assigned to the corner more and more often, and I was there every day regardless, and at some point we became two people doing separate work in the same spot.

We still spoke little. But the silence stopped being uncomfortable. I found that strange. With Damia, silence felt like something that needed filling. Not with him. Quiet could just stay quiet, and it was fine.

I still don't know what to call this feeling. Comfort seems too large a word. I'll only set it down as this much: that there was now someone beside whom it wasn't strange to say nothing at all.

That evening, I told Pikra a little more about him.

"…There's someone who's been at the corner with me lately."

"…A hired hand?"

"…Yes. Doesn't say much, but he just works quietly nearby."

Pikra looked at me a moment.

"…Is he all right?"

"…Yes. He doesn't seem like a bad person."

Pikra nodded. She didn't ask anything further.

That night, lying down, I went back over those last few days. Nothing much had changed. I still went out every day, still got my hands into the dirt, still shared with Pikra in the evening. Only now, somewhere in that day, there was one more quiet person. He'd said hardly a word, but those few words had lifted a little of the day's weight.

Looking back on this stretch of time, I think it matters that I didn't feel, then, as though I were gaining anything remarkable. I only felt that the day was a little less lonely. I never wanted more than that, never suspected more than that. I don't know now what kind of beginning that might have been. At the time it was just one day after another.

I didn't mention that night, either, that there'd been more grain around his spot again. It didn't seem like something worth saying. I still believed, then, that it was only the ditch work turning things up. There was no need to put into words a thing I already believed.

Writing this chapter now, I think it's fortunate, in a way, that I didn't know then. If I had known, I wouldn't have been able to accept it. I believed it was chance, so I simply took it. And that, I think, was exactly what he wanted.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. The Administrator

_Previously_ — At the corner, Xene spent more and more time near the
quiet man. Somehow, there always seemed to be a little more when he
was around. Xene believed it was chance.

The first time I met the administrator was the first time since my registration review. I'd only heard the name before that — the one who scores standing, who oversees the Roll. It had never once occurred to me that the position had a face.

The day I received a standing of zero at the gate, I learned that a post called "the administrator" existed. The one who assessed me that day wasn't the administrator himself, but someone working beneath him. The first time I actually saw the administrator was that day.

That day there happened to be a routine inspection at the corner zone.

I learned later that zones under Article 9 are inspected in person by the administrator several times a year — checking that the marker stands correctly, that the boundary is exact, that the clause is being kept. A formality.

I was sifting the dirt as usual that day. In the distance, I saw a few people walking my way. The one at the center was the administrator. I didn't know it at the time — his clothes were different from the other hired hands', and I only guessed he must be someone important.

The party numbered four. The administrator, two people carrying clipboards, and behind them, a young person walking a little apart. All of them moved slowly. No reason to hurry, it seemed. I only learned afterward that the young person was Lytron. That day I simply took him for one more attendant. Lytron said nothing that day. He stood near the administrator, looking over documents, occasionally jotting something down.
