The two with clipboards worked through the zone item by item. Marker condition, boundary stake spacing, ditch drainage. Each time, they said "no irregularities" and made a note. I started counting how many times I heard that phrase that day, then gave up. Everything was without irregularity. There seemed nothing on that clipboard to note, besides the fact that I'd been sitting there for days.

The administrator was well along in years. His hair had gone half gray, his walk slow. Slow, but unwavering.

When the party reached the corner's boundary, the administrator stopped and looked over the whole zone. Checked the marker. Checked the ditch. Then he looked at me.

My body went rigid in that instant. There was no reason to run. I hadn't done anything wrong. But my body stiffened before I could think.

"…Unregistered?" the administrator asked one of the others. Not directly to me.

"…Yes. Comes every day, apparently." the other answered.

The administrator nodded.

The administrator walked closer to me. I stood where I was and waited. Part of me wanted to run, part of me wanted to ask something. In the end I chose not to run.

"…What's your name?" the administrator asked. The first thing he'd said to me directly.

"…Xene."

"…Xene." The administrator repeated it once. Not as though committing it to memory. Just confirming it.

"…I'm told you come here every day."

"…Yes."

"…What do you get?"

I hesitated a moment before answering.

"…Often not even a full day's food."

The administrator wasn't surprised by the answer. His expression didn't shift at all.

"…That would follow." the administrator said. "The loss rate is 0.02 percent."

I found the nerve, in that moment.

"…Why is my standing zero?"

The administrator looked at me a moment. This didn't seem to be the first time he'd heard the question.

"…It's a figure calculated from origin, history, relations." the administrator said. "You came from outside the city. You have no history here. And no registered relation to vouch for you. The calculation is exact."

"…So the calculation isn't wrong."

"…It isn't wrong." the administrator said. "It's only low. Wrong and low are different problems."

I couldn't help but admit that distinction was exact too. Admitting it only left me more frustrated. It would have been easier if I could have said he was wrong.

I felt, in that moment, as though I might be allowed to ask something more. Looking back, it was a strange feeling to have had. The administrator hadn't treated me unkindly. Nor had he been warm. But something in his manner seemed to draw speech out of a person.

"…Article 9 says the corner has to be left." I said. "The corner is there. Why is there nothing in it?"

The administrator looked at me a moment. Not eyes of anger.

"…The corner is there." the administrator said. "You can see it — the boundary stakes, the marker, a precisely designated zone. The law says to designate the corner. It never says how much is to fall there."

I turned that over in my mind.

"…So it's within the law, even if nothing's there?"

"…That's right." the administrator said. "The loss rate falling isn't a violation of law. It's a good harvest. The law says to leave the corner. It doesn't say to harvest poorly."

I've gone back over this conversation many times. I was looking, then, for some crack in what the administrator said. A lie, a stretch, a dodge. There was none. Everything he said was true. I had nothing to say, standing before that truth.

"…Then what am I supposed to do?" I asked.

For the first time, the administrator tilted his head, just slightly. He looked almost interested.

"…What do you mean, what are you supposed to do?"

"…The law is being kept. And I still can't eat."

"…The law is being kept." the administrator said.

I remember that tone even now. Not angry, not apologetic. The tone of someone stating a fact.

I hold onto that sentence as though it were the title of this chapter. "The law is being kept." I hear that sentence again, many times, through the rest of this city's story. Always in the same tone. I never once found a flaw in that tone. That there was no flaw was the frightening part.

The overseer in Book Two was ashamed of his own work, I learned later, from Mena. He'd stay behind at night, going back over the ledgers alone. The adjudicator in Book Three faltered too, in the end — his hand shook, I was told, standing before the scales. Every time I heard those two stories, I thought: at least there was something, inside those two, that snagged.

This administrator was different. I never once saw him waver. His voice never rose. He never looked away, never hesitated over an answer. A few days later, passing that spot again, I tried, on the chance of it, to recall his expression. However hard I searched, I found nothing that snagged. The reason he never wavered was simple. He had no reason to. He truly hadn't broken anything.

"…Do you know why the corner clause exists?" the administrator asked.

"…To leave something for those in need."

"…Correct." the administrator said. "And that's still exactly how it stands. No one blocks that place. No one drives you out of it. You can come every day. That is a right the law guarantees."

"…But there's nothing there."

"…That falls outside what the law guarantees." the administrator said. "The law guarantees the place. It never guaranteed how much would be in it."

I felt, in that moment, for the first time, how exact that distinction was — and how completely that exactness could leave a person with nowhere to stand.

"…Then who decides how much is left there?" I asked again.

The administrator looked at me a moment. For the first time, his face looked as if he were choosing his words.

"…That's a matter for harvest operations." the administrator said. "There are people who adjust machine placement, harvest speed, that sort of thing. I only confirm that the law is being kept. How much is left is not my remit."

It struck me as odd that this answer didn't sound like an evasion. It really was structured that way. This city had divided the work among so many hands that no one needed to answer for the whole of it.

"…Who are those people?"

The administrator glanced back, briefly. That was the moment Lytron looked up from his documents. I didn't know, at the time, what that meant. I only registered that the administrator had glanced at someone.

"…No need to concern yourself with that." the administrator said, turning back to me. "They're within regulation too. No one here has broken any rule."

Writing this over again later, I've come back, many times, to that moment Lytron briefly looked up. Lytron told me, much later, that he remembered that day. He said it was the first time he'd ever seen, in the same frame, the number that was the loss rate and the person standing in the place that had made it.

The administrator said a few words more, then turned to leave.

"…Keep coming." the administrator said, last of all. "No reason for me to stop you."

That could have sounded kind. I knew, even then, that it wasn't. It was simply a statement of fact. No reason to stop you meant, equally, no reason to help you.

I turned that sentence over many times that night. "No reason to stop you." I came to think that sentence explained this entire city. No one stopped me. No one helped me either. Someone like me stood in the space between those two.

The party left as they'd come. The ones with clipboards went first, the administrator following slowly behind. Lytron, moving last, turned back once. Our eyes met.

I thought, in that instant, that I'd seen that face somewhere before. I couldn't place it exactly. I let the thought pass, a stray one.

I didn't learn until later that this was the same face that had first found me in Chapter 15. I didn't know it that day. My head was too full of the administrator to spare Lytron any thought. In this city, I kept crossing faces I would only recognize later.

Back home, I told Pikra what had happened.

"…I met the administrator today."

Pikra's expression tightened slightly.

"…What did he say?"

"…That the law is being kept."

Pikra was quiet a long while before she spoke.

"…That's the most frightening thing he could say." Pikra said. "The overseer, the adjudicator — people like that at least know what they're doing, and it eats at them. That one doesn't."

"…Why not?"

"…Because he has nothing to feel bad about." Pikra said. "Because everything he says is right."

"…If it's right, why is that frightening?"

Pikra looked at me a long moment.

"…You can't even get angry at someone who's right." Pikra said. "You can fight back against someone who's wrong. Shout that it's unjust. Against someone who's right, you can't even do that. All you can do is sit there and listen."

I sat a long while, saying nothing, after she said that.

I wondered, that day, how Pikra could know so exactly, without ever having met the administrator herself. I understand now. Pikra had already lived it, more than once. Standing in front of someone who is never wrong. Through those years — losing her husband, losing both sons, watching her own standing whittled away — Pikra must have met people like that many times over. People who did nothing wrong at all, and gave nothing at all.

I kept going out to the corner after that.

Nothing changed. I filled about half a palm again the next day, and he still worked quietly beside me. All the administrator's visit had left behind was a marker standing a little straighter than before.

For days after, I kept turning his words over. "The law guarantees the place. It never guaranteed how much would be in it." I kept coming back to that sentence — walking, sifting the dirt, even lying down to sleep. It felt as though there had to be some gap in it somewhere. However hard I looked, there wasn't.

I realized, around then, that this had been the first time I tried to argue with this city over something. The argument never got off the ground. An argument requires the other side to say something wrong, and the administrator never once did. That was the day I first learned that against something true, there's no argument to be had at all.

I met the administrator again much later. By then I had different questions. That day, I only walked home turning over why the words "the law is being kept" could leave a person feeling so utterly without ground beneath her.

Writing this chapter, I felt the pull, more than once, to paint the administrator as worse than he was — to write his voice cold, his expression scornful. That would have been a lie. The administrator never once raised his voice at me. He never mocked me. He was simply exact. That day I learned, for the first time, how frightening an exact person can be.

I will never, anywhere in this book, write a scene where the Roll breaks the law. The helplessness I felt that day, standing before the administrator, is exactly why. If it had been unlawful, I'd at least have had somewhere to fight — someone to appeal to, someone to protest against. Before what is lawful, there is no such place at all. I felt that absence of a place, that day, with my whole body. And that absence of a place is what this book, from beginning to end, turns out to be about. I didn't know that then. I know it now.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. The Arithmetic

_Previously_ — Xene met the administrator. He was not ashamed of his
own system. "The law is being kept." That sentence was true, and so
it could not be argued with.

This is a chapter I didn't live through.

Like Chapter 16, this one is pieced together from what Lytron told me directly, much later, and what Mena confirmed. What Lytron saw and thought that night — without his own words, I would never have known any of it.

Every time I write a chapter like this, I ask myself: do I have the right to set down something I never saw. Mena answered that question for me once. "Don't ask about the right. Ask about the honesty." I hold to that standard here as well.

The night of the administrator's round, Lytron stayed at the office later than usual.

He couldn't quite explain why, even to himself. He only said that something he'd seen that afternoon kept nagging at him. Me, standing before the administrator. And the question I'd asked. Why is my standing zero.

I was careful writing this chapter. If I wrote that Lytron stayed at the office because of me, this story would look more dramatic than it really was. Lytron told me, later: "It wasn't only because of you. The numbers just happened to catch my eye that day." I take him at his word.

That afternoon, accompanying the administrator, Lytron had filled out the round's paperwork. "Zone under Article 9, no irregularities." He'd written that line as always — one line, no different from any other item.

But writing it, he found himself going back over the question I'd asked the administrator. Why is my standing zero. And the administrator's answer. The calculation is exact. Lytron said he'd felt no unease at all, hearing that answer. Not at the time.

I think this detail matters. Lytron felt nothing strange about it that afternoon. What snagged at him only surfaced that evening. I first learned, through Lytron, that a person's feelings can move on that kind of delay.

That night, Lytron spent a long while looking over the harvest settings screen.

It wasn't within the scope of his job. A zone management assistant had no authority to touch loss-rate settings. But viewing them was allowed. Seeing a number and changing a number were different permissions.

Lytron opened the loss-rate item. Several figures filled the screen. Current loss rate, 0.02 percent. Target loss rate, 0.02 percent. Tolerance range, zone-by-zone breakdowns, a graph correlating machine uptime with loss rate. Numbers, and numbers, and numbers.

He said he sat looking at that screen for a long while. He'd seen it many times before. It was exactly this screen that produced the number he reported to his father every morning. But that night, he said, the numbers looked different than they had before. "It didn't look like numbers anymore. It looked like a fence, holding something in." Much later, that was how he put it to me.

Beside it was a record of past figures. This number hadn't always been 0.02 percent.

A decade or so earlier, the loss rate had been far higher.

There had been years in the low 3 percent range. Then, over the course of several years, adjustments had brought it down to today's 0.02 percent. Optimizing machine paths, adjusting harvest speed, changing labor placement. Begun in his grandfather's generation, I was told, and completed in his father's.

Hearing that number for the first time, a chill ran through me. Three percent. Up to then I'd only known the number 0.02. But there had once been a time when a hundred times that much had fallen at the corner. That hundredfold hadn't simply vanished. Someone had erased it, deliberately, over the span of two generations.

To one side of the screen was a short summary of the adjustment process. Below the year-by-year graph, a note: "Two-generation zone optimization; registered as a best-practice case within the Harvest." Lytron said he read that sentence over several times. It was the story he'd grown up hearing. His grandfather had first fixed the machine paths; his father had finished the job. And that story had been reduced, on the screen, to a short phrase: "best-practice case." Looking at that phrase, he said, he felt for the first time as if he were seeing, laid out beside a number, the true nature of the thing his family had long taken pride in.

Lytron noticed, at the corner of the screen, a button for adjusting the setting value. He'd never pressed it before. There had never been any occasion to, in his line of work. But that night, he found himself curious what it might do.

He hesitated a moment, then pressed the setting value, just to see.

The number didn't change immediately. A confirmation window appeared. "Change the loss-rate setting? Projected harvest yield will be adjusted accordingly." Below it, the projected decrease in yield was shown.

Reverting from 0.02 percent to 3 percent would drop the yield sharply. I won't write the exact figure here. I learned it later, but even now it unsettles me every time I write it down.

Below the confirmation window was another item: "Projected impact on linked indices." Lytron pressed that as well. Numbers unfolded. Zone performance score, family-linked score, and beside them a small arrow. Pointing down. He said he looked at that arrow for a long while. The screen, helpfully, had already calculated exactly how far it would fall. Lytron told me he never once looked that number up again, after that night. Once was enough. He never needed to look again.

I want to explain why I'm not writing that number down. Not because the number itself doesn't matter — the opposite. If I wrote it, a reader would use it as a calculator. Too much, too little, worth bearing, not worth bearing. I don't want this chapter read that way. What Lytron saw that night wasn't a number. It was a fork in the road. That's the only thing I want to set down here.

Lytron sat a long while looking at that confirmation window. He didn't press it.

I don't want to write this scene as though Lytron had done something wrong. He did nothing. He looked at a confirmation window and closed it. That's all there was. I've rewritten this chapter more than once, trying not to paint that "doing nothing" as a bad thing.

Before closing the screen, Lytron ran a few calculations. His family's standing score had, for generations, been tied directly to this field's harvest efficiency. The number his grandfather had built and his father had held. If that number wavered, the whole family's score would waver with it.

"…What does this have to do with our score?" he asked himself, that night. The answer came quickly. It had everything to do with it. Very directly.

His brother's words came to mind. Keep the numbers right, and everything else falls into place. Lytron had lived his life believing that. But that night, for the first time, he noticed it was a conditional. Keep the numbers right, and it falls into place — that sentence also meant that if you didn't, it wouldn't. He said he'd never once thought through that second half before.

I've rewritten this passage several times. There was a temptation to write that Lytron had begun to doubt his brother's words. That would be an exaggeration. He hadn't doubted. He had simply seen, for the first time, that the sentence had another side to it. Seeing and doubting are not the same thing.

That night, Lytron followed the thread of what would happen if he reverted the loss rate, one step at a time.

The yield falls. The zone's performance worsens. His father learns of it. His father asks why. Lytron has to answer. There's someone who comes to the corner every day. He already knew, that night, how that answer would sound to his father.

He said he ran that imagined scene through his head more than once. His father asks, "And?" Lytron can't find the next words. "Because I felt sorry for her" sounds too sentimental. "Because of Article 9" would be a lie — Article 9 was already being kept. What he really meant was simpler than either: that it bothered him, that this person sat there at the corner every single day. But that reason looked far too small to justify cutting into the family's standing score.

I think I saw Lytron most clearly in this scene. He was someone whose calculations moved fast. And his calculations weren't wrong. His family's score really would have fallen. His father really would have asked. That answer really would have sounded strange. What Lytron feared wasn't imagination. It was fact.

Lytron thought, for a moment, of telling a friend about it. There was someone among his fellow zone-management assistants he was close with. But he had no idea even how to start. I'm thinking about reverting the corner's loss rate. The moment that sentence left his mouth, it would no longer be his alone. Someone would ask why, and he would have to answer.

In the end, Lytron told no one at all.

I understand Lytron in this part. Some thoughts change weight the moment they're spoken aloud. Held privately, they're only thoughts. Spoken, they become something that demands a decision. I don't think that kind of silence is bad in itself. I only think it needs to be recorded, honestly, what that silence ultimately protected.

That night, Lytron closed the confirmation window.

He didn't press the button. He turned off the screen. He got up from his seat and went home.

Writing this chapter, I don't want to sit in judgment of Lytron. I won't write that he should have pressed that button. I wasn't there in that seat, and I don't know, the way he did, what that seat demanded of him. I only set down what happened. He looked at the window, and he closed it.

Lytron never opened that settings screen again after that night. Not for some time, at least.

He walked home from the office. The hour was late, and the street was quiet. Water was running behind the wall. A sound he heard all the time — but that night, he said, it sounded unusually loud. The water was only there for the yard's sake, let to run freely for no purpose at all. Lytron thought, for the first time that night, about where that water had come from. Water drawn from somewhere, passing through this yard, running straight out into the ditch, unused.

Lytron told me this scene himself. His voice dropped a little, telling it. I didn't ask what that meant. Some realizations can't fully be explained even by the person who has them. Some nights leave behind nothing more than this: that the water sounded loud.

The next morning, at breakfast, his father asked as he always did.

"…Yesterday's loss rate."

"…0.02 percent. No change."

"…Good work."

Lytron said no lie in giving that answer. There really had been no change. He hadn't changed anything. But he said that "good work" sounded strange to him that morning, in a way it never had before. He couldn't have said, exactly, what it was he'd done well.

I've set this breakfast scene down again here to place it beside Chapter 16. Same table, same question, same answer, same praise. Not a single thing had changed on the surface. But something different had passed through the person giving that answer than had the day before. Outwardly, nothing at all had happened. Writing this chapter, I confirm again that this "nothing happening" is the most frequently repeated fact in this whole book.

A few days later, Lytron passed me again on his rounds.

I was sifting the dirt as always. It was a hot day. Lytron paused for a moment, then went on. He said nothing. I had no idea, then, what he knew.

Picturing that moment now, I find myself wondering what his expression must have been. He only ever told me this much: "I just passed by. There was nothing to say." I believe him. There truly would have been nothing to say. He was carrying a problem with no answer to offer for it.

I want to close this chapter with something Lytron said much later.

"…At the time, I just thought I had no reason to take a loss." he told me, much later. "Strange, thinking back on it now. That I called it a loss. You didn't even have a day's food, and I called it a loss."

I said nothing to that.

"…I thought I was right, at the time." Lytron went on. "Protecting the family was my job. I still can't say that was wrong, even now. Only — I know now that what I closed that night wasn't only a window."

"…What did you close?" I asked.

Lytron was quiet a long while before answering.

"…I don't know." he said. "That's probably why I still keep bringing it up."

Hearing that answer, I understood that Lytron was still looking for it. I have no intention of handing him that answer. This book isn't written to give it. I only set down, together, these two facts: that he closed a window that night, and that even years later, he still hadn't been able to put that question down.

I've rewritten this chapter several times. The first time, I wrote that not pressing the button had been cowardice. Reading it back, I saw it wasn't honest. I've never stood before that button myself. I was someone with nothing to lose. I won't pretend to understand how someone with something to lose hesitates before it — but I won't judge it carelessly either.

The night he closed that window, I knew nothing. I came home that day too with half a palm's worth and shared it with Pikra. Two evenings existed, separately, in the same city, the same night. One was an evening of calculation. One was an evening of sharing.

Writing this chapter, I keep coming back to that downward arrow. I've never seen an arrow like that. I had no score to fall, to begin with. So I know I have no right to judge how heavy that arrow was. I only leave here, as it was, the fact that someone looked at it with his own two eyes, and closed the window.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. Transfer

_Previously_ — Lytron learned that the loss-rate setting could be
reverted. He also learned that reverting it would lower his family's
score. He closed the window.

A few days later, I had reason to go to the administration office again.

It was for Pikra's registration renewal. Once a year, the procedure required a confirmation. Pikra wasn't feeling well that day, so it was I who went to collect the paperwork.

Around this time, I'd begun to sense that Pikra's health was slipping a little. She sat down to rest more often, and sometimes had to catch her breath in the middle of supper. I couldn't quite tell whether it was age, or the amount we were splitting between us every evening. Probably both. The whole walk to the office that day, that was what occupied me. It worried me more than the paperwork did.

Pikra hadn't objected when I offered to go in her place. Once, she'd have insisted on going herself — said she couldn't ask that of a daughter-in-law. That day she only said, "…Be careful, go on," and nothing more. The shortness of it worried me more than any protest would have.

The office stood near the gate, in the same building where I'd first been reviewed for registration. I wasn't eager to walk back into that building. The memory wasn't a good one.

I stood in front of it a moment, hesitating. If I opened that door, I felt, the feeling I'd had standing before that counter would come rushing back. Standing zero. I took a breath and went in.

I would end up in that building many more times after that. Every time, I paused a moment at the door. The habit never went away, even years later. Some places never grow familiar, however often you visit them.

I waited a long while in line at the window. Everyone ahead of me carried similar paperwork. Renewals, corrections, confirmations. All their faces wore the same tired look.

The man in front of me said he'd come about a relocation procedure. Moving to a different zone meant re-registering your residence too, he grumbled to the person beside him. Ahead of him stood a woman holding a baby, there about the child's birth registration. She was arguing at length with the clerk — the paperwork had come back wrong.

I killed time listening to that argument. This city seemed to have an endless number of things that required paperwork. Once at birth, once at marriage, once at each move, once at every renewal. Listening to that list, I found myself thinking, again, that outside, none of that paperwork had ever been necessary.

The line behind me kept growing. An old man stood leaning on a cane, shifting his weight from leg to leg, looking for somewhere to sit. Eventually the clerk came out and offered him a chair. The man thanked her, over and over. I thought the thanks a little excessive, at first — then understood it. Inside that building, even a small kindness was rare.

I still remember that old man's face. I never learned his name, never saw him again after that day. But the way he thanked her, so gratefully, for a single chair — it seemed to say something about how stingy this city was, ordinarily, with its people.

The woman with the baby was eventually directed to a different window. Watching her walk away, I thought: even a child born into this city has to be met with paperwork before anything else. It struck me as strange. Outside, when a child was born, you simply gave it a name, and that was the end of it. Here, apart from the name, you had to receive a number too.

At the next window, a young man was arguing in a low voice with a clerk. He seemed to be asking why his history score had dropped a little in this renewal. The clerk flipped through the screen a few times and explained that he'd changed residence twice in the past year. The man said it hadn't been a move — just a temporary relocation for work. The clerk answered that the reason wasn't considered; only the number of relocations counted. The man argued a while longer, then finally said he understood and left with his papers. Watching him go, I thought: in this building, even a grievance only amounts to a single sheet of paper.

When my turn came, the clerk handed over the form and began explaining it. The administrator, passing by just then, recognized me and stopped.

"…The one who comes to the corner." the administrator said.

"…Yes. I'm here for my mother-in-law's renewal papers."

The administrator nodded and gave a brief instruction to the clerk. Then, seeing I wasn't familiar with the procedure, he explained a few things to me himself.

"…Renewal has to be done by the person themselves; a proxy is only allowed in limited cases." the administrator said. "Marriage relations, direct family relations — cases like that."

"…Does a mother-in-law count as family?"

"…A daughter-in-law isn't counted as direct. Marriage relations are handled under a different procedure. Doesn't matter today, though — you're only collecting the form."

I went a little blank, hearing that. Two years living under the same roof, and by this city's rules, that still didn't count as direct family. I thought the distinction strange, but it didn't seem worth arguing over.

The administrator kept going, working through the rest as someone who knew the whole procedure by heart, several items at once.

"…There are a few more things to confirm besides this form." the administrator said. "Residence confirmation, relationship confirmation, and whether there's been any change to history within the past year. As I understand it, your mother-in-law's side has had none — is that right?"

"…Yes, none."

"…Then this one will be simple enough."

"…Score assessment moves through a few different channels." the administrator went on. "The origin item is fixed once, at registration, and adjusted afterward by history and relation. On marriage, it's combined with the spouse's score; on having children, it's inherited by a fixed ratio; on death, an attrition ratio applies. And there's also a thing called registration-item adjustment — transfer's possible too, technically, though in practice it's almost never used. There's a separate application form for it, but I understand there haven't been any filings in recent years."

The administrator said all this while already sorting the next form with his hands.

"…Once a marriage relation is dissolved, it's automatically reassessed, and the appeal period's one month, and appeals only go through the dedicated window, and processing usually takes two to four weeks once filed." the administrator kept on. "Right, write your mother-in-law's name and registration number on this form and take it along. Get the confirmation stamp at that window over there — I'm not sure how late that window stays open today, so you'd best hurry."

The administrator added a few more things in the meantime — notes printed on the back of the form, the reassessment cycle, the correction procedure, which cases were fee-exempt and which weren't. I couldn't hold onto any of it separately. One regulation overlapped another until I lost track of where one item ended and the next began. The administrator's voice moved through all of it at exactly the same pitch. Not a single inflection that told you which parts mattered and which didn't.

I let most of the explanation slide past me. What form to fill in mattered more, just then. The clerk handed me another sheet, and I wrote Pikra's name and registration number on it. I got a digit wrong and had to write it again.

I remember how stifling the dry air in that office felt that day, more than usual. There were no windows in the room. Just the sound of people breathing and paper turning. All I wanted was to finish the form and get out.

It took a long while to get the confirmation stamp. I was sent to two more windows. At the first, they said the registration number I'd written didn't match the required format, and I had to rewrite it. At the second, the clerk was away from the desk and I waited more than ten minutes. Only at the third window did I finally get the stamp.

By the time I got through it all, most of what the administrator had explained earlier had slipped from my mind. One thing stuck: that a daughter-in-law wasn't counted as direct family. Only that.

I kept turning that over as I walked. I didn't even know the name Ruth then. That was a name from an old story I'd only learn much later, from Mena. But in that moment, it struck me all over again how this city sorted me. Two years living here, and I was still, in its eyes, someone "joined by marriage."

I heard, from Mena much later, about an old regulation this city drew on. There'd been a long-standing dispute, apparently, over how to count a relationship like a daughter-in-law's. In the end it had been settled on the side of marriage. Hearing that, I thought back to what I'd been told at that window. At the time I'd taken it for nothing more than paperwork procedure. I hadn't known that someone, long before, had argued over that very distinction.

The light had slanted low by the time I left the building. I'd spent nearly half a day inside. My legs ached the whole walk home. Standing still, waiting, I learned that day, could wear a person down every bit as much as sifting dirt in the field.

On my way back I passed through the market alley. The sounds of people preparing supper drifted by. Listening to them, I tried to reckon up how much of what I'd heard that day was really mine to keep. Not much. Most of it had been regulation with nothing to do with me; the only part that touched me was that single line — a daughter-in-law isn't direct family. At the end of the alley I caught a familiar back — Lytron, carrying a clipboard, apparently on his way back from a round. He didn't seem to see me. I didn't call out either. I just watched his back disappear around the corner, then turned toward the cottage.

Back home, I handed the papers to Pikra.

"…That took a while." Pikra said. Her color looked a little better.

"…The line was long. And they said a daughter-in-law isn't direct, so I couldn't be proxy — I just brought back the form."

"…That's how it goes." Pikra said, looking the form over. "Always has been."

"…They explained a lot of other things too, but I couldn't follow most of it."

"…Don't worry about it." Pikra said. "As long as the paperwork's right, that's what matters. No need to follow everything they say."

We didn't discuss it further.

I studied Pikra's face again. Better than that morning, but still pale.

"…How are you feeling today?" I asked.

"…Fine. Just a little dizzy, that's all."

"…Tell me if it happens again tomorrow."

"…All right." Pikra said. Then, as if changing the subject, she folded the form away. "Better keep this safe. We'll need it again next year's renewal."

That night, I watched Pikra's hands as she folded the form. Her fingertips looked thinner than before. I noticed it and said nothing. Around that time I was learning that noticing something doesn't mean you have to say it out loud.

A few days later, I mentioned the long wait at the office to Damia too.

"…I had no idea the renewal process was that complicated." I said.

"…Everyone says that." Damia said. "I spent half a day at the window myself last year doing mine. Nobody actually understands all the regulations. You just fill in the form the way they tell you and get your stamp, that's all."

"…Is there anyone who actually knows all of it?"

"…Probably just the administrator." Damia laughed. "People like us don't need to."

I agreed.

"…Still, it took forever." I said. "I got sent to three different windows."

"…That's just how it is there." Damia laughed. "I spent half a day there last year too. Next time I'm going first thing in the morning."

"…I should do that too."

"…Funny thing, though." Damia said. "All that complication, and what actually matters comes down to a few lines. The rest you can go your whole life not knowing and be fine."

"…So how do you know which few lines matter?"

Damia shrugged.

"…You just learn it, living long enough." she said. "Or you never learn it at all. I've seen both."

We moved on, after that, to talk about the day's work. Damia had a machine inspection scheduled; I had to get to the corner.

The next day, I went to the corner later than usual. The morning had been eaten up by the paperwork.

He was already working the ditch when I arrived. He saw me and gave one nod. I nodded back.

"…I'm late." I said. There wasn't really any need to explain, but the words came out anyway.

"…That's all right." he said.

I didn't tell him about the office. There wasn't much to tell, and he didn't seem particularly curious either. We each sifted our own patch of dirt that day, same as always. Late start or not, my basket filled to about the usual amount by evening.

It still strikes me as a little odd that I never told him what had happened that day. I'd told him a fair amount, by then. But what I'd heard that day just didn't seem like it would mean anything to him. Paperwork talk. I thought, at the time, it had just been a dull day, nothing more.

Partway through, Damia stopped by and asked again about the office windows. I told her how many tries it had taken me to get the stamp. She clicked her tongue.

"…Longer than me, even." Damia said. "Come with me next time. I know the order of those windows."

"…I'll take you up on that, next time."

Only after I'd said it did it occur to me that I now had one more person in this city I could ask a favor of.

Writing this chapter, I tried to set that day down exactly as it was. Nothing remarkable happened. I picked up a form, stood in line, went home. Some days just pass that way. Without leaving any mark at all.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. Why Don't You Leave

_Previously_ — Xene went to the office for Pikra's renewal papers.
Somewhere in the administrator's explanation of the procedure was a
brief line about the possibility of transferring standing to someone
else. No one lingered on it. Xene soon forgot it too.

Around that time, Lytron began coming to the corner even on days he wasn't scheduled to inspect it.

At first I didn't notice. I assumed the inspection schedule had simply shifted. Then one day, Damia mentioned it in passing.

"…He's not on rounds today." Damia said, tipping her chin toward Lytron. "Wasn't last week either."

"…How do you know?"

"…The round roster's posted, by the storehouse." Damia said. "He came on a day his name wasn't on it."

I gave the remark little thought. I'd never counted the days he came against the days he didn't. To me, he was just someone who occasionally stopped by in passing.

After mentioning that, Damia asked me something else.

"…Why don't you leave, anyway, Xene?"

I'd heard that question before. But coming from Damia, it sounded a little different.

"…Leave where?"

"…Here. This city." Damia said. "I'd have left long ago, in your place. No standing out there, right? Better to stretch your legs out there than sit here like this every day."

"…Why don't you leave, then?" I asked back.

Damia laughed.

"…I've got standing." she said. "Low, but I've got it. If I left, even that would be gone. Staying here, at least there's something."

I thought her answer was exact. Damia had something in this city worth holding onto. I had nothing. And yet I still didn't leave. Damia didn't seem to notice that difference and turned back to her own work.

I couldn't give Damia a proper answer that day. She didn't seem to be waiting for one either — just a remark thrown out in passing. I didn't know, then, that the same question would come back to me, far heavier, from someone else's mouth just a few days later.

And that day was one of those.

The light was overcast. No wind, so the dust hung low. I was sifting the dirt in my usual spot. He wasn't at the ditch that day — assigned elsewhere, I'd heard.

Lytron came over and stood beside me. At first he said nothing. Just stood there. I found that odd. Lytron was always someone who came with something to say. Not that day.

"…Did you have something to say?" I asked first.

"…No." he said. "Just passing through."

I didn't believe him. It wasn't a day for inspection; there was no reason to be passing through here at all. But I didn't press. I'd already learned, by then, that pressing rarely led anywhere good.

He stood a long while, watching me sift.

"…You lay your fingers flat for that." he said.

I stopped my hand.

"…Yes."

"…You used to keep them up."

I was a little startled. I hadn't expected him to remember a detail that small.

"…I learned it." I said. "It's faster this way."

"…From whom?"

I hesitated over the answer. I didn't want to bring up the quiet man in front of Lytron. I couldn't quite explain why, even to myself.

"…Just picked it up." I said.

Lytron looked at that answer a moment, as if turning it over. He didn't seem to believe it. But he didn't press further either.

I still remember that small lie. It wasn't that I was trying to protect him. It was only that something felt like it would break if I brought him up in front of Lytron. By then, I already understood — some good things only last if you don't describe them too closely.

"…Xene." Lytron said, abruptly.

I stopped my hand and looked up at him. That was the first time he'd used my name alone, without any title attached.

"…Yes."

Lytron chose his next words carefully. An unfamiliar hesitation, for him. Usually his questions came right out.

"…Could you not go somewhere else, instead of here?"

I didn't understand the question at first.

"…The corner? Somewhere with a higher loss rate, like you mentioned before?"

"…No." he shook his head. "Not that either. I mean the city."

I let my dirt-covered hand rest still on my knee.

"…What do you mean?"

"…Outside." Lytron said. "You came from outside originally, I heard. There's no such thing as standing out there."

"…That's right."

"…Then you wouldn't have to sit here like this every day, out there." he said. "Here, however long you stay, your standing never rises. I know that calculation — I run it. If your origin is outside, once and for all, that outside never changes."

I couldn't say anything for a moment, hearing that.

"…Are you telling me to go back?"

"…That's not what I mean." Lytron said, a little flustered. "I'm just curious. Whether it's actually better for you, being here."

"…Better how?" I asked.

That question stopped him.

"…Full, and safe." he finally managed. "That kind of thing."

"…You can be full outside too." I said. "As long as it's not a bad year. Safe's about the same too. There's no Roll out there, so no one judges me by standing, either."

"…Then it's better, isn't it." Lytron said. "So why are you here?"

I thought, for a moment, of that settlement. Twenty-two houses, once. How many of them were still standing now, I didn't know. Since my husband died, and then his brother too, I hadn't heard a single word from that settlement.

"…Going back wouldn't be the same as before." I said. "No husband. Neither of that family's sons left."

"…Even so, you could live without standing."

"…Yes, I could live." I said. "But that's not why I'm here."

I turned that question over a long time, that night. Even now I don't think Lytron meant it unkindly. He really would have seen it as a matter of numbers. Outside, no standing, none needed. Inside, no standing, and it's a problem. The same nothing, and yet it's not a problem out there, and it is in here. To him, it must have been a perfectly simple calculation.

I can't say that calculation was wrong. It wasn't. Only, there was something that calculation didn't include. It took me a long while, that day, to put it into words.

Lytron waited for my answer. He didn't press. As he always did.

"…I'm not here because I have nowhere else to go." I finally said.

"…Then why?"

I set my hand back down on the dirt. As if preparing the answer in my hand first.

"…Pikra is here."

Lytron looked, for a moment, like he didn't quite understand the answer.

"…Your mother-in-law?"

"…Yes."

"…Couldn't she come with you?" he said. "You both came from outside, didn't you?"

"…Pikra's not going."

"…Why not?"

I didn't answer that right away. Not because I didn't know the answer. Because I didn't know how to say it, in front of Lytron.

"…It's where she'll be buried." I said. "Her whole family's in this city's graveyard. Husband, both sons. Pikra wants to be laid beside them."

Lytron was quiet a moment.

"…That's the reason." he finally said, just that much.

"…Yes."

"…Then what about you?" Lytron asked again. "You're not someone who belongs buried outside. Couldn't you leave on your own?"

I thought the question was exact. Exact enough that it was harder to answer.

"…That's right." I said. "I could leave, on my own."

"…But you don't."

"…No."

"…Why?"

I held onto that why for a long while. I had several answers ready for it, at the time. All of them true, and all of them incomplete. I can't leave her alone was true but wasn't everything. There's nowhere particularly better to go to was also true, and also not everything.

I've rewritten this passage several times. At first I tried to explain it at greater length — laying out reasons one by one. But what I actually said that day was much shorter. I decided to leave that shortness as it was. Long reasons are usually invented afterward.

"…I go where you go." I said.

Lytron looked at me a moment, hearing that.

"…That's it?"

"…Yes."

"…I don't quite understand." he said, honestly. "You gave up standing, and you sit here every day, and that's all because of that one thing?"

"…Yes."

Lytron looked like a man who couldn't find any words to turn that answer around. I recognized that look. It was close to the look I'd worn myself, in front of the administrator. The look of someone who can't find where to object, standing before something true.

I understood, in that moment, for the first time, that I could put that same look on someone else's face. Until then it had always been my turn to wear it — before the administrator, before this city. That day, for the first time, the seats changed.

"…Isn't it a loss?" Lytron asked. He looked a little surprised at himself for saying it.

"…What is?"

"…You're not gaining anything. Being here."

I turned that over.

"…I'm not here to gain anything." I said.

"…Then what?"

I had no answer. Truly none. There was no right word in me for that question, then. If I wasn't there to gain something, why was I there — I couldn't find the words for that either.

Lytron didn't ask further. We stood, for a while, without speaking. A little wind stirred, and the dust drifted low along the ground.

"…I don't understand it." Lytron said, after a long while. His tone was sincere. "But not understanding it doesn't mean I think it's strange."

I was grateful for that distinction.

"…It's just how it is, that's all." he went on. "I've lived thinking it was natural, not to take a loss. Whether that's strange or not — today was the first time I've ever really thought about it."

I didn't know what to say to that.

I heard this conversation again, years later, from Lytron himself. He added, that time: "That day, I thought you were the strange one. I don't think that anymore. Now I think it was me who was strange. That not taking a loss isn't just naturally right — that was the first time I ever heard it." I set that down here. That was something I hadn't heard, that day.

Lytron got to his feet.

"…I'll head off." he said.

"…All right."

A few steps away, he turned back.

"…Xene." he called.

"…Yes."

"…How's your mother-in-law's health?"

The question surprised me. I hadn't expected him to ask that.

"…Not so good, lately." I said. "Not badly, though."

"…I see."

He didn't ask anything further, and went. Watching his back, I only later understood that the question hadn't been mere pleasantry.

That evening, Pikra sat down by the hearth first and asked.

"…Late today."

"…Lytron came by." I said. I mentioned Lytron; I left out what we'd said.

"…What did he want?"

"…Nothing much." I said. "Just asked this and that."

Pikra looked at me for a moment. She seemed to sense there was more to it. But she didn't press.

"…Things are hard for you here, aren't they." Pikra said instead. Not a question — a confirmation.

"…No, they're not."

"…Liar." Pikra said. There was no anger in her voice. "I know. You're here because of me."

I couldn't say anything to that.

"…Not only because of you." I managed at last.

"…Not only, sure." Pikra said. "But I'm one of the reasons, aren't I."

I didn't deny it. I couldn't have.

I don't think it was chance that Pikra brought that up first, that night. She must have known I'd heard something that day. She may not have known exactly what, but she likely guessed that someone had asked me a question along those lines. Pikra was someone who could guess things like that.

I didn't repeat to Pikra the exact words Lytron had used. That's it? — if she'd heard that, I had no idea how she'd have reacted. Pikra would surely be angry, if she believed I'd given up my standing because of her. So for the rest of that evening, I kept to the usual talk — sifting dirt, the weather.

Pikra went to bed early that night. She coughed a little. I lay awake a long while, listening to it.

That night I went back over Lytron's question. "If you're not here to gain something, then what?" I wrote that I couldn't find the answer, then. I know a little of it now. I wasn't there to gain something. I was there because I couldn't leave her behind. Those are two different sentences. I didn't know, then, how to put that difference into words.

I didn't know the book of Ruth yet, at the time. I first heard that name from Mena, months later. And learned that the answer the woman in that book gave was, almost word for word, the same answer I'd given Lytron at the corner. I don't think that coincidence is a coincidence at all. Some answers cross centuries in exactly the same shape. The gleanings at the corner of the field don't fall, by design. But some things don't fall even though no one ever designed them to.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. The Day It Rained

_Previously_ — Lytron asked whether she couldn't go somewhere else,
instead of here. Xene answered that she wasn't there because she had
nowhere else to go — she was there because Pikra was. Lytron couldn't
understand the answer, but he didn't call it strange either.

The rains came late that year.

They usually ran late anyway. I watched this city's sky for weeks that season. Day after day without a single cloud. The wheat only ripened better for it. Good sun means good wheat, Damia told me.

"…Rain would actually be a disaster." Damia said. "If it hits grain that's already fully headed, the weight bends the stalks over. Then the machines can't harvest it."

I let the remark pass without much thought. Being afraid of rain sounded strange to me. Outside, rain had always been welcome. Here, even rain was a variable to be calculated.

The clouds rolled in late that afternoon.

At first no one thought much of it. A cloud passing through on a summer afternoon was common enough. But this one didn't pass. One whole side of the sky darkened fast. The wind suddenly shifted direction. The smell of dust gave way, all at once, to the smell of wet earth.

I knew that smell. Outside, it came before a late frost too. The smell of something on its way.

Somewhere across the field, something like a horn sounded. I didn't know what it meant. Damia heard it and shot to her feet.

"…Machine-stop signal." Damia said. "It means rain's coming."

It wasn't long before the rain began falling in heavy drops.

The first few were large. They left round marks in the dry dirt. Then, all at once, it poured. I had never seen rain arrive that suddenly. Outside, rain always gave some warning first. Here it came with none at all.

The whole field turned loud in an instant. People running. The harvesters stopping. I had never seen those enormous machines come to a halt so abruptly. Things that had always moved at the same speed, with the same sound, simply went still, all at once.

I remember that moment by its sound, even now. The machine noise cut off, and rain filled the space where it had been. That was the first time I heard this city fall so quiet. A field that had always been full of something running was, for a few seconds, nothing but the sound of rain.

People were shouting, running everywhere.

"…Clear the front rows first! Whatever's already cut!"

Someone yelled that. I didn't know who. Someone in charge, it seemed. The workers surged in one direction at the call. There were sheaves already cut but not yet loaded onto the carts. If the rain got them, they'd spoil, I was told.

I stood in the middle of that chaos, not knowing what to do. I wasn't someone called on for work like this. The unregistered weren't mobilized for urgent tasks. I just stood there in the rain.

Far off, I spotted Lytron. In a raincoat, a terminal in a waterproof sleeve in his hand, running among the workers. Giving directions, checking things, running again. He didn't seem to have any room to notice me. I didn't call out to him either.

I heard later how busy Lytron had been that day. It was a crisis that struck directly at the whole field's yield. It must have been the most frantic hour of his day. I caught a glimpse of his face, briefly, in the middle of that chaos. None of his usual composure left in it. That was the first time I learned that he had a face like that too.

I walked toward the corner, letting the rain fall on me.

No particular reason. It was simply the place I always went. There was nowhere else to go. Pikra was home in the cottage that day. Once the rain stopped I'd have to head back there anyway, and it seemed natural enough to pass by the corner on the way.

As I neared the corner, I saw something strange.

The ground was yellow.

Ordinarily, the corner's ground was the color of dirt, apart from a few handfuls of trampled stalks. Not that day. Grain lay scattered across the rain-soaked earth. Whole stalks of wheat lay flattened too. For a moment I thought I was seeing it wrong.

I knelt down and touched it with my hand. It was real.

I've gone back over that moment hundreds of times since. The weight against my fingertips. Up until then, whatever I'd touched at that corner had always been one or two grains, at most half a palm's worth. That day, wherever my hand landed, there was something there.

I didn't understand why, at first. A moment later, hearing the sound of people running toward the front of the field, I guessed.

That day's final harvest stretch had happened to be the section nearest the corner.

The machine was halfway through that stretch when the rain signal stopped it, I learned afterward from Lytron. Half the cut wheat lay right where it had fallen. On an ordinary day, the machine would have finished crossing and taken every one of those grains with it. That final, precise pass was exactly what made the 0.02 percent loss rate what it was.

That day, the pass never happened.

Everyone was too busy scrambling to save the sheaves already cut and waiting to be carted off. The rows near the corner, still standing uncut, flattened by the rain, had been pushed entirely off that day's list of priorities. That stretch had never counted toward the Roll's harvest tally to begin with. There had been no reason to protect it.

I confirmed this more than once. I asked Lytron, and later the administrator too. Both gave me the same answer. No one had left that section on purpose. No one had thought of me. It had rained, the machines had stopped, people had rushed to save what was urgent, and the corner had never been on that urgent list. That was all there was to it.

I sat there a long while, letting the rain fall on me.

At first I gathered it grain by grain. Then I realized there was no need. I set my sack down entirely and began sweeping it up with both hands. Whole stalks and all — I could thresh them at home later.

I could see the sack filling. I had never, in my whole life, seen a sack fill that fast. Or — I had, once. Back at the outer settlement, gleaning a field others had already harvested, with our own hands. But that had been a different kind of filling. That field had been marked, then, as ours. This one wasn't.

I didn't cry, filling that sack, strangely enough. I'd thought I'd been waiting a long time for a moment like this. But when it finally came, no tears came with it. Instead my hands moved faster. I had no idea how long the rain would last. All I thought was that I had to gather as much as I could before it stopped.

For a long while I was at it like that, when another hand appeared beside mine.

It was him. I didn't notice when he'd arrived. Soaked through with rain, wordlessly gathering fallen stalks beside me. I didn't know what zone he'd been assigned to that day. He might have been called in to help carry the urgent sheaves. But at that spot, it was just the two of us.

"…Isn't this a place they need you more?" I asked. I had to raise my voice over the rain.

"…This is needed too." he said, briefly. He didn't explain what he meant.

After that, we worked in silence. Rain ran down our faces. Our fingers went numb with cold. But I don't think my hands had ever been this busy and felt this good, before that day.

I've turned that brief "this is needed too" over in my mind a long time. I couldn't ask, that day, what it meant. I'm still not certain, even now. I only know this much: that he spent his time that day on work no one had assigned to him. Whether that went beyond the chance the rain had made, or whether he was simply someone conscientious who couldn't walk past grain lying in plain sight — I still don't have that answer either.

In the middle of the rain, I looked up, once.

Far off, Lytron was looking this way. Only for a moment. He was still running back and forth, and that glance lasted no more than a few seconds. I didn't know what was in that gaze. There was no chance, that day, to ask.

I asked him about that look much later. Lytron said this: "That day I'd actually forgotten you were there at all. Then I happened to glance over. You were filling a sack. I didn't think anything, in that moment. I just thought — good, I'm glad. That's all there was to it." I believe that answer, precisely because it wasn't any bigger than that. For him, that's really all it would have been.

The rain stopped toward evening.

The machines didn't run again that day. They'd start up again the next morning, I was told. People carried what wet sheaves they could salvage back to the storehouses. I stood off to the side, holding my sack.

It was more than half full. I kept feeling its weight the whole walk home. I carried it in my arms rather than over my shoulder. I didn't want to let go of it.

On the way to the cottage, I ran into Damia. Her eyes went wide at the sight of my sack.

"…You gathered all that today?"

"…Yes."

"…The rain did you a favor." Damia said. She meant nothing much by it.

"…Yes." I said. "It did."

Saying that, I knew it was exactly right. It was the rain, and nothing else.

Pikra was standing outside the door when I arrived. She seemed to have come out to catch the air, after the rain.

Pikra looked at my sack. She couldn't say anything at first.

"…It rained." I said. There was no other explanation I could find.

Pikra reached a hand toward the sack, stopped, reached again. Her fingers trembled. I didn't stop, then, to think whether it was her age, or something else.

"…Let's go inside and talk." Pikra said.

That night, for the first time, we emptied the whole sack out inside the cottage and sifted through it together. The two of us sat there by a single lamp for a long while. Sorting grain from stalk, picking out the flattened straw, separating out what could really be eaten.

By the time we finished, it was past midnight. In front of us sat more grain than I had ever seen in one place.

Pikra looked at it for a long while.

"…How long has it been, since I saw this much." Pikra said. I couldn't tell if she was speaking to me or to herself.

I didn't answer. Instead I went back, one by one, through what I'd seen that day. The machines stopping. The direction the people ran. No one so much as looking toward the corner. All of it had happened because it rained.

"…Will there be more of this tomorrow?" Pikra asked.

"…I don't know." I said. "I don't think it'll rain tomorrow."

Pikra nodded. She asked nothing further.

I write this night as carefully as anything in this book. I was glad, that day. Truly glad. We ate our fill for once, and there was enough left to divide over the coming days. But alongside that gladness, from the very first, I set down something else too.

This was not mercy.

No one had left that wheat behind for my sake. The administrator hadn't broken the law that day, either. No one had changed the loss-rate rule. Lytron hadn't done anything for me that day, not really. The Roll had been exact that day too. Only the sky had stood outside the Roll's arithmetic. The rain never asked about standing. The rain had never, to begin with, calculated any such thing.

I said none of this to Pikra that night. She probably knew it too. We simply gave ourselves over to dividing the grain. Some truths, even known, have to be set aside — for one night, at least.

The next day, it truly didn't rain.

The sky cleared again. The machines ran again. I went out to the corner at the usual hour. The ground was dirt-colored again. It was hard to believe that so much had been there, in that same spot, the day before. A few handfuls of trampled stalks. Same as it always was.

I sat down and began sifting the dirt, fingers laid flat as always. The same motion as the day before. Only, the day before, that motion hadn't been needed.

That morning I confirmed, all over again, that this city had given me nothing. What I'd received the day before hadn't come from the city. It had come from the sky. And this city would go on giving me nothing, tomorrow, the day after, until the sky chose to do that again. I set this fact down without resentment. There was no one to resent. The administrator had been right. The law had been kept. Yesterday and today, both.

A few days later, I ran into the administrator near the office. Unable to hold back my curiosity, I asked.

"…That day it rained — some wheat was left at the corner. Does that count toward the loss rate?"

The administrator thought a moment.

"…No." the administrator said. "That's pre-harvest scattering. It happened in a section that hadn't yet been logged as harvested, so it doesn't figure into the loss-rate calculation. It's recorded separately, under weather exceptions."

"…So that day just gets treated as if it never happened?"

"…The record stays." the administrator said. "But the loss-rate figure holds. 0.02 percent. That day, and today."

I didn't ask anything further after that. There was nothing left to ask.

I had to set that conversation down here. For a while, I'd hoped that the day it rained had shaken the Roll's number, even a little. It hadn't. That day remained, in this city's record, nothing more than a weather exception. The number never moved at all. Only hearing that answer did I fully understand how much of what I'd received that day had come from outside this city.

That sack was the biggest topic in our house for days afterward.

Pikra stretched it out, using it sparingly. Not using it all in one meal, dividing it instead across nearly ten days. For those ten days I was less hungry. Once they'd passed, it went back to the way it had always been.

I remember those ten days as something special. But the real reason they were special is that they were an exception. I never met rain like that again — not, at least, within the span of time this book covers. I think that fact is one of the most important things to know about this city. The good things came from outside this city, from places it couldn't calculate. What was calculated inside this city was always 0.02 percent.

I close this chapter here.

The story turns a little different from here on. I'm soon to meet a woman. The one who writes things down. She'll tell me a great many things I never knew, about this city, and about myself. I'd thought, until then, that I had been carrying this record alone. I learn, at that point, that this wasn't so.

I'll write that meeting in the next part. For now I set down only this: the sack, on the day it rained. It was not mercy. I knew that, and I was grateful anyway. The two could exist together. I learned that, that day.

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Gleaning Ground, Part Three

Mena's Record

Chapter 25. Someone Passing Through

_Previously_ — On the day the rain stopped the machines, grain was left
in the corner of the field for the first time. I filled a sack that day.
And I understood it was the sky's doing, not the city's mercy.

The first time I saw her, it wasn't at the corner.

It was at the well near the gate. I was on my way to draw water, and she
was sitting there writing something. A flat wooden board across her
knees, paper spread on top of it, a pen in her hand.

It wasn't the first time I'd seen someone write. Most city people used
handheld terminals — small enough to fit a palm, letters arranging
themselves at a touch. But someone writing by hand, on paper, I had
hardly seen in this city at all.

I thought, at the time, that she was strange. Paper was a precious
thing here. Writing by hand was slow, too. And yet she sat there as if
she had nowhere else to be.

Her clothes didn't look like a city person's either. City people mostly
wore clean clothes — the lower your standing, the less true that was.
Hers were neither. Worn, but neat. Worn from long use, not from
carelessness.

At her feet sat a thick leather case, bound with cord in several places.
One of the cords was a different color from the rest, as if it had
recently been replaced.

She looked up. Our eyes met.

"…Come for water?" she asked.

"…Yes," I said.

"…There's room here. You can share it."

I set down my water jar and sat beside her. I had no particular reason.
There was something in her voice that put a person off their guard. I
hadn't sat this easily beside a stranger since coming into the city.

"…What are you writing?" I asked.

She tilted the paper slightly so I could see. I read the letters. A few
names, and beside each one a short line.

One line caught my eye.

"One sack a day at the corner. Only on rainy days."

I knew at once it was about me.

"…Me?" I asked.

"…Yes," she said. "I saw you a few days ago. Filling a sack at the
corner."

"…Why write that down?"

She thought a moment, then set her pen down.

"…That's my work," she said. "My name is Mena."

I thought I'd heard the name somewhere before. But I couldn't place it
then.

I had no idea, in that moment, how many times that name would come to
my lips later on. I only thought I'd met a strange woman writing
something on paper by a well.

"…I'm Xene," I said.

"…I know," Mena said. "I saw it on the gate registry."

I was a little startled.

"…You look at that too?"

"…I do. It's my work," Mena said. "Who's come into this city, and with
what standing. I keep all of that."

"…Why?"

"…I'll tell you later." Mena smiled. "For now, go and get your water.
It's a waste to let that jar sit empty."

I laughed. It was strange how right that sounded.

When I came back with the water, Mena was still there. Watching someone
else now — an old man coming out through the gate. He had a pack on his
back and a slow step. Mena watched him a long while, then wrote
something down.

I sat beside her again and watched her hand move. Not fast. Not hurried
either. Just certain. Each stroke made a small, steady sound as the pen
tip pressed into the paper. The sound never varied.

"…You don't know that man either," I said.

"…I don't know most of them," Mena said. "I'm not from this city."

"…Then where are you from?"

She smiled a little at the question.

"…Nowhere, really," she said. "I go between cities. I don't stay long
in any one place."

"…Doesn't it wear you out? Walking all the time?"

"…It does," Mena said. But her voice didn't sound worn out. "But if you
stay too long in one place, there's something you stop being able to
see."

"…What's that?"

"…How the same thing happens differently, and also the same, in city
after city," Mena said. "If you only ever live in one, you think
everything that happens there belongs only to that city."

I didn't fully understand what she meant, not then. I had lived outside
the city walls before. But I had never met anyone who moved between
cities the way she did. Later I would learn that what she meant, that
day, had to do with this city's Roll.

The old man passed through the gate and vanished into the city. Mena
watched until his back disappeared from sight.

"…Do you know what'll happen to him?" I asked.

"…No," Mena said. "I'm not the kind of person who knows that."

"…Then what kind of person are you?"

"…The kind who knows he passed through here today," Mena said. "That's
all I know. But that's enough."

A few days later, I met Mena again on my way to the corner.

She was standing at the edge of the field, the wooden board still in
her hand. This time she was watching the whole field — the machines'
paths, the workers moving sacks, the men weighing grain at the scales.

"…What are you watching?" I asked.

"…The field," Mena said. "How it turns."

"…Nothing ever falls in it."

Mena looked at me.

"…I know," she said. "That's why I came to watch."

Something about that struck me as strange. Since coming into the city
I'd hardly met anyone who knew that nothing was left at the corner.
Everyone knew the corner clause was being kept. No one asked why, if it
was being kept, there was nothing there.

"…Lytron knows too," I said. "But he won't say why either."

"…Who's Lytron?"

"…The harvest-management assistant."

Mena wrote the name in a corner of her paper. I watched her hand move.
No judgment attached itself to the name. No good person, no bad person.
Just a name, and what he did.

"…Mena," I said. "Why did you come here?"

She looked up at the sky for a moment.

"…I heard a rumor," Mena said. "That this city's Roll is unusually
precise. A precise Roll always catches my interest."

"…Why?"

"…The more precise something is, the more exactly it leaves something
out," Mena said. "I wanted to see what that was."

I thought that was a frightening thing to say. But there was nothing
frightening in Mena's face. Just a plain, even calm — the look of
someone asking how wide a field was.

That was the first time I had any real sense of what kind of person
Mena was. She hadn't come to fix something in this city. She had come to
see what the city had left out. Fixing and seeing were different things.
I didn't know the difference then. I would come to know it very
clearly.

That evening, Lytron passed by our cabin and stopped.

"…Who was that, earlier?" he asked.

"…Mena," I said. "She writes things down."

"…Writes what?"

"…People."

Something odd crossed his face.

"…The Roll writes people down too," he said.

"…It's different," I said. I had no words then to explain exactly how.
I just knew that it was.

Lytron stood there a moment, shook his head, and walked on. I watched
him go and thought: someday Lytron will meet Mena too. I wondered what
his face would look like then.

I had no idea, then, how soon my curiosity would be satisfied.

But he turned back after a few steps.

"…Is she registered in the Roll?" he asked.

"…I don't know," I said. "I never asked."

"…That's strange," he said. "Can someone who isn't registered really
move this freely through a city?"

"…Mena seems to."

Lytron's face went thoughtful for a moment. I saw something uneasy in
it. He was someone who liked everything kept in order. An unregistered
person moving about freely was, to him, like an unfilled box.

"…I'll check on it," he said. And this time he actually left.

I heard that so lightly, at the time. Lytron saying he'd check on
something was nothing unusual. I didn't know yet where that checking
would lead him.

That evening, standing over the water jar, I turned the conversation
over again. Lytron had found it strange that an unregistered person
could move so freely. I didn't think that reaction was his fault. He'd
been born into a world that was kept in order. Of course something
unordered would unsettle him. I only didn't yet guess where that
unsettled feeling would eventually take him.

The next day, at noon, I met Mena at the corner again. This time she
wasn't alone. The administrator stood beside her, explaining something.

I watched them from a little distance.

"…This is the designated corner," the administrator said, gesturing.
"Under Article 9, we leave it exactly, every year."

"…May I look at what's left?" Mena asked.

The administrator smiled. There wasn't the slightest discomfort in that
smile.

"…As much as you like," he said. "We have nothing to hide."

Mena walked to the corner and crouched down, sifting the soil with her
fingertips. Nothing came up but a few handfuls of trampled, broken
stalks.

"…What's the loss rate?" Mena asked.

"…0.02 percent," the administrator said. "As far as we know, the lowest
figure in the region."

"…Are you proud of it?"

The administrator paused at the question before he answered.

"…Not proud, exactly. Accurate, I'd say," he said. "We've never broken
the law. Not then, and not now."

Mena said nothing to that. She only nodded and wrote something down.

I still think back on that scene often. The administrator hadn't lied.
The Roll really hadn't broken the law. And yet there was nothing at all
in the dirt on Mena's fingertips. That day, for the first time, I saw
with my own eyes that both of those things could be true at once.

When the administrator had gone, Mena called to me.

"…Xene," she said. "That man didn't lie."

"…I know," I said.

"…That's the more frightening part," Mena said. "If it were a lie, you
could catch it. If it isn't a lie, there's nothing to catch."

I didn't fully understand what she meant then. Later I would return to
those words more than once.

That afternoon, the man who quietly gleaned among the workers passed
near us. Mena's eyes followed him a moment.

"…And him?" Mena asked.

"…He gleans alongside me," I said. "One of the hired hands."

"…His name?"

"…I don't know. I never asked."

Mena nodded, and instead of a name, wrote a short line: Name unknown —
gleans alongside her.

I felt something strange, watching those words settle onto the page.
Mena never pretended to know what she didn't. When she didn't know, she
wrote that she didn't know.

The man glanced our way for a moment. He seemed to know someone had
been watching him, writing something down. He didn't get angry, wasn't
curious either. He just gave a small nod and went back to his place. A
trampled stalk snapped under his foot.

I heard the sound and looked at Mena. She seemed to have heard it too —
turned her head that way for a second, then back to the paper. She
wrote nothing. I didn't understand, then, why that was worth noticing.
Mena wasn't someone who wrote down everything she saw. She wrote what
was worth writing. What the standard for that was, I didn't ask that
day.

I didn't know then why that was so unusual. In this city, nothing was
ever simply let pass unknown. The Roll always filled every blank. There
was no box for "unknown." With Mena, there was.

Mena hoisted the case back onto her shoulder and asked me,

"…What are you doing tomorrow?"

"…Going out to glean. Same as always."

"…Tomorrow I think I'll look inside the Roll," Mena said. "There's
something called the reputation field, apparently. Where people's
histories collect."

"…Why there?"

Mena looked at me for a moment. For the first time something heavy
passed through her eyes.

"…Because things I wrote long ago might be somewhere in there," Mena
said. "I've never checked what's being done with them now."

I didn't understand, then, why she said that so carefully. Her voice
was the same as always. But her fingers were gripping the wooden board
just slightly tighter. I noticed it by chance. And it would be the next
day before I understood that the chance had been no accident at all.

I didn't know, then, what face that carefulness would turn into by the
next day. I only knew that Mena would be somewhere in this city
tomorrow, watching something.

That night, setting down the water jar, I told Pikra about the woman
I'd met.

Pikra was sitting by the hearth, working over dried stalks — a handful
gathered that day at the corner. Many of them had been trampled and
broken, and it took time to pick out the whole ones.

"…I met someone new today," I said.

Pikra looked up without stopping her hands.

"…What kind of person?"

"…Someone who writes. She watches people at the well and writes things
down."

Pikra listened a long while, then said,

"…Mena?"

"…Yes. You know her?"

Pikra shook her head.

"…No. I think I've only heard the name," Pikra said. "There's supposed
to be someone like that. Going between cities, writing things down.
Has been for a very long time."

"…Doesn't that scare you?"

Pikra smiled — a smile I hadn't seen much of lately.

"…No. If anything, it's a relief," Pikra said. "Someone who writes
isn't someone who takes."

"…What's the difference?"

Pikra thought a moment.

"…The Roll takes a number from me," Pikra said. "Someone who writes
takes nothing from me. They just sit beside you."

I let that pass, that night. Now I know. Pikra already knew, even then,
that what made this city frightening wasn't what it took away. It was
what it never gave. And a person who only writes is neither of those
things.

The next day, I went out to the corner earlier than usual.

Mena was already there. Not at the field, but on the low bank beside
it, watching the morning workers come in.

I sat beside her. Neither of us said anything.

We sat a long while like that, watching the field come to life. The
machines starting up, the scales chiming, the line of workers forming.

Then Mena said quietly,

"…It's loud."

"…Yes," I said.

"…With all this noise, there's not one sound in this field that's grain
falling."

I thought about that for a long time.

After that, every time I went out to the field, I listened. The
machines, the scales, the voices. And there really was never a sound of
anything falling. Mena was the first person who had ever confirmed that
for me — by sound.

Mena stood and brushed the dirt from her clothes.
