"…I'll look over that way today," she said. "You go on as you always
do. I'm only someone passing through."

That phrase stayed with me strangely. Someone passing through. And yet
a few days later, that someone passing through would find something no
one in this city had ever seen.

Watching her hoist the leather case onto her shoulder, I tried to guess
how many names must be inside it. I couldn't guess.

I watched her walk away along the bank for a long time. Her step was
neither slow nor fast. It was the step of someone who knew exactly
where she was going, and yet was in no hurry at all.

Carrying my water jar back to the cabin, I counted through, one by one,
the people I'd met that day. The administrator. Lytron. The man. Mena.
Four people, watching the same field, and all four had seen something
different. The administrator saw a law that had been kept. Lytron saw
one unregistered name. The man saw a few unspoken stalks of grain. Mena
saw dirt with nothing in it.

It struck me as strange that none of them were seeing it wrong. Each of
them had seen exactly what they'd seen. And yet even added all together,
their accuracies hadn't put a single more grain on that ground.

I thought, for the first time, that day: if people looking at the same
spot could see such different things, could any one of them be called
wrong? I fell asleep that night without an answer.

At the cabin door I stopped a moment and looked up. A few stars were
scattered thin across the sky. Not so different from the sky I'd known
outside the settlement. That, at least, seemed to be something even
this city couldn't touch.

He had told me once that what you spill and what you leave behind were
the same thing. I turned that over again that night. Maybe the names
Mena was writing down were something like that, too. Nothing changed —
things were only left behind. I didn't yet know why that was necessary.
But strangely, it eased my mind a little. Somewhere, someone had written
down that I had been at the corner that day.

I didn't know, then, that the woman I'd met at the well would be the
one who made it possible for me to write this record at all. I only
thought, that night, that her case must be heavy.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. The One Who Writes It Down

_Previously_ — I met Mena. Someone who wrote things down without
judging anyone. The administrator hadn't lied, and there was still
nothing at the corner. Mena said she would look into the Roll's
reputation field the next day.

The next day, instead of going to the corner, I went to find Mena
first.

I wasn't entirely sure why. It was just that the carefulness in her
voice the night before had stayed with me. I didn't realize until I
reached the well that I hadn't even brought my water jar.

Mena wasn't there. Instead, she was standing in front of a low
building near the gate, a wooden sign reading "The Archive" over its
door. The door was smaller than the city's other buildings, and looked
old.

"…What is this place?" I asked.

"…This city's archive," Mena said. "A place where a person can look
into what the Roll has piled up. Every city has one."

"…Can we go in?"

"…I can," Mena said. "I'm a recorder. You come sit inside with me.
Quietly."

An older man came out from inside — someone who seemed to keep the
place. He gave Mena a small nod.

"…Back again," he said. "Staying long this time?"

"…A few days, maybe," Mena said.

"…Whatever you need, just ask. We have nothing to hide in this city,"
the man said.

I felt like I'd heard those words somewhere before. It was almost
exactly what the administrator had said at the corner.

That was the first time I noticed it. People in this city worked in
different corners of it, and yet they all said the same thing. Nothing
to hide. What struck me was that it was true — and that made it sound
stranger still.

The man stepped aside, and we went in.

Inside was narrow. A screen sat against one wall, with a single chair
in front of it. Drawers lined the opposite wall — the old-fashioned
kind, opened by hand.

Mena sat down and turned the screen on. Her fingers moved through
several screens, and then she looked back at me.

"…Ask if you're curious about anything. I'm working right now, so my
answers might be short."

"…That's fine."

Mena turned back to the screen. Her hand moved the same way it had
moved over paper the day before. Unhurried. Certain.

I sat beside her and looked at the screen. Names in rows. Numbers
beside the names, short phrases beside the numbers. I read a few.
Commendation for diligence. Cooperative. Compliant with regulations.
All good words.

"…This is all good things," I said.

"…That's the entry for people with high standing," Mena said. "Yours
will be different."

I tensed a little at that.

"…I have one too?"

"…You do." She scrolled a few times. My name came up. Standing zero.
Beside it, a single short line.

"Newly registered. Below threshold for standing. Nothing out of the
ordinary."

I read that sentence twice.

"…I have nothing out of the ordinary about me," I laughed.

Mena didn't laugh.

"…There's plenty. It's just not written down," Mena said. "The Roll
only calls something unusual by its own standard. Going to the corner
every day isn't unusual, by the Roll's standard."

"…Why not?"

"…Because nothing comes of it," Mena said. "The Roll sees people by
their outcomes. If there's no outcome, it treats everything before it
as if it never happened."

I thought about that for a long time.

"…How do you see it?" I asked.

For the first time, Mena took her hand off the screen.

"…I watch what people do," Mena said. "Whether it comes to anything or
not."

Mena pulled a sheet of paper from her case. My name was on it too.
Beside it was written:

"Xene. Goes to the corner without fail, every day. Gives no reason.
Most days, comes home empty-handed."

Reading that line, I felt something strange. It wasn't longer than the
Roll's sentence. But there was something more in it.

"…Why is this different?" I asked.

"…The Roll thinks I'm not there," Mena said. "I think I am. Even when
nothing comes of it, the fact that the person was there — that's still
true."

I didn't fully understand that then. I do now. The Roll saw a person
by numbers. If the numbers didn't move, the fact that the person had
even stood in that place was erased. Mena was the opposite. Even when
the numbers didn't move, the fact that the person had been there was
never erased.

"…Is Pikra in there too?" I asked.

Mena searched the name and found it. Pikra's number was low. Higher
than mine, but far below the city average. Beside it, a short line.

"Standing reduced due to death of spouse and children. Renewal
approved."

Something turned in me, reading that. Losing a husband and two sons —
here, it had been reduced to five words. Standing reduced.

Mena pulled another page from her stack of paper. Pikra's name was
there too. But beside it was a much longer sentence.

"Glyke. Lost her husband first, then two sons in succession within
two years. Even so, told her two daughters-in-law three times to go
back. One stayed, one went."

"…Is this the same person?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. "The same person. But the Roll only writes what
this person lost. It doesn't write what this person did."

That was the first time I understood, in exact words, the difference
between the Roll and Mena. The Roll wrote what was lost. Mena wrote
what the person had chosen to do in front of that loss. One was
subtraction. The other was a person.

"…Why do you write?" I asked.

Mena stopped and looked at me. I'd tossed the question out lightly, but
her face didn't take it lightly at all.

"…Because if it's forgotten, it never happened," Mena said.

I echoed the words back.

"…If it's forgotten, it never happened."

"…Yes," Mena said. "People think that when a memory fades, the thing
itself fades. It isn't so. The thing stayed exactly as it was. It's the
memory that fades, not the thing."

"…But why does that have to be you?"

Mena smiled a little. The first smile of the day.

"…If I don't, no one does," Mena said. "The Roll only writes outcomes.
The administrator only keeps the code. The city only worries about the
city running smoothly. In between all that, there are people no one
writes down."

"…Like me?"

"…Yes," Mena said. "And like me."

I wanted to ask something more about that answer, but I didn't know
exactly what.

"…Are you someone no one writes down too?"

Mena turned the screen off for a moment.

"…Somewhere, probably," Mena said. "I never stay long in any one city.
No one's watched me long enough."

"…Doesn't that lonely you?"

Mena didn't answer right away. The silence felt long to me.

"…Sometimes," she said at last. "But instead of just enduring that
loneliness, I gained something from it."

"…What?"

"…Eyes that don't belong to anyone," Mena said. "If I were from this
city, I'd want to believe it was a good city. It's hard to live
otherwise. I'm not from here, so I don't have to believe that."

Hearing that, I thought I finally understood why Mena carried that
plain, even face wherever she went. It wasn't indifference. It was a
face working hard not to take any side. I didn't know then how much
effort that took.

"…So you're on no one's side?" I asked.

"…There's no side," Mena said. "I'm not someone who decides right and
wrong."

"…Then what are you?"

Mena thought a moment.

"…Someone who leaves behind the fact that something happened," Mena
said. "Whether it was good or bad — the person reading it decides that.
I don't stand in front of the judgment."

"…Isn't that hard? There must be times you want to judge."

For the first time, Mena smiled a little at that. A bitter smile.

"…Every time," Mena said. "Especially when it's someone I know. But the
moment I write down a judgment, I become the same as the Roll. The Roll
believes it's right, too, when it cuts a person down."

I turned that over for a long time.

"…So you don't even write that the Roll is wrong?"

Mena looked straight at me.

"…That's different," Mena said. "Calling something wrong when it's
wrong isn't judgment. It's fact. I write facts. I just don't lay my own
feelings on top of the facts."

At the time, that distinction felt only faintly there. Later I would
learn it was the most important distinction in this city.

Mena turned the screen back on. A different one this time. A list of
city names.

"…What's this?" I asked.

"…The reputation field," Mena said. "What I told you about yesterday.
Each city's Roll exchanges information with the others. Whatever
evaluation a person received elsewhere follows them here — it all
collects in this."

"…So?"

"…So there are things in here that never happened in this city," Mena
said. "Records that came from other places."

I asked, more or less idly,

"…Is anything you wrote in there too?"

Mena's hand stopped.

"…It's possible," Mena said. "I've done this work a long time. I don't
even know how far the things I've written have traveled."

I didn't know then why that answer came out so thin. I do now. Mena
already sensed something, in that moment. She was about to confirm, for
the first time with her own eyes, how the work of a lifetime had
changed somewhere she couldn't see.

Mena got up from her chair and went to the window. The field showed
outside it, the machines still turning. She watched the scene for a
while, then came back to her seat.

"…Xene," Mena said. "I've done this work a long time. There's one
thing you come to know, doing it that long."

"…What's that?"

"…What I write, the moment I write it, stops being mine," Mena said.
"Other people read it, copy it, summarize it, copy it again. I can't
control any of that."

"…Then why write it? If you can't hold onto it anyway."

Mena paused at the question.

"…Even if I can't hold onto it, I have to leave behind what was right
at the start," Mena said. "So that someone, someday, has something to
compare it against."

I didn't fully understand that answer then. Leaving behind what was
right at the start, and stopping something being twisted into wrong
later, sounded like two different things to me. I would come to
understand the difference far more clearly, a few days later.

"…Can I watch with you?" I asked.

Mena looked at me for a moment.

"…You can," she said. "But it might take a while. Once I'm in this, I
don't come out easily."

"…That's fine. I don't have to go to the corner today."

Mena smiled a little at that.

"…You go every day, you said."

"…Today I'll watch something else," I said.

Mena nodded and pulled her chair closer to the screen. Her finger
touched it. A list unfolded, long — city names, dates beside them,
case numbers beside the dates. Too many to count.

"…Are you reading all of it?" I asked.

"…Not all of it," Mena said. "Until a name I recognize comes up."

I sat beside her and watched her hand move down the list, line by line.
Her eyes ran over each row at a steady pace. She never hurried.

I didn't find that slowness tedious. If anything, there was something
like respect in it. Mena never skimmed a single line — even when it
wasn't the name she was looking for.

After a long while, Mena's hand stopped.

A single name sat in one line on the screen. The city wasn't this one —
a different city, a name I'd never heard. Beside it, in small letters,
were the words:

"Source: Recorder Mena. Quoted."

"…Is this you?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. "I remember being in that city. A very long time
ago."

"…Strange, seeing your name here again."

Mena's face wasn't one of surprise. It was the face of someone
measuring something.

"…It isn't strange," Mena said. "I'd only ever heard, as a rumor, that
my records flow into other cities this way. This is the first time
I've seen it myself."

"…So now you've confirmed it, that's that, isn't it?"

Mena turned the screen again at that. The quoted sentence was short and
smooth. It looked flawless.

"…There's one more thing I have to confirm," Mena said. "Whether this
matches the sentence I actually wrote."

"…Could they be different?"

"…I don't know," Mena said. "That's why I have to look."

Mena opened her case. She took out a bundle of old paper. Her fingers
moved through it a little faster than before. She didn't show any
hurry outwardly, but I could feel the change in pace beside her.

I didn't know exactly why that speed had changed, not then. I know
now. In that moment, Mena was already doubting something. It was the
kind of doubt only someone who had done this work a long time could
carry. I didn't know, that night, what that doubt would turn out to be
the next day.

Mena found a sheet of paper that looked like the original. But by then
the day was already fading. The archive began to darken around us.

"…That's enough for today," Mena said, carefully returning the paper
to her case. "I'll look again tomorrow morning, in the light. This
kind of thing shouldn't be checked carelessly in the dark."

I felt something heavy in that. Mena might have stayed up all night
holding onto that paper. But she didn't. I don't know why, but not
checking it carelessly in the dark stayed with me a strangely long
time.

We left the archive together. The sun hung low. The shadows of workers
walking back toward the gate had stretched long.

"…Can I come again tomorrow?" I asked.

Mena looked at me for a moment.

"…You can," she said. "But Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Tomorrow might not be about confirming something good," Mena said.
"That's the feeling I have."

"…What kind of feeling?"

Mena stopped walking for a second.

"…A very long time ago, I saw something I'd written twisted in a
strange way," Mena said. "In another city. I thought it was something
that belonged only to that city."

"…And?"

"…Now I think I'm seeing the same thing in this one," Mena said. "I'm
not sure yet. I'll only know once I check."

Something cold ran through me, hearing that. I didn't know exactly why
at the time.

That evening, for the first time, I saw the plainness fall entirely
from Mena's face. Only for a moment. It settled back into place almost
at once. But I saw the crack. I would come to know what that crack held
the next day.

On the way home, I turned over, one by one, the screens I'd seen that
day. My name. Pikra's name. Lives arranged into numbers.

I passed by the field. The sun was setting, and the machines had
already stopped. A quiet field. The place that was so loud by day made
no sound at all in the evening.

In that quiet, I found myself remembering something my husband used to
say. What you spill isn't wasted. It's left behind. I set that phrase
beside the screens I'd seen that day. The Roll spilled nothing. So
nothing was left behind. Mena spilled nothing either. And yet
something was left behind. I couldn't put into words, then, what that
difference was.

I can say it now. The Roll didn't spill, so that nothing would be
left. Mena, instead of spilling, copied it down by hand. The results
looked similar. Neither had lost anything. But in one case there was
nothing at all left in that place, and in the other, everything was
there, on paper.

When I got home, Pikra was sitting by the hearth, working the stalks
she'd gathered that day.

"…You didn't go to the corner today, they say," Pikra said.

"…No. I went with Mena to the archive."

"…What did you see?"

I paused, choosing how to put it.

"…I saw your name too," I said. "It was in the Roll, and it was on
Mena's paper too."

Pikra's hands stopped.

"…What did it say?"

"…In the Roll, it said 'standing reduced,'" I said. "On Mena's paper
it said... the story about you telling your two daughters-in-law, three
times, to go back."

Pikra was quiet for a long while. She started working the stalks again,
her fingers moving a little slower than before.

"…So she goes around watching everything," Pikra said at last.

"…Yes."

"…And yet somehow, it's not frightening," Pikra said. "A person who
watches everything."

I laughed a little at that. I'd been thinking the same thing.

Before I fell asleep that night, I turned that question over for a
long time. Why wasn't Mena frightening? I know now. The Roll decided
something after it watched. Mena decided nothing after she watched. She
just wrote it down. Eyes that decide nothing aren't frightening, no
matter how much they see.

I lay awake late into the night. I looked out the window toward the
field. Nothing was visible in the darkness. But I knew that even in the
dark, that field was still letting nothing fall. Even at night, that
fact didn't change.

I remember that night clearly, even now. I didn't know yet that what
Mena would discover the next day wasn't a problem belonging only to
this one city. I only meant to remember that day as the day I saw my
mother-in-law's name reduced to a few numbers. But that day was only
the beginning of something far larger than that.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. The Reputation Field

_Previously_ — Mena searched the Roll's reputation field in the
archive. The Roll wrote what was lost; Mena wrote the choices people
made in front of that loss. Turning through the list, Mena found an
entry that quoted her own name. "There's one more thing I have to
confirm. Whether this matches the sentence I actually wrote."

The next morning, I went to the archive before I'd even drawn the
water.

The door was already open. The old man was outside sweeping.

"…Is Mena inside?" I asked.

"…She's been here since before sunrise," the man said. "That room
faces east, you know. She said she'd come with the light, and here she
is, right along with it."

I went in. Mena sat in the same seat as the day before. But something
was different from yesterday. Papers pulled from her case lay spread in
neat order across the table. In sequence. By date, it looked like.

The skin under Mena's eyes was a shade darker than yesterday's. Not the
face of someone who hadn't slept at all. But not the face of someone
who'd slept well, either.

I stood a moment in the doorway and looked around the room. Morning
light lay thin across the papers from the window. Dust drifted slowly
through it. The room felt far quieter than it had the day before.

"…Were you here all night?" I asked.

"…No," Mena said. "I slept at the lodging house last night. I just
came out early, before dawn."

"…What did you find?"

Mena looked at me and shifted her seat to the side.

"…Sit down," she said. "What I'm about to tell you will take a while."

Mena pointed to the papers on the table.

"…These are all things I wrote in different cities," Mena said. "I've
laid them out by date. From yesterday through this morning, I've been
finding and comparing everything in this city's reputation field that
quotes me, one by one."

I tried to count the sheets and gave up. There looked to be more than
twenty.

"…Have you gone through all of them?"

"…No," Mena said. "Only a few. But there's something strange."

"…What?"

"…The wording is all a little off," Mena said. "Not completely wrong.
But always, somewhere, a phrase or two has changed."

"…How does it change?"

Mena laid two sheets side by side. One was old, one relatively new.

I leaned in and looked at them. The old one was handwritten; the new
one had the Roll's smooth printed form. Set side by side, even their
shape was different. One bore the marks of a human hand. The other
looked as if no hand had ever touched it at all.

"…This smooth one is the problem," Mena said. "If a person had copied
it by hand, the one doing the copying would have felt what they were
doing. You have to look at each letter to move it. But this one has no
trace of that."

"…So it wasn't a person who copied it?"

"…I don't know," Mena said. "It could be someone copied it and was too
practiced to feel it. Or it might truly never have passed through a
hand at all. The Roll might have summarized it on its own."

"…Does the Roll do that too?"

"…It does," Mena said. "Shortening a long testimony — the Roll is very
good at that. But shortening and changing are different things. I want
to see exactly where the two started becoming the same thing."

Listening, I wondered for the first time: whom do you even ask about a
wrong when you don't know who committed it? I never found the answer
to that by the end of this chapter. In truth, the answer never comes
out cleanly across this whole book.

"…None of this is confirmed yet," Mena said. "I don't want to say
anything too soon. I might be misremembering."

I found that carefulness puzzling at the time. Mena always spoke with
certainty. That moment alone was unusually cautious. I didn't grasp,
then, that it was because this was something about Mena herself.

"…Mena," I asked. "Has this happened before?"

Mena's hand stopped.

"…Once," she said. "A long time ago."

"…What happened then?"

Mena glanced out the window for a moment.

"…I'm not going to talk about that now," Mena said. "Let me finish
what I'm confirming first. I don't want the old story to blur what's in
front of me now."

That sounded a little contradictory to me. She'd said the old thing was
similar to what was happening now, and yet she wouldn't talk about the
old thing right now.

I didn't understand that contradiction then. I do now. Mena didn't
want the old thing to make her prejudge what was in front of her. If
she assumed it was the same shape too soon, she might miss what was
different about it. Mena doubted herself to that degree.

Watching that carefulness, I felt that the Roll and Mena were exact
opposites. The Roll judged fast. The faster the judgment, the more
precise it was said to be. Mena was slow. The slower she went, the
harder she seemed to be trying to be exact. I wondered, for the first
time, which of the two carried the heavier weight. I found no easy
answer that night, or afterward.

Mena found a few more quotations bearing her name. None from this
city — people from other cities. Unfamiliar names scrolled past on the
screen.

"…These people have nothing to do with this city," I said. "So why are
they all in here?"

"…The Roll doesn't only see a person inside this city," Mena said.
"Wherever they came from, whatever they were judged to be before, it
carries that along too. It thinks it can't fully judge a person
otherwise."

"…So that's true for me too?"

"…You came from outside the city, so there's no city record for you,"
Mena said. "But if you ever leave for another city, whatever this city
has written about you will follow you there."

That was the first time I truly felt the size of this system. I'd
thought of the Roll, until then, as something belonging to this one
city. It wasn't. The Roll was like a river running between cities. If
the water in one city clouded, the city downstream clouded with it.

I thought suddenly of the settlement outside. There was nothing like
this out there. What someone said about you didn't follow you around.
People judged each other face to face. They were wrong sometimes, but
the wrongness ended right there. It never crossed into a city and
built up.

"…It's not like this outside," I said.

"…I know," Mena said. "I've spent a lot of time outside too. There's
nothing like this out there. But there's something else instead."

"…What?"

"…When there are fewer people to remember, forgetting comes faster,"
Mena said. "How many people in the world do you suppose still remember
your husband's name?"

Something went cold in my chest at that. Mena hadn't meant it unkindly.
She was only stating a fact. But the fact hurt.

"…The city doesn't forget," Mena said. "Instead, it remembers wrong. I
still don't know which is better."

That was the first time I felt real fear that day. I'd thought it was
frightening that nothing was left at the corner. This was a different
kind of fear. The fear that when one city goes wrong, the wrong doesn't
stay inside that one city.

"…Are all the cities connected like this?" I asked.

"…To some degree," Mena said. "Everyone says they're independent
cities. But their Rolls aren't independent from each other, not like
that."

"…Why was it made that way?"

Mena gave a small shrug.

"…Convenience," Mena said. "It's faster for a city to receive what
another city already knows than for it to learn a person from scratch.
The Roll likes fast."

"…It wasn't meant with any ill intent."

"…No, probably not," Mena said. "That's what frightens me more. That
something made with no ill intent at all can produce a bad result."

I turned that over for a long time. I remembered the first thing I'd
felt when I came into this city. The city hadn't driven me out. It had
simply given me nothing. What Mena was describing felt similar. No one
had done anything with ill intent, and yet only the result came out
bad.

That was the first time I dimly sensed how this world turned. It
wasn't that bad people existed and made bad things happen. No one was
bad, and yet the bad thing made itself.

Mena paused a moment and looked at me.

"…Xene, let's stop here for today," she said. "There's more I need to
check. My own things."

"…Your own?"

"…Something I wrote once, long ago, in another city," Mena said. "I saw
it twisted this same way once before. I thought it belonged only to
that city."

Something caught in me at that.

"…And?"

"…Now I think I'm catching the same scent here," Mena said. "I have to
check to know. A scent alone is nothing."

I didn't understand, then, why that last line mattered so much. A
scent alone is nothing — I thought it was something Mena was telling
herself. A few days later I would know exactly what that scent was.
And that it wouldn't end at a scent.

I looked at Mena's face again in that moment. Beneath its calm,
something was pulled taut. Like a thread on the verge of snapping. I
had no way of knowing, then, when it would.

I got up to leave, then sat back down.

"…Mena," I said. "Is there anything I can help with?"

Mena looked at me. Her face showed a little surprise for the first
time.

"…Why do you want to help?" she asked.

I couldn't answer right away. I'd never thought about the reason.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "It just seemed strange that you'd
carry all of this alone."

Mena smiled a little. The first smile of the day.

"…Thank you," she said. "But this is something I have to do alone
right now. It's something I have to confirm with my own eyes, from my
own hand. If there's something you can help with, I'll be sure to ask."

I nodded. And I believed her.

I didn't know, then, that the promise Mena made — that there would be
something I could help with — would come back to me, much later, in a
very different shape. It wasn't handing over pages. It was handing over
this whole record.

That afternoon, I went to the corner for a little while. Habit carried
my feet there. Nothing was there, as usual.

On my way back I ran into Lytron.

"…You were at the archive today, weren't you," Lytron said. "That
woman's registered. I just confirmed it."

"…Registered?"

"…Mena," Lytron said. "As an outside recorder. It's some old
arrangement, apparently. I only found out about it today myself."

I was a little surprised at that. I only then realized that what
Lytron had said the day before had been true.

"…So it's all fine now?" I asked.

"…It was never really a problem," Lytron said. "I just hadn't checked
it before."

Something complicated crossed Lytron's face. I couldn't quite read it
at the time.

"…Do you know what Mena's looking into?" Lytron asked.

"…The reputation field."

"…Do you know what that is?"

"…A little."

Lytron looked at me for a moment.

"…I should go take a look too," Lytron said. "My name must be in there
somewhere."

"…Are you curious?"

"…No," Lytron said. "It's just, I've never once thought about how my
name is written there."

That struck me as a little odd.

"…Why not? You talk about standing every day."

"…Standing is a number," Lytron said. "I always look at numbers. But
what words sit beside that number — I've never actually read them."

"…Why not?"

Lytron thought for a moment.

"…I assumed it would be good, whatever it said," he said. "Since my
number's high."

I felt something catch at that answer. Lytron had never wondered why
his number was high, what stood behind it. A high number was simply
taken as given.

I didn't judge that indifference badly at the time. It wasn't ill
intent that kept him from wondering. There was simply no reason for him
to wonder. That day, I saw clearly for the first time what Lytron's
world was. A world where you never have to wonder.

"…Can I go see Mena about it?" Lytron asked.

"…Go ask her. I think she'll say yes."

Lytron nodded and started toward the archive, then stopped again.

"…Xene," he said.

"…Yes."

"…What's written beside your name?"

I found myself briefly at a loss for words.

"…'Nothing out of the ordinary,'" I said.

Lytron said nothing to that. He just looked at me a moment, then left.

Watching him go, I wondered whether Lytron felt that sentence as
strange as I did. I didn't know, then. Later I would learn that it
stayed with him that day, longer than I ever guessed.

That evening I stopped by the archive again. Mena was still in her
seat. The papers on the table had grown more numerous.

"…Find anything more?" I asked.

Mena nodded. But her expression was more complicated than it had been
that afternoon.

"…I'll tell you tomorrow," Mena said. "It isn't sorted out yet today."

"…Are you all right?"

Mena looked at me. She started to smile, and couldn't.

"…Trying to be," Mena said.

That answer sounded more honest to me than anything else I'd heard
that day.

Before I left the archive, I turned back once from the door. Mena was
leaning over her papers again. No lamp lit. Just the last of the
evening light. I watched her back for a long while before quietly
closing the door.

When I got home, Pikra was waiting at the door. She seemed to have been
waiting for me, later than usual.

"…With that recording woman again today?" Pikra asked.

"…Yes."

"…What are you spending so much time looking at?"

I thought a moment about how to put it.

"…Something Mena wrote a long time ago has apparently turned up
strange in another city," I said. "She's checking on it now."

Pikra's hand stopped at that.

"…Her own words changed?"

"…Yes."

Pikra said nothing for a long time. I could feel that she was
remembering something in that silence.

"…Something similar happened to me too," Pikra said at last.

I looked up at her, startled.

"…What was it?"

"…Something I said once came back to me later, out of someone else's
mouth, meaning something completely different," Pikra said. "No one did
it with a bad heart. It just happened, passed along enough times."

"…What did you do, then?"

Pikra shook her head.

"…Nothing I could do," Pikra said. "I didn't even know who to be angry
at. Not the first person who said it, not the last one who passed it
on. Everyone had only changed it a little."

I thought of what Mena had said that day. There's no place left to be
angry. I felt, right then, that this wasn't something only this city
did. Wherever words pass through many mouths, it could happen anywhere.
Only here, it had turned into numbers, and truly moved a person's
place.

"…So how did you get through it, then?" I asked.

Pikra set down the stalks she'd been working.

"…I just lived," Pikra said. "When there's no way to set it right, you
live instead of setting it right. That's the only way I know."

"…Weren't you bitter about it?"

"…I was," Pikra said. "I still am, sometimes. But Xene, carrying
bitterness and being eaten alive by it are different things. I worked
hard not to be eaten."

I held that for a long time. The hearth fire made shadows on Pikra's
face, and erased them, over and over. I saw, in that face, an old
bitterness, and the face of someone who had endured it.

That night, before sleep, I remembered Mena's hands arranging the
papers in order. Those hands hadn't come apart, even when she'd been
angry. I didn't know then that it was simply how she worked. I would
watch, the very next day, exactly what those hands found.

I lay awake a long time. Beside me, I could hear Pikra's breathing,
even and steady. I stared at the ceiling and counted through, one by
one, the things I'd heard that day. Nothing out of the ordinary. A
scent alone is nothing. I worked hard not to be eaten.

I felt that those three sentences, though they'd come from three
different mouths, strangely lined up into a single thread. The Roll had
told me I was nothing out of the ordinary. Mena refused to say anything
until she was certain. Pikra had worked hard not to be devoured by her
own bitterness. That night I couldn't say, in words, what those three
had in common. I can now. All three were a fight against jumping too
fast to a conclusion.

Outside, the wind brushed against the roof. I listened to it and slowly
closed my eyes. Tomorrow, Mena would finish her confirmation. I hoped
the result would turn out well. But some part of me already knew — if
it had been going to turn out well, Mena wouldn't have been so
careful.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. That Isn't What I Wrote

_Previously_ — Mena found a record in this city's reputation field
that quoted her name. The sentence was smooth. It looked flawless.
Mena said she needed to confirm whether it matched what she had
actually written, and decided to wait until daylight.

The next morning, I went straight to the archive without even filling
the water jar.

The walk was short, but I slowed my steps more than once along the
way. I didn't know exactly why. My heart simply didn't want to go
forward. I couldn't explain it to myself then. I know now. I already
knew it wasn't going to be good news. And knowing that, I still walked
as if arriving late might make the news arrive a little later too.

The archive door stood open. The old man wasn't sweeping this time; he
just stood in the doorway. He saw me and, without a word, tipped his
head toward the inside.

Mena sat in the same seat as the day before. More paper had piled up
on the table. It looked like it had come from some compartment deep in
the case I'd never seen before. Every sheet was old. Some had crumbling
edges; some were splitting along their folds.

"…Did you find it?" I asked.

Mena nodded. The answer didn't come right away.

I could tell this silence was different from yesterday's. Yesterday's
silence had been careful. Today's was the silence of someone who
already knew something. I stood a moment in the doorway and waited. It
seemed right to wait for Mena to speak first.

"…Sit down," Mena said at last.

I sat beside her. The papers on the table were far more orderly than
yesterday. Somehow that made me more uneasy, not less. I learned that
day, for the first time, that things arranged too neatly can be more
frightening than things scattered in haste. Watching Mena, I learned
that when a person's mind is unsteady, they reach instead for
something they can set right with their hands. That neatness wasn't
calm. It was a gesture reaching for calm.

Mena slid two sheets to the middle of the table.

One was handwritten. The ink had faded, the letters tilted slightly.
Not the writing of someone rushing — the writing of a hand worn into
its shape by long use. I thought the handwriting looked familiar. It
matched the other pages Mena had shown me exactly.

"…This is what I wrote," Mena said. "A long time ago. I won't say the
name. It doesn't matter now. Or which city."

I read the line.

"He did not explain what he had done. I did not ask why. That was
not my work to do."

A short sentence. I didn't know what event it was about. It didn't
seem necessary to know. The sentence said everything on its own. What
someone had done, and what Mena hadn't done.

"…What's wrong with this?" I asked. "It's just stating the facts."

"…That's right," Mena said. "There's nothing wrong with this. Looking
at it again now, I wouldn't change a word. The problem is this one."

Mena slid the other sheet in front of me.

This one wasn't handwritten. It was printed — the Roll's distinctive,
smooth typeface. I read the sentence beneath it.

"Witness testimony, Mena, cited. Subject refused to explain the
act. [Assessment: no remorse.]"

I read that sentence twice and still couldn't quite place what was
wrong with it. It looked similar. It looked like the same story, told
differently.

"…Isn't this saying about the same thing?" I asked.

Mena looked at me. For the first time, I saw something give way in her
face. Not a collapse. Only, briefly, beneath the calm, something
shook.

"…It isn't the same," Mena said. Her voice was lower than usual. "I
didn't write that he refused to explain. I wrote that he didn't
explain. I didn't know why he hadn't spoken. I never even asked. That
wasn't my work to do."

I still hadn't quite caught the difference.

"…Is refusing and not explaining really different?" I asked.

"…Very different," Mena said. "Not explaining tells you nothing at
all. There might have been a reason, or there might not. He might not
have been able to, or he might have chosen not to. I didn't know, so I
didn't write it. Leaving it not-known was my work."

"…And this one?"

"…This one decided, without knowing," Mena said. "It says he 'refused'
to explain. Refusal means you could, and chose not to. I didn't even
know whether he could have. But here, that's already been decided."

I read the last line again as I listened.

[Assessment: no remorse.]

"…What's this? This part in the brackets."

"…A classification," Mena said. "The Roll adds it. Below my
testimony. I never once wrote the word remorse in that sentence. I
never even looked at whether he felt remorse or not. That's a matter
of the heart. I don't write the heart. I only write what was done."

"…And the Roll wrote the heart in for you?"

"…In effect, yes," Mena said. "I write 'he did not explain,' and
somewhere — someone, or something — turns that into 'refused to
explain,' and attaches the assessment 'no remorse' beside it. I only
wrote the first of those three steps. The other two aren't mine."

I read the three steps aloud to myself. Did not explain. Refused to
explain. No remorse. With each step, the man became a little worse. And
yet at no point between them was there any new fact about what he had
actually done. There had only ever been one fact from the start. That
he had said nothing at all. That single fact had grown three times
heavier by the time it passed through those three steps.

"…Who does this?" I asked. "Is it a person doing it, or does the Roll
just do it on its own?"

"…I don't know either," Mena said. "That's the part that maddens me
most. If a person did it, I could go find that person and ask. If the
Roll did it on its own, I have nowhere to go. Right now I don't even
know which it is."

That was the first time I began to guess, dimly, at what whoever wrote
Book 3 must have gone through. I won't set down here exactly what that
person discovered — Mena herself never told me the whole of it. I'll
only note what she said that morning: "It wasn't the same shape. But
the direction was the same."

"…What direction?" I asked.

Mena looked out the window for a moment. Morning light lay long across
the papers.

"…The direction that turns an absence of explanation into an absence
of remorse," Mena said. "The direction that turns silence into guilt."

I turned that over for a long time.

"…You said this happened before, once," I said. "What shape did it
take, then?"

Mena looked at me a moment, then shook her head.

"…I won't talk about it today," Mena said. "What I want to say right
now is that this happened in a completely different city, in a
completely different way, and the result is identical. I wanted to
think it was coincidence."

"…And now?"

Instead of answering, Mena set down the paper in her hand.

I saw Mena's hand shaking.

It wasn't a large tremor. Just a small slip as she set the paper down.
I had never once seen Mena's hand shake before that. Not pouring
water, not surveying an unfamiliar field, not standing before the
administrator. That hand had always moved the same way. I think I'd
been quietly relying on that, without realizing it — on the hand
always moving at the same pace. Seeing it shake, I felt something I'd
been standing on shake with it.

"…Are you all right?" I asked.

Mena didn't answer. Instead, she laid the two sheets side by side
again. Handwritten and printed. What she had written, and what she had
not. The gesture of laying them side by side was the same gesture as
yesterday. But yesterday it had been the gesture of confirming
something. Today it looked like the gesture of someone enduring
something.

"…This sentence," Mena said at last. "This isn't what I wrote."

Her voice wasn't loud. But something was in it. I couldn't put a name
to it then.

"…That isn't what I wrote." Mena said it again. This time she wasn't
speaking to me. She seemed to be speaking to the paper.

I remember those words exactly, even now. That isn't what I wrote. So
few syllables. And yet I dimly felt, in that moment, that it was a
sentence shaking the whole of what Mena had done with her life. Mena
was someone who wrote things down. For someone who writes, to find
words she never wrote still standing under her own name — that was a
kind of theft no one else could quite feel.

"…What happened to that person?" I asked. "The one written about here."

"…I don't know," Mena said. "I don't even know who he is. There's no
name. Only a case number."

"…Can't you find him?"

Mena shook her head.

"…I looked by the case number. He's not from this city. He must have
passed through here once, or this city took in the record from
somewhere else."

"…So you don't even know where he is now?"

"…No," Mena said. "I have no idea what standing he received after
this, what he was made to go through, where. There's exactly one thing
I know. That my sentence changed, somewhere along the way."

I felt, for the first time, the full size of that helplessness. Mena
couldn't resent anyone. Couldn't go find anyone. A wrong had clearly
happened. But whose hands it had happened in, no one knew. A wrong with
no face to it.

I had always thought, until that day, that a wrong always had someone's
face attached to it. That was how it had been outside the settlement.
If someone blocked a water channel, that person had a face. If someone
stole grain, that person had a face. If there was a face, you could go
and confront it. You could get angry. That day, for the first time, I
learned there was such a thing as a faceless wrong. And that it lasted
far longer than a wrong with a face.

After that, I began to see everything in this city differently. The
nothing at the corner. The "nothing out of the ordinary" beside my
name. All of it was a faceless wrong. A wrong you couldn't grab hold of
anyone and ask why about. I was, that day, only just beginning to
understand that this was the thing this city did best.

"…If you can't find him," I asked, "does this just stay this way
forever?"

Mena gave no answer. I understood the silence was the answer.

Something else occurred to me, and it frightened me.

"…Mena," I said. "Could my story get twisted like this in some other
city someday?"

Mena looked at me. She hadn't expected the question.

"…Why do you think that?" Mena asked.

"…I might leave this city someday too," I said. "Then whatever gets
written about me would flow somewhere else. If someone changed it
there, I wouldn't have any way of knowing."

Mena was quiet a moment.

"…It's possible," she said at last. "I don't lie to you. It's
possible, Xene."

"…Then why do you keep writing? It might all get changed anyway."

For the first time, Mena smiled a little at that. A sad smile.

"…If I stop writing because it might get changed," Mena said, "then
there's no original left to be changed in the first place. I'm the one
who leaves the original. I can't stop what anyone does with it later.
But if there were no original at all, I couldn't have laid these two
sentences side by side today and compared them."

Listening, I began to understand a little why Mena spent so long
arranging her papers in order. That order was the only thread that
might let someone, later — maybe Mena herself — compare something
against something else.

That was the first time I dimly felt that recording wasn't a matter
of protecting something. It was a matter of leaving it behind. You
might not be able to protect it. But you could leave it. I would hold
onto the difference between those two for a long time to come.

Mena carefully returned the papers to her case.

"…Xene," she said. "I spent the whole day yesterday confirming this. I
wanted to be certain. I kept hoping I'd made a mistake."

"…It wasn't a mistake."

"…No," Mena said. "It wasn't a mistake."

Mena closed the lid of the case. The sound was quiet. But it felt to me
like it would stay in the room a long time.

"…What will you do now?" I asked.

Mena looked at me for a moment. What remained of the tremor in her
face was slowly settling. She was returning to her usual calm. But not
completely. I chose not to press further, out of some respect for that
incompleteness.

"…I have to keep looking," Mena said. "This won't be the only one.
That's what frightens me most."

I didn't fully understand what that meant then. The next day I would
know exactly the size of it. What Mena found that day wasn't just one
thing at all.

Mena said she wanted to be alone for a while. I knew there was no ill
will in that. I thought of my water jar and went outside.

The sun was strong. I started toward the well, then found my feet
turning toward the field instead. Habit. I walked all the way to the
corner without even the jar.

Lytron was there. On patrol, it looked like — a handheld terminal in
his hand, watching the machines. He raised a hand to wave at me, then
stopped.

"…Is something wrong?" he asked. He'd asked as soon as he saw my face.

"…No," I said. It was a lie. I hadn't wanted to lie that day, but I
didn't know how to explain, so I said it anyway.

"…You're coming from the archive," he said. Not a question. A
statement.

"…How did you know?"

"…There's talk going around," he said. "That Mena found something
serious. I heard the archive keeper reported something to a superior
this afternoon."

I was startled at that. I hadn't even thought yet that this might leak
outside.

"…What did he report?" I asked.

"…I don't know that much," he said. "I only heard the rumor."

That was the first time I felt that what Mena had found no longer
belonged only to Mena. What had happened behind the archive door was
already walking around out here, wearing a different shape entirely. I
saw, that very day, with my own eyes, how quickly something like that
could deform.

"…I don't know the details either," I said. "It's Mena's business."

Lytron nodded. He didn't press further. But after a moment's
hesitation, he spoke again.

"…I should go see Mena myself soon," he said. "You said my name's in
there somewhere too."

"…Yes. You should."

I let that pass lightly at the time. I had no idea that the day he
actually went to see her would become another starting point for
everything that followed.

I crouched at the corner for a moment. I sifted a few handfuls of dirt
with my fingertips. Nothing there, same as always. Strangely, that
nothing was a comfort to me that day. At least this dirt was the same
today as it had been yesterday.

That evening I went home empty-handed, without even the water jar.
Pikra noticed first.

"…Where's the water?" Pikra asked.

Only then did I remember I hadn't taken the jar out that morning.

"…I forgot," I said.

Pikra looked at me a moment by the hearth. Her eyes read something in
my face.

"…That recording woman again?" Pikra asked.

"…Yes."

I told her, briefly, what had happened that day. That Mena had
confirmed her own sentence had been changed. How it had been changed.
Pikra listened without stopping her hands. Only once I'd finished did
her hands slow.

"…She must have had a hard day, that woman," Pikra said.

"…Yes. Her hand was shaking. I'd never seen that before."

Pikra nodded. She was quiet a moment, then spoke.

"…I know that feeling," Pikra said. "Something not yours going around
in your own mouth. How hollow it feels."

"…Has that happened to you too?"

Pikra's hands stopped. The hearth fire cast a brief shadow across her
face.

"…It has," Pikra said. "But I never tried to find a way to fix it. I
didn't have the strength for that. She's different. She has the
strength to search. That might be harder for her, not easier."

"…Why?"

"…Without strength, you give up quickly," Pikra said. "With strength,
you have to try everything you can before you give up. You don't know
yet how long that takes, or how many times you break along the way."

I thought of Mena's shaking hand again, listening to that. Pikra's
words felt true. Mena wasn't someone who could give up yet. I couldn't
decide, that night, whether that was fortunate or something to grieve
over.

I thought about that sentence late into the night. That isn't what I
wrote. I didn't know, then, that those five words would carry the
weight of this entire book. I only fell asleep hoping Mena would return
to that plain, calm face again.

I saw the two sentences in my dreams that night too. Did not explain.
Refused to explain. Two sentences lying side by side, and then, quietly,
one settling over the other. When I woke that morning, I still
couldn't quite shake the dream.

Writing this chapter, I find myself circling back, again and again,
to those five words. That isn't what I wrote. I'll hear that same
sentence, later on, from a mouth other than Mena's. That is much later.
For now, I'll only set down Mena's trembling hand that night, and the
way that hand worked to steady itself again.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. The Same Pattern

_Previously_ — Mena confirmed her own testimony had been changed.
"Did not explain" had become "refused to explain," which fed into
the assessment "no remorse." Mena said this wouldn't be the only
one. That was what frightened her most.

The next morning, I smelled the paper before I even opened the archive
door.

The smell was stronger than usual. Old paper, mixed with the scent of
a lamp that had burned all night. From the smell alone, I could guess
what kind of night had passed in that room.

The old man at the door hadn't swept or wiped anything down that
morning. He was just leaning against the doorframe. He spoke to me in
a low voice.

"…She's been in there the whole time. I haven't gone in myself, this
morning."

"…Why not?"

"…The air in there." He gave a small shrug. "I just felt like I
shouldn't disturb it."

I hesitated a moment at the door, then pushed it open quietly.

Mena didn't look like she'd slept at all. Her eyes were red, and twice
as much paper as yesterday was spread across the table.

"…Were you here all night?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. She didn't hide it. "This time I couldn't wait, the
way I did yesterday."

I sat beside her. The papers on the table were lined up differently
from the day before. On the left, city names; on the right, pairs of
short sentences. I recognized it as a table. Mena must have spent the
whole night building it.

I thought, suddenly, that the room felt narrower than it had yesterday.
It wasn't the paper making it feel that way. It was the weight of what
that paper held, grown heavier overnight.

"…Can I help?" I asked.

"…Just don't let the order get mixed up," Mena said. Her voice had no
strength in it. "Take the next one from that case and hand it to me.
From the top, in order."

I did that. I pulled the sheets out one by one and handed them to her.
Mena took each one, checked it against the original, and wrote a line
into the table. We didn't talk much. I didn't find the silence
uncomfortable. If anything, moving my hands helped. If I'd had to sit
there doing nothing, I think I would have found it hard to bear.

The light from the window crept slowly across the room. By the time it
reached the floor past the table's legs, I'd stopped counting how many
sheets we'd gone through. Counting felt pointless. What mattered wasn't
the number, but the direction.

"…How many have you found?" I asked.

"…Seven," Mena said. "Five of them certain. Two I haven't been able to
find the original for, so those aren't confirmed."

I read down through the table. Most of the city names were ones I'd
never heard before. A few sounded vaguely familiar, but I couldn't
place where.

The first line.

Original: "He stood without kneeling."
Quoted: "He refused submission and displayed a defiant attitude."

The second line.

Original: "The woman said nothing about how she would repay the
debt."
Quoted: "Debtor: expressed no intent to repay."

The third line.

Original: "He did not explain what he had done. I did not ask why."
Quoted: "Witness testimony, Mena, cited. Subject refused to explain
the act. [Assessment: no remorse.]"

That third one was the one I'd already seen.

I ran my finger along the line.

"…This is yesterday's."

"…That's right," Mena said. "But this line turned up again today. In
a different place. I only realized yesterday that a single sentence,
once made, gets reused across different cases. Once one is twisted, it
gets recycled again and again, the way a precedent does."

I read on through the rest of the table.

The fourth line.

Original: "The child covered for her sibling. I didn't ask why."
Quoted: "Guardian: withheld information regarding harboring of
accomplice."

"…Is this about a child too?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. "When I wrote it, it was just about a child covering
for a sibling. I didn't even say whether it was good or bad. But here,
the word 'harboring' has been attached. I didn't even know what she'd
covered up."

The fifth line was shorter still.

Original: "He said nothing that day."
Quoted: "Silent. Deemed to be evading testimony."

Reading that line, a chill ran through me. The original sentence was
nothing at all. He said nothing that day. Everyone has days like that.
I'd had many days like that myself. And yet that one sentence had
become, on the other side, "evading testimony."

"…I have plenty of days I say nothing too," I said. My voice shook
without my meaning it to. "So could that happen to me someday too?"

Mena looked at me for a moment. She didn't answer. I didn't want to
know, right then, that the silence was the answer.

I stopped at the sixth line.

Mena's hand had stopped there too. I read it aloud.

Original: "This one never explained himself."
Quoted: "This one never repented."

"…This," I said. "This one feels familiar, somehow."

Mena looked at me. Her face had gone strangely pale.

"…It can't feel familiar," Mena said. "This isn't about anyone in this
city. It isn't from any event in this city."

"…Then why does it feel familiar?"

I didn't know why, then. I had no way of knowing what happened outside
this city, in a book that wasn't mine to know. I only thought, at the
time, that the shape of this sentence resembled the shape of the one
I'd seen yesterday, and that was why it struck me as familiar. I know
now. What I felt wasn't familiarity. This sentence, in the whole of
this world, had truly happened exactly once. I was only standing at
the far end of its ripples.

Mena quietly laid another sheet beside that line. A sheet far older
than the others.

"…This isn't something I wrote three years ago," Mena said. "It's much
older than that. I wrote this sentence, a very long time ago, in
another city. Or rather — I didn't write it exactly. Someone took what
I'd written."

"…Took it?"

"…Yes," Mena said. "At the time, I didn't think of it as theft. I just
thought it had flowed away somewhere. Records flow, that's how it
works. I didn't know how far it had gone."

"…And now it's turned up here."

Mena nodded, very slowly.

"…It's turned up here," Mena said. "A single sentence that started in
a city before I was even born has made its way into this Harvest's
reputation data. I find that frightening, Xene."

That was the first time I dimly sensed what this whole book was
heading toward. This wasn't the story of one city. I couldn't put that
feeling into exact words that night. Even now, years later, I still
choose my words very carefully around it.

"…Mena," I asked. "Are all seven of these going the same direction?"

Mena scanned through the table again.

"…Yes," she said. "Every single one."

"…None the other way? Someone who did something wrong, and it got
turned into something good?"

Mena shook her head.

"…No," Mena said. "I checked for that too. All seven lean the same
way, without exception. Absence of explanation becomes absence of
remorse. Silence becomes guilt. Never once the other direction."

I slowly felt the weight of that. If it were one, you could call it a
mistake. If it were two, you could call it coincidence. If seven, and
all seven leaned the same way, it was no longer either.

"…If it were a mistake," I said, "shouldn't it sometimes go wrong the
other way too?"

Mena looked at me. Something in her face, for the first time,
acknowledged the point.

"…That's right," Mena said. "I spent all night thinking that too.
Mistakes scatter both ways. This only ever leans one way. Something
that only ever leans one way isn't a mistake."

Mena tucked those two sheets away into the deepest compartment of her
case, apart from the others, so they wouldn't mix.

"…Mena," I asked. "Is this the story from before? The one you didn't
tell me yesterday?"

Mena looked at me for a moment, then nodded. Very slightly.

"…That's right," Mena said. "I thought, at the time, that this
sentence had ended in exactly one city, exactly one case. I never went
back to that city after that. I couldn't."

"…Why not?"

Instead of answering, Mena slowly ran her hand over the lid of the
case.

"…I'll tell you later," Mena said. "For now, just know that this
sentence has traveled all the way here. Even after I left that city,
this sentence kept walking on, without me."

That struck me as strange, hearing it talked about like a person. A
sentence walking. But from that day on, I really did begin to feel
that way. As if sentences had feet, and even when the one who wrote
them stopped, they kept walking on their own.

"…So what is this?" I asked. "Is someone doing it on purpose?"

Mena didn't answer for a long time.

"…I don't know that yet either," Mena said at last. "If it's
deliberate, then who? I don't think there's a single hand behind these
seven. The cities are too far apart, and too much time separates them."

"…But the direction is the same."

"…Yes. That's the strangest part," Mena said. "Many hands. One
direction."

"…Doesn't that frighten you?" I asked.

Mena closed her eyes for a moment.

"…It does," Mena said. "I always believed that if I ran into a wrong,
that wrong would have a face. So I kept looking for the face. This
city's administrator's face. That city's archivist's face. But right
now, I'm facing something with no face at all."

"…Can you even run into something like that?"

"…You can," Mena said. "The most frightening things, most of the time,
have no face. When there's a face, a person can hate it, or fear it —
and that makes it, in a way, less dangerous. When there's no face,
there's nowhere for the hatred to land, and so no one ever tries to
stop it."

I held onto those words for a long time. Many hands, one direction.
It struck me as even more frightening than if someone had simply
ordered it. If there were an order-giver, you could find him. If no one
had ordered anything, and everyone still leaned the same way, there
was no one to go and find at all.

I ran my eyes back over the sentences once more. Did not explain,
refused to explain. Said nothing about repayment, expressed no intent
to repay. Covered for her sibling, harbored an accomplice. Said nothing
that day, evading testimony. Laid side by side, the four pairs looked
like they'd been struck from the same mold. Different cities, different
eras, and yet one and the same mold.

"…Then why does it only ever lean this way?" I asked. "Why never the
other?"

Mena thought for a moment.

"…I've thought about this a long time too," Mena said. "My guess is,
it's easier to see the side with no explanation as bad. Hearing an
explanation makes a person complicated. You come to know the reason.
With no explanation, you can just decide. Fast, and simple."

"…What does that have to do with it?"

"…I told you the Roll likes fast," Mena said. "A fast judgment always
leans toward the simple. And the simple side is almost always the bad
one. To see something as good, you have to look a long time. Seeing it
as bad, you can do at a glance."

I thought of Lytron, hearing that. What he'd written about me.
Nothing out of the ordinary. Maybe that had come from the very same
laziness. Not laziness that decided to write something bad — laziness
that never bothered to write something good, either, because there was
nothing bad to write.

I didn't understand all of that, that day. I still can't say I
understand it completely. But I think of it every time I look at this
world since. Many hands. One direction.

Mena wrote one more line at the bottom of the table. I watched her hand
move.

"No explanation → no remorse. Silence → guilt. Seven for seven.
No counter-example."

"…What will you do with this?" I asked.

"…I don't know yet," Mena said. "I'm not trying to fix this right now.
I don't even think I'll be able to."

"…Then why go this far?"

Mena set down her pen and looked at me.

"…So that later, someone can say the wrong thing was wrong, you have
to leave behind what was right," Mena said. "I'm not building this
table to change anything right now. I'm building it so that someone,
someday, can compare against it — and for that, I have to leave this
shape exactly as it is, right now."

I remembered something Mena had told me before. Even if I can't hold
onto it, I have to leave behind what was right at the start. I hadn't
understood that well then. Now I thought I understood it a little.

I heard the field outside the window. Machines running, scales
chiming. I realized, suddenly, that out there, today was flowing on
exactly as any other day. While seven sentences lay side by side in
this room, the field kept ripening without a care.

"…Not going to the corner today?" Mena asked.

"…I'll stay here today."

Mena nodded, and leaned back over her papers. Her hand began to move
again. Slow, and certain. From last night until now, without rest,
that hand had never changed its pace.

I sat beside her and pictured something like a river flowing between
this city and every other one. Mena had said something like that
before — that cities seemed independent, but their Rolls weren't
independent from each other at all. That day, for the first time, I
knew which way that river flowed. Always the same way. From silence
toward guilt.

I left the archive close to evening. Mena stayed behind. For the first
time, I told her I would go on ahead, and left.

On the way home through the field, I stopped by the corner for a
moment. He was there, as always, moving his hands quietly.

"…You didn't come today," he said. Not a scolding tone.

"…I was helping Mena with something," I said.

He stopped his hands and looked at me.

"…What kind of something?"

I thought for a moment about how to explain.

"…Something Mena wrote keeps getting twisted into something worse in
different cities," I said. "Seven times now. All the same direction."

He was quiet a moment, hearing that. He looked down at the few stalks
of grain in his hand.

"…Seven times," he repeated. It sounded like a thought spoken aloud.

"…Do you know about this? This kind of thing?"

He looked at me. There was something old in that look. It wasn't the
first time I'd seen it. But that day it lingered unusually long.

"…I know," he said at last. "For a very long time."

"…Then why doesn't anyone fix it?" I asked again. My voice came out
sharper than I meant.

He slowly added the stalks in his hand to my sack. A little more than
he needed to.

"…Fixing and leaving something behind are different things," he said.
"I've watched that difference for a very long time."

He started moving his hands again.

"…Ask Mena directly about that," he said. "She knows it better than I
do."

I found that answer strange at the time. He always spoke like someone
who knew more than he was saying. That day, he took what he knew and
handed it off to Mena instead. I would only come to understand, much
later, why that was.

At home, Pikra was waiting by the door.

"…Late again today," Pikra said.

"…Mena found something overnight," I said. "Seven. Her own words,
twisted. All in the same direction."

Pikra set down what she'd been working on.

"…Seven of them." Pikra's voice was low.

"…You don't seem surprised."

"…It's not something to be surprised at," Pikra said. "One, it's a
mistake. Two, coincidence. Seven, and it already means those cities are
all connected to each other somewhere. I've had a dim sense of that
since I was young."

I was startled at that.

"…How?"

Pikra looked at the hearth fire for a moment.

"…When I was young, something I heard in one city came back to follow
me around in a completely different one, years later," Pikra said. "At
the time I thought rumor just travels fast. Hearing your story now, I
think maybe it wasn't rumor at all."

I held that thought for a long time.

"…Why didn't you tell me that back then?" I asked. "You'd gone through
something like that."

Pikra smiled a little. A bitter smile.

"…Would telling you have changed anything," Pikra said. "You were
young, and I didn't want to hand you that weight along with it. Some
things, staying quiet is how you share the burden."

I had no answer to that. I had always thought Pikra's silences were
just her nature. They weren't. Some silences were a choice.

That night, before sleep, I thought again of that river. Flowing
between city and city, invisible. That night, for the first time, I
felt its true size, and it frightened me. Something I could never have
imagined, living outside these walls.

I kept coming back that evening to that table. Seven lines. No
counter-example. I think those two lines run straight through the
middle of this book. I didn't feel their size that night. I do now.

I fell asleep having already decided the first thing I'd say to Mena
the next morning. Then just fix it. I thought it was an obvious
question. I wouldn't learn how naive it was until the following day.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. Then Why Not Fix It

_Previously_ — The quotations Mena found overnight came to seven. All
the same direction. No explanation becoming no remorse, silence
becoming guilt. He said he'd known about this for a very long time.
Xene decided on the question she'd ask Mena the next day.

That morning, before I'd even reached the archive door, I ran the
question through my head several times.

Then why not fix it.

I thought it was an obvious question. I wasn't so naive, at sixteen, as
to not know how hard it is to set right something you know is wrong.
And yet in that moment it felt simply obvious. If you'd found seven
wrongs, the next step was to fix seven wrongs.

Mena was there. She looked a little better than the day before. A face
that had slept some. The case sat closed beside her. I felt a small
relief seeing the lid shut. At least this morning, no new sentence
seemed about to leap out of it.

The old man was drawing water in the yard. He saw me and gave a light
wave. It felt different from yesterday's wave — yesterday's had been
careful; today's was just an ordinary greeting. This was a city that
didn't carry yesterday's weight into today. Whether that was fortunate
or something to regret, I still don't fully know.

"…Mena," I said, from the doorway. "Then why not fix it."

Mena looked at me. Not a surprised look. If anything, it seemed she'd
been expecting that exact question.

"…Sit down," Mena said. "It'll take sitting down, to talk about this."

I sat beside her. The table was cleaner than yesterday. All the paper
had gone back into the case. I guessed that meant Mena had made some
kind of peace with it, overnight.

"…What do you want to fix?" Mena asked first.

"…Those sentences," I said. "They were twisted, weren't they? Just set
them back the way they were."

"…Where would you fix it?" Mena asked again.

I couldn't answer right away. Thinking about it, there was no
"where."

"…This city?" I finally managed.

"…This city's Roll only quoted my sentence," Mena said. "This city
didn't twist it. This city only took what came from another city and
used it as it was."

"…Then that other city."

"…That other city is the same," Mena said. "I traced five of the seven
back yesterday and this morning. I couldn't find a starting point
anywhere. Everyone says they received it from the city before them."

"…Then the very first city," I said. "It must have started
somewhere."

Mena nodded.

"…That's right. It started somewhere," Mena said. "I know one of
those. It's the one I lived through myself. But if I went to that
city, what would I be able to do?"

I couldn't answer that.

"…Go and confront them," I said. "Tell them they got your words
wrong."

"…Say I confront them," Mena said. "Say the city says, 'Yes, we were
wrong,' and fixes the sentence. Then what about all the judgments
already handed down on the basis of it? What about the other cities
that already quoted it? I couldn't even finish counting how many times
that sentence has been re-cited."

That was the first time I saw the true size of this problem. I had
thought of a wrong, until then, as something like a point — something
you find and erase. This wasn't a point. It was water already spilled.
Where do you erase water from?

"…Then from now on," I said. "Just make sure it doesn't happen
anymore."

"…How?" Mena asked.

"…Make a rule," I said. "That whoever quotes something has to check the
original first."

Mena smiled a little for the first time. A weary smile.

"…Xene, that's a good idea," Mena said. "But who makes that rule?"

"…The administrator."

"…This city's administrator can only make rules for this city," Mena
said. "He has no power over another city's administrator. This city
could make a rule to always check originals, and it would mean nothing
if the other cities didn't follow it."

"…Then what if all the cities decided it together?"

Mena looked at me steadily.

"…For that, every city would have to gather in one place," Mena said.
"I've never seen such a gathering. Cities exchange Rolls with each
other. They don't meet to decide anything. Exchanging is easy. Meeting
to decide is hard."

Listening, I thought suddenly of the phrase "Book 3." The thing Mena
had only called "that other city, a long time ago" the day before. I
grew curious about it for the first time.

"…That thing that happened in that city," I asked, carefully. "What
happened then? Was there no place to fix it there either?"

Mena looked out the window for a moment.

"…Someone else tried to fix it, back then," Mena said. "Not me. Someone
else. Very hard."

"…What happened?"

"…That person ended up at the same place, in the end," Mena said. "The
place where there's no place left to fix it. That city's judgment
system had never once broken the law. Not a single time. So there was
no way to fault the system itself."

Something felt familiar in that.

"…That," I said. "Isn't that the same story as this city?"

Mena looked at me. A little surprised.

"…How did you think of that?"

"…This city's Roll has never broken a law either," I said. "The corner
clause is being kept, isn't it. And yet there's nothing at the corner.
The administrator said it himself. We've never broken the law."

Mena looked at me for a long moment. Something else was in her eyes.

"…That's right," Mena said finally. "Exactly that. I've had a feeling
of this for a few days now, and just now, Xene, you put it into words
before I did."

"…So is it all the same thing? Nothing at the corner, and your words
being changed?"

"…The shapes are different," Mena said. "But the frame underneath is
the same. No one breaks the law. And yet someone, always, is left
empty-handed. It's true for the owner of that sentence. And it's true
for you."

I felt a chill hearing that. I had never once thought, until that
moment, that the empty hands at the corner and Mena's shaking hand
might be the same story.

"…So does the Roll, and the judgment system Mena went through in that
city, do exactly the same thing?" I asked.

"…Not exactly the same," Mena said. "The Roll measures people. That
city measured guilt. They measure different things. But the manner of
measuring is alike. Both are extremely precise, both work hard never
to be wrong, and both, within that precision, leave someone out."

"…It's not on purpose, is it."

"…That's right," Mena said. "It isn't on purpose. Which is exactly why
it never gets fixed. If it were on purpose, you could change the mind
behind it. If it isn't, there's no mind to change at all. No one thinks
they've done anything wrong."

I thought of Lytron again, hearing that. He truly didn't think he'd
done anything wrong. Neither did the administrator. I already sensed,
dimly, that Lytron wasn't the only person in this city like that.

That was the first time I fully felt what this book was really
asking. Whether not breaking the law and being good are the same
thing. I had no answer to that question then. I don't have a complete
one now, either. But I would spend the years after that learning how
to go on living while holding that question in my hand.

"…So there's really nothing at all we can do?" I asked. My voice, even
to my own ear, carried something like resentment.

Mena shook her head.

"…It isn't that there's nothing we can do," Mena said. "It's that
there's nowhere to fix it. Those are different things."

"…What's different about them?"

"…If there's somewhere to fix it, you go there and fix it, and that's
the end," Mena said. "If there's nowhere to fix it, you have to do
something else. I don't know exactly what that is yet. But I know one
thing. Keep leaving it, exactly, accurately, behind. So that someone,
someday — someone with more power than I have — can come and see it."

"…When would that be?"

Mena thought for a moment. The moment felt long to me.

"…I don't know," Mena said. "I'm not the one who gets to decide that.
I'm only someone who leaves this accurately behind now, so that
whatever there is to see, when that day comes, is there to be seen."

"…What if it takes too long? What if that person never comes?"

Mena looked at me a moment. Sorrow and stubbornness sat together in
her face.

"…I'll still leave it behind," Mena said. "If I don't, then even if
that person comes, there'll be nothing for them to see. I don't know
when they'll come. But I don't want to send them away empty-handed
when they finally do."

Strangely, that made me think of the corner. Nothing in that field, and
yet I went out there every day all the same. Mena kept writing, knowing
she couldn't fix it. I understood, for the first time, that those two
things were the same kind of stubbornness.

I still wasn't satisfied with the answer. But at the same time, I knew
Mena wasn't saying it to soothe me. It was what she actually believed.

I stood up.

"…Where are you going?" Mena asked.

"…To the administrator," I said. "I'll ask him myself."

Mena didn't stop me. She only nodded.

"…Go ask him," Mena said. "I'm curious too. What he'll say."

The administrator was at his office by the gate. I hesitated a moment
outside, then went in.

The administrator recognized me. No surprise showed. He offered me a
seat, without ceremony.

"…What can I do for you."

"…Mena's own words are getting twisted into something bad, over and
over, in different cities," I said. "Seven times now. It's here in
this reputation field too. Can't this be fixed?"

The administrator tapped the screen a few times. It seemed he'd found
the entry I meant, and looked it over for a moment.

"…I've confirmed it," he said. "This entry came from another city's
Roll. We didn't generate it."

"…Can it be deleted, then?"

"…That isn't within our jurisdiction," he said. "We only reference the
data we receive. We have no authority to alter it. You'd need to
request that from the originating city."

"…I don't even know which city that is."

The administrator looked at me for a moment. For the first time,
something like it crossed that plain face. Not sympathy. More the
expression of a man hearing a question he'd already heard many times
before.

"…That happens often," the administrator said. "Data grows less
traceable the more hands it passes through. Our city has committed no
violation. We're presenting what we received, honestly."

I thought of what I'd heard at the corner, a few days before, hearing
that answer. We have nothing to hide. We've never broken the law. I
felt, in that moment, that though those words had come from different
mouths, they still sounded, somehow, like a single voice.

"…So this city has no responsibility at all?" I asked.

"…Not legally, no," the administrator said. "I'm not in a position to
say anything beyond that."

I had no answer to give him. The administrator wasn't a bad man. He was
simply a man who drew, exactly, the line between what he could say and
what he couldn't.

On my way out of the office, I ran into Lytron. He stopped when he saw
my face.

"…Something happen?" he asked.

I told him briefly what had happened with the administrator. Lytron
listened to it all and was quiet a moment.

"…I'd have said the same thing, in his place," he finally said.

I felt a flicker of anger at that.

"…You too?"

"…Don't be angry," he said. "I'm not saying the administrator's
