wrong. I know this city's systems. Tracing where data came from is
outside our authority. I don't know anyone who could do that either."

I felt it as evasion and asked again.

"…Then who's the most powerful person in this city? Can't even they do
it?"

Lytron thought a moment.

"…Not even my father can," he said. "That much I know for certain. No
matter how high our family's score is, we have no authority to touch
another city's Roll. Authority isn't something you buy with a score.
There's simply no such position at all."

"…So no one can do it?"

Lytron thought again.

"…Unless there's someone who can view every city's Roll," he said.
"But there's no such person. Each city manages only its own Roll. They
receive what's sent to them, but no one holds the authority to look
into another city's Roll."

"…What about Mena? Mena goes between cities."

Lytron looked at me for a moment at that.

"…Mena has no authority either," he said. "As far as I understand it,
Mena is someone who watches, not someone who fixes."

I found that strangely exact. Someone who watches, and someone who
fixes. That was the first time I heard that distinction stated so
plainly.

That day I understood that Lytron hadn't meant it unkindly. He was
simply someone who knew this city's structure precisely. And I learned,
that day, that the more precisely someone knew it, the faster they
arrived at a dead end in front of a question like this.

I thought of the field for a moment. And what Lytron had said the day
before came back to me. He'd said something like the administrator too.
That the man hadn't been wrong either. That it wasn't his.

That was the first time I felt that everyone I'd met in this city —
the administrator, Mena, even Lytron — stood, in the end, before the
same wall. No law broken. And yet not good. I felt, that day, for the
first time, that this wall was the hardest wall in the whole book. As
if I had reached out and touched it with my own hand.

Mena had begun putting her papers back in order. Her hands were
steadier than yesterday's.

"…Xene," Mena said. "I've carried this question for a very long time.
Then why not fix it. I think a person who asks that question is better
off than one who doesn't. Not stopping that question is the one
resistance I have left."

"…Resistance?"

"…Yes," Mena said. "Not fixing it, but also not accepting it as
something natural. I believe there's a very important difference
between those two."

I held onto those words for a long time.

That evening, I told Pikra everything that had happened that day. The
administrator, Lytron, what Mena had said.

Pikra listened all the way through and was quiet for a long time.

"…Aren't you angry?" I asked. "Everyone keeps saying it isn't their
fault."

"…I'm angry," Pikra said. "But Xene, I spent that anger a long time
ago."

"…What do you mean, spent it?"

Pikra set down what she'd been holding.

"…When I was young, I asked the same thing you're asking," Pikra
said. "Then why not fix it. I don't remember who I was talking to
then. But I remember what they said back. 'There are wrongs you don't
even know who to ask about.'"

I was startled at that.

"…You heard the same thing."

"…I did," Pikra said. "At the time, it sounded like an excuse. Now it
doesn't. Now I think it was just a plain statement of how this world
is actually built."

"…Then what do we do? Just put up with it?"

Pikra shook her head.

"…Putting up with it and going on living are different things,"
Pikra said. "I don't tell you to put up with it. I tell you to go on
living. You'll learn the difference between those two, someday."

"…What was he like?" I asked. The question came out before I meant
it to. "Did he carry something he couldn't fix, too?"

Pikra's hands stopped. She looked at me for a long time.

"…He was a little different," Pikra said. "When he saw something he
couldn't fix, instead of getting angry, he changed whatever he could
reach with his own hands. He couldn't change the whole world, so he
gathered his own little plot of field a little differently. Left
something behind, spilled something."

Hearing that, something ached in my chest. Longing for that man came
over me again.

"…Could I live that way too?" I asked.

Pikra smiled for the first time. A real smile.

"…You already are," Pikra said. "Going out to the corner every day —
that's exactly it."

I lay in bed that night and thought of Pikra's smile again. A real
smile. Only then did I realize how long it had been since I'd seen
one.

I thought about it long into the night. Putting up with it, and going
on living. I couldn't quite grasp the difference then. I know a little
of it now. Putting up with it is doing nothing and simply enduring.
Going on living is doing today's work while still carrying the thing
you can't fix. Mena arranged her papers again that day. Pikra worked
her stalks again that day. I went out to the field again that day. None
of us fixed the wrong. And yet none of us stopped, either.

Since that day, I've asked myself this question, over and over. Then
why not fix it. I still haven't found a clean answer to it. But I know
now what it means not to stop asking. That's the thing Mena taught me
that has stayed with me the longest.

I fell asleep that night having decided to ask Mena a different
question the next day. Not how to fix things, but how to leave things
behind. I had no idea, then, how far that question would take me.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. Learning

_Previously_ — Mena said there was nowhere to fix it. Leaving it
accurately behind was the only resistance left to her. That night,
Xene decided to learn how to leave things behind, instead of how to
fix them.

The next morning, I went to Mena with a different question.

I'd tossed and turned over that question half the night, revising it
again and again. First I thought I'd say, "I want to become a
recorder too." Too grand. Then, "Teach me how to do that." Also
somehow awkward. In the end I opened the archive door with nothing
polished at all.

"…Mena," I said. "Can you teach me how to write things down?"

Mena's hand stopped. She looked at me for a long moment. I felt
something being weighed in that look. Not weighing me — weighing, I
think, how heavy this question was for her to receive. The case on
the table still sat closed today. I guessed that the seven sentences
inside it had shaped, in some way, how she was receiving this
question.

"…Why?" Mena asked.

I couldn't answer right away. I hadn't prepared a reason.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "It just feels like what you do is
the most necessary thing in the world. But you won't stay in this
city much longer. If you leave, it feels like no one here will be
writing anything down at all."

Mena closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them, something had
been decided in her face.

"…All right," Mena said. "I'll teach you. But it isn't something
you learn in a day. Today I'll teach you the most important thing."

Mena handed me a sheet of paper and an old pen.

"…First rule," Mena said. "Write only what you've seen. Not what you
haven't."

"…Isn't that obvious?"

"…It's obvious, and it's the one people break most," Mena said.
"When people see something, without realizing it, they attach a story
to it afterward. That person must be angry. That person must be sad.
But that isn't what you saw. It's what you guessed."

"…So can I never guess at all?"

"…You can guess. Just keep it separate when you write it down," Mena
said. "'That person's face was scowling' — that's what you saw. 'That
person was angry' — that's a guess. I always attach 'it seemed to me'
or 'I guessed' when I write a guess. Otherwise, whoever reads it later
will take the guess for a fact."

I wrote that down on the paper. My hand was slow. Mena waited.

"…Second rule," Mena said. "Write down when you don't know. Say you
don't know."

"…Isn't that the same as the first?"

"…Similar, but different," Mena said. "The first is separating what
you saw from what you guessed. The second is knowing when to leave
something blank because you know nothing at all. People can't stand a
blank. They want to fill it with something. I guard against that
temptation more than anything else."

Mena pulled out the sheet I'd seen before — the one with no name, only
a case number.

"…Remember this?" Mena asked. "The one where there was no name, only
a case number."

"…Yes."

"…I could have guessed at his name," Mena said. "If I'd dug through
records from around the same period, a few likely names would have
come up. I didn't. If I wrote a guessed name, someone later might take
it for the real one and copy it further."

That was the first time I understood how heavy those two syllables,
"unknown," could be. Until then I had thought not knowing something
was a thing to be ashamed of. Mena taught me it was a thing to
protect.

"…Third rule," Mena said. "Write the action. Don't write the heart."

I thought at once of the sentences I'd seen the day before.

"…Like 'no remorse'?" I asked.

Mena nodded. I felt a small pride at having found the question myself.

"…That's right," Mena said. "Whether he repented or didn't — that
isn't visible to me. I've never once gone inside another person. I
only see what they said, what they did. And that's all I write."

"…But people keep wanting to know the heart."

"…They do," Mena said. "I do too. But if you write down what you want
to know, it stops being a record. It becomes a wish. Writing down what
you'd like to believe."

I felt that distinction was subtle, and yet, once I'd grasped it, very
clear.

"…Fourth," Mena said. "Don't write everything down."

I was startled at that. Everything until now had been about writing
exactly, in detail. This was the opposite.

"…Why?"

"…There's what's worth writing and what isn't," Mena said. "I don't
write down what won't be forgotten even if I don't write it. I write
down only what would stop having happened, if it were forgotten."

I remembered what Mena had said before, when I'd asked why she wrote.
Her answer, then.

"…Because if it's forgotten, it never happened," I said, echoing it.

Mena looked at me. A little surprised.

"…You remembered that."

"…I've been thinking about it ever since," I said.

Mena smiled a little.

"…That's the bottom of every rule I have," Mena said. "All the other
rules come out of that one. Writing only what I saw, writing that I
don't know when I don't, writing action instead of heart — all of it
is so nothing gets forgotten. If you're not exact, something worse than
forgetting happens. Being remembered wrong."

"…Fifth," Mena said. "Write on paper. Not on a screen."

I found that strange. Until then I'd only known paper as slower and
more inconvenient than a screen.

"…A screen's faster," I said. "The Roll does everything on a screen
too."

"…That's the problem," Mena said. "Anything written on a screen, if
someone decides to, can be changed without anyone knowing. Paper isn't
like that. Once something's written on paper, erasing it leaves a
trace. Scrape marks, overwriting. I like that trace. It's honest."

"…What if the paper's destroyed? Burned, or soaked through?"

"…Then the record is lost," Mena said. "I'm afraid of that too. But I
think it's better for something to disappear entirely than to be
secretly changed. You can know a thing that's gone is gone. Something
changed in secret, you never even know was changed."

I thought of the smooth typeface I'd seen on the screen the day
before. Flawless letters, without a single blemish. That flawlessness
looked different to me now. Being clean didn't mean no hand had ever
touched it. It might mean, instead, that whatever hand had touched it
left no trace behind.

Mena pointed to the paper in front of me.

"…Try writing something," Mena said. "Anything from today."

I hesitated a moment, then picked up the pen. I thought of what Pikra
had said that morning.

I wrote:

"Pikra seemed angry this morning."

Mena read the sentence and shook her head.

"…This is a guess," Mena said. "What did you see, to make you say
'seemed angry'?"

I thought again.

"Pikra said only three words this morning. Fewer than usual."

Mena read that one again. This time she nodded.

"…This is good," Mena said. "No one can read this differently. That
she said only three words is a fact. Whether that was because she was
angry, or tired, or simply had nothing to say — that's for the reader
to decide. I don't do that deciding for them."

I looked at the sentence again. Even though I'd written it, it felt far
more honest than the first one had.

"…Write one more," Mena said. "Something harder, this time. About
someone who matters to you."

I hesitated, then thought of Lytron. I wrote:

"Lytron is not a bad person."

Mena read that one and said nothing for a moment.

"…Is this a guess too?" I asked first.

"…No, this is a different problem," Mena said. "This is a judgment.
Whether he's bad or not isn't something you get to decide, Xene.
That's for the reader."

I felt a small unfairness at that.

"…But I really do think that."

"…Thinking it is your own business," Mena said. "But if you write it
into this record as a fact, whoever reads it will take it as a fact.
You can write what he did. Leave out whether it was bad or not."

I wrote again.

"Lytron saw me empty-handed at the corner, and came to check on me
more often than others did."

Mena read it and nodded.

"…This is much better," Mena said. "Whoever reads this sentence can
decide for themselves whether he's a bad person or not. You don't
decide it for them."

I felt something strange in that moment. Once the judgment was taken
out, more showed through, not less. He isn't a bad person said only
one thing. Came to check on me more often said far more about who
Lytron was. I read the sentence over several times. Each time, I saw
something slightly different. First I saw kindness in it. Then I
thought — maybe it was only curiosity. I felt that letting those
several layers of possibility stand, rather than covering them over
with one judgment, was in fact more honest.

That day I learned, for the first time, that judgment actually hides
a person. Calling someone good or bad locks that person inside a
single word. Writing the action as it was leaves them complicated
still. I understood, that day, for the first time, that leaving that
complication in place might be the most honest way to treat someone.

"…Mena," I asked. "What's the use of writing this way? It could get
changed somewhere else anyway."

Mena looked at me for a moment.

"…Even so, what it was at the start stays there," Mena said. "I told
you yesterday. You have to leave behind what was right at the start,
so someone can compare it later. What you compare against is exactly
this kind of exact sentence. A loosely written sentence has no power
to be compared against."

I looked again at the paper in my hand. A short single line. And yet
there was a different weight to it than there had been a moment
before.

That was the day I first learned that this whole record would come to
be written in the manner I learned that day. I didn't know, then, that
it would become a book. I only started forming the habit of setting
down each day this way.

"…Mena, why teach me?" I asked. "You could teach anyone else."

Mena looked out the window for a moment.

"…I taught this to one other person, a long time ago," Mena said. "She
asked me something similar then."

"…What is that person doing now?"

"…Writing, I'd guess," Mena said. There was something warm in her
voice. "I haven't seen her in a long time. But I believe she's still
writing the way I taught her."

"…Will I become that too, someday?"

Mena looked at me for a long moment.

"…That's up to you," Mena said. "I can only teach. Continuing to write
is up to the one who learned."

"…Wasn't it hard? Passing this on to someone else?"

Mena thought a moment.

"…It was hard," Mena said. "I've done this work alone a long time.
Handing it to someone means letting go of what I've been holding. I
was afraid of that. Afraid of handing it over wrong. Afraid I'd taught
it the wrong way."

"…Then why did you hand it over?"

"…If I don't, it ends with me," Mena said. "That frightened me more.
If I died, everything I'd seen would disappear along with me. I
couldn't bear that."

For the first time, I fully understood why Mena was teaching this, in
a strange city, to a stranger like me. It wasn't kindness. It was
closer to a struggle to survive. From that day on, I saw Mena a little
differently. Behind that calm face was someone just as desperate as I
was.

I didn't know, then, who that person from long ago was. Later I would
hear the name. For now I'll only set down the expression on Mena's
face when she spoke of that name. Half longing, half pride.

That evening, for the first time, I wrote out the day with my own
hand. It was short. Only a few lines. But it took far longer to write
than I expected. I revised a single word several times.

Pikra saw and asked.

"…What are you doing?"

"…Practicing what I learned," I said. "Mena taught me."

Pikra glanced at the paper. I didn't hide it from her.

"…What's different about this? Isn't it just a diary?"

"…It's different," I said. "I don't write my feelings here. I write
only what happened."

Pikra looked at the paper a moment and nodded.

"…That sounds harder," Pikra said. "Leaving your feelings out."

While Pikra worked her stalks beside me, I recited aloud, one by one,
what I'd learned that day.

"…Write only what you saw. Write that you don't know when you don't.
Write action, not the heart. Don't write everything. Write on paper."

Pikra listened without stopping her hands.

"…That's five," Pikra said. "Did you memorize them all?"

"…I'm not used to them yet," I said. "But I feel like I shouldn't
forget them."

Pikra set down the stalks for a moment.

"…Say them to me again sometime," Pikra said. "So I don't forget
either."

On my way to draw water, I stopped by the corner for a moment. He was
there.

"…What did you learn today?" he asked first. That was unusual.

"…How to write things down," I said. "Mena taught me."

He stopped his hands and looked at me. Something glad passed through
his eyes.

"…Good," he said.

"…Why is it good?"

"…Someone has to keep doing it," he said. "Mena will leave this city
someday too."

I was a little surprised at that.

"…How do you know?"

"…All recorders are like that," he said. "They never stay in one
place long. That's their work."

I felt an unexpected sadness at that. It wasn't that I hadn't already
thought Mena wouldn't stay in this city forever. But hearing it said
outright felt different.

"…Then when Mena leaves, will I have to write alone?"

He nodded. He didn't try to console me. Somehow that was a comfort
itself.

"…You won't be doing it alone," he said. "If what's written stays
behind, that isn't doing it alone."

I didn't fully understand that, that day. I do now. Even in this
moment, I'm not writing alone. The five rules Mena left me are still
holding my hand.

That evening, I wrote out the day with my own hand for the first
time. It was short. Only a few lines. But it took far longer to write
than I expected. I revised a single word several times.

Pikra saw and asked.

"…What are you doing?"

"…Practicing what I learned," I said. "Mena taught me."

Pikra glanced at the paper. I didn't hide it from her.

"…What's different about this? Isn't it just a diary?"

"…It's different," I said. "I don't write my feelings here. I write
only what happened."

Pikra looked at the paper a moment and nodded.

"…That sounds harder," Pikra said. "Leaving your feelings out."

"…It is. My feelings keep jumping out first," I said. "Crossing that
out and starting over takes the longest."

"…Maybe I should try it too," Pikra said, half joking. But something
sincere seemed folded in under the joke.

"…Try it. I'll teach you. It isn't hard."

Pikra laughed. A real laugh, out loud. I thought it had been a long
time since I'd heard that laugh.

"…You, teaching me?" Pikra said. "The world's really changed."

"…Maybe Mena learned from her own student once too."

Pikra laughed even harder at that.

"…Keep at it, anyway," Pikra said. "I want you to keep doing this."

"…Why?"

Pikra looked at the hearth fire for a moment.

"…No one's ever written down exactly what I did, my whole life," Pikra
said. "Everyone just guessed at me, however they liked. A sad woman. A
pitiable woman. But no one ever wrote down what I actually did. I want
you to be the one who does that."

Something caught in my throat at that. I couldn't answer. I only
nodded.

I read that paper over many times that night. Short sentences. I had
no idea, then, how important those sentences would become to someone,
much later, much further on. I had only left the day behind, exactly
as it had happened, the way Mena taught me.

I tried to write every sentence of this book by the five rules I
learned that day. Whether I succeeded, I can't say. But I can say, at
least, that I tried.

And I'll set down one more thing. That night, for the first time, I
gave this bundle of paper a name. Nothing grand. I only wrote, at the
top, "Starting today." I think of that as the true first line of this
book. Every chapter I've written since has grown out of those two
words.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. Where There Is Always a Little More

_Previously_ — Mena taught Xene the five rules of writing: write
only what you've seen, write that you don't know when you don't,
write action and not the heart, don't write everything, write on
paper. That night, Xene wrote down a day with her own hand for the
first time.

From that day on, I wrote a few lines every evening.

At first I didn't know what to write, so I wrote anything at all.
Today's weather. Three sentences Pikra had said that day. How much I'd
gleaned at the corner. I tried to record that last one exactly — not
"gleaned a lot," but "a palmful of three," that kind of thing. The way
Mena had taught me.

I didn't know, then, what this habit would grow into. I only wrote so
as not to forget. But writing exactly, it turned out, was something
that eventually made me see things I'd been passing over without
knowing it.

I kept that paper behind a wooden frame on one side of the cabin. I
took it out from the same spot every night. The first few days, the
sentences ran long. Soon they shortened. Just as Mena had said, once I
kept only what was worth keeping, that happened on its own.

I never dated the page. Instead, I put the single surest thing about
that day first. It rained, or it didn't. Pikra coughed, or she didn't.
Something was gleaned at the corner, or nothing was. I had no idea,
then, that this order would become, later on, the thread by which I
found my way back into a day. At the time it was only habit.

I keep that old bundle of pages beside me now, writing this book.
Some pages are stained; on some the ink has run, barely legible. But
without those pages, I couldn't have written this thirty-second
chapter. Memory alone would never have given me a sentence like "over
two days I confirmed it." Memory blurs things into "it happened
several times."

Damia laughed the first time she saw the paper.

"…What are you writing so hard at?" Damia asked. We were working side
by side at the machine.

"…What happened that day," I said.

"…A diary?"

"…Similar, but different," I said. "I don't write my feelings. Just
what happened."

Damia tilted her head at that.

"…What's fun about that, if there's no feeling in it?"

I couldn't answer right away. I'd thought the same thing myself, at
first. But writing it for a few days, I began to see things that only
came into view once the feeling was left out.

"…I'm not writing it for fun," I finally said.

Damia didn't press further. She just gave a shrug and went back to her
own work. I wasn't hurt by that. I already knew, by then, that some
people would understand this and some wouldn't, and that neither side
was wrong for it.

Within the week, I noticed something strange.

It was the numbers. Setting side by side the days I'd written "a
palmful of three" against the days I'd written "a palmful of five," I
saw that the days with five always came from the same spot. His spot.

At first I thought it was coincidence. The field was wide, and the
machines' paths shifted a little from day to day. It wasn't strange
for one side to leave a little more some days, the other side other
days.

But writing it down for several more days, it was too consistent to be
coincidence. I flipped back through the pages. A week's worth of
entries.

Monday: his spot, a palmful of five.
Tuesday: the other side, a palmful of two.
Wednesday: his spot, a palmful of four.
Thursday: the day he didn't come, and his spot, too, a palmful of
two.

My hand stopped at that last line. On the day he wasn't there, nothing
extra was left at his spot either.

I thought back, at this point, to something further back — Chapter
19, "The One Who Gleans Beside Me." I had already half-noticed this
once, back then. I'd taken it for coincidence and let it go. Why hadn't
I asked further, then? Thinking of it now, the answer is simple. Back
then I didn't yet know how to write exactly. Noticing something by
instinct and confirming it with numbers are different things. Instinct
can be let go of. A number can't.

I decided to test it.

The next day, I went early and sat at the spot he always took. He came
a little later than usual, and, seeming unconcerned, went and sat
somewhere else — a few steps from where I'd been, not his usual spot.

That evening, I counted the palmfuls. The place I'd sat, his old spot,
was the same as always. But the new spot he'd taken that day — the
place where he'd actually been — had more than usual.

I was certain, in that moment. It wasn't the place that was good. It
was the place he was in that became good.

I didn't want to be certain from just one day. I tried the same thing
the next day. This time he took his seat first, and I went and sat far
off to the side, somewhere he wouldn't be likely to sit. The result was
the same. Where I'd been was no different from usual. Where he'd been
had a little more than usual.

That night I wrote it in my paper.

"Wherever he sits, that spot always leaves a little more than the
others, even when the spot itself changes. Confirmed over two days."

I read that sentence twice. I hadn't mixed in a single guess, and yet
what it said was entirely clear.

On the second day I tested it, Lytron happened to pass by on patrol.
He stopped and scanned the field — the machines' path, the line at the
scales, the placement of the workers. The same routine as always.

"…Anything unusual today?" he asked.

"…No," I said.

I nearly laughed, right then. Just a few steps away, I was running my
own experiment, and none of it registered with him at all. He saw only
the numbers he always saw, the routes he always saw. What I was
watching that day wasn't numbers. It was a person. The same field, and
he and I were looking at entirely different things.

I found that fact frightening for the first time, right then. There
was so much to see in that field. And yet everyone saw only what they'd
been trained to see. Lytron had been trained to see numbers. I was
only just then learning to see a person exactly.

By the end of that week, I told Mena what I'd found.

"…I found something strange," I said. "There's always a little more
at his spot. I confirmed it over two days."

Mena stopped and looked at me.

"…Did you ask him?" Mena asked.

"…No. Not yet."

"…If you ask," Mena said, "an answer might not come. Know that going
in."

"…Why?"

Mena thought a moment.

"…Some answers aren't a matter of can't. They're a matter of won't,"
Mena said. "I've worked out that difference over a long time. Someone
who can't answer, their face shakes. Someone who won't, doesn't shake.
They just go still."

I didn't fully understand that, then. A few days later, at the ditch,
I would see exactly what she meant.

That night before sleep, I thought of something from long ago.

My husband, teaching me to swing the sickle, the first autumn. I was
scolded then for reaping too cleanly. "What you spill isn't wasted,"
he'd said. "It's left behind."

I'd taken that as a joke, at the time. But since finding that a little
more always turned up where he sat, that memory kept overlapping,
strangely, with this. What my husband had spilled, and what this man
left behind. I couldn't be sure, at the time, whether they were the
same thing.

I never wrote that thought down on paper that night. It was a guess.
But now, setting it down in this book, I've chosen to keep the guess
rather than erase it. This is a thought I actually had, that night.
Mena's rule was to separate fact from guess — not to pretend a guess
never existed.

I hesitated for several more days.

Mena had taught me not to write everything. She'd never taught me not
to ask. I knew those were different things. Writing, and asking.

Even now, I'm not entirely sure why I hesitated so long. Maybe I
wanted to hear the answer, and at the same time didn't want to. Some
questions, once answered, you can't go back from. I think I already
knew that, on some level.

Once, I sat down beside him meaning to ask, and in the end said
nothing at all, just sifting the dirt. That night I wrote in my paper,
"Still didn't ask, today." A short line. But it took longer to write
than any other line I'd written.

In the end, one evening, I followed him down to the ditch, where he'd
gone to wash his hands, and asked.

"…Why does a little more always turn up at your spot?"

I asked it plainly, without dressing it up. Days I'd spent choosing the
question, and what came out, in the end, was the shortest sentence of
all.

He stopped washing his hands and looked at me.

It wasn't an angry face. I thought I knew that face well. But that
day it was none of the faces I knew. He didn't smile. That struck me
as strange. Usually, when a question was hard to answer, he smiled.
That day he didn't.

He kept shaking the water from his hands for a long while. He didn't
seem to be preparing an answer. He was just shaking off water. Only
the sound of the ditch went on for a time.

"…I don't know," he said at last.

"…What does 'I don't know' mean?"

"…It means I'm not sure either," he said.

That didn't sound like a lie to me. But it didn't feel like the whole
of it, either.

"…So did I just imagine it, then?" I asked.

He looked at me for a moment. Then turned his eyes back to the ditch.

"…I didn't say you imagined it," he said.

I thought about pressing further. But his shoulders had gone a little
stiffer than usual. Not from anger. I couldn't put a name to what it
was, then.

"…You don't have to answer if you don't want to," I said.

"…It isn't that I don't want to," he said. "There's just nothing to
say."

I understood there was nothing more I could draw out of that answer.

He wasn't angry, and he didn't leave. He just finished washing his
hands and, as always, picked up his basket and walked back toward the
field. Watching him go, I felt a strange kind of relief. Watching
someone avoid an answer was somehow easier to bear than hearing a lie.

That evening I wrote down the question and the answer exactly as they
were. What his face had looked like, how many words he'd said. I
wanted to write "he was hiding something." I wrote it down, and crossed
it out, several times. It was a guess. In the end, I only left this:

"He said, 'I don't know.' He also said, 'I didn't say you imagined
it.' He didn't get angry. He didn't smile."

Writing that sentence, I felt, for the first time in my body, why
Mena's fifth rule mattered. Written down on paper, I could take that
face back out and look at it again, later. Memory faded. Paper didn't.

That night, Pikra saw me writing and asked.

"…Him again?"

"…Yes," I said. "There's always a little more where he sits. I asked
him. He wouldn't answer."

Pikra set down the stalks she was working.

"…Didn't he get angry?"

"…No. He didn't smile either."

Something in that made Pikra's face turn thoughtful.

"…That's strange," Pikra said. "I've seen him smile plenty of times."

"…That's why it seemed strange to me too."

"…Someone who smiles their way past things is covering something up
with the smile," Pikra said. "But not smiling means—"

Pikra stopped there.

"…What does it mean?"

"…Either there's nothing to cover, or there's nothing they can cover
it with," Pikra said. "One or the other. I don't know which."

I held onto that a long time.

I still didn't know, that night, why a little more always turned up
at his spot. I can't say I fully know it even now. But since that day,
I've come to tell a little more clearly when he was answering and when
he was avoiding. It was a small kind of learning. But that small
learning became, later, the eye that let me recognize something much
larger.

By that point I had already been watching two different kinds of
silence for a while. One was the silence Lytron showed in Chapter 16,
talking about his family — not silence from having nothing to say, but
from having no need to say it. The other was the silence he showed at
the ditch that day — silence with something to say, and a choice not
to say it. I hadn't thought to set those two side by side, back then.
Only in writing this book do I come to know how different a weight
those two silences carried.

The next morning, I went out to the corner as usual. He was there in
his usual spot too. As if yesterday had been nothing at all, he gave a
single nod, and I nodded back. We sat side by side, as always, sifting
the dirt.

I found that calm strangely welcome. Some people, once you ask them
something awkward, avoid you afterward. He didn't. He hadn't given me
an answer, but he hadn't taken away my place beside him either.

That evening I wrote this down too. "He came to the same spot the day
after the question, as always." I think this one line, in its way,
says more than that day's "I don't know" did. Avoiding an answer and
avoiding a person are different things. He only ever did the first.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. Lytron and Mena

_Previously_ — Xene confirmed with numbers that a little more always
turned up wherever he sat. When she asked him why, he neither got
angry nor smiled — he only said, "I don't know."

This chapter, too, is a story I didn't live through myself.

I wasn't there for it. This is pieced together from what Lytron told
me himself, much later, and from a few things Mena confirmed. I wrote
this chapter in the same spirit I wrote Chapters 16 and 21. Even
something I didn't witness, if I carry it over without lying, isn't a
story I've invented.

By then, Lytron had already said, three separate times, that he meant
to go see Mena.

Once to me, twice to himself. I asked him afterward why he kept putting
it off.

"…What was holding you back?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "It just felt like, if I went, I'd learn
something."

"…Why was that so unwelcome?"

"…It isn't that it was unwelcome," he said. "It's that I always
calculate before I know something. But in front of that woman, I had
no idea what there even was to calculate."

In the end, one afternoon, his patrol having ended early, he found his
steps turning toward the archive. There hadn't been any particular
resolve behind it, he said. His feet had simply gone that way that
day.

The archive door was open.

Mena sat at the table, sorting through paper. Lytron hesitated in the
doorway for a moment.

"…Excuse me," he said.

Mena looked up. No surprise showed.

"…Lytron," Mena said. "Xene's told me about you."

Lytron told me, later, that he hadn't given much thought, in that
moment, to already being a known quantity to someone. He was used to
his own name arriving somewhere before he did.

"…I hear my name is in here somewhere," he said. "May I see it?"

"…Yes," Mena said. That was all.

When I heard about this part from Lytron, I could tell he'd been
briefly thrown off by that short answer. He'd expected something to
follow that single word — who are you, why do you want to see it,
which household are you from. Nothing followed at all.

Mena took a thin sheet of paper from a drawer and handed it to him.

Lytron took the page and read it for a long while. His own name, his
family's score, three generations of harvest-management history. And
beneath all of it, a single short line.

"Zone management assistant. No change in score history."

That was all.

"…Is this all?" he asked.

"…Yes," Mena said.

"…I expected something more."

Mena looked at him for a moment.

"…What did you expect?" Mena asked.

He couldn't answer that right away, he told me later.

"…I don't know. I thought it would say something about me, good or
bad."

"…This is saying something about you," Mena said. "You've had no
change. I've written that down, exactly as it is."

Lytron set the page down on the table, and his eyes drifted to the
papers stacked beside it. Names showed on the pages. One of them caught
his eye.

"…Isn't this Xene's name?" he asked.

Mena nodded.

"…Can I see it?"

"…That one, no," Mena said. It was the first real refusal she'd given
him. "That belongs to her to show, if she chooses to show it. I won't
show it for her."

Lytron nodded at that. He told me, later, "I felt a little ashamed, in
that moment. I'd brought up your name first, and now that it was
actually your own to keep, I'd asked to see it without thinking twice.
I'd never questioned looking at my own — and there I was, asking to
see someone else's without a second thought."

Hearing this part, I was struck that he'd even noticed that shame in
himself. The Lytron of Chapter 16 probably wouldn't have felt anything
about why that question shouldn't be asked. He'd already been
changing, in those weeks, in small ways he himself hadn't noticed
yet.

A leather case sat on one side of the table, its lid closed.

"…What's that?" Lytron asked.

Mena glanced toward it for a moment.

"…Things I wrote a long time ago," Mena said.

"…May I see?"

"…That one, no either," Mena said.

This time Lytron didn't ask why. He explained it to me, later, this
way: "Looking at her face, I could tell — it wasn't that it was
forbidden to me. It was a door not yet open even to her. I didn't know
I could sense something like that. That day was the first time I
could."

Lytron studied that sentence for a long time.

"…Is no change a good thing?" he asked.

"…I don't know," Mena said. "I'm not someone who writes good or bad."

"…Then why do you do this work?"

Mena's hand paused.

"…To leave behind, accurately, what someone did and didn't go
through," Mena said. "No change, too, is a very exact record, for
some people."

I heard this part from Lytron many times over, afterward. He said he
hadn't fully understood it, that day. Now I think it was a sentence
that quietly foretold the next chapter. But the Lytron standing there
that day only felt a small sting — that nothing at all had been
written beside his own name.

He told me, later, that he had tried to do something, right there.

That was simply who he was. Someone who read what another person
wanted from him first, and gave it. He assumed Mena would want to know
more about his family. So he offered it up himself.

"…My family has managed this field for three generations," he said.
"I can tell you more, if it helps. If it's useful for your record—"

"…That's all right," Mena said. "I'll ask if I need to."

Lytron's words trailed off there. He told me he felt as if he'd walked
into something, from that small refusal.

"…I felt something strange, right then," he said, later. "I've always
given people what they seemed to want from me. But she didn't take
what I offered. Not angrily. Just — she didn't need it."

He tried again.

"…If there's anything I can help with, let me know," he said. "My
family has some standing in this city. If you need more material—"

"…I don't," Mena said. Still short. "I don't need anything right now."

Lytron said that was the moment he first truly felt himself standing
somewhere he didn't know what to do.

Writing this, I found myself thinking back to Chapter 16 again. There
I'd described Lytron's world as one where he never had to wonder. Now
I have to add another sentence to that world. Lytron hadn't only lived
in a world where he never had to wonder — he'd lived, too, in a world
where he'd never once gone unasked-for. People always wanted something
from him. Respect, goodwill, a nod to his family's name. He was
skilled at giving those things. Mena wanted nothing.

"…Does this make you uncomfortable?" Mena asked.

Lytron said he was startled by the question. He hadn't known he was
showing discomfort.

"…Does it show?" he asked.

"…A little," Mena said. "People make that face, often, when they try
to give me something and I don't take it."

"…Why do you think they make that face?"

Mena thought a moment.

"…I'm not certain. I can guess," Mena said. "I guess people feel safe
when they give something. If it isn't taken, they lose the sense of
what they are."

Lytron had no answer to that, he told me.

He tried one last time, he said. He'd turned to leave, then turned back
and said,

"…Do you need any supplies? Paper, or food. There's some to spare at
our house."

Mena looked at him for a moment. Something different crossed her eyes
for the first time, Lytron said. Not irritation. More the look of
someone hearing a familiar question yet again.

"…That's kind of you," Mena said. "But I carry what I need. I've
traveled this way a long time."

"…Still, if there's ever—"

"…Lytron." Mena cut him off. The first time she'd used his name. "You
keep trying to give me things. Have you ever thought about why?"

Lytron said the question left him speechless. He walked out without
being able to answer it at all.

I want to pause here and set this down. When I first heard this
exchange from Lytron, I thought Mena was testing him on purpose. I
don't think that anymore. Mena tests no one. She simply looked, as she
always did, at the person in front of her, wanting nothing. Lytron's
discomfort didn't come from anything Mena did. It came from her doing
nothing at all.

Lytron told me he'd walked out of the archive that day with something
unresolved still hanging on him. Normally that wasn't like him at all.
He always left an errand knowing exactly how it had ended. That day
wasn't like that.

"…I didn't even know what the problem was," he said, later. "I'd just
gone to see what was written beside my name. I came out carrying
something completely different."

He told me he turned that question over the whole walk home. Have you
ever thought about why.

He stopped for a moment in front of his own gate. He could hear the
sound of water running inside, as always — water that simply ran, for
show, to dress up the yard.

That evening at dinner, his father asked the question he always asked.

"…Anything unusual today."

"…No," he said.

"…Good."

Lytron said that, hearing that "good," he thought for the first time
about what it took for granted. It always came when he'd kept
something, when he hadn't lost anything. His father hadn't wanted
nothing from him. His father had always wanted exactly one thing. That
the number stay the same. That one thing had felt so obvious, for so
long, that Lytron had never even recognized it as a demand.

Hearing him describe this dinner, I understood, at last, why what he'd
felt at the archive that day had struck him as so foreign. In his own
house, everyone — even the silences — wanted something of him. In
Mena's room, no one wanted anything at all. He had never once, in his
whole life, had the chance to feel that difference.

That evening, I ran into him by chance at the corner. He wasn't on
patrol. He was simply standing a long while at the edge of the field.

"…Is something wrong?" I asked.

"…No," he said. But his voice wasn't its usual self.

"…Did you see Mena?" I asked.

He looked at me, a little startled.

"…How did you know?"

"…Your face looks strange."

He said nothing for a while, looking out at the field. The evening
light lay low, and the stubble cast long shadows.

"…Is Mena a little frightening?" I asked. I'd meant it as a joke, but
he took the question seriously.

"…Not frightening," he said. "But none of the things I usually do work
in front of her. I find that unfamiliar."

He started to smile, and the smile wouldn't quite come.

"…She didn't ask me for a single thing," he said. I didn't fully
understand what he meant, then.

"…Why is that strange?"

Lytron was quiet a long while before answering.

"…I don't know," he said. "But it's the first time in my life. That no
one wanted anything from me."

I didn't think much of that, hearing it that day. I didn't understand
why Lytron was carrying those words with such weight. I had lived my
whole life never able to demand anything from anyone. It hadn't seemed
strange to me at all. But for him, going unasked-for was something he
was living for the first time in his life.

I set these two sentences side by side now and look at them a long
while. I went out to the corner every day because I could demand
nothing. He wavered, for the first time, because nothing was demanded
of him. The same city, the same evening, two people standing, each for
their own reason, in front of the same word. Demand.

I didn't know, then, what the discomfort he felt that day would come
to open up inside him, a few days later.

That night, writing this down, I found myself, for the first time,
careful about how I described Lytron.

I wanted to write, "Lytron was shaken." That would have been a guess.
I wrote instead only that his face had looked strange, that he'd
spoken differently than usual, that he'd used the phrase "the first
time in my life." Whether he'd really been shaken, he himself probably
didn't know that night.

Looking back now, I know. He was shaken. Only the shaking made no
sound. Outwardly, nothing at all happened that day. He ate dinner as
usual, heard "good" said to him as usual. I'll write this same sentence
several more times in this book. Outwardly, nothing happened. But this
time, inside that nothing happening, something was, for the first
time, quietly changing places.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. I Have Never Lost Anything

_Previously_ — Lytron met Mena. She wanted nothing from him. In front
of that fact, he was shaken for the first time in his life. "Have
you ever thought about why," Mena had asked him.

This chapter, too, is a story I didn't live through. But this time it's
a little different.

The two chapters before this one pieced together a day Lytron had
lived. This one pieces together thoughts he carried alone over several
days. I know this is a more delicate thing to do. A day is made of
events, and easy enough to confirm. A thought has no way to be
confirmed. I have filled this whole chapter only with what Lytron told
me himself, later, across several separate conversations. I know it's
possible he remembers it differently than it truly was. Even so, I
believe this much is exact: this is the memory he handed to me.

The day after meeting Mena, Lytron spent an ordinary day, same as
always.

He went on patrol, checked the numbers, wrote up his report. At dinner
that evening, his father asked, as he always did, "Anything unusual
today," and he answered, "No," and heard, "Good," in return.

But that night, he told me, he couldn't sleep.

"…It was strange," he said, later. "Normally I would have just gone to
sleep. But that question kept coming back to me. Have you ever thought
about why."

At first he'd tried to brush the question off lightly, the way he had
in Chapter 16. Carry something for one night, and when the next day
brought something to do, set it down. That had always been his way.

This time he didn't set it down.

I think this difference matters. In Chapter 16, he'd felt something
looking over my entry-approach history, and forgot it by the next day,
buried under some other report. This time he didn't forget. I thought
a long time about where that difference came from. The answer was
simple. That earlier question had been about me. This one was about
himself. A question about someone else, you can forget. A question
about yourself doesn't let go so easily.

For several days, he told me, he turned that same question over from
several angles in his own mind.

Why did I keep trying to give her something. At first he thought it
was simple kindness. But he soon knew it wasn't only that. In trying
to give Mena something, he'd also been trying to reassure himself of
something. He'd wanted confirmation that he was a useful person in
that room.

"…I'd always been in a position to give something," he said, later.
"I thought that was just my nature. But in that room, once I had
nothing left to give, I didn't even know who I was."

He followed that thought and began counting through his own life again
from the start.

He was a man who was quick with numbers. He was used to thinking in
them. So he laid his whole life out as figures and went through it,
one by one.

From the moment of his birth to now, his family's score had never once
gone down. A number his grandfather had built and his father had kept.
Not a bad year, not an accident, had ever touched that number. Not
when his brother went out on his own, not when he became a zone
management assistant — the number had stayed the same or climbed.

He tried to find some point where he had lost something. No matter how
far back he searched, nothing came up.

I want to pause here for a moment. I rewrote this passage more times
than any other in this book. My first draft read: "In the end he found
nothing. That was his problem." I struck that line. It was a judgment.
I chose instead to keep only what he actually gave me, word for word.

He said he thought back, too, on the house he'd grown up in.

The house I'd already described in Chapter 16 — so many rooms he'd
never once counted them. The water channel that ran through the yard
purely for decoration. Thinking of that channel, he remembered that on
that night described in Chapter 21, he had already, once, heard that
water differently.

"…That night I wondered, for the first time, where that water even
came from," he said. "And even so, afterward, I never once turned it
off. Wondering about something and actually doing something about it
are different things, it turns out."

He took that channel as one example and looked over his whole life
again through it. All the things that existed not because they were
needed, but simply because they'd always flowed. He tried to count how
many things like that filled his life. He gave up counting. There were
too many.

One evening, his brother came from another zone.

Not for any holiday. It was because their father had asked him to come
check in on how Lytron had been lately — something Lytron only learned
afterward. He hadn't known that, that day.

His brother spoke to him as easily as ever. "Just keep your numbers
straight. Everything else follows."

Lytron said that on hearing those words, for the first time, he asked
back.

"…Do you think that's true for everyone?"

His brother paused a moment at the question.

"…What are you talking about?"

"…Xene," he said. "She has no number to keep. She started at zero.
Does that hold true for her too?"

His brother couldn't answer. Instead he said,

"…You've been saying strange things lately."

Lytron said he didn't ask anything further after that. But later he
told me: "I knew, right then. My brother had never once been asked that
question. Neither had I. Both of us had lived our whole lives thinking
those words held true for everyone."

A few days after that, he went to see the administrator.

There was no particular business, he said. He just wanted to ask
something.

"…You've said the Roll has never broken the law," he said. "Does that
mean it's never taken anything from anyone, either?"

The administrator looked at him for a moment. The same plain
expression as always.

"…No," the administrator said. "The Roll takes nothing from anyone."

"…Then why is there nothing for some people?"

For the first time, the administrator paused before answering.

"…Never having taken something, and having given something — those are
two different statements," the administrator said. "The Roll only
calculates. Calculation is neither taking nor giving."

Lytron said he stood there a long while after hearing that.

Hearing this exchange later, an old scene came back to me. Something
Glyke had said in Chapter 1. If someone takes something from you, at
least you can say you were robbed. Just being given nothing at all —
there's nowhere even to say that. I'd heard those words outside, back
then. Lytron heard almost the same words, years later, in the middle of
a city, from a different mouth entirely. Setting these two scenes side
by side, I confirm again how simple this city's secret was from the
very start. Not taking and giving are different things. But to the one
on the receiving end, they feel exactly the same.

"…Do you remember the day it rained?" he asked me once. "The day you
filled a sack for the first time."

"…Yes," I said. It was the day I'd written down in Chapter 24.

"…I remember that day too. But I lost nothing that day," he said. "The
harvest was only delayed. Our family always keeps reserves for that.
One day's delay doesn't take anything away from us."

"…What does that mean?"

"…The same rain fell," he said. "For you it was the first sack you'd
ever earned. For me it was nothing at all. I'd never once thought
about that difference before."

From that night on, he started looking for something else. Not what
he'd lost, but a moment where he'd taken a risk.

He thought back to that night in Chapter 21. The loss-rate setting
screen. The night he'd opened the confirmation window and closed it
again. At the time, he'd felt himself standing in front of danger. But
thinking it over again, that hadn't been danger at all. As long as he
didn't press that button, he was in a position to lose nothing. Not
pressing it had always been the safe side. He had stood on that safe
side, and called it, to himself, a struggle.

"…I thought, at the time, that I was weighing something," he told me,
later. "But really, I wasn't weighing anything. I was just choosing
between two safe paths. Press it, and the family score drops. Don't,
and nothing happens. There was never a dangerous path for me to begin
with."

He spent several more days holding onto that thought.

Then one day, he thought back to a phrase he'd carried for a long
time. I haven't done anything wrong. The thing he'd said to himself
in Chapter 16.

He looked at that phrase again. It was true. He truly had done nothing
wrong. But that day, for the first time, he set another phrase beside
it.

I have never lost anything.

He said he read those two sentences over, back and forth, for a long
while. At first they seemed like two separate facts. One a proud fact,
one simply a lucky one. But at some point, he saw that they were,
actually, one and the same.

"…That day I understood, for the first time," he told me, later. He
spoke very slowly, choosing each word. "It wasn't that I hadn't done
wrong. It was that I never had to. Every time I made a choice, the
right one and the one that benefited me were always the same one. I
thought that was my character. It turns out it was my position."

I said nothing for a long while, hearing that. The sound of the
machines came faintly from the field. The sound felt louder to me that
day than it usually did.

"…What do you mean?" I finally managed.

"…Someone like you," he said, "loses something every time you choose
what's right. Every day. I never lost anything, choosing what was
right. So the fact that I've done nothing wrong isn't something I paid
a price for, the way you did. I was simply never tested."

Hearing that, I remembered, too, my husband's old words. What you
spill isn't wasted. It's left behind. I felt his words and Lytron's
were pointing, from different directions, at the same thing. My
husband, having little, had known what it meant to leave something
behind on purpose. Lytron, having so much, was only just then learning
that he had never once, truly, left anything behind at all.

I felt the temptation to bind these two into a single sentence. But
that would have been too easy a conclusion. I chose instead to lay
them only side by side. Not stitched together.

I write this moment more carefully than any other in this book.

I know that Lytron didn't say these words to punish himself. I found
no room to hate him, hearing them. He wasn't saying he'd been a bad
person. He was only seeing, for the first time, that his never having
been a bad person and his never having paid a price both came from the
same place.

I thought longest, of any chapter in this book, about how to write
this one. I didn't want this chapter to turn Lytron into someone bad.
Nor did I want it to let him off entirely. In the end I chose to carry
over exactly what he gave me. Both things were already inside it. He
had done nothing wrong. And he had just seen, for the first time, what
that absence of wrongdoing had been made of.

He did nothing right away, after that.

I once thought that strange. Having seen something so large, shouldn't
something have changed at once? He answered that question this way.

"…Seeing something large doesn't mean you can do something about it
right away," he said. "For those few days, I just carried the fact
around with me. It followed me everywhere. Looking at the field,
looking at the numbers, going home."

"…Was it hard?"

"…It was hard," he said. "But strangely, I didn't want to go back to
before."

Around that time, he said, he started staying alone in his office each
evening, opening that same screen from Chapter 21 again. The loss-rate
setting. 0.02 percent. The confirm button beside it.

"…You opened it again?" I asked.

"…Yes. A few times," he said. "But I looked at it differently than
before."

"…How differently?"

"…Before, I thought that button decided whether I'd lose something,"
he said. "Now I know. That button is my first chance to actually lose
something. I still haven't pressed it. But now I know why I haven't,
and it isn't the same reason as before."

I held onto that answer for a long time. Some kinds of knowing don't
move a person right away. They only settle inside, quietly, adding
weight. I wanted this book to set down the fact that Lytron carried
that weight alone, for several days, before anything else happened.

I want to end this chapter here — not as a chapter with an answer, but
as a chapter where the shape of a question changed completely. The
Lytron of Chapter 21 had asked, "If I press this, do I lose something."
The Lytron of Chapter 34 held a different question. Why have I never
once lost anything. I think the distance between those two questions
is the longest road he walks in this book. And he never took a single
step to cross it.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. Setting It Back

_Previously_ — Lytron saw that his never having done wrong and his
never having lost anything were the front and back of the same
fact. He had carried that weight alone for days.

Lytron told me about that morning much later, and very briefly.

"…Nothing special about that day," he said. "I just got up early, as
usual, and went to the office, as usual. And then I opened that screen
again."

"…And after that?"

"…I pressed it," he said.

That was the whole of his account. I think the brevity itself is more
exact than anything longer would have been. Some moments come closer
to a lie the longer they're told.

I felt the temptation, more than once, writing this chapter, to swell
that moment out — his hand trembling, a long hesitation, his eyes
closing and opening again. I chose not to write it that way. Lytron
never told me anything like that. He only said he pressed it. I choose
to believe that, exactly as he said it.

I know the days hidden behind that short answer. He'd carried that
weight alone through the whole of Chapter 34. I asked him, more than
once, what had been different that particular morning. Each time, he
answered a little differently.

Once he said, "I just didn't want to put it off any longer."

Another time: "What Mena had said kept staying with me. Have you ever
thought about why. That morning, for the first time, I understood that
knowing the answer and doing nothing about it would from then on just
be pretending not to know."

Another day still: "My father said 'good' again that day too. I heard
it, but this time I understood what it meant while I heard it. That was
the hardest part."

I want to note that these three answers came on three separate days.
I didn't want to stitch them into one tidy reason. A person's reasons
for deciding something usually aren't just one. I chose to leave all
of them standing.

The confirmation window came up. The same wording as the one in
Chapter 21. Change the loss-rate setting? Projected yield will be
adjusted accordingly.

Beneath it, a number. From 0.02 percent to 3 percent. The same screen,
the same figure he'd opened weeks before, one night. What had changed
wasn't the number. It was the person standing in front of it.

He said he checked that number once more. It wasn't a large figure. A
single decimal point had moved. But the drop in projected yield that
followed that small movement wasn't small at all.

He said he didn't look at that window for long.

He pressed Confirm.

I won't set that number down here either, for the same reason I
didn't set it down in Chapter 21. Writing the number turns this into
a matter of arithmetic. Much, little, worth bearing. I don't want this
chapter read that way. I'll only say this. The number was large. Large,
measured against everything Lytron's family had spent generations
keeping.

The confirmation message appeared. Setting changed.

That was all. No alarm sounded. No lock engaged. No request for a
superior's approval. The screen flickered once and moved on, without
concern, to the next item. The Roll accepted the change at once.

"…It was strange," Lytron told me, later. "I thought there'd be
something more. Some procedure to stop it. There wasn't. I pressed it,
and it just changed."

He said he left the office that day, and didn't go home right away.
Normally, he'd have spent the morning on patrol and then written his
report. That day he simply walked toward the field instead.

He watched the corner from a distance. He said he didn't go closer,
afraid something might change if he did.

"…What did you see, from there?" I asked.

"…You," he said. "Crouched, looking at something. I couldn't see your
face from that far. But your hands kept moving."

"…Did you see him too? The one who glean's beside me."

"…Yes," he said. "He didn't see me. I was too far off. But strangely,
what he was doing kept catching my eye. Just his hands moving, next to
you. I don't know why that stayed with me so long."

I felt something strange, hearing that. I hadn't known, that day, that
Lytron had even been there, or that he'd watched the two of us for so
long. Three of us in the same field, that same afternoon, and only one
of us had been watching the other two the whole time.

"…What were you thinking, watching me?"

He was quiet a long while before he answered.

"…Nothing came to me," he said. "I just watched. That's all there
was."

Hearing this scene, much later, I came to know something I'd never
known at all, that day. I'd thought I was crouched alone at the corner,
counting grains of fallen wheat. I wasn't. Someone was watching me
from a little way off. That person said nothing, and never came
closer. I didn't know, then, how heavy that silence would turn out to
be.

I thought, too, of something the administrator had said in passing,
in Chapter 22. Transfer is possible, in principle, but rarely used in
practice. I'd let that pass, at the time. Now I know. This city's doors
had never truly been locked. They'd only been closed because no one
had ever opened them. That morning, Lytron pushed on a door no one had
ever pushed. And it simply opened.

That day, I went out to the corner as usual.

The sky was clear. No sign of rain. I set about sifting the dirt with
no great expectation, same as any other day. Since Chapter 24, I'd
known I only ever gained anything on rainy days. That day, it hadn't
rained at all.

And yet something was there at that spot.

At first I doubted my eyes. It wasn't trampled, broken stalks. It was
whole grain. Scattered here and there, not much, but unmistakably
there. I crouched and looked at it for a long while.

"…And no rain fell," I said, half to myself.

He was there too, that day. I watched his face as he looked down at the
dirt in front of him. The same face as always. But that day, that
sameness felt strange to me in a way I couldn't place.

"…Doesn't today feel a little different?" I asked.

He glanced for a moment at the few stalks in his hand.

"…It does," he said.

"…Why do you think that is?"

He didn't answer right away. After a moment he only said,

"…I don't know. But it's a good thing, isn't it."

I believed that, plainly, that day. I know better now. He must have
known. I've thought a long while about why he didn't just tell me the
truth that day. What I guess now is this — he wanted, still, to leave
that fact as Lytron's to give. Some news carries its true weight only
when it comes from the mouth of the one who lived it.

He paused a moment in gathering his own share. I saw his hand stop.

"…What are you doing?" I asked.

"…Nothing," he said. And then his hand started moving again.

I let that brief pause go, that day, without much thought. But as
evening came on, he said, out of nowhere,

"…Days like today don't come often." he said. "Take a good look while
it lasts."

"…Why?"

"…There'll be times, later, you'll want to see it again," he said.
"And it might not be there to see."

That struck me, strangely, as something to hold onto. But for some
reason, I didn't ask any further, right then.

I didn't come back to that remark until much later. I can guess, now,
something of what he already knew that day. I don't know exactly what
he knew. But I believe, now, that he already knew, that day, that this
sight wouldn't last long.

Damia passed by the corner that afternoon too, and stopped.

"…Oh, there's something here today," Damia said. Not a surprised
voice. Just someone noticing, in passing.

"…Yes," I said.

"…Well, how about that. No rain either."

"…I know."

Damia wasn't curious beyond that. She stood a moment, brushed the dirt
with her foot a few times, and went on her way, saying only, "…Nice
day for it," as she left.

I think that was the most honest response I saw that whole day. Damia
hadn't been born and raised in this field. To her, the corner was just
a stretch of ground she passed on her way to somewhere else. A good day
was just a good day. I didn't envy that lightness. It only felt
unfamiliar. To me, that something existed in that spot wasn't a thing
you could pass through in a day.

I filled a sack nearly full that day.

Back home, when I showed it to Pikra, she went quiet for a long while.

"…Did it rain?" Pikra asked.

"…No."

Pikra looked into the sack again.

"…Then why is it like this?"

"…I don't know," I said. "I was just there, at that spot, today."

Pikra didn't look especially glad to hear that. I found that strange.

"…Aren't you happy?" I asked.

"…I am," Pikra said. But there was something careful in her voice.
"But I don't much trust something turning good for no reason. When
something turns good, there's always a reason behind it. Not knowing
that reason unsettles me."

"…Then how do you receive it?" I asked. "You say a good thing unsettles
you."

Pikra ran her fingertips once over the grain in the sack.

"…I receive it," Pikra said. "Receiving it without knowing why, and
refusing it because you don't know why, are different things. I've
never lived a life where I knew all the reasons. Even so, I ate what
there was to eat."

Somehow, that felt like comfort to me.

"…Will it be like this tomorrow too?" I asked.

"…I don't know," Pikra said. "But worry about tomorrow tomorrow. Today,
eat what's today's."

We ate well that evening, for the first time in a long while. Strangely,
that fullness made me uneasy. It felt, somehow, borrowed.

I let that thought pass, that night. Later I would learn how exact
that feeling had been. Pikra had lived more than a generation among
this city's numbers. She knew, in her body, long before I did, that
there's always a price behind something good.

Pikra was quiet a long while that night, by the hearth, working the
grain. I wasn't unfamiliar with that silence. I'd already learned, in
that house, that some people go quiet in front of good things too.

I thought again, that night, of my husband's words.

What you spill isn't wasted. It's left behind. I'd first heard those
words two autumns before, in a field. At the time, I'd thought he only
meant, go easy on your wrists.

That night, I think, I understood the meaning of those words fully,
for the first time. Spilling and leaving something behind come from the
same gesture. Only whose hand makes that gesture differs. My husband
had made that gesture with his own hand. That day, I still didn't know
that another hand, for the first time, had made the same gesture in
this field.

I stop long over this sentence as I write it. I didn't know this, that
night. I only had a handful of grain in front of me, and pulled up an
old, dead man's words again. That those words described, exactly,
something that had actually happened that morning in some office in
this city — I wouldn't know that for a few days yet.

I wrote that day down on paper, the way Mena had taught me.

"It didn't rain, and there was grain at the corner. I nearly filled a
sack."

I wrote no reason behind that sentence. I didn't know one. That day, I
was content with that alone.

Now I can add another sentence beside that one. That day, in some
office in this city, a person pressed a confirm button. No one broke
the law. The procedure was exact. The Roll accepted the change exactly
as it was given. And that person's family score, still unknown to
anyone, was quietly changing direction.

I couldn't set these two sentences side by side, that night. I set
them side by side now, in this book. One I saw with my own eyes. One I
heard of later. But they were a single event, happening the same day,
in the same city.

I want to state one thing plainly as I close this chapter. That day,
no one broke the law. Lytron only changed a setting within his own
authority. The Roll accepted the change precisely, without resistance.
The administrator's office, Article 9, none of it was violated. I will
write this sentence many times more in this book. No one broke the law.
And that day, for the first time in years, grain fell at the corner of
this city's field.

I touched that sack once more that night before sleep. Every kernel
was rough and solid to the hand. I didn't know where it had come from.
I only knew, for certain, that it was truly there in my hands. That
night, for the first time in a long while, I waited for the morning
without worry.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. Found Out

_Previously_ — Lytron reverted the loss rate to 3 percent. No one
broke the law. That day, for the first time in years, grain fell at
the corner. Xene nearly filled a sack.

The first part of this chapter, too, is a story I didn't live through.
I heard it from Lytron himself, much later. He spoke more slowly,
telling this one, than any other chapter he ever gave me.

The next morning, at the Lytron family table, his father asked the
question he always asked.

"…Yesterday's loss rate."

This same question had already come up twice before, in Chapters 16
and 21. Each time, Lytron had given the same answer. 0.02 percent. No
change.

That morning was different.

"…3 percent," Lytron said. "There was a change."

His father set down his chopsticks. The sound of it, Lytron said, was
unusually loud.

"…What did you say?"

"…I changed the setting yesterday," Lytron said.

His father looked as if he couldn't believe it at first, Lytron said.
The same table described in Chapter 16, the same seat. For three
generations, the same question and the same answer had passed across
that table every morning. And now, for the first time, it was that
table's turn to hear something other than "good."

"…That was within your authority?" his father asked.

"…Yes. It's within a zone management assistant's authority," Lytron
said. "I thought it was view-only. It turned out I could change it
too."

"…So it just sat untouched for years, is that what you're telling
me?"

"…Yes."

His father was silent for a long moment after that. Lytron said that
silence had weighed heavier on him than anger would have.

I've pictured this scene over more than once. I think Lytron could
have lied, in that moment. Called it a system error, or said it was
still being confirmed. He didn't. I believe this is the quietest,
and also the most courageous, thing he does in this whole book.

His father was silent for a while. Then he asked,

"…Why."

Lytron had already imagined this question many times before. In
Chapter 21, he'd rehearsed it in his mind already. Say "because I felt
sorry for her," and it sounds sentimental. Say "because of Article 9,"
and it becomes a lie. He hadn't found a better answer, even then.

That morning, weeks later, he still hadn't found a better one.

"…I don't know," he said.

His father looked at him a long while.

"…You don't know," his father said. "You're the one who did it."

"…I'm the one who did it," Lytron said. "But I can't quite put into
words why I did it."

"…You did something like that with no reason at all?"

"…There's a reason," Lytron said. "But if I tell you the reason, I
don't think you'd understand it."

Hearing this answer, I understood that it wasn't distrust that had
made Lytron say it that way. He simply couldn't yet translate his own
reason into words. Someone who had just seen, for the first time, that
he'd never lost anything — that knowledge needed more time before it
could become a sentence. It wasn't a sentence you could use at a
morning table, in front of his father.

His father asked no further.

That same morning, he learned later, his father had sent someone
directly to the administrator's office. A request to confirm the
change history on the setting.

The administrator met the request plainly. The change history was
already recorded, exactly, within the Roll. Who, when, what, by how
much. Nothing to hide, nothing to erase.

"…There's no procedural problem," the administrator told his father's
man, so Lytron later heard. "Lytron acted within an authority that
belonged to him. It wasn't an item that required approval."

"…So you're saying there's no problem at all?"

"…Legally, no," the administrator said. "Though the yield will be
affected. That's already been reflected."

I heard this part directly from the administrator himself. I asked
him, at the time, "So nothing at all was done wrong?" The
administrator answered, as calmly as ever.

"…There's no violation," the administrator said. "There's a loss.
We keep the two separate."

I've thought a long time about that distinction. Violation, and
loss. I confirmed again, that day, that this whole book has been
unfolding in the space between those two words.

Within days, word went around that Lytron's family score had been
adjusted. Not a large drop. But by that household's standard, it was
the first thing of its kind in several generations.

In the field, people's manner toward him began, little by little, to
change.

Nothing anyone could point to outright. A greeting arriving half a
beat late. People not quite stopping their conversation when he passed,
but lowering their voices a little. I only noticed it later. Lytron
said he'd noticed it from the very start.

"…People treat me differently now," he told me. "Not badly. Just —
not as a given thing, the way they used to."

"…Is it hard?"

"…Yes," he admitted. "But strangely, I think I understand now what
that is. I was given respect my whole life as a given thing. I thought
that was simply how it was. Now I know it isn't."

His brother, hearing the news, came to see him once. He wasn't angry.
He only said,

"…I don't know what you were thinking. But know one thing. This isn't
something you can undo. A score falls fast and climbs slowly."

"…I know," Lytron said.

"…You knew, and you did it anyway?"

"…Yes," Lytron said.

His brother seemed about to say something more, then only shook his
head and left. Lytron said, watching him go, he couldn't tell if his
brother was worried for him or simply couldn't understand him at all.

I don't want to paint his brother badly in this scene either. He was
only looking at his brother through the arithmetic he himself had been
taught. Within that arithmetic, what Lytron had done had no other name
but loss.

That evening, Lytron sat across from his father again.

This time his father didn't raise his voice. He only looked at him a
long while, and asked the same question once more.

"…Why."

Lytron said he still couldn't answer. But this time the silence was
different.

"…The first time, I was quiet because I couldn't find the answer," he
said. "That evening, I was quiet because the answer was too large.
Some things don't shrink down into one sentence."

His father sat with that silence a long while, then rose from the
table.

"…I still don't know what it is you've done," his father said. And
he walked out.

I asked Lytron about this more than once. I wanted to know whether he
resented his father. He always said no.

"…My father isn't a bad man," he told me. "He spent his whole life
believing that keeping the number was the right thing to do, and he
taught me the same. I can't say that teaching itself was wrong. That
number kept our family standing for generations. I only saw, for the
first time, what stood behind that number."

Hearing that, I confirmed again what this book has been trying to say
from the very start. This book doesn't paint Lytron's father as a
villain. Nor the Roll, nor the administrator. No one here is a bad
person. Only, a household that had spent generations teaching itself
never to lose anything had, for the first time, raised a son who lost
something.

I heard the news a few days later. Lytron told me himself.

"…It was me," he said. "The loss rate. I'm the one who reverted it."

I couldn't say anything for a long while, hearing that.

"…So that's why grain was at the corner, that day?" I finally managed.

"…Yes," he said.

"…Why didn't you tell me?"

He thought a moment.

"…I don't know," he said. "I thought something would change, if I
told you. That you'd come to see it as something you got because of
me."

"…And isn't it?"

"…I can't say it isn't," he said. "But I didn't give you anything. I
only put it back where it belonged. Back where it should have been all
along."

I turned that over in my mind for a long time.

That evening, I told Pikra too.

Pikra was quiet a long while by the hearth.

"…He did that?" Pikra asked at last.

"…Yes."

"…Cutting his own family's score to do it?"

"…Yes."

Pikra shook her head. Not an angry face. I saw something old in it.

"…I only ever knew one person like that," Pikra said. "My husband. When
he was young, he once paid off another family's debt on their field.
A family with no connection to ours at all."

"…Why did he do it?"

"…I don't know," Pikra said. "I asked. He never answered. Thinking of
it now, I don't think even he knew. Some things aren't done for a
