reason you can fully name."

I thought of Lytron again, hearing that. He'd said the same thing to
me. I don't know.

"…Is that a bad thing, then?" I asked.

"…No," Pikra said. "It isn't bad. But being near someone like that
means, someday, there's a price you have to pay along with them too."

I didn't fully understand what she meant, then.

The next day, at the corner, I told him about it too.

He stopped his hands and listened a long while. He said little,
afterward.

"…Did you know?" I asked.

"…I guessed," he said.

"…Why didn't you tell me sooner?"

He brushed the dirt off his hands.

"…It wasn't mine to tell," he said. "That was his to say for himself."

I thought that answer sounded like something he'd said before, in
Chapter 35. Good news carries its true weight only when it comes from
the mouth of the one who lived it. I understood, only then, that he'd
been keeping that same rule with me, and with Lytron, exactly alike.

"…What happens to this place now?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But once even one person has seen a day
like this, this place doesn't go all the way back to what it was
before."

I held onto those words for a long time.

A few days later, I stopped by the archive and told Mena too.

Mena listened to all of it and wrote a few lines on her paper. She
attached no judgment at all.

"…What do you think?" I asked. "Have you seen this kind of thing
often?"

Mena set down her pen and looked at me.

"…Not often," Mena said. "I've traveled through many cities. Someone
who cuts down their own share to give it to another — that's truly
rare. But not entirely unheard of, either."

"…What happened to those people, afterward?"

"…All different," Mena said. "Some came to regret it, later. Some
didn't. I can't tell you ahead of time which it'll be. I only write
down what a person has done, now."

I wasn't satisfied with that answer. But at the same time, I knew it
was exactly the kind of answer Mena would give.

That evening, I set these two meetings down side by side. In what
Lytron had done, Pikra saw her own husband. Mena saw the names of
similar people from several cities. It had only been Lytron's own act.
And yet that single act called up, inside each of them, an entirely
different memory. I think this is the thing I saw most often, in this
third part. That the same fact settles into a different shape inside
each person who receives it.

That night, I wrote this on my paper. "Lytron reverted the loss rate.
His family's score fell. His father asked why, and he could not
answer." I attached no judgment after that. I didn't write that it was
good, or that it was a pity. Just as Mena had taught me.

But writing this book now, I want to set one more line beside those
sentences. The Roll broke the law not even once, that day. Not
Lytron's father, not the administrator, no one at all committed a
violation. Every procedure was exact. And within that exactness, a boy
lost something, for the first time in his life, and beside him, a
girl, for the first time, held something whole and entire in her own
hands. I set these two sentences down again now, closing this third
part, and confirm once more that both of them were true, the same day,
in the same city, at the very same time.

I began this third part with the scene of Mena teaching me how to
write. Closing it now, I know I couldn't have written this final
chapter without those five rules. I wrote this chapter without hating
Lytron, without hating his father, without hating the administrator.
That isn't because I'm a good person. I only learned how to write
action instead of the heart. And once I had set down only the actions,
exactly, there turned out to be, strangely, no place left for hatred
to stand at all.

I named this part Mena's Record. I know now. What I truly learned in
this part wasn't Mena's five rules. Those were only the method. What I
came to know, by that method, was something else entirely. That having
done no wrong, and being good, are not the same thing. And that the
difference between them never shows itself in any courtroom, or on any
screen of the Roll. It shows itself in exactly one place. In who has
lost something, and who has not.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Gleaning Ground, Part Four

The Price

Chapter 37. The Nearer Kinsman

_Previously_ — Lytron's tampering with the loss rate was discovered. It
wasn't illegal. It was simply a loss. His family's standing fell, and his
father asked him why he'd done it. Lytron had no answer.

The Roll measures closeness in two ways.

One is the closeness I know. Seeing a face every day. Eating from the same
bowl. Knowing, when you hear someone crying at night, when not to go to
them. That kind of closeness is built out of time. It takes long to make,
and long to lose.

The other is the closeness the Roll knows. Blood, marriage, and the number
the two leave behind. This closeness holds even if you've never seen the
face. It holds even if you've never met. Two people who have never once
stood in the same room can be recorded, inside the Roll, as the closest of
kin.

Until then I had forgotten the second kind of closeness existed.
Forgotten isn't quite right — I had never known it at all. There was no
such closeness in our settlement. Out there, there was only the one kind.
Seeing someone every day.

I mean to write, in this chapter, how different the two kinds of
closeness are from each other. And what that difference finally made
possible.

Mena told me once, later, that a record only ever keeps the second kind
of closeness. The Roll, the paperwork, even this book. The first kind is
never written down. I remember that now, writing this chapter.

That summer was unusually long.

Since Lytron's family standing had fallen, the way people treated him in
the field had shifted, little by little. Nothing large. A greeting arriving
half a beat slower. People not quite stopping their talk when he passed.
Things like that.

I noticed. I didn't tell him. There was nothing to be gained by saying it.

We still went out to the corner every day. The loss rate, set back to 3
percent, hadn't been touched. No one had reset it to 0.02 percent again.
Resetting it was work, and no one cared enough to do that work. Lytron's
family standing had already fallen; turning the setting back now wouldn't
have undone that.

So something kept falling at the corner of the field. Not much. But more
than before.

I still remember that summer as a strange season. Something bad had
happened, and because of it, there was a little more to eat every evening.
It took me a long time to find a way to make sense of that strangeness.

Pikra brought up the name on an ordinary evening.

We were washing the barley from that week's ration. Water was scarce, so
the three of us shared one basin, taking turns. Pikra spoke without
stopping her hands.

"…Medon might still be here."

"…Who?" I asked.

"…Your husband's cousin," Pikra said. "The only one of that family who
never left."

I stopped my hands.

"…My husband had a cousin?"

"…He did," Pikra said. "You must have seen him a few times as a child.
Though you wouldn't remember."

I didn't remember. I had never even heard his name spoken. In two years
as that family's daughter-in-law, that name had never once come up. Later
I learned it wasn't an accident. The family had chosen not to speak of
him.

"…Why didn't he leave?" I asked. "The whole family went outside."

Pikra gave herself, for a moment, entirely to washing. Only the rasp of
barley in the basin.

"…He was never blood to this house," she said, finally. "He's my
husband's brother's son. That brother died young, and his widow stayed on
here with just her boy. We'd already split from that side long before. It
happened before he'd turned ten."

"…So he isn't close family."

Pikra looked at me. Water dripped from her hands.

"…He's close to the Roll," Pikra said. "By blood."

"…What's the difference?"

"…That it's a different closeness than the one I know," Pikra said.
"Would he know me? Probably not. He wouldn't know my face. But the Roll
has us written down as close kin."

I let the words pass by that night. It wasn't unusual for Pikra to bring
up a name from the past. I took it for something said over an evening
chore. I still thought so the next morning.

Lytron came out to the field the next day.

He didn't smile much lately. His face had changed a little, since his
family standing fell. Not for the worse. Just a face that seemed always to
be calculating something.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…I need to ask you something," he said. "On your mother-in-law's side —
does anyone still have family left in this city?"

I didn't know why he was asking.

"…There is, apparently. I only heard it yesterday. Someone named Medon."

Something registered on Lytron's face. It was an expression I would learn
to recognize only later — not the face of calculating something, but of
confirming it.

"…Medon," he repeated. "I know that name."

"…How?"

"…He's in the Harvest management Roll," he said. "His family standing is
high. Very high. Among the highest in this district."

"…What does he do?"

"…Supply allocation," Lytron said. "That family's never wavered,
generation to generation. They never went outside, and they've never had
much occasion to lose anything."

Something in that caught at me, though I couldn't have said what.

I can name it now. Lytron already knew, even then. That a family that
had never wavered meant a family that had never lost anything. And that
this wasn't only a good thing.

The administrator sent for us that same week.

Three of us sat before the Roll terminal — Pikra, Lytron, and I. The
administrator paged through the screen, explaining something. His voice
was even, as always. The same pitch whether the news was bad or good.

"…There's a procedure called standing transfer," the administrator said.
"You may already know of it."

I didn't. Lytron looked as though he did.

"…It lets a person lower their own score to add to someone else's," the
administrator went on. "It exists in the system. It's rarely used."

"…How rarely?" Lytron asked.

The administrator paged through once more. His fingers moved with
practiced ease.

"…According to the record, it has never once been used," the
administrator said, still even. "The provision exists, but there is no
case of it having been carried out. Not once, since it was created."

That didn't strike me as strange. I didn't yet know how strange it was —
that a door could exist and no one had ever tried it.

I know now. It wasn't that the provision existed and nobody used it. It
was that nobody used it, and so the provision simply existed. A door no
one has ever tried isn't open, even when it's open. I didn't understand
the difference then.

The administrator pointed to a corner of the screen.

"…In a case like this, there's a procedural order of priority," the
administrator said. "The first opportunity goes to a blood relation. It's
not fixed by law — it's custom. And this person" — he pointed to a name
on the screen — "is the only one registered as blood kin to you, Miss
Xene."

It was Medon.

I looked at the number beside his name. I couldn't read numbers well, but
I knew it was a large one. Far larger than Lytron's. Several times larger,
it looked like.

"…At this size," Lytron said quietly, "it wouldn't even show. For him."

The administrator gave a small lift of the shoulders.

"…It wouldn't."

"…Then why hasn't anyone done it already?" I asked. "If it wouldn't even
show."

The administrator looked at me. For the first time, something crossed
that even face. I couldn't name it then.

"…Not showing is still not nothing," the administrator said. "Not in the
direction of going down."

I didn't fully understand that at the time. I understand it now. Not
showing means others don't see it — not that the person himself doesn't
feel it. And a person knows the things others can't see.

I raised my hand.

"…What changes, if I'm entered?" I asked.

The administrator looked at me. He didn't seem surprised by the question.

"…Rations come regularly," he said. "You can apply for housing. You can
hold a proper job. And" — a brief pause — "the Roll records you. Right
now, the fact that you live here isn't recorded anywhere. Once you're
entered, it is."

That caught at me strangely.

"…So right now, my living here isn't recorded anywhere?"

"…A person with zero standing isn't a subject of record," the
administrator said. "The Roll doesn't note a birth. It doesn't note a
death. It isn't that you're treated as not existing. There's simply no
field to write you into."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time what registration actually
meant. It wasn't a matter of rations or a place to live. It was whether
the fact of my being here was preserved anywhere at all. I had been in
this city more than two years by then, and inside the Roll, I was a
person who existed nowhere.

The administrator said he would begin the procedure. Medon would be
contacted.

For the first time, I felt something like hope. Relief, more precisely.
If someone could do this without any loss at all, Lytron would no longer
need to lower his own family standing further. I thought that was a good
thing.

I looked over. Lytron's face wasn't relief.

"…What's wrong?" I asked.

"…Nothing," he said. "It's good news."

I believed him, when he said it. Thinking back on it now, what was on
his face then wasn't relief but something else. I wouldn't learn what it
was for several months.

Pikra stayed quiet even after hearing the news.

"…They've contacted Medon," I said, on the way home. "The administrator's
started the procedure."

Pikra stopped walking.

"…All right," Pikra said. That was all.

"…What's wrong?"

Pikra looked at me for a long time. The evening light touched only half
her face.

"…Nothing," Pikra said. "Let's just wait and see."

"…Is Medon a bad person?"

"…He isn't a bad person," Pikra said. She started walking again. "He
never was."

The answer struck me as strange. Saying it twice felt like leaving
something else unsaid.

"…Then why did the families split?" I asked.

Pikra slowed her steps.

"…His mother — Medon's mother — left our family," Pikra said. "After her
husband died. She decided there was nothing to gain by staying. But she
didn't go outside. She stayed in the same city. She only split from us."

"…Why?"

"…Because our family's standing was already bad by then," Pikra said.
"One of my husband's brothers had already died. She didn't want her son
tied to that number. She was a clever woman."

Something went cold in me at that.

"…So Medon's family ran from the bad blood."

"…You could say that," Pikra said. There was no resentment in her voice.
Just the tone of someone stating a fact. "She was protecting her son. I
might have done the same."

That stayed with me a long time. Pikra didn't resent the choice. I found
that more unsettling, somehow — that there are choices in this world one
cannot resent. And that the outcome of such a choice could reach all the
way to me, decades later.

A person, protecting themselves, often can't see who falls because of
it. Or perhaps they can, and simply don't look. I still can't tell the two
apart.

I didn't understand then why Pikra was so cautious. I know now. Pikra
knew Medon. His face, his voice, the way he chose his words. I didn't
know him. Pikra did.

That difference decided the days that followed.

A few days later, an answer came. Medon would see us in person. He asked
us to come to his office near the gate.

I couldn't sleep that night. Not the good kind of sleeplessness. It had
been a long time since I'd lain awake for a good reason.

Pikra didn't so much as turn over beside me. I knew she was only
pretending to sleep.

I didn't know then that it would be the last night I lay awake for a
good reason.

That night, unable to sleep, I thought about my husband.

He had never once mentioned a cousin. I'd taken that for how little he
spoke in general. He was the sort of person who said a thing once and let
it lie for a long while before saying it again, so there was no
particular reason a cousin should have been an exception.

I think differently now. Some silences aren't simply an absence of
speech. Someone made them, deliberately. He may not have chosen not to
speak of his cousin. He may have been unable to — if the family had
already erased that story, long before.

I'd asked him once whether he had relatives in the city. He'd been quiet
for a long while, and then said only this.

"…Even if I have one, it's as good as not having one."

I let it go at the time. It catches at me now. To say even if I have
one, it's as good as not having one is to say you know he exists. That
isn't something you say about a man who isn't there at all.

I went out to the field the next day, at midday, and he was in his usual
spot.

I didn't glean much that day. My mind was elsewhere. He worked quietly
beside me, and then looked at me once.

"…Something wrong?" he asked.

I was surprised. It was rare for him to ask first.

"…A relative on my mother-in-law's side has turned up," I said. "He might
register me."

He nodded. No sign of surprise.

"…That's good news."

"…Then why can't I let myself feel easy about it?"

He paused, hands still.

"…That's how it is, waiting for something big," he said. "Waiting for
something small is easy — if it doesn't come, that's the end of it.
Waiting for something big, if it doesn't come, that's never the end of
it."

I turned that over for a long time.

I didn't understand then why he spoke that way. I didn't know what he
had ever waited for. I think now that he must have been waiting a very
long time, for something.

The day Medon was contacted, Lytron didn't come to the field.

He didn't come the next day either. On the third day, when he reappeared,
I didn't ask where he'd been. His face was the face of someone who had
already come to know something.

"…I went to Medon's office," he said first.

"…What did he say?"

"…He'll meet with you," he said. "You and your mother-in-law."

Something in his tone struck me as wrong. It wasn't the tone of someone
delivering good news.

"…And?"

"…Nothing," Lytron said, shaking his head. "See for yourself."

I should have asked more, then. I didn't. I think I wanted only to
believe it would go well. A person, sensing that the answer they want is
coming, asks fewer questions. I learned that for the first time that
day.

I heard, later, how Lytron's father had taken the news.

His father had been glad. Very glad, apparently. A way had appeared to
stop his son's family standing from falling any further — that was reason
enough. He'd sent someone directly to the administrator, asking that
Medon's side of the procedure be hurried along.

"…My father said something," Lytron told me a few days later. There was
something held back in his voice. "That it was good news. That I could
finally stay out of this."

"…Why does that bother you?"

"…It's nothing," he said, shaking his head. "The words just sounded
strange to me."

I couldn't name what was strange about it, then. His face wasn't relief.
It was closer to a face holding something in.

I can name it now. His father had seen this, from beginning to end,
purely as a matter of loss. His son had taken a loss; if a way existed to
end it without further loss, that was that. That I was inside that way,
that whether I was registered at all hung on it, had no place in his
father's arithmetic. Whichever way the answer came out, I was an item
that mattered very little to him.

Lytron was the one who had begun this from outside that arithmetic. I
didn't know it then. I should have. I learned, very slowly, in that city,
how to tell the people inside the arithmetic from the people outside it.

The morning we were to meet Medon, Pikra took my hand.

It wasn't something she usually did. Her hand was rough, the knuckles
thick.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Whatever happens," Pikra said, "you're already mine. Whatever the Roll
says."

I couldn't answer. My throat closed.

I took the words for comfort, that day. Thinking back, it wasn't comfort
— it was a warning. Pikra already knew what Medon would say.

We walked toward the gate. It was the same road I had walked when I first
entered this city, two years before. Then, I'd been on my way to be
assessed for standing. This time, I was on my way to be registered.

It was strange that the two roads were the same road.

Pikra walked without speaking. I walked beside her, watching the office
buildings near the gate grow larger as we approached. Medon's office was
among the largest of them.

I remember exactly the moment I stood before that building. Before the
door opened. Before any answer had been given. Up until that moment, I
too had standing enough to hope. I know how strange it is to use that
word this way. But that is exactly how it felt, in that moment.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. The Refusal

_Previously_ — A relative was found who could register Xene — far
better positioned than Lytron to do it without cost. The husband's
cousin, Medon. The morning we went to meet him, Pikra took Xene's hand
and said, "You're already mine."

Medon's office was clean.

Clean wasn't quite the word for it. There was no dust. I had never seen a
room without dust before. Outside, a room was always something that came
with dust attached.

He rose from his seat to greet us. Tall, well-dressed. Older than Lytron,
younger-looking than the administrator.

"…Mrs. Pikra." His voice was gentle. "It's been a long time."

"…Medon."

That was all, from the two of them. No embrace, not even a hand taken.
The distance between them struck me as strange. In the Roll they were
close kin; in that room, they looked like two strangers meeting for the
first time.

I didn't fully understand that distance then. I understand it now. I saw
with my own eyes, in that room, how different the Roll's closeness is
from a person's.

There were three chairs in the room. They'd known we were coming and set
them out ahead of time. I took that for kindness. Thinking back now, it
wasn't kindness — it was procedure. Set out three chairs for guests. That
was all it was, a rule.

A painting hung on one wall. A field of ripe wheat leaning in the wind. I
looked at it and thought, briefly, of our settlement's field. It never
lay down that way, like a picture. It was always a little thin somewhere,
patchy.

There was no corner in that painted field. I don't know whether the
painter had simply not drawn it, or thought there was no need to. I
couldn't take my eyes off that painting the whole time we were in the
room. I learned later that it's the most common painting in this city. A
field with no corner. No one finds that strange.

He offered us seats. We sat.

"…I've heard the situation," he said. "The administrator explained it in
detail. You're Xene, correct?"

"…Yes."

He looked at me for a moment. Not an unkind look. Courteous, if anything.
That made it stranger.

"…I understand the circumstances," he said. "It's an unfortunate
situation."

The moment I heard that word, something felt wrong. Unfortunate is a
word for something already over. I still believed, at that point, that
this thing hadn't ended yet.

"…And how have you been, Mrs. Pikra?" he asked. He didn't go straight to
the matter at hand.

"…Getting by," Pikra said.

"…I heard about your husband. And your sons." His voice held something
genuine. That confused me more. That a person could be genuinely sorry and
do nothing at all — until then, I hadn't known such a person existed.

"…And your mother? Is she well," Pikra asked.

"…She passed away two years ago."

The room went quiet for a moment. I felt something old passing between
the two of them. Twenty years, thirty years I knew nothing of, sitting in
that room along with us.

He opened a screen and turned it toward us.

There was a number. His standing. I still couldn't tell, looking at it
again, anything more than that it was large. Beside it, a smaller number
— the amount required for transfer.

"…At this level," he said, pointing to the screen, "it's true that it
would barely register for me. I checked."

My heart jumped at that.

"…Then—"

"…But I'm not going to do it," he said.

The room went quiet.

"…Why?" I asked. I felt I'd asked something I shouldn't have, and yet the
word had come out first.

He looked at me. He didn't grow angry, didn't look uncomfortable. He
answered in the same ordinary tone as before.

"…My score would be cut," he said.

I didn't understand the words. Hadn't he just said, himself, that it
wouldn't even show?

"…You just said it wouldn't show—"

"…It wouldn't show," he said. "Not to anyone else. But being cut is being
cut. I have never, not once, had my score go down. Not since I was born."

The words didn't sound like a boast, to my ear. His face wasn't the face
of a man boasting. It was the face of a man stating a fact.

"…Why should I be the first case?" he asked. It seemed half addressed to
me, half addressed to himself.

I had no answer then. I have none now. There was, in truth, no reason it
had to be him. Nothing in the Roll required it of him. It was purely a
door the system had left open, and opening it had never been anyone's
obligation.

"…I don't understand," I said again. "If the amount is that small, why
is this such a difficult conversation?"

Medon smiled, for the first time. Not a scornful smile. Just a bitter
one.

"…It's difficult because it's small," he said. "If this were a request
for my entire fortune, I'd have already refused and been done with it.
This is small enough that no one could blame me for it. Which means I
have no choice but to blame myself."

I felt I might understand that, and didn't want to.

"…Are you saying a small amount weighs more than a large one?" I asked.

"…No," he said. "The weight is the same. It's only that in front of a
small amount, there's nowhere to hide. In front of a large amount, you
can say it couldn't be helped. In front of a small one, that excuse
doesn't hold."

I have never forgotten that answer. He knew exactly how thin the ice was
that his refusal stood on. And knowing it, he stood on it anyway.

Pikra had said nothing this whole time. She looked at the screen, at
Medon, back at the screen.

"…Your father," Pikra said, finally, "wouldn't have done this."

Something in Medon's face shifted, for the first time.

"…Mrs. Pikra," he said. "My father was a man who took losses. That's why
our family ended up the way it did."

"…So that's why your mother split off."

"…Yes," he said. He wasn't ashamed of it. "I believe my mother was
right. I don't live that way."

I felt something solid in that answer. A solidity that couldn't be
argued with.

Pikra said nothing more. I understood, at that moment, completely, why
she had been so careful these past several days. She had known Medon
would say something like this. She had known his mother. And she had
known the son took after her.

"…Your mother," Pikra said again, her voice gentler now, "was protecting
you. I don't resent her for it. But you're not the one who needs
protecting anymore. Your score is already large enough. Larger than
large."

Medon lowered his eyes for a moment.

"…Do you know when 'large enough' gets decided?" he asked. "I don't. My
father probably thought his was large enough once, too. Then he started
taking losses, and it went down and never stopped."

"…That's a different matter."

"…You would think that, Mrs. Pikra," he said. "I don't. I was taught
that protecting what you have is a habit it's never too late to start."

Hearing that, I understood he really wasn't a bad man. He was only
living the way he'd been taught to live. It was only that what he'd been
taught and what I'd watched, for two years, pointed in completely
opposite directions.

Medon closed the screen. He looked at each of us in turn.

"…I'll tell you one thing," he said. "I could have blocked this
procedure entirely. If I'd asked the administrator. When the person with
priority claim refuses, there are ways to delay how quickly it moves to
the next in line. I didn't do that."

"…What difference does that make?" I asked. I don't think I expected the
words to bring any comfort.

"…Maybe none," he said. "It only means I won't stand in the way. I can't
do this myself. But someone else might."

I took those words, at the time, for one more excuse. I hear them a
little differently now. He really hadn't locked that door — though he
could have. It seems like nothing. It wasn't nothing. There are people in
this world who could block a thing and don't, and people who never had
the power to block it in the first place. I learned that day not to
weigh those two the same.

As we rose to leave, Medon looked once more at the paperwork the
administrator had given us. The standing-transfer procedure form. He
studied it for a long moment.

"…The procedure itself is simple," he said. It sounded almost like he
was speaking to himself. "One signature on the application, and it's
done. The Roll calculates the rest. There isn't even a separate
verification step."

"…Why does that sound strange, the way you say it?" I asked.

"…Because it is strange," he said. "That something this large ends in a
procedure this small. I don't know what the person who built this was
thinking."

I didn't understand those words then. I understand them now. He was, in
that moment, already weighing exactly what he was refusing. How far
apart the size of the procedure was from the size of the outcome — he
had seen that before we had.

One signature. I remembered those words for a long time. I had no idea,
then, that they would come back to stand in front of me a few days
later.

"…Don't misunderstand me," Medon said. His voice softened again. "I don't
dislike either of you. I'm sorry for this. Genuinely."

"…And yet you won't help," I said.

"…Helping and giving up what's mine are different things," he said. "I
can help in other ways. I could add to your rations, if you need it. But
my standing — that's mine. I've kept it my whole life."

What was hardest was that nothing he said was wrong. He didn't tell a
single lie. He showed no hypocrisy. He was genuinely sorry, and genuinely
wanted to help in other ways. Only one thing he wouldn't do — let his own
number fall.

And the Roll found no fault in him. It couldn't. He had broken no law.
He had simply stood in front of an open door and not walked through it.

The administrator, in a moment like this, would have stayed even. Medon
wasn't even. He kept offering explanations, all the way until we left the
room. That he'd send more rations if we needed it, that we should send
someone if we ran short of fuel in winter, things like that.

I tried to take those words as kindness. If I hadn't tried, I think I
would have been angry instead.

Looking back now, the rations and the fuel were real. He sent someone
every winter after that, without fail. I don't want to dismiss that. He
did exactly as much as he'd decided he could do. He simply never stepped
one pace past that line.

Before we walked out the door, Pikra called to him once more.

"…Medon."

"…Yes."

"…I don't resent you," Pikra said. "But remember this. You did, today,
what you believed was right. I know that. But right and —"

Pikra stopped. She never finished it.

"…Good?" Medon finished for her. He knew the word too.

"…Yes," Pikra said. "That."

Medon said nothing. His silence was his answer.

I remember that silence exactly, even now. He didn't argue. Not because
he had no argument, I think, but because he didn't want to make one.
Some people, told they were wrong, neither agree nor disagree — they
simply listen. I think that, too, is a kind of honesty.

We left the room.

The sun outside was strong. I stood for a while, blinded.

"…Are you all right?" Pikra asked.

"…Yes." I lied.

We walked without speaking. Past the gate district, toward the field.
Somewhere along that road, I stopped and asked.

"…You said standing transfer's been around a long time."

"…That's right," Pikra said.

"…And no one's ever used it," I said. "How many Medons must there be in
this city?"

Pikra didn't answer.

That was the day I first saw the real shape of standing transfer. It
wasn't a locked door. It was a door wide open. All it required was one
thing — the will to give up what was yours. And that will was not
something the system could manufacture.

Instead of going to the field, we passed through the market for a while.
Pikra wasn't buying anything. I think she just needed somewhere to walk.

The market was as it always was. People buying wheat, buying cloth,
children running. No one looked at us. Whatever had just happened to us,
whatever words had passed between us in some office of this city, left
no trace in that market.

That was strangely comforting, somehow. That the world doesn't stop
just because I have. And at the same time it was the most frightening
thing of all. That the world doesn't stop just because I have.

Pikra spoke suddenly, walking.

"…I always knew he'd grow into that." She wasn't pointing toward the
market lane, but I knew she meant Medon.

"…How?"

"…He was quick with numbers, even as a child," Pikra said. "If someone
gave him something, he took it. If he borrowed something, he paid it
back. Exactly that much. No more, no less. I thought that was cleverness."

"…And now?"

Pikra stopped for a moment.

"…It is cleverness," Pikra said. "But I've learned it's a different
thing from being a good person. I thought the two were the same. When I
was young."

I turned that over many times. Being quick with numbers, and being good.
Keeping the law, and being righteous. I kept seeing those pairs pull
apart from each other in this city. The Roll was one of them.

A stall gave off a familiar smell. Roasted barley. I stopped in front of
it for a moment.

"…Hungry?" Pikra asked.

"…No."

"…Then why'd you stop?"

I had no answer. The smell was simply familiar, and I'd stopped for it.
Standing in front of something familiar felt like safety. That day, it
was the only safety I had.

Lytron came out to the corner of the field that evening, once he heard
the news.

I was standing there alone. Not really there to glean. Just there to
have somewhere to sit.

"…How did it go?" he asked. His face already guessed the answer.

"…He won't do it."

Lytron nodded. He wasn't surprised.

"…Aren't you angry?" I asked. "You heard him in that room. It really
would have cost him nothing."

"…I'm not angry," he said. After a while, he added, "What he said wasn't
wrong, either. It's his. That's his."

Something caught at me in that. I felt I could almost tell why he was so
quiet, and yet not quite.

I didn't know it then. What he was already thinking, in that moment. I
despaired in front of Medon's door. Lytron was looking at something
else. That another door was still open. His own.

That night, I told "him" what had happened. It wasn't the hour to still
be at the field, and yet he was still there.

"…I was refused," I said.

"…I know," he said. He didn't say how. He sometimes already knew things
no one had told him.

I sat down beside him. The field was darkening. Evening insects were
singing.

"…I really thought, right up until today, that something would come of
this," I said. "Not because I deserved it, but just because I'd come
this far — I thought something would."

"…That's not a strange thing to think," he said.

"…But it didn't."

"…No," he said. He didn't try to console me. That, somehow, was the
comfort.

"…He wasn't even a bad person," I said. "That's what makes it hardest.
If he'd been bad, I could at least hate him."

He nodded.

"…A refusal you can't hate is the one that lasts longest," he said.

I almost asked what he meant, and didn't. He always spoke that way, and
I always understood, only later.

"…So what do I do now?" I asked. I didn't know myself who the question
was addressed to.

He didn't answer. He only gathered a few stalks from his hand and set
them down beside me. A little less than I needed, as he always did.

I thought, that day, it was simply his habit. I think differently now.
Maybe he wasn't refusing to give me an answer that day. Maybe he already
knew the answer would come from somewhere else.

I got home late that night. Pikra was already lying down. I thought she
was asleep, but her eyes opened when I came in.

"…You're late."

"…Yes."

"…Are you all right."

I lay down beside her instead of answering. After a long while, I said,

"…I'm going out tomorrow too. To the field."

Pikra made a sound of laughter in the dark. A silent one.

"…You're really something." There was something more behind those words
that she never said aloud.

I didn't know it then. That within a few days, Lytron would already have
made up his mind. I only thought about going to the field again the next
day, and the day after that. It was the only way I knew.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. The Price

_Previously_ — Medon refused. Not because he was a bad man, but for one
reason: "My score would be cut." The Roll found no fault in him. He had
broken no law. That night, Lytron sat quietly with his own thoughts.

For a long time, I thought the word price belonged only to things.

The price of a sack of wheat. The price of a goat. A number set against
something you could hold in your hand. That people could have a price
felt, to me, like counting a person as an object, and I never much liked
the word for that reason.

But that summer, I learned the word again. Price wasn't only a thing's to
carry. Some prices a person pays with their own two hands. And the moment
of paying it, I found, could be astonishingly small.

Outside, paying a price was always visible. You gave wheat and received
salt; you lent a hand and borrowed one back. What passed between people
always passed from hand to hand. In the city it was different. A price
moved inside a screen. The only thing your hand ever touched was the
screen itself. And yet what moved inside that screen was never lighter
than what moved from hand to hand.

This was the hardest chapter to write. The largest thing in this book
happens here, and yet what actually happened was so small that a few
lines hold all of it. I decided to leave it that small. Not to swell it.

Mena told me once that the thing a recorder must guard against most
isn't writing a small thing large. It's writing a large thing small. A
large thing tends, by its nature, to look small, and it is easy to be
fooled by that smallness and miss its true size.

I kept remembering that, writing this chapter. What is recorded here
really was very small. And knowing that smallness was no lie, I still had
to write it so the reader would never forget how large it truly was.

I learned only later that it took Lytron three days to reach his
decision.

He'd begun calculating, he told me, the night he left Medon's office. His
own family standing, the registration threshold, the distance between
them. He couldn't say exactly how many times he ran the numbers again.

"…At first I thought half would be enough," he told me later. "It won't
clear the threshold with half. It has to be the whole thing."

"…And if it's the whole thing?"

"…My family standing drops one full tier," he said, "from where it is
now." His voice was even, but I only learned later how much effort had
gone into that evenness.

Lytron came to our house three days after he'd met Medon.

He stood at the door for a long time without speaking. I knew, just from
his face, that he'd come with something already decided.

"…Come in," Pikra said.

He stayed where he was and spoke.

"…I'll do it."

I didn't understand at first what he meant.

"…The standing transfer," he said. "I'll give mine to Miss Xene."

Pikra reacted first.

"…Your family standing's already fallen once," Pikra said. "If you cut
it further—"

"…I know," he said. "I'll do it anyway."

"…How much does it come to?"

"…I've calculated it," he said. "It's enough. Enough to bring Miss Xene
above the registration threshold."

I knew, that moment, I should say something, and no words came. Not
because my throat had closed, but because I truly did not know what to
say. Thank you seemed far too small a thing to say. No, don't seemed
far too cold. I found nothing that stood between them.

"…Why," was all I managed.

Lytron looked at me. There was nothing of resolve in his face, nothing of
heroism. He only looked tired.

"…I'll tell you the reason later," he said. "Right now I just want to do
it."

I would hear that reason later. Many times, in many ways. But even now,
I think what he said that day — I just want to do it, right now — was the
truest answer of all. A great decision sometimes arrives before its
reasons do.

Pikra asked again.

"…Have you told your parents?"

"…No," Lytron said.

"…You should tell them first."

"…If I tell them, they'll stop me," he said. "So I haven't."

Something went cold in me at that. He had already decided to cross some
bridge with his family. In the direction there was no going back from.

"…They'll have things to say, later," Pikra said.

"…I know," he said. "But if I don't do it now, I don't think I ever
will."

I've never forgotten those words, even all these years later. He knew
exactly what kind of person he was. That if you don't act on a resolve
the moment you feel it, that resolve doesn't come again. Everyone is
given a handful of such windows, in their life. Most people don't see
them standing open until they've already passed.

We went to the administrator's office the next day.

The administrator received us. Even as always. Lytron stated his
purpose, and the administrator showed no surprise.

"…I'll confirm this," the administrator said. "You wish to transfer your
own standing, of your own will, to Miss Xene."

"…Yes."

The administrator tapped the screen a few times. I watched his fingers
move — neither slow nor fast.

"…The application is prepared," the administrator said. "Read it, and
sign if you're satisfied."

That was all.

I looked at the application. A single sheet of paper. Few words on it.
The one transferring. The one receiving. The amount transferred. A
signature line below.

"…Is that all?" I asked. The words came out of me before I meant them
to.

The administrator looked at me.

"…That's all there is, procedurally," the administrator said. "There's
no reason for it to be complicated. The Roll already knows each person's
value precisely. A transfer is only a reallocation of that value."

"…No one checks it? There's no approval process—"

"…Why would approval be needed?" the administrator asked, in return.
"This is a person using what is theirs to use."

Those words stayed with me. Using what is one's own to use. The Roll had
never had any logic by which to prevent it. This wasn't breaking a rule.
It was a rule that had permitted this from the beginning. It was only
that no one had ever used it.

"…I'm curious about something," I asked the administrator. "How long has
this procedure existed?"

The administrator checked the screen for a moment.

"…It's existed since the Roll was first established," the administrator
said. "It's an older provision than this city's entire Roll system."

"…Then why has no one—"

"…I don't know the reason myself," the administrator said. For the first
time, something like hesitation crept into his voice. "May I offer my own
opinion?"

"…Please."

"…A person who gives up what is theirs doesn't boast of it," the
administrator said. "And a person who doesn't give up what is theirs has
no reason to even know this procedure exists. So the procedure was never
going to be widely known. No one has used it, so there's no story to
tell about it, and with no story, the next person never learns of it
either."

"…May I ask one more thing?" I said.

"…Yes."

"…If the first person to use this procedure used it with bad intent —
what would happen? Forcing it on someone, for instance."

The administrator thought for a moment.

"…A transfer is only completed by the giving party's signature," the
administrator said. "The receiving party's consent isn't required. In
that sense, yes, it could be abused. Someone forcing their low standing
onto another person, for instance."

"…Has that happened?"

"…No," the administrator said. "In a world where no one is willing to
give up what's theirs, there's not much reason to force anything onto
anyone else, either. Taking something from someone starts, in the end,
from wanting to gain something. This is a losing procedure, not a
gaining one."

That explanation sounded, strangely, sad to me.

Good things come from the hands of people who don't boast, and so they
never pass on to the next person. Bad things spread easily precisely
because no one bothers to hide them. I saw that imbalance for the first
time that day. And I would learn, much later, that it isn't only found
in this city.

Lytron stood before the signature line.

I remember that moment exactly, even now. No special instrument, no
ceremony. He simply touched his finger to the signature line on the
screen — the same way he always did to collect a ration, or leave a
record at a gate.

"…Wait," I said.

He stopped his hand and looked at me.

"…Are you sure about this?" I asked. "You said it can't be undone."

"…It can't be undone," the administrator answered, in his place. "A
transferred value is not returned."

Lytron looked at me and smiled. It had been a long time since I'd seen
that smile.

"…I'm sure," he said.

And he touched his finger to the screen again.

I found myself holding my breath, without meaning to. It was a very
short moment. The screen flickered once, then returned to itself.

That was all.

I had expected something more. A confirmation screen, perhaps, or a
second prompt asking again. There was none. Only a small word, printed
above the application: Processed.

The numbers on the screen changed.

His number went down. Mine went up. That was all. No sound. No light
flickered. The screen only showed two new numbers.

I'd expected something grand to happen, in that moment. Nothing did. The
air in the room was the same. The sounds outside the window were the
same. The administrator was already paging to the next screen.

That quiet was the strangest part of all. That the moment a person set
down a number they'd carried their whole life could pass as though it
were nothing at all. I understand it now. That's the true face of a
price. A price always looks small, in the moment it's paid. You only see
how large it truly was later, looking at what's left in its place.

The administrator turned the screen toward us. Two numbers side by side.
One that had gone down, one that had gone up.

I looked for a long time at the number now beside my name. It was the
first number I'd had beside my name in sixteen years of living. Not
zero.

"…Is this mine?" I asked. I knew how foolish the question sounded, and
asked it anyway.

"…It's yours, Miss Xene," the administrator said.

Lytron wasn't looking at the screen. He was looking out the window.

I wanted to ask why he wasn't looking. I didn't ask. Some moments, I
have learned from this family, are not meant to be asked about.

There was someone else in the office besides us. Someone in the corner,
there on other business, waiting their turn. That person never once
looked our way. Theirs was the face of someone only waiting for their own
name to be called.

That, strangely, stayed with me. That a moment when one person's life
was being changed was, to the person sitting right beside it, nothing
more than the background of their own waiting. That's how the world is
made. Some moments are enormous only to the person inside them, and
nothing at all to anyone standing a single step away.

The administrator gathered his papers as he spoke.

"…Your registration will be completed today," the administrator said,
turning to me. "Congratulations."

I have never heard the word congratulations land so heavily.

The administrator turned next to Lytron.

"…Your side requires processing as well," the administrator said. "The
family standing reassessment. It will be reflected at the next scheduled
renewal."

"…Understood," Lytron said.

"…Would you like to reconsider—"

"…No," he said. Fast. Firm. The administrator didn't ask again.

I learned later that the brief exchange was the administrator's own
kind of consideration. It was a question he was required, by procedure,
to ask once, and he closed it quickly, as a formality, to spare him. It
was his own way of being kind.

I looked at Lytron. He was already looking elsewhere. Out the window.

"…Are you all right?" I asked again.

"…Yes," he said. Short. As though he didn't want to say more.

I didn't press him further then. Later, I asked him many times what he'd
been thinking in that moment. He gave a different answer every time. I
think that means he still hasn't found the exact answer himself.

Lytron's father heard the news that evening.

I never saw the scene myself. Lytron never described it in detail,
either. All he said, a few days later, was this.

"…My father didn't say anything at all."

"…He wasn't angry?"

"…I would rather he'd been angry," he said. "Saying nothing at all is
more frightening."

I understood what he meant. I already knew, myself, that some silences
weigh heavier than any anger.

"…Do you regret it?" I asked.

Lytron thought for a long while.

"…No," he said. "But I am afraid. I only found out this time that the
two things can exist together."

No regret, and afraid. I didn't fully understand what that meant then. I
understand it now. Being the right choice doesn't mean it isn't
frightening. Sometimes it's precisely the rightest choice that's the most
frightening one — because there's no way back to where you were.

I couldn't sleep that night and went out and sat outside. I looked up at
the sky. The same sky I used to watch from the outer settlement. The
stars were in their same places.

That was the first time I truly felt how the sky over the city and the
sky over the outside were the same sky. The Roll had completely remade
the way a person was counted, and the sky hadn't changed at all. There
are some things that don't change, no matter how great a price passes
beneath them.

Pikra came out and sat beside me.

"…Awake?"

"…Yes."

"…Me too," Pikra said.

We sat a long while without speaking. Then Pikra said,

"…What do you think your husband would have said."

I had no answer for that. I truly didn't know what he would have said. But
I thought I knew one thing.

"…I think he would have said not to be sorry for what's spilled," I
said. "That what's spilled is left behind."

Pikra nodded. She said nothing more.

Outside, the sun was strong.

The three of us stood for a moment without speaking. What had just
happened inside that office already felt like something far away, out
here.

Pikra took hold of Lytron's arm for a moment. She said nothing. She just
held it, and let it go.

"…I don't know if 'thank you' is the right thing to say," Pikra said,
finally. "I don't even know if that's the right word for this."

"…You can just say that," Lytron said.

"…No," Pikra said. "This isn't a thing that thanks can settle. I know
that. That's why I don't know what to say."

Lytron smiled a little.

"…Then leave it," he said. "It's all right if it doesn't come out
right."

I've never forgotten that exchange. Pikra was never, in her life, a
woman who was careful with words. That day, she couldn't find the right
ones. Some things simply can't be priced in words. Gratitude, apology —
both grow small in front of a thing like this.

"…Can I ask you something?" I said to Lytron.

"…Yes."

"…That signature, just now — how many seconds did it take?"

Lytron looked at me. He didn't seem to think the question strange.

"…A few seconds," he said. "I couldn't say exactly. It was short."

"…That something that short could be something this large — I still
find that strange."

Lytron smiled a little. A different smile than before.

"…So do I," he said.

I meant to end this chapter here. But I'll add one more thing.

I told "him" the story that night, too. He was alone at the corner of
the field. Quietly working, as always.

"…Lytron transferred his standing to me," I said.

He stopped his hands. He looked at me for a long time. Something crossed
his face — not surprise, I felt. More the face of someone who had waited
a long time for something.

"…I see," he said.

"…Did you know? That this would happen?"

"…I didn't know," he said. "But I hoped for it."

The words struck me strangely. He had always seemed a man who hoped for
nothing at all.

"…What were you hoping for?"

He began moving his hands again.

"…For there to be someone who knows how to give up what's theirs," he
said. "In this city, too."

I let that answer pass by that night. Reading it again now, there was a
weight in those words that reached beyond this one city. I wouldn't
understand that until much later.

Even years after, I keep returning to this scene. The Roll broke no law
that day. Neither did the administrator, nor Medon, nor Lytron. Each of
them moved only as far as they were permitted. Medon refused as far as
he was permitted to refuse. Lytron gave as far as he was permitted to
give. The Roll received both, equally, in the same silence.

I think that is this city's truest face. The Roll compels no one. It
only leaves the door open, and leaves it entirely to the person whether
to walk through it. And in front of that door, some people walk in and
some don't. The Roll does not weigh that difference. A person does.

I mean to end this chapter here, truly. One signature. A few seconds.
That was all of it. And that it was all of it is the heaviest thing I
learned from this whole book.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. Going Down

_Previously_ — Lytron transferred his standing to Xene. The procedure
was one signature, a few seconds. The numbers changed on the screen —
that was all, quiet as though nothing had happened. "He" heard the news
and said he had "hoped" for it.

I had only ever known the word going down from the field.

A stalk goes down. Grain goes down. Wheat leans one way when the wind
blows. I didn't hear that word used for a person until I came to this
city. A score goes down. A family goes down.

I thought those two kinds of going down were different. Wheat goes down
and stands up again, once the wind stops. I learned, that autumn, that
this was wrong. A person's going down doesn't stand back up just because
the wind stops.

I can't write this chapter through Lytron's eyes. I was never inside him.
I only set down what I saw from the outside. What he lost, only someone
watching him from outside could count precisely. The one who loses rarely
knows, at first, exactly what they've lost. I learned that too, that
autumn.

When I entered this city with zero standing, I didn't know precisely what
I had lost. There was simply nothing. Never having a thing from the start
and having a thing and then losing it are different. Lytron was the
second kind. So he had to feel the size of what he'd lost, new, every
single moment. I never had to. I had no before to compare against.

For the first few days, nothing seemed to have changed.

Lytron still went to the Harvest management office. Still sat in the same
seat. I thought he was fine.

But the way people treated him was shifting, little by little. It began
with the smallest things. The order of greetings changed. Where people
used to bow their heads first when he passed, now sometimes he had to
bow first.

"…It's nothing," he told me. He said it before I'd even asked. "I'll get
used to it."

I wanted to believe that. I would learn later that nothing* was, in
fact, something.*

Pikra noticed the change before I did.

"…Doesn't he seem different lately?" Pikra said, one day.

"…He seems tired."

"…No, not that," Pikra said. "His walk's changed. Before, he walked like
a man who decided where he was going before anyone else did. These days
he walks like he's watching people's eyes."

I hadn't noticed it. Once she said it, I looked again, and it was true.

"…Is that a bad thing?" I asked.

Pikra thought for a moment.

"…Not bad," Pikra said. "But it's unfamiliar to him. Anything unfamiliar
is hard, at first."

I turned Pikra's words over for a long time. Watching people's eyes. I
had known that since I was born. Which door to knock on for food, which
look meant a bad mood that day. Lytron was only now beginning to learn
that language.

About a week later, I saw him standing in a line outside the office.

A line for confirming rations. He'd never had to stand in a line like
this before. His family name alone used to be enough to have things
processed on the spot.

Not anymore.

He waited his turn like everyone else. When the person ahead moved
slowly, he simply stood and waited. He didn't grow impatient. That
struck me as stranger than if he had. He should have been angry. He
simply stood there.

"…First time in a line?" I asked, coming up beside him.

He looked at me and smiled. A bitter smile.

"…Not the first," he said. "But this time is a little different."

"…How?"

"…Before, I could have stood in a line. I didn't have to, but I could
have, if I wanted. Now I have to stand in it."

I thought I understood the difference. Doing something because you could
choose to, and doing it because you had no choice.

What he learned in that line, I think, I had already known, probably
since I was born. Not a line you stand in because you want to, but a line
you must stand in. I already knew what that was. He was only now
beginning to learn it.

His seat at the Harvest management office had changed too.

Before, it had been by the window, a good seat. Now it had been moved
inward. No one had ordered it moved. Seats were simply arranged by tier.
The rearrangement had just, naturally, sorted him into his proper place.

"…Who told you to move?" I asked.

"…No one," he said. "Seating goes by rank. It always did. I just never
had to notice it. When my rank was high, there was nothing to notice."

Something important was in those words. The Roll's order had always been
there. Lytron had lived on top of that order his whole life without ever
seeing it. Order is invisible from above. You have to come down to see
it.

One of his colleagues had never spoken to him first before. It had
always been Lytron who opened a conversation. That was rank.

Now that colleague spoke first.

"…Are you all right?" the colleague had asked, he told me later.

"…What do you mean?" he'd said back.

"…No, nothing," the colleague said. And smiled, awkwardly.

Lytron told me the moment had been strangely difficult.

"…I understood, for the first time, what it feels like to be pitied," he
told me. "I never knew, my whole life."

I said nothing to that. I couldn't. I knew that feeling very well. I only
didn't want to hand him that knowledge in that particular moment.

That was the first time I felt he and I were looking at the same thing,
from some place. The place he had come down to and the place I had
always stood weren't identical, but they were, for the first time, close
enough to see each other.

Something had changed at the market too.

There was a stall he always went to. The owner used to smile and pick out
the best of whatever he had. He'd cut the price, more than once, I heard
later.

I happened to pass that stall one day and ran into Lytron there. The
owner still recognized him, still greeted him. But he no longer offered
his hand first. He simply watched Lytron choose, until Lytron had paid
the full price.

"…It never used to be like this," he said quietly, coming out of the
stall. It sounded less like something said to me than said to himself.

"…What do you mean?"

"…He took the full price," he said. "That's the first time I've paid
full price at that stall."

I didn't know what to say to that. It wasn't unjust. It was, in fact,
exactly correct. He had simply paid the price of the thing.

I searched his face, that moment, for any sign of resentment. There was
none. He wasn't resentful. He was only bewildered. It was the face of a
man in the moment when something that had been ordinary his whole life
suddenly wasn't ordinary anymore. I already knew that face. I had worn
it myself, my first days in this city.

"…It's a strange thing to say," he said, "but I don't dislike that man
for it. He's just doing what's ordinary. I was the strange one. I lived
my whole life thinking something that wasn't ordinary was ordinary."

I thought that was the most exact thing he'd said in all these weeks.

What changed most was his house.

Lytron's father, from then on, hardly spoke to him at all. Not angry, not
scolding. Only what was needed, said and no more.

"…Do you still eat together?" I asked.

"…Yes," he said. "But no one says anything. You just hear the dishes."

"…That must be hard."

"…Yes," he admitted, plainly. "Much harder than I expected."

I understood, for the first time, that what Lytron had lost wasn't only
a number. He had lost some place in his own father, too. A price isn't
only ever counted one way. There are prices the Roll never registers.

One day, out at the field, I saw him at the ration office, requesting
something. Borrowing the key to a grain storehouse. Once, his name alone
would have gotten it handed to him instantly.

The clerk asked for paperwork.

"…I'm sorry," the clerk said. "There's a verification requirement now."

Lytron stood there while the paperwork was prepared. A few minutes. It
must have felt long, to him.

I watched from a short distance away. There was no irritation on his
face, no anger. Only the face of someone encountering something unfamiliar
for the first time.

I remember that moment exactly. He was standing, for the first time in
his life, in a place where he could demand nothing. A place where there
was nothing to do but wait. I knew that place very well. I had stood
there since I was born.

Around then there was a gathering he wasn't invited to.

A summer gathering the young of certain families held every year. It had
no name; people just called it "the gathering." I only heard of it
later. Lytron never told me himself.

"…You didn't go this year?" I asked, after hearing of it from someone
else.

"…No invitation came," he said. It wasn't the face of a man pretending
not to care. It was the face of a man trying very hard not to.

"…Did they leave you out on purpose?"

"…I don't know," he said. "Whether someone left me out on purpose, or
whether the list is just automatically sorted by rank and I fell below
it. There's no one to ask."

The words there's no one to ask* stayed with me a long time. The Lytron
of before would have made somewhere to ask. His name itself was standing
enough to ask. Now he had become someone who had to go looking for a
place to ask at all.*

He smiled a little.

"…The strange thing is," he said, "I never even liked that gathering.
Bored every time I went. And yet, being uninvited, I feel strangely hurt
by it."

"…Losing something you never even liked."

"…Yes," he said. "I didn't expect I'd feel that either."

I learned something from that. Some things are worth nothing to you
while you hold them, and only become a space the size of an absence once
they're gone. Not because of the thing that was there. Because of the
fact that the space is now empty.

A few days later, I watched him ask for something directly, for the
first time.

He'd asked the office to let him see something ahead of schedule. The
next season's ration schedule. It was a document he'd once have received
the moment he asked for it. That had been the sort of seat his was.

"…I'm sorry," the clerk said. "That's released by rank, in order.
Lytron's tier is released in three days."

"…Three days."

"…Yes."

"…Is there no exception," he asked. I felt, in that moment, that he
hadn't yet fully shaken his old manner of speaking. Asking for an
exception was itself proof that he still believed, somewhere, in a world
where exceptions existed for him.

"…There are no exceptions," the clerk said. He looked apologetic, but
nothing changed. "Those are the rules."

Lytron stood there a moment. He seemed about to say something more, and
didn't.

"…Understood," he said. And turned away.

I watched his back. He wasn't angry. It was the walk of a man who knew
there was no longer any place left for anger. That was the first moment
I felt he was truly standing in the same line as me. The line where a
request is not simply granted. I had lived my whole life in that line. He
was only now standing at its entrance.

"…Are you all right?" I asked, catching up to him.

"…Yes," he said. He paused, then added, "But it's strange. I've never
once, in my life, heard the word no. Not really. Whatever I asked for,
it was always granted. If it couldn't be, there was always another way."

"…And now?"

"…Now no is just the end of it," he said. "There's no other way. Just
wait three days."

I said nothing back. I only reached out and touched his sleeve for a
moment, then let go. It was the only answer I had.

I remember those three days. Nothing extraordinary about them. He
waited three days, and on the fourth he had his paperwork. That was all.
And yet I think those three days were among the longest of his life. For
the first time, he had to watch time pass without being on his side.

I shared some bread with him that evening. Baked from what I'd gleaned
at the corner.

"…What's this for?" he asked. He looked puzzled.

"…No reason," I said. "You looked hungry."

He smiled. Not bitterly this time.

"…It's strange," he said. "Me receiving this."

"…What's strange about it?"

"…I used to be the one giving," he said. "I never knew receiving would
feel so different."

I said nothing to that. I only sat beside him until he'd finished the
bread.

That day, for the first time, I confirmed once more that there are
things you cannot know without having stood in them. He'd watched me for
more than two years. He'd felt sorry for me, and helped me. But not until
he stood in the place of receiving something himself did he truly know.

I was probably the same. I still don't fully know what it's like for
him to stand in the place of giving. I likely never will.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. Entered

_Previously_ — After Lytron's standing fell, the way people treated him
shifted, little by little. The order of greetings. Where he sat. How he
stood in line. For the first time in his life, he had to accept the
word "no" and simply stand there with it.

Someone came from the Roll office.

It was four days after the day of Lytron's signature. No one came in
person. A small slip of paper arrived. A notice of completed
registration, it said.

I took the paper and looked at it for a long time. My name was on it.
Xene. Below it, a city Roll number. Below that, a date of registration.
Below that, a standing score.

I still have this piece of paper. The color has faded and the corners
have folded. I never threw it away. I couldn't.

Pikra looked at the paper and said nothing at first. She just took it
from my hand, studied it for a long while, and handed it back.

"…We should go confirm it," Pikra said.

"…Confirm it?"

"…On the screen," Pikra said. "Not the paper. You have to see how the
Roll itself has it written to know it's real."

That sounded strange to me. It was already written on the paper. I
didn't understand why we'd need to look at a screen as well.

I know now. In this city, what was real was always on the screen's side.
The paper was only a copy of what the screen said. It wasn't that the
paper came first and the screen confirmed it after — the screen was the
original, and the paper was its shadow. Pikra already knew that.

We went to the office.

The administrator received us. Even as always.

"…You've come to confirm the registration," he said. He already knew.

He tapped the screen and turned it toward us.

There was a name. Xene. Beside it, a number.

I looked at that number.

It wasn't zero.

I looked at that number for a long time.

It wasn't a large number. What Lytron had given wasn't the whole of his
family standing — only enough to clear the registration threshold. The
administrator had explained that already. "It's enough. Enough to bring
Miss Xene above the registration threshold."

So this number wasn't a generous one. It was barely enough.

And still I looked at it for a long time. Whether it was generous or
not didn't matter to me then. All that mattered was that it wasn't zero.
I had lived sixteen years without ever once seeing a number beside my
name that wasn't zero.

"…Would you like to see more?" the administrator asked.

"…Yes," I said.

I don't know how long I stood in front of that screen. The administrator
didn't hurry me. Someone else must have been waiting, and he never
showed a trace of it to me.

There was more on the screen. A field called history. I opened it and
found my whole life summarized in a few lines. Place of birth, marriage,
bereavement, migration. Four lines. Sixteen years, into four lines.

Looking at those four lines, I felt something strange. I should have
been angry, and I wasn't. I thought, instead, that the shortness was
honest. The Roll hadn't inflated my sixteen years. It had set them down
exactly as they were, in four lines. The problem was only that the
number beside those four lines carried a weight far heavier than the
lines themselves.

My hand stopped at the third line. Spouse — deceased, it read. A date
beside it. I knew that date. I knew it without needing to count.

"…Does this count too?" I asked. My voice shook a little.

The administrator looked at me for a moment.

"…It counts," he said quietly. "I'm sorry. This is how the Roll is
built. It doesn't look at what a person went through. It looks at the
value that experience left behind."

I felt, in that moment, that the man genuinely was sorry. And at the
same time, I knew that sorrow changed nothing at all.

That was the day I saw, exactly, how the Roll reads grief. It doesn't
read grief as grief. It reads it as value. I loved that man; that's why I
called him my husband. The Roll called him by a single number. That love
and that number were written side by side on the same line — that was
the hardest thing to bear, that day.

Below the number were other fields.

Residency standing. Ration standing. Labor standing. Each marked
valid. I read the word, one field at a time, without speaking it
aloud.

I'd seen these fields before. At the gate. They had all read invalid*
then. The administrator had explained it to me then, too. Not
expulsion. Refusal to renew. No one was driving me out. Only, nothing
was being given.*

The words hadn't sounded like comfort then. They sound a little
different now. Between giving nothing and giving something, there is a
distance no words can fully hold. I saw that distance, with my own eyes,
in front of this screen, for the first time.

"…What changes now?" I asked.

The administrator tapped the screen a few more times.

"…You can apply for housing," he said. "Until now you were listed under
Miss Pikra. Now you can apply in your own name. Rations will come to you
separately, too. There are jobs only registered people can apply for —
you'll be able to see that list now, as well."

I didn't grasp, right away, what each of those things meant. I would
come to know them, one by one, in my own body, later. I no longer had to
stand behind Pikra in the ration line. There was a share now that came
out under my own name. My name could be on the door of the house — I
wasn't putting it there yet, but that it could be there at all made a
difference.

I felt something strange, each time one of those things happened. I
would be lying to say I wasn't happy. But there was something larger than
happiness underneath it. I didn't know, then, exactly what it was. I
know now. It was the feeling of owing something. I hadn't earned that
number. I had received it. And what's received always carries the face
of the one who gave it.

Before he closed the screen, I asked one more thing.

"…How long is this number valid for?"

"…The same as any other standing," the administrator said. "It's
reassessed at the regular renewal. Except—"

He paused.

"…Except what?"

"…A transferred value is calculated a little differently," the
administrator said. "It wasn't built up from your own history. How you
live in this city from here on will matter more to how long this number
holds than it would otherwise."

Something went cold in me at that.

"…So this is," I said, "a number I have to keep proving?"

The administrator looked at me. For the first time, something cautious
crossed his face.

"…Honestly," he said, "yes. But that's true of every standing in this
city. You're not uniquely disadvantaged."

I took that for comfort, at the time. I know now it wasn't comfort at
all — it was simply a fact. The administrator hadn't meant to console me.
He had only stated it precisely. Everyone in this city is always
proving themselves. I had only known that since I was born. Lytron was
only just learning it, that day.

We left the office.

Pikra was waiting outside for me.

"…How was it," Pikra asked.

"…It wasn't zero," I said. No other words came to me.

Pikra nodded. She neither smiled nor wept. She only took my hand once,
and let it go.

"…Come on," Pikra said. "To the field."

"…Today too?"

"…I know you want to go out," Pikra said. "You don't have to, anymore."

I said nothing to that.

That day I went out to a field I no longer had to go out to. Before,
I'd gone because I had to. From that day, I went because I wanted to. I
couldn't have explained that difference in full, then. I still can't
explain it completely, even now. I only know it appeared, very quietly,
somewhere inside me.

Lytron was at the field.

He watched us approach and didn't come toward us. He just stood where he
was. I knew he was keeping his distance on purpose.

"…Did you confirm it?" he asked first.

"…Yes."

"…Not zero?"

"…No," I said. "Not zero."

He nodded. It wasn't quite a smile, but very close to one.

"…Good," he said. That was all.

I felt something more should have followed that short word. He said
nothing more. I asked nothing more either.

I understood only later why he hadn't come closer. He didn't want to
show me what he'd lost, standing right in front of what I'd gained. On
the day I received, he didn't want to bring what he'd given up along
with him. I think that was a consideration all his own. One no one had
asked of him.

"…This is strange," I said. I held it back and finally said it. "I
should be happy today. My heart keeps turning wrong instead."

"…Why?"

"…You gave it," I said. "I received. You gave it. That keeps catching at
me."

Lytron glanced toward the field for a moment.

"…Is that strange?" he asked, back. "I think it's the natural way of it,
actually. Giving and receiving is supposed to be that — one gives, one
receives. If both give, that's not giving and receiving anymore, that's
—"

He stopped. He seemed unable to find the right word.

"…That's just standing still," he finished. "Neither one moving."

I felt I understood what he meant, and didn't fully. I understand a
little more now. I think, that day, he wanted to tell me not to feel
sorry for receiving. But he couldn't say it directly, and said it
sideways instead. The moment was awkward for him too.

The next day, I went to the ration office alone for the first time.

Before, I'd always gone behind Pikra. I had no share of my own; we ate
from what was given to her. That day I stood in line under my own name.

The clerk called my number. The call was unfamiliar enough that I
answered a beat late.

"…Miss Xene," the clerk called again.

"…Yes." I answered, only then.

He entered my number into the screen. Checked something for a moment,
then handed me a sack.

"…This season's share."

I took the sack. It wasn't heavy. But the weight in my hands felt
strangely unfamiliar. I had always been the one sharing in what someone
else received. This was the first time I'd received something with my
own hands, under my own name.

I stood there a long time, holding that sack. There was someone in line
behind me, and I still couldn't move. Someone nudged me lightly on the
back before I came to myself.

"…I'm sorry," I said, stepping aside.

The clerk said nothing more. He didn't look at me specially, didn't look
at me strangely. He simply called the next person.

I have never been so glad of anyone's indifference. I hadn't wanted the
man to recognize me. I had only wanted to be treated the same way as
everyone else. That day, for the first time, I was. Not specially at
all.

On the way home I met "him." He was putting away his tools at the edge
of the field.

"…I received something for the first time today," I said, holding up
the sack a little. "In my own name."

He stopped his hands and looked at me.

"…How does it feel," he asked.

I thought for a moment.

"…Strange," I said. "Good, but strange."

"…Why strange?"

"…It's not because I did anything," I said. "I just received it. Someone
paid for it instead."

He nodded. And went back to his work.

"…There are people who find it strange to receive," he said. "People who
think it isn't real unless they earned it themselves."

"…Am I one of them?"

"…A little," he said. It didn't sound unkind, said with a smile.

"…Is it wrong to find it strange?"

"…Finding it strange and refusing it are different things," he said.
"You can find it strange and still receive it. That's actually the most
honest kind of receiving there is."

I didn't fully understand that, that day. I came to understand it, a
little at a time, over the years that followed.

I remember that moment still. He didn't teach me something new that
day. He only put into words something I had already been feeling. I
think it was moments like that, stacked one on another, that led me to
write this record.

That night, I took out the notice again.

Name, number, date, standing. That was all that was written on that one
sheet of paper.

I felt, that night, how thin that paper truly was. A single sheet. And
behind that thin sheet lay one signature, a few seconds, and a number a
man had kept his whole life. I folded that paper and put it against my
chest. From that day I carried it with me always.

Even now, years later, I still take it out sometimes. The color has
faded further. The words remain. Name, number, date, standing. I know
that not one of those four was made by me. My name was given by my
parents; my number was set by the Roll; the date was only the day it
happened to be; the number was given to me by someone else. There is
nothing of my own doing anywhere on this paper. I think this is the way
this city let me in.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. Pikra's Answer

_Previously_ — My name went into the Roll. Not zero, anymore. For the
first time I collected a ration in my own name, went out to the field
in my own name. And in every one of those moments, the weight of what
Lytron had given followed after me.

It was about ten days after my registration.

In that time I had wanted, more than once, to call that name again. I
never let it out. I felt, somehow, that I had no right to. While I was
still zero, I hadn't felt I was in any position to give anything back to
this household.

Looking back now, it was a strange logic. What did a name have to do
with rights? And yet that was truly how I felt, at the time. That a
person without standing couldn't demand anything, and couldn't give
anything back, either. No one had taught me to think that way, and
still, somehow, I'd already learned to think the way the Roll thinks.

Once I was registered, I thought it would finally be all right. I had a
