number now. I was someone who could give something now, too.

I brought it up with Pikra. Something I'd wanted to say for a long time.
Something I hadn't felt I had the right to say, before I was registered.

"…I'm going to call you Glyke again."

Pikra set down what was in her hands. We were cleaning vegetables that
evening. The sound of the knife against the board stopped.

"…What did you say?"

"…I have standing now too," I said. "I have a number. You don't have to
be Pikra anymore. Not anymore."

I thought, saying it, that my heart would grow lighter. I thought it
would feel like paying off a debt I'd carried a long time. I was wrong.

Pikra looked at me for a long time.

"…Who decided you get to decide that," Pikra said.

I didn't know what to say to that.

"…What do you mean?"

"…When I asked to be renamed," Pikra said, "it wasn't because of your
number. It was because of mine."

I had no words.

"…Your being registered," Pikra said, "doesn't give me back what I
lost."

I felt something ashamed at that. I had thought my registration was
this whole household's recovery. It wasn't. It was mine. Pikra's place
hadn't moved at all.

"…I'm sorry," I said. "I only meant—"

"…There's nothing to be sorry for," Pikra said. Her voice softened. "I
know what you meant. I'm grateful, even. But there's something you
still don't understand about this."

"…What."

Pikra picked up the knife again, then set it down again.

"…Do you know the last time I used the name Glyke," Pikra asked.

"…Before your husband passed."

"…That's right," Pikra said. "In those days I was someone who had
things to give. I had a husband, two sons, two daughters-in-law who came
to me. I was someone who could share something every day — food,
clothes, a place at the table. Glyke was that person's name."

I listened quietly.

"…I'm not that person anymore," Pikra said. "I still don't have much to
give. You being registered doesn't make me someone with something to
give again. I'm still someone who has lost."

"…But," I said, carefully. "That hurts me."

"…What does."

"…Mother," I said, "you still make me see what's lost, every time I look
at you. I don't want to see you only that way."

Pikra looked at me a long time.

"…Xene," Pikra said. "There's something you've misunderstood."

"…What."

"…I don't use this name to show what's lost," Pikra said. "It's the
opposite."

I didn't understand what she meant.

Pikra went on, slowly.

"…If I were called Glyke again," Pikra said, "people would look at me
and think — she's all right now. She's become a good person again. A
smiling person again."

"…Why is that bad?"

"…It isn't bad," Pikra said. "But it isn't true. I'm not all right. I
still have no husband, no sons, either of them. That fact doesn't
change. Your being registered doesn't bring my husband back to life."

Something went cold in me at that.

"…The moment I'm called Glyke again," Pikra said, "people will forget
that grief. That's how everyone thinks, once a name changes. Ah, it's
over now. It's passed. I don't want that."

I sat quiet for a long while, then said,

"…So you'll go on being Pikra?"

"…Yes," Pikra said. Quite plainly.

"…For the rest of your life?"

"…Probably," Pikra said.

I thought that would be a sad answer. Pikra's face wasn't sad. It was,
if anything, a face that had settled something.

"…The bitter didn't go away," Pikra said. "I just learned I could live
with it still there."

I said nothing to that. I couldn't.

I thought, in that moment, of the day we left the outer settlement, long
before. The day Pikra — she was still Glyke, then — first asked to be
renamed. "Don't call me Glyke. Call me Pikra." I couldn't say the name
at first. My tongue wouldn't shape it. It took weeks before it came
naturally.

I had thought, at the time, that changing her name was only a mark of
grief. A name changed to flee what she couldn't bear. Two years later, I
know now that it wasn't flight. It was honesty. She had decided she
would not pretend to still be someone who smiled. And now, even after
things had gotten better, she had decided not to give that honesty up.

I turned that sentence over all night. I had always thought recovery
meant returning to how things were. A sick person healing. A lost thing
found again. What was bitter turning sweet again. Pikra wasn't talking
about that kind of recovery.

"…So how does Mother," I asked, carefully, "live now, still bitter?"

Pikra smiled a little. A smile I hadn't seen in a while. It resembled
the old Glyke's smile, and wasn't quite the same.

"…I live knowing it's bitter," Pikra said. "Before, I didn't know it was
bitter. I thought having a husband and sons meant everything was sweet.
Now I know. Sweet turns bitter, too, in time. But bitter isn't only
bitter, either."

"…What do you mean?"

"…You," Pikra said. Just that.

Something caught in my throat at that one word.

"…Me?"

"…You followed me," Pikra said. "I told you three times to go back. You
didn't go. I think that's a sweet thing. One sweet thing inside a bitter
life. Not bitter turning sweet. A sweet thing, inside the bitter."

I didn't fully understand what that meant until much later. Pikra had
decided, by not changing her name, to neither reduce nor enlarge her
grief, but to simply carry it as it was. She was telling me, that day,
that other things could live alongside that grief.

I looked down at the vegetables on the board. My hands started moving on
their own again. My mind was still on that sentence.

"…So," I asked again, "I'm not supposed to call you Glyke."

"…No," Pikra said. Firm.

"…I should keep calling you Pikra?"

"…Yes," Pikra said. And picked up the knife again.

"…Doesn't it bother you?" I asked.

Pikra spoke as she trimmed the vegetables.

"…Bothering and being wrong are different things," Pikra said. "Your
being registered is right. My staying Pikra is right, too. Both are
right. One doesn't cancel the other."

A few days later, I got the name wrong, in the market.

A stall owner had asked Pikra's name. I said, out of habit, "Glyke—" and
stopped. The owner gave me a strange look.

"…Pikra," I corrected quickly.

Pikra said nothing. But I saw her shoulders stiffen for a moment.

I apologized on the way home.

"…I'm sorry. It just came out."

"…It's fine," Pikra said. Her voice sat lower than usual.

"…Are you angry?"

"…No," Pikra said. After a moment, she added, "But it frightens me a
little, that the name still comes that easily."

"…Why?"

"…As much as you want to call me that name," Pikra said, "there are
times I want to live under it too. But I shouldn't. I hold that back
every time. You slipped once. I hold it back every single day."

I had no answer for that.

That was the first time I saw that the decision hadn't been easy for
Pikra, either. I had thought she'd finished making that decision, long
before. I was wrong. She was making it again, every single day.

A neighbor woman stopped by that evening.

Someone she'd known a long time, she said. Seeing Pikra, she spoke
warmly.

"…You've been looking well lately, Pikra. Your daughter-in-law's
registered too, I hear. Couldn't you go back to your old name now?"

Pikra smiled a little.

"…Why should I?" Pikra asked back.

"…Something good's finally happened," the neighbor said. "No need to
keep hold of a bad name."

"…It isn't a bad name," Pikra said. "It's the name I chose."

The neighbor looked a little taken aback at that. She didn't press
further, and turned to other things — a rumor the ration schedule was
changing, someone's leaking roof — and Pikra's name scattered off into
the air like that.

I've kept that brief exchange a long time. People saw the name Pikra
as a mark of misfortune. A garment to be shed, they thought. Pikra
herself wore it not as a garment but as her own face. Not many people in
this city knew the difference.

That evening, I asked something I'd been curious about.

"…What does my name mean?" I asked. "Does Mother know?"

Pikra stopped her hands.

"…I don't know," Pikra said. "Your name was given by your own family,
not by me."

"…And Mother's name? Glyke?"

Pikra thought for a moment.

"…I heard it's an old word. I don't know exactly, myself," Pikra said.
"Mena would probably know. She knows things like that."

"…Mena?"

"…She's a recorder," Pikra said. "People like that know the roots of
words. I never asked her. I didn't want to know."

"…Why?"

Pikra looked at me.

"…Once you know the meaning," Pikra said, "you hear the name
differently. I wanted to hear Glyke just as my own name. I was afraid
that once I knew the meaning, the name would start sounding like it was
asking something of me."

I thought I understood what she meant, and didn't fully.

I didn't know then that my name had a meaning too. And Lytron's. Nor
that the moment those meanings would be revealed was still a few
chapters away. Pikra's words, that day, were only something passing by.
Later, they sounded like prophecy.

That night I lay down and took the notice out again.

Name, number, date, standing. I folded the paper away and thought. I was
registered now. Not zero. And yet there was still someone in this house
who had chosen to remain zero. By their own will.

That was the first time I dimly felt that standing wasn't only the
number the city assigned me. Pikra carried a number inside her that was
different from the one the Roll gave her. A number the Roll couldn't
see. It was probably something beyond price.

I lay a long time that night, beside Pikra, without sleeping. She
didn't seem to be sleeping either. We lay there like that, in the dark,
each holding our own number. One was the number the Roll had given. The
other was a number no one could ever assign.

In the dark, Pikra spoke once more. I'd thought she was asleep. She
wasn't.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Thank you," Pikra said, "isn't for your being registered."

"…Then what is it for?"

"…For your going on calling me Pikra," Pikra said. "Except for just now.
That one thing, I'm more grateful for than anything else."

I felt tears come at that. I tried not to make a sound.

"…Why is that the thing you're grateful for?" I finally managed.

"…Everyone in this city tells me to become Glyke again," Pikra said.
"They all mean well by it. But no one ever asks why I don't. You did.
And then you just accepted it."

I began, that night, listening to those words, to understand a little of
what my place in this household was. I hadn't come here to save anyone.
I was only someone who asked, and then accepted. That was all. And yet I
felt, for the first time that night, that it hadn't only been that.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. The Corner

_Previously_ — I tried to call Pikra Glyke again. Pikra refused. "The
bitter didn't go away. I just learned I could live with it still
there." She had chosen, of her own will, to go on being Pikra.

Lytron reset the loss rate before summer's end.

From 0.02 percent to 3 percent. Seen as a number, it was a small
difference. The administrator once showed us that figure. "That's all
it is," he said. Truly, that was all.

I heard that phrase — that's all it is — many times in this city. That's
all a signature is, that's all a number is, that's all a loss rate is. I
know now. The largest things in this city always hid behind the words
that's all it is.

Once that number changed, the corner of the field changed.

The first day, I went out to the corner as always.

I didn't expect much, as always. It was habit. My body was simply used
to going out. All I knew was that I'd come back with a few handfuls of
trampled stalks.

That day was different.

There was grain in the corner. Real grain. Unbroken, standing on the
stem, the ear full. Scattered here and there.

I stood a long time without moving.

I remember that moment exactly. What came before the surprise was
suspicion. Had someone dropped it by accident. Had someone put it there
to mock me. It took time before I believed the grain was real.

I bent down and picked one up.

It was real. The kernels were ripe, the stalk still alive. I held it in
my hand and looked at it for a long time.

I filled a whole sack that day. The first time since the day it rained.
That day, the machines had stopped. This time they hadn't. The machines
still ran. The workers still moved as always. But somewhere in that
motion, a gap had opened that hadn't been there before. A gap the size
of one falling ear of grain.

I understood that difference exactly, then. The rain had been the
sky's doing. This was a person's doing. Filling that sack, I knew that
inside it was something someone had set down with their own hands.

I brought the sack home that night and spread it out and looked at it
for a long while.

Pikra came and looked with me.

"…You gleaned a lot," Pikra said. She didn't sound surprised. She
seemed to already know.

"…Something's changed," I said. "At the corner."

"…I'd heard," Pikra said. "Something about Lytron."

"…You knew?"

"…I guessed," Pikra said. "He's been strangely quiet since he gave up
his standing. It was the quiet of someone with something left to do."

I was surprised at that. Pikra had noticed before I had.

I felt again, that moment, how much deeper Pikra's eye for reading
people ran than mine. She had lived her whole life that way. Telling
apart the one who has something to give from the one who has nothing
left, the one still willing to give from the one who has already given
everything. I still haven't fully learned that eye.

Word got around within a few days.

There was grain at the corner, the word went. No one believed it at
first. Then a few came to check. Then more came.

I watched those people. People with low standing or none at all. The
old, those with children, those whose bodies weren't strong. They came
to the corner and gleaned.

I felt something strange, watching that scene. I had stood alone at
that corner for a long time. Not anymore. I couldn't tell whether that
meant my loneliness had ended, or my place had simply disappeared.

I once saw the administrator pass by the corner. He stopped and watched
the people gleaning for a long while. I was nervous he might do
something about it.

He said nothing. He only watched, and then walked on.

I asked him about it later, when I ran into him at the office.

"…Why didn't you say anything, that day at the corner?"

The administrator thought for a moment.

"…There was nothing to say," he said. "Article 9 says a corner must be
left. It doesn't specify how much. What Mr. Lytron changed in the
settings wasn't a violation. His family standing paid the price for it.
I have no place in that matter."

"…Doesn't it bother you," I asked, "that so many people are suddenly
coming?"

"…Whether it bothers me is unrelated to my job," the administrator
said. "My job is only to see whether the law is being kept. It's being
kept."

I confirmed, once more, why he frightened me. He didn't judge by
feeling. He didn't judge by good or bad. He judged only by whether the
law was kept. And by that measure, he was always right.

An old man was gleaning beside me.

"…Used to be nothing here at all," he said, half to himself. "There's a
bit, lately."

"…Yes," I said.

"…Someone must have changed something," he said. And asked no further.
He didn't seem curious about the reason. It seemed enough for him just
to know there was something to gather today.

I felt hurt by that indifference, at first. He didn't know what Lytron
had paid. Didn't seem to think he needed to know. I think differently
now. It was right, in fact. That was what the corner clause had always
been. The giver's name was never meant to remain. Leviticus never said
that either. It only said to leave it.

I no longer needed to go out to the corner.

My own ration came separately now. Registered as I was, I had a right
to it. I was no longer someone who lived by gleaning at the bottom.

And still I kept going.

Pikra found it strange, at first.

"…You don't have to go anymore," Pikra said.

"…I know."

"…Then why do you go."

I couldn't answer right away. I didn't know the exact reason myself.

I carried the question with me for a few days.

One day I met "him" at the corner. He was still there. Among the other
hired hands.

"…Why come out, when you don't have to anymore?" he asked. The same
question as Pikra's.

"…I don't know," I said. "It's just familiar here."

"…Is that all?"

I thought a moment.

"…No," I said. "I don't want to forget what I was here."

He stopped his hands and looked at me.

"…What were you?" he asked.

"…Someone who was nothing," I said. "I only learned what that meant
after I came here. I don't think I should forget it."

That was the first time I made that answer for myself. No one had
taught it to me. I kept gleaning, even after being registered. Not out
of need, but for some other reason. That day was the first time I put
the reason into words.

He nodded.

"…That's a good reason," he said.

"…Why do you keep staying here?" I asked. A question I'd always wondered
about.

He smiled a little. He didn't answer. He just started moving his hands
again.

I let that silence pass by, back then, the way I always did. Now I find
myself missing that silence, sometimes. I learned, being with him, that
some questions carry farther left as questions than answered.

The corner stayed changed, from that autumn on.

Not fully generous. 3 percent was still a small number. Not enough to
feed a whole family. But it was different from before. Before, it had
been close to zero. Now it wasn't zero.

I think that difference is a large one. People often ask whether it can
really matter that much. 0.02 percent to 3 percent. It might not look
like a large number. But I can say this, having stood at that corner
myself. Between having nothing at all and having a little, there is a
distance no number can hold.

The administrator said nothing about the change. I understood why,
later. The Roll had broken no law. The loss rate wasn't a value the Roll
set — it was a value a person set. Lytron's resetting it hadn't been
illegal. It had simply been a loss. His family standing took that loss
exactly as it was.

I would confirm this fact again and again, throughout this book. The
Roll never broke the law, not once. The corner clause had been there
from the beginning. It had simply been designed so that nothing would
be left there. What turned that design back wasn't the Roll — it was one
person. And that person paid the price with his own body.

One day I saw Medon passing near the corner.

He stopped and watched the people gleaning. I tensed for a moment,
afraid he might recognize us.

He found me. He didn't come closer. He only nodded slightly, from a
distance. I nodded back.

I don't know what he was thinking in that moment. I never asked. I only
remember that the time he stood there watching wasn't a short one. He
must have seen, that day, for the first time, someone else walking
through the door he had refused.

He walked on before long. He didn't look back.

I told Pikra about it that night.

"…I saw Medon. Near the corner."

Pikra said nothing for a moment.

"…He must know now too," Pikra said, finally. "What he didn't do."

"…Do you hate him?"

"…No," Pikra said. "I told him, that day, in that room. I don't resent
you. That was true then, and it's true now."

Listening to that, I felt that what Pikra carried toward Medon was a
little different from what I carried. I still felt some leftover
disappointment, whenever I thought of him. Pikra didn't seem to. She had
already accepted, in the way of someone who has lived long, that right
and good are not the same thing.

A few days later, I met a girl at the corner.

She looked a year or two younger than me. She didn't know how to glean
and was struggling. She kept pulling the whole stalk and breaking it.

"…Try it like this," I said, coming closer to show her. "Leave the
stalk. Just take the ear. Gently."

The girl copied what I did. She failed a few times, and then got the
knack of it.

"…Thank you," the girl said.

I felt something strange, hearing that. Not long ago, I had been the one
who knew nothing at this corner. Now I was the one showing someone else.

That day I found one more reason, the true reason I kept gleaning at
this corner. I could stay here and show something to those who came,
knowing nothing, just as I once had. That wasn't a place the Roll had
given me. It was a place I'd found for myself.

As autumn deepened, more people came to the corner.

I didn't know most of their names. They didn't know mine either. We
were only people who happened to bend down in the same place at the same
hour.

One day I saw a child among them. About eight years old. He'd found one
ear of grain and was carrying it to show his mother. His mother stroked
his head.

Watching that, I thought of Lytron. He didn't know that child. He never
would. That child, too, would go his whole life never knowing where the
grain in his hand had come from. That didn't sadden me. I thought,
rather, that it was right. That the name of the one who paid the price
doesn't remain — that was the oldest rule of the corner clause.

I told Lytron about that child that evening.

"…Did you see it?" he asked.

"…Yes."

"…That child doesn't know what I did," he said. Not in a hurt tone.

"…Do you want them to know?" I asked.

He thought for a moment.

"…No," he said. "I think it's right to leave it as it is. Not knowing."

I knew that answer came from the same place as the day he'd given up
his standing. He had paid a price and still didn't want that price to be
known. I had never seen that kind of mind before then.

That night I recalled the corner again. The day I first came and
gathered a few handfuls of trampled stalks, and now, this day, when I
filled a sack and went home. The same place. A completely different
place.

I still remember these two corners side by side, even now. One where
the law was kept exactly and nothing at all was left. The other where
the law was kept just as exactly and something was left. The law didn't
make that difference. The law was exactly the same in both corners. What
made the difference was what one person decided to give up, from what
was his.

I think that fact explains this whole city. The Roll didn't change. The
regulations didn't change. Only one thing changed — a single person's
choice. And that one thing changed a single corner into an entirely
different place.

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. What the Names Mean

_Previously_ — After the loss rate changed to 3 percent, grain began,
for the first time, to be left at the corner. Those without standing
gathered there. I no longer needed to glean, and kept going anyway.
Pikra said she didn't know what my name meant, either. "Mena would
probably know."

It was a few days before Mena left.

I went to see her that day. Not for any particular reason. I knew she
would soon be leaving this city, and I wanted to see her once more
before she went.

She was in her room, sorting through papers. Months of records had
piled up. I looked at that stack of paper and felt freshly surprised. I
hadn't known she'd written so much.

The room was quiet. Late afternoon light came through the window. Only
the sound of pages turning, now and then a sound of her setting one
aside. I liked that sound. I hadn't sat in a room that quiet often,
since coming to this city.

"…Getting ready to leave?" I asked.

"…Yes," Mena said. Her hands kept moving.

I sat beside her. For a long while I said nothing, just watched her
sort the papers.

"…I wanted to ask you something," I said finally.

"…What is it."

"…My name," I said. "Pikra said you'd know the meaning."

Mena stopped her hands. She looked at me. There was something in that
look. I couldn't have named it, then.

"…I know it," Mena said. "Why are you asking now?"

"…No reason," I said. "I was just curious."

Mena looked at me a moment longer, then set the papers aside.

"…Sit down," she said. "This is something to hear properly seated."

I settled myself.

"…Xene," Mena said. "This name comes from Greek. ξένη. Xenē."

"…What does it mean?"

"…Stranger," Mena said. "A foreigner. Someone who doesn't belong to
this city."

I nodded, hearing that. It didn't surprise me. I really had been a
stranger. I'd come from the outer settlement; I had never belonged to
this city.

"…That sounds right," I said. "I really was a stranger."

Mena looked at me steadily.

"…But that isn't all of it," she said.

"…What do you mean, not all of it?"

"…The same word," Mena said, "carries another meaning, at the same
time. Guest. That's what it means, too."

I didn't understand at first.

"…Stranger and guest at once? How can that be the same word?"

"…To people of the old world, it was the same word," Mena said, slowly.
"When a stranger came to your door, there was an obligation to take them
into your house without knowing who they were. They called it xenia.
The taking-in of a traveler. It was a sacred obligation. You could not
turn a stranger away, because—"

Mena paused for a moment.

"…Because a god might come to you in the shape of a traveler."

I sat a long while without a word, after hearing that. Mena didn't
press. She seemed to know exactly how to hold a silence like that.

"…So those people," I finally managed, "weren't afraid of strangers,
but careful with them? Was that because—"

"…Yes," Mena said. "Because you never knew. The one standing at your
door might be nothing but a passing traveler. Or he might not be. There
was no way to be sure. So they treated everyone the same way."

"…Wouldn't it have been easier to simply turn away anyone you couldn't
be sure of?"

"…It would have," Mena said. "But those people chose not to live that
way. Since there was no way to be sure, they chose to take everyone in
instead. That's xenia."

I said nothing for a long while after hearing that.

I remember that moment exactly, even now. The air in the room, the
smell of the paper, the way Mena's voice dropped a little lower. I
couldn't move from where I sat.

"…So," Mena went on, "your name says two things at once. You were a
stranger. Someone who didn't belong to this city. Someone with no
standing. And at the same time—"

"…A guest," I managed. Barely.

"…Yes," Mena said. "Someone who must be received. Someone the receiver
is obligated toward."

I couldn't speak for a long time.

I had lived sixteen years knowing this name only as my own name. I
never knew it held the meaning of stranger. And once I knew, something
struck me strangely. I had been a stranger from the very first day I
arrived in this city. From the moment I was classified as zero standing
at the gate. And that strangeness had, at the same time, been an
obligation someone else owed — to receive me. I only learned that, that
day.

"…Then," I asked, "the people who took me in — Pikra, Lytron, the
administrator — was it obligation, for them?"

Mena shook her head.

"…For some it was obligation. For some it wasn't," Mena said. "Pikra
was more than obligation. She took you in as family. Lytron —"

Mena thought for a moment.

"…Lytron is a different matter," she said.

"…What do you mean?"

Mena looked at me.

"…I'll tell you that name too, now," she said. "You'll need to know it
eventually, and I think now is the time."

I held my breath.

"…Lytron," Mena said. "λύτρον. Greek."

"…What does it mean?"

"…The price paid to get someone back," Mena said. "Ransom, is another
way it's translated. The price paid to buy back someone, or something."

I couldn't breathe, hearing that.

I couldn't say a single word, in that moment. Truly, not one. Mena
didn't press me. She simply sat and waited.

I thought of how many times I had said his name. In the field, outside
the office, on sleepless nights. Every time I said it, I'd thought I was
simply saying a person's name. A sound. I never knew an answer had
already been sitting inside that sound.

His name had already been saying, from the moment he was born, what he
would go on to do. The price paid to get someone back. He had lived out
his own name. Without ever knowing it himself.

"…Isn't that strange?" I finally managed. My voice cracked. "How can a
name know something like that, in advance?"

Mena shook her head.

"…I don't know either," she said. "I'm not someone who explains things
like that. I'm only someone who writes them down. But I can say this. I
knew these names before I ever came to this city. And over these past
months, I watched them line up, exactly, with what actually happened
here."

"…Is it a coincidence?"

Mena looked at me for a moment. She didn't answer.

I remember that silence exactly, even now. Mena wasn't someone who
judged. She never told me it was a coincidence, and never told me it
wasn't. She only left the fact where it was. The rest, I think, she left
for me to see for myself.

"…Then why tell me now?" I asked. "You could have told me sooner."

Mena went back to sorting her papers as she spoke.

"…If you know the meaning first," she said, "you already know the
ending before the thing happens. If you'd known, ahead of time, that
Lytron would give up his standing, you wouldn't have been able to see
that moment the way you saw it."

"…How do you mean?"

"…You'd have watched it as someone living up to their name," Mena said.
"Like watching fate play itself out. I didn't want that for you. I
wanted you to see that moment as a person actually choosing something.
Not because a name told him to."

I had to sit a long while, turning that over.

I know it now. Mena hadn't hidden a prophecy from me. She had given me
the chance to see a real choice. When Lytron signed that day, it wasn't
his name leading him. He was living up to what his name meant. The order
matters. I understand that order now, precisely.

I said nothing for a long while.

Mena didn't rush me. She just kept sorting papers. The room held only
the sound of turning pages.

After a long while, I finally managed to ask.

"…My name too — did I live up to what it means?"

Mena stopped and looked at me.

"…Do you think you did?" she asked back.

I had no answer to that.

I still haven't fully answered that question, even these many years
later. I was a stranger. And I was received. Both of those had been
inside my name from the start. I don't know, even now, whether I made
that happen, or whether I simply lived out what my name had always
meant.

I know only one thing. I never heard my own name the same way again,
after leaving Mena's room that day. Xene. A name that means stranger and
guest, both at once. Every time I say it now, I hear both, at the same
time.

I left Mena's room and went straight to the field.

The whole way there, I wondered whether to tell Lytron. It felt like it
would be wrong to tell him. It also felt like it would be wrong not to.

He was tidying something near the corner. He looked up and saw me.

"…Something wrong?" he asked. He seemed to read it in my face at once.

"…I heard the meaning of my name, from Mena," I said. "My name, and—"

I stopped there.

"…Mine too?" he asked.

I nodded.

He was quiet for a moment.

"…What did she say?" he asked. His voice was quieter than usual.

"…The price paid to get someone back," I said. "Ransom."

Lytron stood a long time after hearing that. He didn't even set down
what was in his hands. He just stayed frozen in that posture.

"…Did you know?" I asked.

"…No," he said. "I had no idea."

I studied his face. It was surprised, but not the kind of surprise I'd
expected. Not fear, not the weight of a burden. Something quieter than
that.

"…That's strange," he said, finally.

"…What is?"

"…I told you, that day I signed," he said, "that I'd tell you the
reason later. Remember?"

"…Yes."

"…I still can't say the reason exactly, even now," he said. "I just
wanted to, in that moment. That was all of it. But hearing that my name
meant this, from the start—"

He stopped.

"…What is it," I asked.

"…I don't feel like I invented anything," he said. "I thought, that
day, that I'd made some great decision. Now I wonder whether I just did,
late, what my name had always meant to do."

I wanted to argue with that. I wanted to tell him his choice had been
truly his, not just his name playing itself out.

"…That was still you," I said. "The name didn't sign for you."

Lytron looked at me. And smiled, a little, for the first time.

"…I suppose so," he said. "Is that what Mena said? That the name didn't
make me do it?"

"…Yes. Something like that."

"…I think she's right," he said. "I didn't know my name. So the name
couldn't have led me. I just lived the way I lived, and later on, it
turned out the name had already been standing there."

I thought that was exactly right.

We stood together a while without speaking. An evening wind passed
through the field.

"…Aren't you curious about my name's meaning too?" I asked. Meant as a
joke.

"…I am," he said. Seriously.

I told him. Stranger and guest, at once.

He listened, and looked at me for a long time.

"…It suits you," he said.

"…What does?"

"…That you're a person living both those things at once," he said. "The
first time I saw you, I just thought you were a stranger. I wondered why
you kept coming out to a place with nothing in it. But now—"

He paused.

"…And now?"

"…Now you feel like the least strange person in this field," he said.

I laughed at that. It had been a long time since I'd laughed.

That night, back home, I told Pikra everything as well.

Pikra listened quietly. She was silent a long while, even after I'd
finished.

"…I should have asked about Mother's name too," I said. "While I was
with Mena."

"…Didn't you ask?"

"…You said you didn't want to know," I said.

Pikra smiled a little.

"…That's right, I did say that," Pikra said. "And I still feel the same
way. I want to hear my own name just as my own name. I don't need to
know what it means."

"…I was glad to know mine," I said. "Strange as that is."

"…You've always been that kind of child," Pikra said. "The kind who
wants to know. Sometimes I like that about you, and sometimes it
worries me."

I smiled a little at that.

"…And today?"

Pikra looked at me a long while before answering.

"…Today it's the good kind," Pikra said.

I lay awake late into that night. Xene. Stranger and guest. Lytron. The
price paid to get someone back. I laid the two names side by side and
thought. One was the name of a person being received. The other was the
name of a person paying a price. I couldn't find an answer, that night,
to whether the two names had met by chance or not. I still can't find
it, now. I know only this. The two names really did meet. That isn't a
meaning on a page. It's something I watched happen with my own eyes.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. The Standard

_Previously_ — Mena told me what the two names meant. Xene — stranger,
and at the same time, guest. Lytron — the price paid to get someone
back. I lay awake that night, unable to say whether the two names had
met by chance.

The administrator came to find me not long after that.

He had never come to us first before. We had always gone to him. I was
briefly startled, seeing him standing at our door.

"…What brings you here?" I asked.

"…I have something to explain," he said. "Do you have a moment?"

We sat on the bench by the door. The scalding heat of summer had already
taken a step back. The wood of the bench was cool to the touch, and a
few persimmons were ripening red in the corner of the yard. Pikra came
and sat with us.

"…Miss Xene's case," the administrator said, "has come to remain in the
Roll as a record."

"…A record?"

His tone was, as always, the same. A voice reading off a document,
nearly without inflection. There was something in that flatness that
caught at me. What he was speaking of had been the heaviest season of my
life, and the moment it left his mouth, it became a single entry.

"…Precedent would be the more precise word," the administrator said.
"This is the first case in which the standing-transfer procedure was
actually used. Since the founding of the Roll."

I didn't understand the words at first.

"…Why does that need to be explained to me?"

"…Because this case will now serve as a reference," he said. "If anyone
else wishes to use this procedure, the office will now be able to show
them this case. How the procedure actually proceeds. What paperwork is
required. What happens after the transfer."

"…What am I supposed to do?" I asked.

"…Nothing," he said. "I only came to inform you. And this is also to
ask your consent. Whether you agree to have this remain as a case
record."

Something struck me strangely, hearing that.

"…And if I don't agree?"

"…The name can be withheld," the administrator said. "The case itself
will remain, but your name won't be attached to it."

I thought for a moment.

"…The name can remain," I said.

Pikra looked at me.

"…Why?" Pikra asked.

I didn't have an answer ready. I needed to gather my thoughts.

"…I think," I said, slowly, "I don't want this to have happened to me
alone. If someone else ends up in this same place someday, I think it's
better if this exists."

The administrator looked at me steadily.

"…May I ask why?" he asked.

I thought a moment, then said,

"…Medon," I said. "He didn't do it. But maybe the next person who stands
where he stood — because of me — might decide differently."

The administrator was quiet a moment, hearing that.

"…That's an interesting reason," he said. "The Roll wasn't designed that
way. A case is kept for procedure's sake, not to persuade anyone."

"…Then it won't persuade anyone," I said.

"…Probably not," he said. "The Roll doesn't change a person's mind. It
only shows a case. Whatever the person who sees it decides to do with it
— that's theirs."

Those words stayed with me. The Roll, once again, promised me nothing.
It only left a door a little easier to see. Whether to walk through it
remained, still, entirely, each person's own.

A few days later I went to the office myself and looked at the record.

My case was summarized on the screen. Name, standing before the
transfer, value received, the one who transferred it, the date
processed. Below it, in small print: First recorded use.

I looked at that phrase for a long time.

The sentences on the screen were short and dry. A name, a number, a
date. I thought of Mena, looking at them. She had told me once that a
single line she'd written had traveled through other cities and come
back meaning something entirely different. I'd thought, at the time,
that was someone else's problem. Now my own name, too, had been reduced
to a few words and set down on a screen. That those words might someday
travel through other mouths, in other contexts, changed from what they
were — for the first time, that thought frightened me.

I felt something strange, at that moment. A few months earlier, I hadn't
even existed in the Roll. I didn't have a name. Now my name had become
the Roll's standard. I know exactly what happened in between. I hadn't
done anything remarkable. I had only gone out to the field every day,
and someone had given up what was theirs.

The administrator showed me one more document.

"…This is still a draft," he said, "but we've drawn up a new
information sheet on the standing-transfer procedure, based on this
case. There was almost no guidance on it before."

I read the sheet. Procedure, required documents, expected outcome. At
the bottom, one line read:

Reference: the Xene case (Registration, Chapter 41).

I laughed, reading that. I hadn't expected my story to end up as a
footnote in an official document.

"…What will this change?" I asked the administrator.

"…I don't know," he said. "I'll be honest with you. I can't promise this
procedure will see much use. There may well be more Medons than not."

"…Then why go to all this trouble? Making up an information sheet and
all."

The administrator thought for a moment.

"…Because it's my job," he said. "I'm a man who keeps the law
precisely. But if a door the law has left open sits unseen and untended,
that's a failure on my part too, I think."

I was a little surprised by that. For the first time, I saw something
human inside the administrator. He wasn't only a cold man. In his own
way, within precision, he was doing his best.

About a month later, a stranger knocked at our door.

A woman. A few years older than me. Her clothes were worn, her face
tired.

"…Are you Xene?" she asked.

"…Yes."

"…I'm sorry to bother you," she said. "I heard about you, at the
office. That you'd had your standing transferred to you."

Something occurred to me at that.

I brought her inside. Pikra brought tea.

"…My mother-in-law," she said, "has low standing. I have a little more.
I wondered if I could do what you did. But no one seemed to know how."

"…Did you go to the office?"

"…I did," she said. "They only said the procedure was complicated.
Nothing more. Then I heard about you. The first case."

I felt something strange, hearing that. That my story had traveled all
the way to someone's door — it still didn't feel real.

"…I'll tell you what I know," I said.

I explained what had happened, step by step. The application, the
signature, the threshold. She wrote it down on paper.

"…But," I said, carefully, "you're giving up your own standing. It
isn't an easy decision."

She nodded.

"…I know," she said. "That woman raised me. My husband lost his father
young, and she raised him alone."

I couldn't help thinking of Lytron, hearing that. He hadn't raised me.
We hadn't even known each other very long. And still he'd given up what
was his. This woman, at least, had a clear reason. I envied that a
little, and at the same time understood, more than ever, how much
harder Lytron's act had been to explain.

After she left, Pikra spoke.

"…There'll be more," Pikra said. "People like her."

"…Why do you think that?"

"…Word doesn't stop once it starts," Pikra said. "Whether you want it
to or not, you've become a certain kind of name in this city now. The
one who knows the way."

That felt heavy, hearing it.

"…I'm not someone who knows the way," I said. "I'm just someone who
received."

"…That's the same thing," Pikra said. "Only someone who's received can
show another person the way. Someone who's never received doesn't even
know the door exists."

A few days later, I heard, through the office, that the woman had
actually gone through with the procedure. I didn't know the details.
Whether it had succeeded, or whether she'd stopped partway, like Medon
— I never learned.

I never did hear the rest of that story. That was how this city was.
One person's choice opened a door for another, but whether that person
walked through it was never reported back. I found that frustrating,
and, at the same time, somehow right. Not every choice needed to be
reported back to me.

I told Lytron about it that evening.

"…What you did became a precedent," I said. "My case."

Lytron looked briefly surprised.

"…Me?"

"…None of this exists without you," I said.

He shook his head.

"…I only did one thing," he said. "Becoming a precedent isn't up to me
to decide. That's for the Roll — or the people who come after — to
decide."

I thought that was exactly like him. He was never a man who measured
the size of what he'd done.

"…Can I ask you something?" I said.

"…Yes."

"…What would Medon say, if he saw this record?"

Lytron thought for a moment.

"…I don't know," he said. "But I don't think seeing it would change his
mind. He already knew everything he needed to know, that day, when he
decided."

"…Then what use is this record?"

Lytron looked out over the field.

"…It probably isn't left there to change Medon," he said. "It's for
someone else. Someone who doesn't even know this procedure exists yet.
Like me. If the administrator hadn't mentioned it, in passing, I never
would have known it existed either."

I thought again, hearing that, about the way this city worked.

The Roll persuades no one. It only records. What's recorded remains for
the next person to see. And what that person does with it is always,
always, left to them. I understand now that this is how this city
works. There's no compulsion. Only a door made a little easier to see.

I found that both sad and fortunate. Sad, because even with this
record, the next Medon might still not walk through the door. Fortunate,
because with this record, the next Lytron will at least know the door is
there.

I went out to the corner late that night. "He" was still there.

"…Did you hear?" I asked. "My case became a standard in the Roll."

He nodded.

"…I know," he said.

"…You knew that too?" I asked, smiling. He always already knew things
no one had told him.

"…I heard it from the administrator, this time," he said. Almost like
a joke. He rarely spoke that way.

I sat down beside him.

"…It's a strange thought," I said. "My story is a line in this city's
rulebook now. And I'm still just myself. Going out to the field,
gleaning grain. Nothing's changed, it feels like, and at the same time
something's completely changed."

He stopped his hands and looked at me.

"…That's not strange," he said. "That's how a big thing always is. To
the person living through it, every day feels the same as the last.
Only later, looking back, do you see that a line's been drawn somewhere
between those days."

"…Where does it get drawn?"

"…No one knows," he said. "No one knows, in the moment the line is
drawn, that it's a line at all. You only see it later."

I let that pass by that night. I hear it differently now. That might
not have been a thing he only said about this city. Every time he spoke
that way, I felt his words came from somewhere larger than this one
city. I thought, at the time, that it was only my imagination.

"…One more thing I wanted to ask," I said. "Does a standard like this
exist in other cities too? Someone like me?"

He didn't answer for a long time. His hands kept moving.

"…There probably is," he finally said. "This probably isn't something
that only happened in this city."

I found that answer strangely comforting. I couldn't explain why.

I lay awake that night, thinking for a long while about the fact that a
name that had never been in the Roll had become the Roll's standard.

When I first came to this city, I had zero standing. I existed, but I
wasn't counted. Now my name remains, a single line, in this city's
rulebook. In between, I hadn't done very much. I had only gone out to
the same place, every day. It wasn't I who had paid the price.

And still I think I understand, a little now, why my name became the
standard. It didn't become the standard because of anything I did. It
became the standard because of what someone did for me. I want to set
that down exactly, in this book. This standard isn't mine. I was only
the place where that price came to rest.

I wonder, sometimes, whether that footnote — "Reference: the Xene
case" — will still be there in this city's rulebook, years from now,
decades from now. Whether someone will read it and go looking for my
name. I don't know the answer. But I know exactly what lay behind that
footnote. One signature. A few seconds. And the quiet ordinary days of
one person that went on, after those few seconds ended. A footnote
can't hold all of that. That's why I write this record.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. I Hadn't Done Anything Wrong

_Previously_ — My case became a precedent in the Roll. The first
recorded use of the standing-transfer procedure. The office added it
to their information sheet. A name that had never been in the Roll had
become the Roll's standard.

As autumn deepened, Lytron and I began walking together often.

Not for any particular purpose. From the field to the office, from the
office to the market. Our errands simply started overlapping more.

That day was the same. We were walking along the edge of a field the
harvest had already left. Dry stubble crunched underfoot at every step.
The air was turning cool.

It was that time when the morning and evening air had grown noticeably
different. The field lay empty, the harvest done, and on some days a
thin frost sat on the bare stubble.

A few days before, he'd told me he had finally had a long conversation
with his father. Not what you'd call a reconciliation. Only a few words
had passed between them.

"…My father asked me something," he'd said, then. "Whether I regretted
it."

"…What did you say?"

"…I said I didn't know," he said. "I didn't want to lie."

"…And your father?"

"…He didn't say anything," he said. "But we ate dinner together that
evening. The first time, since it happened."

I felt I understood how large that small change was for him.

"…You seem better lately," I said.

"…I've gotten used to it," he said. "A few months ago, every day felt
unfamiliar. Now it's just my life."

"…Standing in line, too?"

"…That too," he said, smiling a little. "It'd feel stranger now, not to
stand in it."

I found that both bitter and, in its way, fortunate.

We walked a while without speaking.

"…There's something I keep thinking about," Lytron said, suddenly.

"…What?"

He stopped walking. I stopped too.

"…Do you remember," he said, "the first time we met, something I said?"

I searched my memory.

"…You asked why I kept coming out to a place with nothing here."

"…Not that," he said. "After that. Something I said, not to you — to
myself."

I thought a moment, and remembered.

"…I haven't done anything wrong," I said. "That's what you said."

Lytron nodded.

"…Yes," he said. "I meant it, truly, when I said it then. And I still
don't think those words were wrong. I really hadn't done anything
wrong."

I watched him quietly.

"…And?" I asked.

He looked off toward the field. The corner was faintly visible, far
off.

"…I hadn't done anything wrong," he said again. A different voice, this
time.

"…So why wasn't that enough?"

The silence stretched. Wind swept through the stubble. He wasn't waiting
for an answer. It was the face of a man who had simply set the question
up in the open air. I felt an urge to say something, anything —
something comforting, some plausible reason. I held it back. I knew,
somehow, that such words had no place here.

I had no answer to that question.

I understood, in that moment, that he had pulled his own sentence back
out and set it before himself for the first time. I remembered the day
I'd first heard those words. Then, he'd stood surrounded by his own
house, his own room, the things he'd had since birth. His world was
complete, then. Those words had been the door that closed to protect
that complete world. Now he was asking the same words as a question. The
words were identical. The direction had changed entirely. Then, they
closed a door. Now, they knocked on one.

"…What do you mean, asking that?" I said, carefully.

"…I hadn't done anything wrong," he said, slowly. "From the day I was
born until now, I kept every rule the Roll set. I paid my taxes, I never
harmed anyone, I tried to give back as much as I received. And yet—"

He paused.

"…And yet something was lacking," he said. "I didn't know what, for a
long time. I thought not having done anything wrong and being enough
were the same thing."

I felt I ought to say something back. Nothing came.

I set this moment down more carefully than anything else in this book.
Lytron wasn't asking me for an answer that day. That's how it felt. He
was only speaking aloud, for the first time, a question that had circled
inside him a long while.

"…I don't know," I finally managed. "I don't know the answer either."

He looked at me. He didn't look disappointed.

"…It's fine," he said. "I wasn't hoping for an answer, either."

"…Then why did you say it to me?" I asked.

Lytron thought for a moment.

"…Because you're the only person I could say it to," he said.

"…What do you mean?"

"…You never once asked anything of me," he said. "That surprised me,
at first. People usually want something, especially from someone with
what I have. You never did. You just came out here, every day."

I didn't know what to say to that.

"…I thought that was a strangely honest way to be," he said. "So I
think I can speak honestly to you, too. Even without an answer."

We started walking again.

"…I keep thinking about that question lately," he said. "Why wasn't
that enough, when I hadn't done anything wrong. The more I try to find
an answer, the more certain I become of one thing only — that it's a
question with no answer."

"…Doesn't that make it harder?" I asked.

"…It's hard," he admitted, plainly. "But it's a different kind of hard
than before, strangely."

"…Different how?"

He stopped walking again to think.

"…Before, when something felt wrong, it felt like there was something
to fix," he said. "Try harder, keep more rules, and it would resolve.
This time it isn't that. I don't need to do anything better. I just—"

He stopped again.

"…Just what?" I asked.

"…There's some way to be all right, being not enough," he said. "I
think that's what I had to learn. But I still don't know exactly what
that way is."

Something went cold in me at that.

I thought, that day, that Lytron was searching for an answer. Looking
back now, he wasn't searching for an answer. He was learning to bear a
question. Those are two entirely different things.

"…I can't give you an answer," I said again. "Really."

"…I know," he said. "But I wanted to ask, anyway. Anyone."

We started walking again.

"…I see the Roll differently, these days," he said, suddenly.

"…How?"

"…I used to think the Roll was fair," he said. "Cut for wrongdoing,
raised for doing right. I believed it was exact. And I believed exact
was the same as good."

"…And now?"

"…I still don't think the Roll is inexact," he said. "It really is
exact. It's never once been wrong. But now I know. Exact and enough are
different things."

I thought that summed up this entire city.

"…What do you mean?" I asked.

"…The Roll knew, precisely, that I hadn't done anything wrong," he
said. "That was my score. An exact score. And that exact score wasn't
enough to register you. The Roll calculated that lack precisely too. The
Roll was never wrong about anything. There was simply a place where
exact wasn't enough."

I thought a long time about that. I felt, for the first time that day,
that Lytron was looking more deeply into this city's rules than I had.
He had lived his whole life inside those rules, and now, from within
them, he was seeing their limit.

The evening light stretched long across the field.

"…There's one thing I can say," I offered, carefully.

"…What?"

"…Hearing that question," I said, "I like you better now than the day
you first said those words."

"…Why?"

"…Then, those words closed a door," I said. "Now they sound like they're
opening one. I don't know which door. But something that was shut is
open now. That much I think is true."

Lytron said nothing to that. He only looked out over the field for a
long while.

We parted that day with no answer to that question.

I told Pikra about it that night.

Pikra stopped trimming vegetables and sat with it a while.

"…He's finally asked it," Pikra said.

"…You knew he was carrying that question?"

"…I guessed," Pikra said. "Everyone who's given up something of their
own meets that question, once. I met it too."

I was surprised.

"…You did too?"

"…I asked it differently than he did," Pikra said. "I asked, what did I
do wrong. After I lost my husband and my sons. He asks, I did nothing
wrong, so why. The direction's opposite. But it's the same place the
question comes from."

"…Same place?"

"…The place where you've met something you can't understand," Pikra
said. "I lost without doing wrong. He was lacking without doing wrong.
Neither one resolves with reason."

I saw, hearing that, that the two questions faced each other. Pikra had
been wrongly deprived; Lytron had rightfully possessed. And yet both of
them, in the end, stood before the same wall. The wall where the
question why* gives no answer.*

"…Did you find an answer to that question?" I asked.

Pikra shook her head.

"…No," Pikra said. "I never found it. I found something else,
instead."

"…What?"

"…Someone to sit with," Pikra said. "The answer never came. But a
person came. Like you."

A few days later, I told "him" about it too.

He was moving his hands at the corner of the field when I told him. He
stopped.

"…Lytron asked something like that," I said. "He hadn't done anything
wrong, and asked why that wasn't enough."

He said nothing for a long while. He only looked down at the few
stalks in his hand.

"…You didn't give him an answer?" he finally asked. I couldn't tell if
he was asking me, or asking himself.

"…No. I didn't know one either."

"…Good," he said.

I found that strange, hearing it.

"…Why good? I didn't help him at all."

He looked at me. Something was different in his face from usual. I
didn't want to call it sorrow. It was something older than sorrow.
Something settled deeper down.

"…Some questions," he said, "are better shared than answered."

He said nothing more after that. I asked nothing more either.

I remembered his face a long time, that day. I didn't know it then.
That the question may not have been unfamiliar to him, either. I only
watched him, quietly moving his hands, as he always did.

I felt the temptation, more than once, writing this chapter, to write
in an answer. Anyone reading this book will want to know it, too. Why
wasn't it enough, when he'd done nothing wrong. I could have written
that I know. I decided not to. Lytron didn't find that answer that day,
and neither did I. This record isn't only a record of what we found. It
is also, honestly, a record of what we never did.

I told Mena about it, the next day. She had nearly finished packing to
leave.

"…Lytron asked something strange," I said. "That he'd done nothing
wrong, so why wasn't it enough."

Mena stopped and looked at me. It was the face of someone hearing a
question she'd known a long time.

"…What did you say back?" she asked.

"…Nothing. I had no answer."

"…I actually wanted to make something up," I added. "A few plausible
things came to mind. Comforting words. But that would have been me
pretending to know what I didn't. So I held it back."

Mena nodded.

"…Good," she said. He had said the same thing.

"…Why does everyone say that?" I asked. A little frustrated. "Why is not
answering the good thing to do?"

Mena went on sorting her things as she answered.

"…I've traveled through many cities," she said. "I've seen many people
ask that question. And I've seen many people rush to answer it. A hasty
answer is always wrong. Because that isn't what the person actually
wanted to hear."

"…Then what was it?"

"…Confirmation that their question wasn't strange," Mena said. "You
gave him that. That was enough."

I felt some relief, hearing that. I had believed, until that day, that
I'd given him nothing at all. Mena told me that wasn't true. I wanted to
believe her.

I know only one thing for certain. The day he first spoke that question
aloud, his face looked at ease. Not the face of a man who'd found an
answer. The face of a man who no longer had to carry the question
alone.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. The One Who Gleaned Beside Me

_Previously_ — Lytron pulled his own line back out, for the first
time, as a question. "I hadn't done anything wrong. So why wasn't that
enough?" No one gave an answer. Not me, not Pikra, not "him."

The day before Mena left, "he" disappeared with her.

I thought of the day I first met him. He'd been just another quiet
hired hand. I hadn't singled him out. I'd only thought him a man of few
words.

Then the day he began gleaning beside me. I hadn't thought much of it,
then, either. Just someone working the same hour, the same corner.

And then the day I noticed there was always a little more, in the spot
where he worked. From that day I began to see him differently. Exactly
how, I could never quite say, even to myself.

Looking back over these months, I keep forgetting exactly when he
began standing beside me. It feels as though he had always been there,
and at the same time it feels as though he appeared, one day, out of
nowhere. I think both feel true. I still cannot say for certain, even
now, whether he had been there from the start and I was only slow to
notice him, or whether he truly did begin standing at my side one day.

I didn't notice, at first. I went out to the field that morning and he
wasn't there. I thought he was only late. He still hadn't come by
midday. He hadn't come by evening.

I asked another of the hired hands.

"…Have you seen him today?"

"…Who?"

"…The one who always worked at that far end. The quiet one."

The man thought for a moment, then shook his head.

"…Oh, him. He wasn't here today, now that you mention it."

Something cold went through me in that moment. The field without him
felt unfamiliar. His usual spot, empty now, suddenly looked enormous.
Even the sound of the wind passing through that empty place sounded
different.

He didn't come the next day, either.

I went to the field office and asked. I didn't even know his name. I'd
only ever called him "he." I wasn't the only one. No one else seemed to
know exactly what to call him either.

"…The quiet one who worked at the far end," I said to the clerk. "He
hasn't come today either."

The clerk checked the roster.

"…He's off the hired-hand list," the clerk said. "A request to leave the
work came in two days ago."

"…Where's he going?"

"…That's not recorded here," the clerk said. "Hired hands aren't
obligated to state that."

I sat alone at the corner that evening.

The spot he always sat in was empty. I looked at that empty spot for a
long time.

That was the first time I understood he had always been someone free
to leave. I had come to think of him as part of this field. Someone
always there. But he had always, in truth, been someone passing
through. I had forgotten that.

That night I told Lytron he was gone.

"…That quiet one?" he asked. "The one at the end of the field."

"…Yes."

Lytron thought for a moment.

"…That's strange," he said. "I hardly knew him, and hearing he's gone
still leaves something strange in me."

"…What way strange?"

"…Like I've missed something important," he said. "That I never got to
say a proper goodbye."

I felt the same way.

"…He said something to me, once," Lytron said. "That day I reset the
loss rate and stayed alone in the field afterward. He passed by and said
one thing."

"…What did he say?"

"…'You did well,'" he said. "That was all. But it was the most support
I felt that whole day. Not my father, not the administrator — no one
else said anything like that to me."

Hearing that, I understood for the first time that he had quietly
passed through more lives than just mine. I had thought of him only as
part of my own story. He had been much wider than that.

Pikra stopped her hands too, hearing the news.

"…He's gone?"

"…Yes."

"…I saw him once too," Pikra said. "Around the time you first started
going out to the corner. The quiet one working at the end. I just
thought, then — there's a man who doesn't talk."

"…And now?"

Pikra looked out the window a moment.

"…Now I'm not sure," Pikra said. "But it's strange — hearing he's gone
leaves something empty in me. And I hardly ever saw him at all."

I saw, in that moment, that this regret wasn't only mine. No one had
really known him well. And still no one heard the news of his leaving
without some regret.

On the third day, I saw him one last time.

Near the gate. He was carrying a small pack. Not much. He was, as
always, a man traveling light.

"…Are you leaving?" I asked, coming closer.

He looked at me and smiled.

"…Yes," he said. "Today."

I didn't know what to say, in that moment. I had gleaned beside this man
for months in this city, and never once prepared myself for a goodbye.

"…Where are you going?" I asked.

"…Somewhere else," he said. The same vague answer as always.

"…Another field?"

"…Probably," he said. "Wherever there are people who need to glean."

The words sounded strange to me. He'd never once named a place. I used
to think that was just his manner. Now it felt like something else.

"…Can I ask your name?" I asked. The first time I'd asked, in all these
months.

He looked at me and smiled.

"…Why ask now?" he asked back.

"…I thought you wouldn't tell me, if I asked," I said, honestly.

"…That's right," he said. Gently. "I won't."

I wasn't hurt by that answer. It felt, if anything, exactly like him.

"…Then what should I call you? Keep calling you 'he'?"

"…That'd be best," he said. "That suits me exactly."

"…Pikra and Lytron say hello," I said. "They were sorry they couldn't
see you themselves."

"…Give them my regards," he said. "I'm sorry too."

"…Lytron told me — the day he reset the loss rate and stayed alone in
the field, you passed by and said 'you did well,' just that one thing."

He smiled a little.

"…I remember," he said. "His face looked hard, that day."

"…Did you go by others too, the same way?"

"…Sometimes," he said. "There isn't much I can do. But I can say one
thing."

I felt something old in that answer, some habit worn deep. He seemed a
man who had always gone about this way. Not only in this city. For much
longer, through many more places. Through several seasons, several
cities, moving in the same manner.

We sat a moment on a bench near the gate.

"…I wanted to ask you something," I said. A question I'd carried a long
while.

"…What is it?"

I hesitated. A question I hadn't managed to ask, all these months.

"…At your spot," I said, "there was always a little more. Beside me. I
don't think that was chance."

He said nothing. He only smiled.

"…Did you do it on purpose?" I asked. "Did you drop grain there, so I
could gather it?"

He looked at me for a long time. Something warm crossed his face.

"…Why do you think that?" he asked back.

"…At first I thought it was just chance," I said. "But it happened too
many times. Always at my spot."

He laughed. He didn't answer.

"…You're not going to answer?" I asked.

"…Well," he said. "If I said yes, what would change?"

I had no answer to that.

"…I'm just curious," I finally managed. "I just wanted to know."

He looked up at the sky for a moment. Evening was coming on.

"…Some people are the kind who spill," he said. "That's just their way.
But a person who spills doesn't say they spilled it. The moment you say
it, it stops being spilled, and becomes given."

"…What's the difference?"

"…What's spilled is something the gleaner discovers," he said. "What's
given is something the receiver owes. I didn't want you to owe
anything."

I didn't fully understand that answer then. I understand a little of
it now. Even in that moment, he never answered my question directly.
And yet the answer was already inside the refusal. It took me reading
it back many times before I saw it.

"…One more thing," I said. "You've never once been angry. Not when
Medon refused, not when people only ever saw you as a hired hand. Why?"

He thought for a moment.

"…There was no reason to be," he said.

"…Not even when Medon refused? I was angry, that day."

"…You had every right to be angry," he said. "I was watching from a
different place."

"…What place?"

He smiled again, and didn't answer.

I remember that smile exactly, even now. It wasn't the smile of a man
holding back anger. It was the smile of a man whose anger had already,
long ago, turned into something else entirely. I didn't understand the
difference then. I understand a little of it now.

"…I have to go now," he said, standing.

I wanted to hold him back. I had no words to hold him back with.

"…Will I see you again?" I asked.

"…I don't know," he said. "But I think this. The ones who once gleaned
together go on gleaning together, somewhere. Even if it isn't the same
field."

I wanted to ask what he meant, and didn't. As always.

"…Thank you," I said. No other words came.

"…And you," he said.

"…What is there to thank me for? I only ever received."

He looked at me quietly.

"…Receiving is doing something too," he said. "Not everyone knows how
to receive. I liked watching you receive."

I nearly wept at that. I held it back.

He walked toward the gate. A few steps on, he turned back once.

"…Xene."

"…Yes."

"…Keep gleaning," he said. "Even after you no longer need to."

"…Why?"

"…Because if there's someone gleaning, then the one who spills has
meaning too."

And he passed through the gate. I stood there until he was out of
sight.

The road stretched straight out past the gate. Dust rose at its far end
— other travelers walking, a few passing carts. He walked into that
dust, and before long, I could no longer tell him apart from anyone
else.

I felt that moment was strangely familiar. I had once walked the
opposite way down that same road — into a strange city, with no
standing at all. He was walking out of it now. The direction was
opposite. The lightness of the step looked much the same.

I couldn't leave the gate that evening. I kept feeling, more than
once, that he might reappear. He never did. I finally went home once it
had grown dark.

I never saw him again after that day. Not within this book. I never
learned his name. I never learned where he went. I never did find out
whether he'd dropped the grain on purpose. He deflected, to the last,
with a smile.

I've read that conversation over many times, writing this chapter. I
don't think it's that he never answered me, that day. He answered in a
different way. What's spilled is discovered by the gleaner; what's given
is owed by the receiver. I've decided to take that as the method of
this whole book. I cannot prove, exactly, what he did for me. I only
know this. Where he stood, there was always a little more. That's
enough, I think now.

The next day, I went out to the field again.

His spot was still empty. I passed it and went to my own usual place. I
bent and gleaned. My hands moved by their own habit.

I felt something strange, looking at that spot, that day. Too quiet to
call grief; too deep to call nothing at all. I still can't put an exact
name to the feeling somewhere between those two.

That evening, filling my sack with what I'd gathered at the corner, I
found myself remembering what he'd left me with. Keep gleaning. Even
after you no longer need to. Because if there's someone gleaning, then
the one who spills has meaning too.

I think that was the day I finally understood that fully. Not knowing
whether he would ever come back, in a field where he no longer stood, I
kept gleaning, exactly as he'd told me. It was the only goodbye I knew
how to give him.

(Continued in Chapter 48.)

Chapter 48. The Writing Down

_Previously_ — "He" left. I asked him, at the last, whether he'd
dropped the grain on purpose. He smiled and turned the question aside.
"What's spilled is something the gleaner discovers." I never saw him
again after that.

Mena left the next day.

I saw her off at the gate. The same spot where I had said goodbye to
"him" a few days before. The coincidence struck me as strange.

"…Same spot," I said.

"…There's only one gate," Mena said. Plainly. "Everyone comes in through
here, and everyone goes out through here."

The words sounded less simple to me than she meant them.

I looked up at the gate. I tried to count how many times I had passed
through it. Once, coming in. Once, going to my first standing hearing.
Once, on my way to meet Medon. And now I was standing here a second
time, seeing two people off.

That gate never felt unfamiliar to me, somehow. When I first stood
before it, I thought this city was a place meant only to push me away. I
know now. That gate was both a door that pushed away and a door that
took in. The same door had always done both, at once.

Mena didn't have much to carry. A few articles of clothing, a bundle of
papers, some writing tools. Her whole life fit inside that much.

"…You're taking all those records with you?" I asked.

"…Of course," Mena said. "This is my work. Write it, carry it, write
some more."

"…It looks heavy."

"…It is heavy," Mena said, and smiled. "But I can't leave it behind. If
I left it, I'd just be someone who passed through. What's written down
is what turns passing through into staying."

I held that in my heart. I didn't yet know, then, that Mena's own name
meant to remain. When I learned it later, I would come back to this
moment.

We sat a while on the bench.

"…There's something I wanted to ask before you left," I said.

"…What is it."

"…What you found in the reputation field," I said. "Records that came
in from other cities. Did you confirm all of it?"

Mena nodded.

"…I confirmed it," she said. "There was more than I'd expected. Lines
I'd written were being quoted in several cities, each time slightly
differently. The direction was always the same. No explanation became
no repentance. Silence became guilt."

I had heard this before. Hearing it again, it was still heavy.

"…Can you fix it?" I asked.

Mena shook her head.

"…No," she said. "No city is breaking the law. Every one of them
believes it's quoting its own records exactly. I never found a place to
correct it."

"…Then what will you do?"

"…Keep writing," Mena said. "There's no other way. I can't erase a
false quotation. But I can leave one more true record."

I think I understood that, fully, for the first time that day. Mena
wasn't someone who fought. She was only someone who went on writing. I
keep learning that those are two different things, writing this book.

"…Can I ask one more thing?" I said.

"…Ask."

"…These cities," I said, carefully. "Do you think they're all
connected? Seeing how records move between them this way?"

Mena didn't answer for a long time. I felt, the longer that silence
stretched, that something large was approaching.

"…Yes," she said, finally. "I've come to think so."

"…And before?" I asked.

"…Before, I wasn't sure," Mena said. "I traveled through many cities for
a long time. Each one had its own AI, its own rules. A ledger, scales,
a roll. Different names, work that differed a little from place to
place. I thought they were simply different cities, each facing a
similar problem in its own way."

"…And now?"

"…Now it feels like more than that," Mena said. "A line I wrote in one
city shows up, twisted, in exactly the same way, in another city's
precedent. It's too precise to be coincidence. I still don't know
exactly what it is. But—"

She paused for a moment.

"…But what?" I pressed.

Mena took a single sheet of paper from her bag. The last thing she'd
written, she said.

"…I wrote this this morning," she said. "I wanted to show you first."

I took the sheet from her.

A single line was written on it.

"I have come to think these cities are all one city."

I read the sentence several times over.

I set this sentence down here, exactly as it was. I didn't fully
understand, that day, why it mattered so much. I still don't fully
understand it, even now. I know only this. Mena never once wrote this
thought down, in all the years and cities she'd traveled through. And
here, in this city, on a bench near our house, she wrote it for the
first time.

"…What does this mean?" I asked. My voice shook.

"…I don't know exactly, myself," Mena said. "I only wrote down what I
feel. I can't prove it. But I couldn't go on without setting this
feeling down, any longer."

"…Aren't you afraid, writing it?"

Mena looked at me.

"…I'm afraid," she said, honestly. "But not writing it was more
frightening. Because if it's forgotten, it never happened."

Something raced in my chest at that. It was the same reason I had once
found for myself, without her ever teaching it to me.

"…What will you do with this page?" I asked.

"…Carry it," Mena said. "To the next city. And if I find something more
there, I'll write it beside this."

"…What about me?" I asked. "What should I do with this record?"

Mena looked at me for a long time.

"…You keep writing," she said. "What you lived, what you saw. That's
why I came here. To teach you that. Did you know?"

I started to say I hadn't, and realized, saying it, that I had.

"…Can I do this well?" I asked.

"…Doing it well isn't what matters," Mena said. "Being honest is what
matters. I didn't teach you how to write well. I taught you how to write
honestly."

"…Are those different?"

"…They're different," Mena said. "A well-written record moves the
reader. An honest record makes the reader believe it. I think the
second one lasts longer."

Mena stood.

"…I have to go now," she said.

We stood facing each other for a moment. I'd prepared words for this
moment, and forgotten every one of them.

"…Thank you," I finally managed.

"…And you," Mena said. "You're the fastest learner of anyone I've
taught."

"…What did I learn fastest?"

Mena smiled.

"…How to ask," she said. "A recorder isn't someone who knows the
answers. A recorder is someone who keeps asking. You were that, from
the start."

Before Mena left, the administrator came briefly to see her off, too.

I was a little surprised by that. I'd never seen the administrator see
anyone off outside the office before.

"…You came," Mena said.

"…A recorder isn't someone I meet often," the administrator said. "I
wanted to ask you something before you left."

"…Ask."

"…How did you write me?" the administrator asked. "In this record."

Mena looked at him for a moment.

"…Exactly as you are," she said. "A man who keeps the law precisely.
No more, no less."

The administrator nodded. His expression barely changed.

"…That's enough," he said. "I don't want to be written as more than
that. Not as a good man, and not as a bad one. I only did my work."

I've kept that brief exchange a long time. The administrator was the
most frightening person in this entire book, and, at the same time, one
of its most honest. He never worried how he'd look. He only wanted to be
exact about what he'd actually done. Even now, I can't easily bring
myself to hate that honesty. He was never, not once, a bad man in this
book. And I can't write that he was a good one, either. I've decided to
remember him simply as an exact man.

He bowed his head briefly to us and went back to the office. His
retreating figure, and "his" from before, and soon Mena's — I was about
to watch all of them, that day.

She walked toward the gate. I watched her go for a long time. The same
road he'd walked a few days before. A different person, the same road,
the same dust.

I stood at that gate that day and thought of the people who had come
into this city and the people leaving it, side by side. Xene, who had
entered as Xene and stayed as Xene. Mena, who had come as Mena and left
as Mena. He, who came without giving a name and left without giving one.
I thought about how each of the three of us had come to this city, and
passed through it, in our own different way.

I thought, too, that of the three of us, I was the only one who stayed
in this city. Mena went on to the next city. He went to some other
field. I stayed. I don't think that's because I was somehow less than
the other two. I was only, simply, the one whose place this was. Not
everyone has to stay in the same place.

That night, I began writing this record in earnest, for the first time.

I had jotted a few lines before, the way Mena had taught me. That night
I began again, from the start. From the sentence about the outside — how
I didn't know that word until I turned sixteen.

My hand shook, writing that first sentence. I didn't know, until then,
whether I had any right to write down what I'd lived through. After
Mena left, I had to find the answer to that question myself.

Pikra watched me writing and asked.

"…What are you doing."

"…Writing," I said.

"…Writing what."

"…Our story," I said. "From the outside, on."

Pikra came and sat beside me.

"…Why are you writing it," Pikra asked.
